# Batch hypothesis runs

Unreviewed drafts written by AI. Each model got the same question and the same papers.

## 1. How does internal state (hunger, mating status, sleep pressure) switch the fly between competing behaviors, and which neurons in the FlyWire connectome act as the switch?

PubMed search: `internal state behavioral choice neurons`, genes none, 8 papers

### Sonnet 4.6

**Hypothesis:** Tyramine-receptor-expressing (TyrR+) neurons that are presynaptic to P1 courtship command neurons, as identified in the FlyWire connectome, serve as dedicated hunger-state-dependent behavioral switches: acute silencing of these specific TyrR+→P1 connector neurons in starved (24 h food-deprived) mated male w1118 flies will reduce the feeding-over-courtship priority index by ≥40% (i.e., starved-silenced males will initiate courtship within 5 min at rates ≥40% higher than starved GAL4-only controls), while having no significant effect (≤10% change) on courtship latency in fed males, under 25°C, 12:12 LD standard conditions.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: FlyWire Connectome Mapping — Identify all TyrR-expressing neuron types that form direct synapses onto P1 (pC1) courtship command neurons in the FlyWire adult male brain connectome, rank candidates by synapse count, and generate anatomical predictions for intersectional driver targeting.
- Aim 2: Circuit-Level Causal Testing — Use split-GAL4-driven constitutive (Kir2.1) and acute (shi^ts1) silencing of connectome-identified TyrR+→P1 switch neurons to test whether their activity is necessary for hunger-state-dependent suppression of courtship in favor of feeding, quantified as a feeding-versus-courtship priority index in starved versus fed mated male flies.
- Aim 3: Sleep-State Integration — Determine whether the TyrR+→P1 switch neurons also mediate sleep-pressure-dependent suppression of courtship and feeding by applying a 2×2×2 factorial manipulation of hunger, mating status, and sleep deprivation, and testing whether silencing these neurons is sufficient to rescue courtship in sleep-deprived males or whether additional circuit nodes are required.

**Approach:** We will first use the publicly available FlyWire adult male brain connectome to identify all neurons that (1) express TyrR based on transcriptomic cell-type annotations mapped onto connectome identities and (2) form direct chemical synapses onto annotated P1 (pC1) neurons. Candidate 'switch' cell types will be ranked by synapse count and anatomical position. We will then use the split-GAL4 intersectional strategy to restrict UAS-effector expression to TyrR+→P1-adjacent neurons: a DBD line expressed in TyrR+ cells will be combined with an AD line expressed in neurons anatomically overlapping the identified connectome cell types (e.g., using existing TyrR-GAL4 intersected with a connectivity-informed second driver). Behavioral switches will be assayed in a two-choice arena where a starved or fed mated male is simultaneously presented with a conspecific virgin female and a sucrose-yeast food patch; the latency to first feeding contact and latency to first courtship song bout will be measured over 10 min trials. Acute neural silencing will be achieved with UAS-Kir2.1 (constitutive) and UAS-shi^ts1 (temperature-sensitive, 30°C shift), allowing both chronic and acute dissection. Controls will include: GAL4-only, UAS-only, fed versus starved cohorts, and mated versus virgin males. Sleep pressure will be introduced as a third state variable by sleep-depriving a subset of males for 12 h prior to behavioral testing using the sleep nullifying apparatus (SNAP) protocol, enabling a 2×2×2 factorial design (fed/starved × mated/virgin × rested/sleep-deprived) to test whether the identified switch neurons also integrate sleep homeostatic signals.

**Expected outcomes:** Primary outcome: Silencing of TyrR+→P1 connectome-identified switch neurons (UAS-Kir2.1 or shi^ts1 at 30°C) in starved mated males will increase courtship initiation rate by ≥40% and reduce mean feeding latency increase by ≥40% compared to starved GAL4-only controls, quantified as a priority index = [time feeding / (time feeding + time courting)] over 10 min trials (n ≥ 30 per genotype per state, Mann-Whitney U, α = 0.05). Fed males with identical neuronal silencing will show ≤10% change in priority index, confirming state-dependence. Calcium imaging in P1 neurons (GCaMP7s) will show ≥25% reduction in peak ΔF/F response to female cues in starved versus fed controls, restored to fed levels upon TyrR+ neuron silencing. Sleep-deprived males are predicted to show an intermediate priority index shifted toward sleep-seeking (reduced courtship and feeding initiation), and silencing of the switch neurons in sleep-deprived flies will be insufficient to rescue courtship initiation to rested levels (≤15% rescue), indicating that sleep pressure signals engage additional circuit nodes beyond TyrR+→P1 synapses. Statistical threshold: all primary comparisons corrected for multiple testing (Bonferroni), with effect sizes reported as Cohen's d.

**Novelty:** This study addresses three explicitly identified gaps in the literature synthesis. First, no prior work has mapped the TyrR+ and P1 neurons functionally identified by Cheriyamkunnel et al. (PMID 34358444) onto specific FlyWire connectome neuron identities with confirmed connectivity — this experiment directly closes that gap by using connectome-informed intersectional genetics. Second, the interaction between sleep pressure and the feeding/mating switch has not been mechanistically dissected at the circuit level, and no neurons are known to simultaneously receive sleep homeostatic signals and regulate feeding-versus-courtship trade-offs — our 2×2×2 factorial design is the first proposed test of this interaction at single-cell-type resolution. Third, the borboleta region (PMID 35794476) lacks characterized input/output connectivity to P1 and TyrR+ neurons — our connectome-first approach will reveal whether borboleta neurons are upstream, downstream, or parallel to the TyrR+→P1 switch, addressing this structural gap.

**Feasibility:** The FlyWire connectome (adult male brain, v783) is publicly accessible and fully proofread, making in silico identification of TyrR+→P1 synaptic partners feasible within 1–2 months using existing transcriptomic cell-type annotations (e.g., FlyWire CAVE database cross-referenced with FCA single-cell atlas). The primary technical risk is the unavailability of a validated TyrR-splitGAL4-DBD line with sufficient specificity; this may require 4–6 months of fly line generation via CRISPR T2A-splitGAL4 knock-in if community resources are insufficient. The two-choice behavioral arena is established but requires careful counterbalancing of female and food patch position across trials to avoid position bias confounds. The 2×2×2 factorial design requires n ≥ 30 per cell (8 cells = 240 flies minimum per genotype), which is achievable but demands rigorous husbandry. Temperature-shift experiments with shi^ts1 require precise temperature control (±0.5°C) and rapid behavioral scoring. Estimated total timeline: 18–24 months (2 months connectome analysis, 6 months line generation/validation, 4 months behavioral screen, 4 months calcium imaging, 4 months data analysis and writing). Key pitfall: GAL4 lines may not recapitulate endogenous TyrR expression with sufficient fidelity; independent validation with smFISH or immunostaining against TyrR will be required.

### Sonnet 5.5

**Hypothesis:** Proposed (not established): a small set of FlyWire-identified interneuron cell types that sit between TyrR-expressing feeding neurons and P1 courtship neurons acts as a shared state-dependent switch. Optogenetic silencing of this population in 24 h food-deprived, 5-7-day-old virgin male flies (w1118 background, 25°C) will reduce the courtship-over-feeding choice index by at least 30% relative to genetic controls (p<0.05, n>=30 per genotype). In control males, 12 h of mechanical sleep deprivation is predicted to lower the same index by at least 20%, whereas in males with the candidate population silenced the shift will be under 10%.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Connectome mapping of the TyrR-to-P1 interface — identify FlyWire interneuron cell types that connect TyrR-expressing feeding neurons to P1 neurons and rank them as switch candidates.
- Aim 2: Hunger and mating-state causality — test by silencing, activation and calcium imaging whether the top candidates control the courtship-versus-feeding choice index in fed, food-deprived, virgin and mated males.
- Aim 3: Sleep-pressure test — determine whether 12 h sleep deprivation shifts the choice index through the same candidate population, and whether silencing it abolishes the shift.

**Approach:** Aim 1 is a connectome analysis of the FlyWire dataset. It will identify TyrR-expressing feeding neurons and P1 neurons by morphology and cell-type annotation, then enumerate their shortest 1-3 hop paths and shared interneuron partners. It will rank candidates by synapse count, predicted neurotransmitter and convergence with ventral-brain regions such as the borboleta. Aim 2 builds split-GAL4 drivers for the top candidate cell types (or uses existing drivers if verified). These drive UAS-GtACR1 or UAS-Kir2.1 for silencing, UAS-CsChrimson for activation and UAS-GCaMP for calcium imaging. A two-choice assay offers food and a female target to males in four states: fed vs 24 h food-deprived, crossed with sex-deprived vs recently mated. Choice index is scored blind as the fraction of time spent courting vs feeding. Aim 3 adds 12 h mechanical sleep deprivation, checked with DAM or video sleep monitoring and followed by a recovery-sleep readout, to test whether the same neurons mediate sleep-pressure effects. Controls are empty-split/GAL4-only, UAS-only, no-retinal (ATR-free) and light-off groups. Statistics are a genotype x state interaction test (two-way ANOVA or a permutation equivalent) with multiple-comparison correction.

**Expected outcomes:** Proposed prediction: Aim 1 yields 1-5 candidate interneuron cell types with at least 5 synapses of connectivity (threshold to be refined) linking TyrR-expressing neurons and P1 neurons. Aim 2 predicts that silencing the top candidate cell type in 24 h food-deprived males lowers the courtship index by at least 30% versus controls, with no more than a 15% change in locomotion, so that the effect is not generic motor impairment. Calcium responses of the candidate neurons are predicted to differ by at least 25% between fed and food-deprived males. Aim 3 predicts a significant genotype x sleep-deprivation interaction (p<0.05). The hypothesis is falsified if silencing changes the choice index by under 10% in every state, or if the sleep-deprivation effect is identical in silenced and control flies. In that case separate circuits would handle each pair of competing behaviors.

**Novelty:** Paper 34358444 reported that tyramine signaling mediates the feeding-versus-courtship choice in food- and sex-deprived males by inhibiting feeding-promoting TyrR neurons and activating P1 courtship neurons. The supplied literature gives no synaptic path between these populations: no supplied paper uses FlyWire connectome data, so the synaptic connectivity between TyrR-expressing neurons and P1 neurons is unknown. The supplied sleep literature is review-level only. Sleep pressure as a driver of behavioral switching is discussed only in reviews, and no supplied primary study identifies the neurons that switch the fly between sleeping and feeding or mating. No supplied study tests hunger, mating status and sleep pressure together on a single candidate switch neuron population. This proposal tests all three states on one connectome-defined candidate population.

**Feasibility:** Estimated timeline is 18-24 months: connectome analysis 2-3 months, split-GAL4 construction and validation 6-9 months, behavior and imaging 6-9 months, sleep-deprivation experiments 3 months. Pitfalls: (1) the FlyWire dataset is of a female brain, so P1 and TyrR cell-type matching to males is uncertain and may need male-specific data or light-level confirmation; (2) split-GAL4 specificity may be low, so off-target expression must be checked; (3) sleep deprivation can reduce courtship through general effects on arousal, so locomotion and feeding-only controls are required; (4) the neurotransmitter and sign of the candidate connections are predicted rather than measured; (5) all lines require stock verification, since none were confirmed in the supplied literature. Human orthologs are not addressed because the supplied literature does not establish human orthologs of the TyrR or P1 switch circuit, so their translational relevance is unverified.

## 2. Where does the whole-brain connectome fail to predict behavior? Which strongly connected pathways have no measurable effect when silenced?

PubMed search: `connectome behavior prediction neural circuits`, genes none, 12 papers

### Sonnet 4.6

**Hypothesis:** At least 30% of the top-200 strongest inter-region connectome edges (ranked by total synaptic weight in the Dorkenwald et al. 2024 female adult Drosophila central brain connectome) will produce no statistically significant behavioral change (p > 0.05, effect size Cohen's d < 0.2) when their presynaptic neuronal populations are acutely silenced with UAS-Kir2.1 or UAS-TNT, measured across a standardized panel of four behaviors (odor-evoked approach/avoidance, proboscis extension reflex, locomotor speed, and courtship initiation) in mated female w1118 flies at 25°C under 12:12 LD conditions, and that these behaviorally silent strong connections will be enriched (≥2-fold over chance) in pathways lacking neuromodulatory receptor gene expression as identified by single-cell transcriptomic data.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Connectome edge prioritization and driver validation — Computationally extract the top 200 inter-region edges by total synaptic weight from the Dorkenwald et al. 2024 connectome, match each presynaptic cell population to an existing or constructed GAL4 driver, and validate driver expression specificity by confocal imaging of UAS-mCD8::GFP in adult female brains (n ≥ 5 per driver).
- Aim 2: Systematic behavioral silencing screen — Cross each validated GAL4 driver to UAS-Kir2.1 (with tub-GAL80ts for adult-restricted silencing) and measure all four standardized behaviors (odor T-maze, PER, open-field locomotion, courtship index) in mated female flies (n ≥ 30 per genotype, three independent crosses), computing Cohen's d and adjusted p-values for each driver-behavior pair to generate a quantitative map of synaptic weight versus behavioral effect size.
- Aim 3: Mechanistic dissection of silent strong connections — For all edges classified as behaviorally silent (Cohen's d < 0.2, p > 0.05), test whether presynaptic neurons express neuromodulatory receptor transcripts (dopamine, octopamine, serotonin receptors) below brain-wide median using Fly Cell Atlas single-cell data, and validate a subset of silent connections by re-testing under neuromodulatory challenge conditions (24h starvation, octopamine receptor agonist feeding) to determine whether behavioral silence is context-dependent.

**Approach:** We will perform a systematic silencing screen of connectome-predicted high-weight inter-region pathways in the adult Drosophila central brain using the GAL4/UAS system. First, the top 200 inter-region edges by total synaptic weight will be extracted from the publicly released Dorkenwald et al. 2024 connectome dataset, and split-GAL4 or existing GAL4 driver lines will be matched to the presynaptic cell types for each edge using the Janelia FlyLight and Vienna Tile GAL4 collections; each driver will be validated by GFP expression to confirm restricted expression. Second, each driver will be crossed to UAS-Kir2.1 (chronic silencing) and, for a subset, to UAS-TNT (synaptic silencing) to generate experimental flies; Canton-S/w1118 × driver and driver × UAS-mCD8::GFP crosses will serve as genetic controls. Third, all experimental and control genotypes will be subjected to a standardized four-behavior panel: (1) T-maze odor-evoked valence (attractive apple cider vinegar vs. aversive benzaldehyde), (2) sucrose proboscis extension reflex (PER) assay, (3) open-field locomotor tracking (5 min, DAM-equivalent single-fly arenas), and (4) male courtship index in mixed-sex chambers; each behavioral assay will use n ≥ 30 flies per genotype across three independent crosses. Fourth, behavioral effect sizes (Cohen's d) will be computed for each driver/behavior combination relative to controls, and the distribution of effect sizes will be regressed against synaptic weight and against neuromodulatory receptor expression scores derived from published single-cell transcriptomic datasets (FCA, Fly Cell Atlas) to test whether low modulatory receptor expression predicts behavioral silence.

**Expected outcomes:** We predict that ≥30% of the top-200 high-weight connectome edges will show no significant behavioral change upon silencing (Cohen's d < 0.2, p > 0.05 by two-sample t-test with Bonferroni correction for 200 × 4 = 800 comparisons, α = 0.05/800 ≈ 6.25 × 10⁻⁵). Among the four behavioral assays, we expect the PER and odor-valence assays to show the highest prediction success rates (≥60% of edges affecting these behaviors will show significant effects), consistent with prior connectome model validation (Shiu et al. 2024; Someya et al. 2025), while locomotor and courtship assays are predicted to show higher failure rates given the greater degeneracy in central complex and courtship circuitry. Behaviorally silent high-weight edges are predicted to be enriched in pathways where presynaptic neurons have low expression of neuromodulatory receptors (dopamine, octopamine, serotonin receptor transcripts from Fly Cell Atlas) compared to behaviorally effective edges (Fisher's exact test, expected OR ≥ 2.0, p < 0.05). We further expect that a subset of behaviorally silent edges will become significant when flies are tested under neuromodulatory challenge (e.g., starvation or heat stress), confirming context-dependent behavioral relevance. Primary statistical approach: linear mixed model with synaptic weight, brain region pair, and neurotransmitter identity as fixed effects and replicate cross as random effect.

**Novelty:** No prior study has performed a systematic, connectome-ranked silencing screen mapping synaptic weight quantitatively to behavioral effect size across multiple behaviors in the same fly line panel. The identified gaps establish that (1) the Shiu et al. 2024 whole-brain model was validated only for feeding and grooming and has not been tested across the full behavioral repertoire; (2) degeneracy in pathway elements is acknowledged (Meinertzhagen 2016) but has never been quantified in terms of what fraction of dense connectome edges are functionally redundant; (3) the hypothesis that neuromodulatory receptor expression—absent from static wiring diagrams—explains a class of prediction failures is proposed but untested; and (4) brain-wide matching of transcriptomic to connectomic cell types is incomplete (Bates et al. 2019), making molecular competence of anatomically connected neurons uncharacterized. This experiment directly quantifies the gap between structural connectivity rank and behavioral effect size and tests a mechanistic explanation (low modulatory receptor expression) for silent strong connections.

**Feasibility:** Technical feasibility is high: the Dorkenwald et al. 2024 connectome dataset is publicly available and edge weights can be extracted computationally; the GAL4/UAS silencing approach is standard and well-validated in Drosophila. The main challenge is driver availability and specificity — matching GAL4 lines to connectome-defined cell types is non-trivial and some high-weight presynaptic populations may lack specific drivers, requiring split-GAL4 construction (adds 6-12 months per novel line). Estimated timeline: Month 1-2, computational extraction of top-200 edges and driver-cell-type matching; Month 3-6, driver validation by GFP imaging (confocal, n=5 brains per line); Month 7-18, behavioral screen (batches of 20 genotypes per month, four assays each, n=30 per genotype); Month 19-22, transcriptomic correlation analysis and follow-up neuromodulatory challenge experiments; Month 23-24, data synthesis and manuscript preparation. Total: ~24 months with a team of 2-3 experimenters. Major pitfalls: (1) GAL4 driver expression bleeding into non-target neurons — mitigated by split-GAL4 intersectional approach and anatomical validation; (2) Kir2.1 developmental lethality or sickness confounding behavioral results — mitigated by using tub-GAL80ts for adult-stage restricted expression with temperature shift to 30°C for 48h before assay; (3) behavioral assays may lack sensitivity for subtle phenotypes — mitigated by large sample sizes and multiple complementary assays.

### Sonnet 5.5

**Hypothesis:** Proposed: In adult mated female flies at 25°C, acute silencing of cell types carrying the 30 highest-weight connectome edges (at least 100 synapses per connection in FlyWire, selected across gustatory-feeding, antennal-grooming and olfactory-valence circuits) will produce no significant behavioral effect (equivalence within ±0.5 SD, TOST p<0.05) for at least 25% of these edges. The null rate will be at least twofold higher for edges whose presynaptic cell type has a parallel same-neurotransmitter partner with output cosine similarity of 0.5 or more than for edges without such a partner. Combinatorial silencing of the redundant partners will convert at least half of the redundancy-associated null cases into significant phenotypes (Cohen's d of 0.8 or more).

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Quantify null edges — silence cell types carrying 30 top-weighted connectome edges across three circuits and test each for equivalence to controls in a matched behavioral assay, comparing outcomes with connectome-model predictions.
- Aim 2: Test redundancy — compare null rates between edges with and without parallel same-neurotransmitter partners (output cosine similarity ≥0.5) and test whether combinatorial silencing of partners unmasks phenotypes.
- Aim 3: Test state dependence — determine whether starvation or mating state converts null strong pathways into behaviorally effective ones.

**Approach:** Proposed: Select 30 high-weight edges from the FlyWire connectome in three circuits for which model-predicted behaviors exist (proboscis extension/feeding, antennal grooming, odor attraction/aversion). For each, use cell-type-specific split-GAL4 drivers to express UAS-Kir2.1 under tub-GAL80ts control, so silencing is restricted to adulthood and developmental compensation is minimized. Score each genotype in the circuit's matched assay (n≥30 flies per genotype, 3 independent cohorts) against empty-split-GAL4>UAS-Kir2.1 and GAL4/UAS-only controls, with a second silencer (UAS-TNT, or UAS-shibire-ts if a suitable stock is verified) to confirm nulls. Test nulls for equivalence (TOST) rather than failure to reject. Confirm that each driver is expressed in the intended cell type, and confirm silencing efficacy by calcium imaging or an assay of a known positive-control phenotype. Compare outcomes with the predicted effect from a leaky integrate-and-fire connectome model. Then silence parallel partners pairwise, and repeat assays under starved versus fed states to test whether neuromodulatory state unmasks null pathways.

**Expected outcomes:** Proposed predictions, not established results: Of 30 edges, at least 8 (≥25%) will show equivalence to controls within ±0.5 SD, while the remainder will show phenotypes consistent with model predictions. The null rate will be at least twofold higher among edges with a redundant parallel partner (cosine similarity ≥0.5) than among edges without one (Fisher's exact test, p<0.05). Pairwise silencing of redundant partners will yield significant phenotypes (d≥0.8, Holm-corrected p<0.05) in at least 50% of redundancy-associated nulls. Starvation or other state manipulation is predicted to convert at least 20% of the remaining nulls. If fewer than 10% of edges are null, or null rates do not differ by redundancy, the hypothesis is falsified.

**Novelty:** The provided set contains no systematic silencing screen of strongly connected pathways that reports null behavioral phenotypes, so the frequency of strong synapses without behavioral effect is unquantified. Existing connectome-based models were validated mainly on pathways with strong phenotypes such as feeding, grooming and odor valence. Degeneracy is named as a confound for silencing, but the literature does not identify which Drosophila pathways are degenerate. This proposal quantifies null edges, tests redundancy as an explanation by combinatorial silencing, and tests state dependence, which the connectome literature does not cover.

**Feasibility:** Estimated timeline is 18-24 months: 3 months for edge selection, modeling and line sourcing; 8-10 months for single-silencing screens; 4-6 months for combinatorial and state experiments; 2 months for analysis. Pitfalls: split-GAL4 lines may be unavailable for some cell types (the edge list will be filtered to those with verified drivers); incomplete silencing can mimic a null (mitigated by a second silencer and efficacy checks); behavioral assays may be insensitive to a pathway's true function (mitigated by testing multiple assays per circuit); and one connectome from a single female brain may not match the tested flies' wiring. Combinatorial genotypes require careful GAL4 or LexA design and may need verification of available reagents.

## 3. What signal tells the brain that sleep need has been discharged, and does it come from neurons, glia or peripheral tissues?

PubMed search: `sleep homeostasis sleep need signal`, genes none, 12 papers

### Sonnet 4.6

**Hypothesis:** Termination of astroglial Spätzle/Toll signaling to R5 neurons constitutes the primary 'sleep-discharge' signal in Drosophila melanogaster: specifically, we hypothesize that sufficient sleep reduces astroglial L-type Ca2+ channel activity and TyrRII levels by ≥40% relative to sleep-deprived controls (measured by GCaMP fluorescence and RT-qPCR), that this reduction decreases Spätzle release and R5 Toll-receptor activation by ≥30%, and that acute genetic blockade of astroglial Ca2+ signaling during an ongoing sleep bout in 3–5 day-old mated female w1118 flies at 25°C will truncate sleep rebound by ≥50% compared to genetic controls, demonstrating that astroglial Ca2+ reduction is necessary for sleep-need discharge.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Astroglial Ca2+ reset dynamics — Determine the temporal profile of astroglial GCaMP7s signal and TyrRII mRNA decline during sleep rebound, and test whether Ca2+ reduction precedes full restoration of sleep bout structure.
- Aim 2: Causal necessity of Ca2+ reduction for sleep discharge — Test whether acute, temporally restricted astroglial blockade (TNT or L-type Ca2+ channel RNAi) during the rebound window truncates sleep rebound by ≥50%, establishing Ca2+ termination as causally necessary for sleep-need discharge.
- Aim 3: Peripheral tissue screen — Determine whether fat body or gut tissues display sleep-pressure-correlated Ca2+ dynamics that could contribute a peripheral discharge signal to the brain under standard laboratory conditions.

**Approach:** We will use an intersectional GAL4/UAS strategy to manipulate and monitor astroglial Ca2+ signaling specifically during sleep rebound after 12-hour mechanical sleep deprivation (vortex method). Repo-GAL4 (pan-glial) will be intersected with R58E02-GAL80 (R5-neuron exclusion) to restrict UAS-GCaMP7s expression to astrocytes, allowing live two-photon imaging of Ca2+ dynamics at defined sleep-rebound time points (0 h, 2 h, 4 h, 8 h post-deprivation). In parallel, UAS-TNT (tetanus toxin, to block vesicular release) and UAS-Dnai2-RNAi (to inhibit L-type Ca2+ channel) will be conditionally expressed in astrocytes using a temperature-sensitive tub-GAL80ts system: flies will be shifted from 18°C (GAL80ts repression) to 30°C (GAL80ts derepression) at the start of the rebound window to acutely block astroglial signaling only during recovery sleep. Sleep will be quantified using the Drosophila Activity Monitor (DAM) system with ≥30 flies per genotype per replicate across three independent biological replicates. Peripheral tissue contribution will be tested in a secondary aim using FB-GAL4 (fat body) and Myo1A-GAL4 (gut) drivers expressing UAS-GCaMP7s to screen for Ca2+ or redox changes correlated with sleep pressure, compared to astroglial dynamics. All experiments use age-matched, mated female w1118 background flies at 25°C, 12:12 LD cycle, with genetic driver-only and UAS-only sibling controls.

**Expected outcomes:** Aim 1: Astroglial GCaMP7s signal (ΔF/F) will decrease by ≥40% within 4 h of sleep rebound onset relative to the 0-h post-deprivation peak, and TyrRII mRNA measured by RT-qPCR will decrease by ≥40% at 8 h rebound vs. sleep-deprived controls (paired t-test, α=0.05, power=0.80, n≥15 fly brains per time point). If the hypothesis is correct, Ca2+ decrease will precede rather than follow restoration of normal sleep bout duration. Aim 2: Acute astroglial blockade (tub-GAL80ts + Repo-GAL4 > UAS-TNT) during the rebound window will reduce total sleep gained in 12 h post-deprivation by ≥50% relative to GAL4-only and UAS-only controls, without altering baseline sleep in non-deprived flies (DAM system, ANOVA with Tukey post-hoc, n≥30 flies per group, three replicates). Aim 3: Fat body and gut GCaMP7s signals will show no significant correlation (r<0.3) with astroglial Ca2+ dynamics across the sleep-deprivation/rebound cycle, providing negative evidence against a dominant peripheral discharge signal under standard conditions. Null results in Aim 3 would direct future work toward neuronal/astroglial mechanisms; positive correlations would constitute a novel finding warranting follow-up.

**Novelty:** This study directly addresses the most critical identified gap: the literature describes how sleep need is encoded and transmitted (astroglial Ca2+ → Spätzle → Toll → R5) but explicitly does not identify what molecular event signals sleep need has been *discharged*. No prior study has examined whether reduction of astroglial Ca2+ or Spätzle release serves as the off-signal, nor has any study tested whether manipulating glia during (rather than before) a sleep rebound truncates it. Additionally, the peripheral tissue contribution to sleep homeostasis is wholly unaddressed in current Drosophila literature, and this proposal includes a prospective screen of fat body and gut. The temporal, conditional manipulation design (GAL80ts restricted to rebound window) is mechanistically distinct from all prior constitutive knockdown or overexpression studies in the field.

**Feasibility:** Core genetic tools (Repo-GAL4, tub-GAL80ts, UAS-GCaMP7s, DAM system) are established and widely available; temperature-shift protocols for GAL80ts are well-characterized for 12 h windows. Two-photon imaging of GCaMP in Drosophila brains during and after sleep deprivation has technical precedent but requires a sleeping-fly preparation, adding complexity — restrained preparation or ex vivo imaging within 10 min of sacrifice will be validated in pilot experiments. The intersectional LexA/GAL4 two-color imaging (Aim 1 + simultaneous R5 readout) is technically demanding and may require sequential rather than simultaneous imaging in initial experiments. Estimated timeline: months 1–3, stock acquisition and backcrossing; months 4–6, baseline Ca2+ imaging validation and DAM sleep profiling; months 7–12, conditional blockade experiments; months 13–18, peripheral tissue screen and data integration. Primary pitfall: Repo-GAL4 drives expression in all glia, not astrocytes exclusively — astrocyte-specific alrm-GAL4 (if available and verified) should be tested in parallel to confirm astrocyte specificity. A second pitfall is that 30°C temperature shifts required for GAL80ts derepression may independently affect sleep; temperature-shifted w1118 and driver-only controls at 30°C must be included in every experiment.

### Sonnet 5.5

**Hypothesis:** Proposed (not established): In 3-5-day-old mated female flies at 25°C, a fall in astroglial Ca2+ during recovery sleep reports discharge of sleep need. After 12 h of overnight mechanical sleep deprivation, astroglial GCaMP signal will fall by ≥30% from its post-deprivation peak by the time 50% of the lost sleep has been recovered. Optogenetically holding astroglial Ca2+ elevated during recovery will extend rebound sleep by ≥30% relative to no-retinal controls. Knocking down spz in fat body, gut or muscle will change rebound sleep by <10%.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Astroglial Ca2+ during sleep discharge — image astrocyte GCaMP across baseline, 12 h deprivation and recovery sleep, and test whether the signal falls ≥30% by 50% recovery of lost sleep.
- Aim 2: Necessity of Ca2+ decline and Spz/Toll signaling for sleep termination — clamp astroglial Ca2+ high during recovery only, and test whether astrocytic spz or R5 Toll knockdown suppresses the extended rebound.
- Aim 3: Peripheral-tissue contribution — knock down spz in fat body, gut and muscle and test whether rebound sleep after deprivation changes by ≥10%.

**Approach:** Aim 1 uses an astrocyte-specific GAL4 to express GCaMP and image astroglial Ca2+ in vivo across baseline, 12 h sleep deprivation and recovery sleep, with simultaneous behavioral sleep scoring. Aim 2 uses an astrocyte-specific GAL4 to express a red-shifted channelrhodopsin and clamp Ca2+ high only during recovery sleep, using all-trans-retinal-fed versus vehicle-fed siblings, light-only controls and GAL4-only and UAS-only genotypes. This is paired with spz or Toll knockdown (in astrocytes and R5 neurons respectively) to test whether sustained signaling through the Spz/Toll pathway is required for the prolonged rebound. Aim 3 tests peripheral tissues by knocking down spz in fat body, gut and muscle with tissue-specific drivers and measuring baseline sleep, sleep loss, and rebound at 25°C. Sleep is scored with DAM/ethoscope-type activity monitoring (5-min inactivity criterion), with n≥32 flies per genotype and condition, and genotypes are compared by ANOVA with correction for multiple comparisons.

**Expected outcomes:** Proposed predictions: (1) astroglial GCaMP signal rises after deprivation and falls ≥30% from peak by 50% recovery of lost sleep (paired test, p<0.05). (2) Retinal-fed flies with astroglial Ca2+ clamped high during recovery show ≥30% longer rebound or ≥30% greater cumulative rebound than controls (p<0.05). (3) Astrocytic spz or R5 Toll knockdown suppresses this prolongation by ≥50%, placing Spz/Toll downstream of astroglial Ca2+ in sustaining sleep drive. (4) Peripheral spz knockdown changes rebound by <10% and shows no significant effect. If Ca2+ does not fall during recovery, or clamping it high does not alter rebound duration, the hypothesis is falsified.

**Novelty:** Astroglial Ca2+ signaling increases with sleep need and is required for homeostatic sleep rebound [33186550], but no supplied paper identifies a signal that reports discharge of sleep need. The synthesis also lists that it is unknown whether astroglial Ca2+ or TyrRII levels fall during recovery sleep, and whether any fall is required for sleep termination rather than sleep induction. Ca2+ clamping during recovery isolates termination from induction. None of the supplied abstracts tests peripheral tissues as a source of a sleep-need or sleep-discharge signal, so the peripheral arm addresses a second stated gap. Because the mechanism that terminates Spätzle release or Toll signaling in R5 neurons is not described, the Spz/Toll epistasis tests address a third gap.

**Feasibility:** Estimated timeline is 18 months: months 1-4 for line verification, construction and validation; 5-10 for imaging with behavior; 11-14 for optogenetic clamping and epistasis; 15-18 for peripheral knockdowns and analysis. Pitfalls: (a) deprivation itself and imaging preparations may stress flies and confound Ca2+ readouts, so non-deprived imaged controls are needed; (b) optogenetic light can disrupt circadian or behavioral state, so light-only and no-retinal controls are required; (c) RNAi efficacy and off-target effects need validation with a second independent RNAi or knockdown measurement; (d) spz also has immune roles in peripheral tissues, so tissue-specific knockdown may alter health, which requires lifespan or survival checks; (e) all stock identities and availability are unverified here.

## 4. How does one neuromodulator (dopamine or octopamine) reconfigure the same circuit to produce different outputs in different contexts?

PubMed search: `neuromodulation dopamine octopamine circuit`, genes none, 7 papers

### Sonnet 4.6

**Hypothesis:** The single octopaminergic neuron VPM4 reconfigures the mushroom body output circuit in a hunger-state-dependent manner by suppressing Dop1R2-mediated MBON activity: silencing VPM4 in hungry (24-hour starved) w1118 female flies will increase odor-tracking persistence scores by ≥40% relative to fed controls, while simultaneous loss of Dop1R2 in the same MBONs will abolish this increase, measured as number of consecutive tracking bouts in a standardized 10-trial olfactory persistence assay at 25°C.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: State-dependent behavioral reconfiguration — test whether constitutive silencing of VPM4 selectively increases odor-tracking persistence in hungry but not fed w1118 female flies by ≥40% in a 10-trial olfactory persistence assay
- Aim 2: Epistasis of dopaminergic and octopaminergic inputs — determine whether Dop1R2 knockdown in the two odor-tracking MBONs suppresses the VPM4-silencing-induced persistence increase in hungry flies, establishing the hierarchical relationship between the two neuromodulatory inputs
- Aim 3: Real-time MBON reconfiguration by state and VPM4 activity — use in vivo GCaMP7s imaging combined with optogenetic VPM4 activation (CsChrimson) to quantify how hunger state attenuates VPM4's suppression of odor-evoked MBON Ca2+ responses, providing a mechanistic cellular correlate for state-dependent circuit switching

**Approach:** We will use intersectional GAL4/UAS and Split-GAL4 strategies to selectively manipulate VPM4 and the two odor-tracking MBONs identified in Sayin et al. 2019. First, a VPM4-specific Split-GAL4 driver (requiring verification of availability from the Bhaskara/Bhaskara lab or BDSC) will be used to express UAS-Kir2.1 (constitutive silencing) or UAS-TNT (tetanus toxin light chain) in VPM4 to test whether VPM4 silencing in hungry flies phenocopies increased persistence. Second, Dop1R2 RNAi will be expressed in the two odor-tracking MBONs using a verified MBON-specific GAL4 driver to assess whether dopaminergic input is required for the persistence increase caused by VPM4 silencing. Third, in vivo calcium imaging (UAS-GCaMP7s) will be performed in the MBONs of fed versus 24-hour starved flies during odor presentation, with and without optogenetic activation of VPM4 (UAS-CsChrimson), to measure how VPM4 activity suppresses MBON Ca2+ responses in a state-dependent manner. All behavioral assays will use a standardized 10-trial olfactory persistence paradigm adapted from Sayin et al. 2019, with vinegar odor as the food-associated stimulus; controls will include GAL4/+ and UAS/+ heterozygotes, as well as fed versus starved cohorts for each genotype.

**Expected outcomes:** Aim 1: VPM4-silenced hungry flies will show ≥40% increase in mean consecutive odor-tracking bouts (from ~4 to ≥6 bouts per 10-trial session) compared to hungry GAL4/+ and UAS/+ heterozygous controls; fed VPM4-silenced flies will show no significant change (p > 0.05, ANOVA with Tukey's post-hoc, n ≥ 20 flies per group). Aim 2: Co-expression of Dop1R2-RNAi in MBONs of VPM4-silenced hungry flies will suppress the persistence increase to wild-type hungry levels (≤10% above baseline), confirming that dopaminergic Dop1R2 signaling is epistatic to VPM4 octopaminergic input. Aim 3: GCaMP7s imaging in MBONs will show that optogenetic VPM4 activation reduces odor-evoked MBON Ca2+ peak amplitude by ≥30% (ΔF/F) in fed flies but by ≤10% in 24-hour starved flies, demonstrating that hunger state attenuates VPM4's suppressive effect on MBONs; statistical threshold: paired t-test, p < 0.05, n ≥ 10 flies per condition.

**Novelty:** This experiment directly addresses two explicitly identified gaps in the literature synthesis: (1) how internal state dynamically shifts the relative weighting of dopaminergic versus octopaminergic input onto mushroom body output neurons in real time — currently unmeasured with simultaneous neuromodulator-resolution imaging; and (2) whether dopamine and octopamine act on the same postsynaptic compartments or separate dendritic domains of MBONs — currently unclear. While Sayin et al. 2019 (PMID 31471123) established that VPM4 acts as a brake and that Dop1R2 promotes persistence, no study has (a) measured how hunger quantitatively shifts VPM4's suppressive efficacy on MBON Ca2+ dynamics in real time, or (b) tested the epistatic relationship between VPM4 octopaminergic input and Dop1R2 dopaminergic input at the level of individual identified MBONs. The proposed simultaneous optogenetic-plus-imaging design in state-defined animals is not present in the supplied literature.

**Feasibility:** The core behavioral paradigm is established in Sayin et al. 2019 and is technically feasible in a standard fly lab. The main risk is availability of the VPM4-specific Split-GAL4 driver — this must be confirmed with the authors of PMID 31471123 or through BDSC before experiments begin; if unavailable, a Tdc2-GAL4 intersectional approach with a VPM4 morphological marker could substitute but would reduce specificity. In vivo two-photon or confocal calcium imaging in MBONs is technically demanding (~6 months of optimization) but is standard in connectomics-era Drosophila labs. Optogenetic experiments require all-trans-retinal supplementation in food (0.2 mM) and appropriate light delivery controls. Estimated timeline: line verification and acquisition (months 1–2), behavioral optimization (months 2–4), imaging setup (months 3–6), full experimental runs (months 5–10), analysis and replication (months 10–14). Primary pitfall: hunger state is difficult to standardize precisely — flies must be assayed at a fixed time window (e.g., 20–24 hours post-food removal) with hemolymph glucose monitoring as a proxy. A secondary pitfall is that Kir2.1 developmental silencing of VPM4 may cause compensatory rewiring; a conditional silencer (UAS-shi[ts1] at restrictive temperature) should be included as a parallel tool.

### Sonnet 5.5

**Hypothesis:** Proposed (not established): In 24-h starved mated female flies, but not in fed flies, the MBON innervated by the octopaminergic neuron VPM4 integrates a Dop1R2-dependent dopaminergic persistence signal and an octopamine-receptor-dependent VPM4 braking signal. Prediction 1: MBON-restricted RNAi knockdown of Dop1R2 will reduce odor-tracking persistence by at least 30% relative to genetic controls in starved flies. Prediction 2: knockdown of the relevant octopamine receptor in the same MBON will increase persistence by at least 30% in starved flies. Prediction 3: both effects will be 10% or smaller in fed flies (n >= 30 flies per genotype, p < 0.01, 25 C). Prediction 4: VPM4 optogenetic activation will change the MBON cAMP FRET response to dopaminergic stimulation by at least 25%, and this change will depend on state and on the octopamine receptor.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Receptor requirement in the MBON — test whether MBON-restricted Dop1R2 and candidate octopamine receptor knockdowns bidirectionally change odor-tracking persistence in starved versus fed flies.
- Aim 2: cAMP integration — measure MBON cAMP responses to VPM4 and dopaminergic input activation in fed versus starved flies, with and without receptor knockdown.
- Aim 3: Interaction test — determine by double knockdown whether the dopamine and octopamine receptor arms combine additively or non-additively, and whether the interaction depends on hunger state.

**Approach:** Use a split-GAL4 or LexA driver for the VPM4-innervated MBON, with the identity and driver to be verified from the cited study, to express receptor RNAi (Dop1R2, candidate OctβR/OAMB receptors) cell-autonomously. Test fed versus 24-h starved flies in an odor-tracking assay that measures persistence versus withdrawal. Run a parallel two-photon imaging experiment with a genetically encoded cAMP sensor in the MBON, using CsChrimson activation of VPM4 and of the dopaminergic input, in fed and starved flies. Controls: driver-only and UAS-only parents, a non-targeting or mCherry RNAi, a second independent RNAi per receptor, and a receptor-knockdown-by-driver-in-unrelated-neuron control. Epistasis: Dop1R2 plus octopamine receptor double knockdown versus single knockdowns to test additive versus non-additive integration.

**Expected outcomes:** Starved Dop1R2-knockdown flies show at least 30% lower persistence than controls. Octopamine-receptor-knockdown flies show at least 30% higher persistence. Fed flies show 10% or smaller differences. Imaging shows at least a 25% state-dependent shift in the cAMP FRET response when VPM4 and dopaminergic inputs are co-activated compared with either alone, which would indicate non-additive integration. Double knockdown testing: if the two arms act on a shared node, the double knockdown will differ from the sum of the single effects (interaction term p < 0.05). Falsification: no effect of MBON receptor knockdown on persistence, or equal effects in fed and starved flies, would reject the hypothesis.

**Novelty:** Paper 31471123 shows that dopaminergic neurons and Dop1R2 signaling, together with two MBONs, control behavioral persistence in hungry flies, and that the octopaminergic neuron VPM4 acts as a brake on odor tracking. How these two inputs converge on the same MBON at the receptor and cAMP level is not resolved in the supplied literature. Paper 32277043 shows that OctβR acts in different cell types for aversive and appetitive learning and that cAMP signaling is a key downstream effector for OctβR function. Whether dopamine-octopamine receptor interactions extend to state-dependent persistence has not been tested. This proposal tests the receptor-level and cAMP-level convergence directly in one identified MBON across hunger states.

**Feasibility:** Estimated 12-15 months: 3 months to verify drivers and obtain or build RNAi lines, 4 months for behavior, 4 months for imaging, 2 months for double knockdown and analysis. Pitfalls: the identity of the octopamine receptor in the MBON is unknown and may require screening of several receptors; RNAi knockdown efficiency needs validation; optogenetic stimulation may recruit non-target neurons; cAMP sensor signals in small MBON dendrites can be noisy; the behavioral assay needs standardization of starvation and odor. Stock numbers are not asserted here and must be verified before ordering.

## 5. Why do isogenic flies raised in identical conditions show stable individual differences in behavior, and which developmental variation causes them?

PubMed search: `individuality behavior isogenic variation`, genes none, 5 papers

### Sonnet 4.6

**Hypothesis:** Stochastic variation in histone H3K9 acetylation at promoters of central complex development genes during the larval-to-pupal transition (hours 0–24 APF) causally determines stable adult locomotor handedness bias in isogenic w1118 Drosophila melanogaster, such that pharmacological reduction of HDAC activity (via 5 mM sodium butyrate feeding) during this window will reduce the variance in adult handedness scores (measured as |lateralization index|) by ≥30% compared to vehicle controls, while the same treatment applied outside this window (adult stage) will produce no significant change in handedness variance, in a cohort of ≥200 flies per condition at 25°C on standard food.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Critical-window mapping — Determine whether sodium butyrate feeding restricted to defined developmental windows (embryonic 0–24 h, larval L1–L3, 0–24 h APF, 24–48 h APF, or adult day 1–5) reduces variance in adult locomotor handedness (|LI|) in isogenic w1118 flies, identifying the window of maximum effect.
- Aim 2: Epigenetic profiling — Characterize the temporal dynamics and inter-individual variance of H3K9ac at central complex developmental gene loci (rx, ey, toy) across the larval-to-pupal transition (0, 12, 24 h APF) using CUT&RUN in staged w1118 pupae, and test whether sodium butyrate treatment during the critical window identified in Aim 1 reduces this epigenetic variance.
- Aim 3: Neuronal causal validation — Test whether pupal-restricted (0–24 h APF), central-complex-specific (R9D11-GAL4 with GAL80ts) knockdown of Hdac3 recapitulates the reduction in adult locomotor handedness variance seen with systemic pharmacological HDAC inhibition, establishing cell-autonomous epigenetic stochasticity in central complex neurons as a causal source of behavioral individuality.

**Approach:** We will use a combination of pharmacological HDAC inhibition (sodium butyrate feeding) applied in temporally restricted windows across Drosophila development to identify the critical period during which epigenetic stochasticity is set. Locomotor handedness will be quantified using a Y-maze assay across ≥200 isogenic w1118 flies per treatment group, scoring left/right choice at each branch point over 20 consecutive trials per fly to compute a lateralization index (LI = (R–L)/(R+L)); variance in |LI| will be the primary endpoint. Concurrently, we will use CUT&RUN chromatin profiling on dissected larval/pupal central complexes at 0, 12, and 24 h APF to map H3K9ac levels at candidate central-complex development gene loci (including rx, ey, and toy) in individual-pooled samples, correlating epigenetic variance with downstream behavioral variance. To establish causal neuronal specificity, we will employ a conditional UAS-dHDAC3-RNAi line driven by the central-complex-specific R9D11-GAL4 driver during pupal development (using a temperature-sensitive GAL80ts to restrict knockdown to 0–24 h APF at 29°C) and measure adult handedness variance. Parallel controls will include vehicle-treated w1118, adult-stage sodium butyrate treatment, and R9D11-GAL4/+ and UAS-dHDAC3-RNAi/+ heterozygous controls.

**Expected outcomes:** Primary outcome: Sodium butyrate (5 mM) feeding restricted to 0–24 h APF will reduce the variance of |LI| in adult w1118 flies by ≥30% relative to vehicle controls (F-test for variance equality, p < 0.05, n ≥ 200 per group), with mean |LI| unchanged (indicating dampened stochasticity, not a directional shift). Adult-stage sodium butyrate treatment will show <10% change in variance (non-significant). CUT&RUN will reveal that H3K9ac variance across central-complex loci is significantly higher at 12 h APF than at 0 h APF (Levene's test, p < 0.05), and that sodium butyrate reduces this epigenetic variance at the same loci. Pupal-restricted R9D11-GAL4–driven Hdac3-RNAi will phenocopy pharmacological HDAC inhibition, reducing adult |LI| variance by ≥25% relative to GAL4/+ and UAS/+ heterozygous controls (n ≥ 150 per genotype, p < 0.05). Conversely, UAS-HDAC3 overexpression during the same window is predicted to increase variance. If sodium butyrate treatment outside the 0–24 h APF window fails to alter behavioral variance while treatment within it succeeds, this will confirm a critical developmental window for epigenetic individuality.

**Novelty:** This experiment directly addresses two major gaps identified in the literature synthesis. First, the specific developmental time windows during which stochastic variation in gene expression or neural circuit wiring is established remain uncharacterized (Gap 1), and this design uses temporally gated pharmacology and GAL80ts to resolve this. Second, the mechanistic link between epigenetic variation (epialleles defined by histone modification) and specific adult behavioral phenotypes in isogenic flies has not been directly demonstrated — such a demonstration would establish epigenetics as a causal, rather than merely correlative, source of behavioral individuality (Gap 2, referencing PMID 15273410). Prior work showed that central complex columnar neurons regulate handedness magnitude (PMID 25953337) and that histone modification variance exists in isogenic flies (PMID 15273410), but no study has connected a specific developmental epigenetic window in central complex neurons to the establishment of stable adult behavioral individuality. Additionally, this design partially addresses Gap 4 by testing whether central complex neurons buffer developmental stochasticity through chromatin-level mechanisms rather than moment-to-moment neural variability.

**Feasibility:** Sodium butyrate feeding is well established in Drosophila and is technically straightforward; the primary challenge is achieving reliable, non-toxic delivery at the larval-to-pupal transition. CUT&RUN on small numbers of dissected pupal central complexes is technically demanding due to tissue availability — approximately 50 dissected central complexes per replicate will be required, necessitating careful staging. GAL80ts-based temporal restriction is a validated approach in Drosophila but requires careful temperature-shift timing and monitoring of developmental staging at 29°C (which accelerates development). The Y-maze handedness assay is established (PMID 25953337) but requires automation for n ≥ 200 flies; a Raspberry Pi-based or commercial Y-maze array is recommended. UAS-HDAC3 overexpression lines may need to be generated if unavailable from stock centers. Estimated timeline: Aim 1 (pharmacological critical window mapping): 4 months; Aim 2 (CUT&RUN epigenetic profiling): 5 months (concurrent with Aim 1); Aim 3 (neuronal genetic validation): 6 months (including line construction). Total: approximately 12–15 months. Key pitfall: sodium butyrate may affect developmental timing or viability at the pupal stage, requiring dose optimization; lethality controls (eclosion rate, pupal size) must be included. A second pitfall is that R9D11-GAL4 may not exclusively target the columnar neurons implicated in PMID 25953337; immunostaining to confirm driver expression pattern is required before behavioral interpretation.

### Sonnet 5.5

**Hypothesis:** Proposed: in singly reared isogenic w1118 flies at 25C, a fly's stable locomotor turn bias (Y-maze handedness index) reflects left-right asymmetry in central complex columnar neuron synaptic output that is set during pupal development. Predictions: (a) day-to-day test-retest Spearman correlation of the handedness index is >=0.4 over 7 days; (b) per-fly handedness index correlates with left-right asymmetry of a presynaptic marker in columnar neuron terminals measured in the same brains (|Spearman rho| >=0.4, FDR <0.05); (c) transient pupal-stage inhibition of these neurons changes between-fly variance of the handedness index by >=30% versus controls, while equivalent adult-only inhibition does not.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Common individuality axis - longitudinally track handedness and sensory reactivity/habituation in the same singly housed isogenic flies and test for correlation, with a grouped-housing comparison cohort.
- Aim 2: Cellular correlate - quantify left-right asymmetry of columnar neuron presynaptic markers in the same brains and test correlation with each fly's handedness direction and magnitude.
- Aim 3: Developmental window - use GAL80ts-gated silencing of columnar neurons in larval, pupal or adult windows to test when activity shapes between-fly variance of handedness.

**Approach:** Individual isogenic flies will be tracked longitudinally in Y-mazes (turn bias) and in a sensory reactivity/habituation assay across 7-14 days, to test whether the two behaviors share an individuality axis. Flies are housed singly to remove the group-level effects seen in replicate groups, and a grouped-housing comparison cohort is included. After behavior, the same brains are fixed and imaged with a columnar-neuron split-GAL4 (driver to be identified and verified) driving a membrane marker and a presynaptic marker such as Brp-short, quantifying left-right asymmetry in synapse number and intensity by blinded analysis. For causality, a tub-GAL80ts; columnar-driver combination drives Kir2.1 or a similar silencer in restricted windows (larval, pupal, adult) with temperature-shifted GAL80ts-only and UAS-only controls. Main readouts are the mean and between-individual variance of the handedness index, compared by Levene/Brown-Forsythe tests and bootstrap variance ratios.

**Expected outcomes:** Proposed, not established: handedness index will show persistent individual differences (test-retest rho >=0.4, n >=100 flies per group). A significant correlation (|rho| >=0.4, FDR <0.05) between behavioral bias and columnar-neuron synaptic asymmetry would support a wiring or synaptic source. Pupal-window silencing is predicted to change variance by >=30% (variance ratio, bootstrap 95% CI excluding 1), while adult-only silencing may shift the mean or magnitude without altering rank-order. A weak cross-assay correlation (|rho| <0.2) between handedness and reactivity/habituation would indicate separate individuality axes, and a null brain-asymmetry correlation would falsify the synaptic-asymmetry part of the hypothesis.

**Novelty:** Central complex columnar neurons modulate the magnitude of handedness (PMID 25953337), but the supplied literature does not test whether they set each fly's direction or bias. Stable individual reactivity and habituation differences in isogenic flies persist over two weeks (PMID 41894333), yet whether they share a mechanism with handedness is unknown. No supplied paper identifies which developmental variation produces these differences. Replicate groups of genetically identical flies differ persistently in activity (PMID 16153038), and this social-group effect has not been separated from developmental individuality in singly housed flies. Epigenetic variation has been proposed to contribute to trait variation in an isogenic strain for a bristle phenotype (PMID 15273410), but it has not been tested for behavior.

**Feasibility:** Estimated 12-15 months: 2 months for line verification and construction, 4 months for longitudinal behavior, 3 months for imaging and quantification, and 4 months for stage-specific silencing. Pitfalls: the behavioral measure may have limited test-retest reliability, so many flies are needed; synaptic marker asymmetry may be too subtle to detect by light microscopy (EM connectomics would be a later follow-up); GAL80ts leakiness and temperature shifts can themselves alter behavior, so temperature-matched controls are essential; the specific driver for the handedness-relevant neurons must be confirmed. Chromatin profiling of flies with extreme handedness is a possible extension but is not included here.

## 6. What role do glia play in memory consolidation in the mushroom body, separate from their role in neuronal support?

PubMed search: `glia memory consolidation mushroom body`, genes none, 6 papers

### Sonnet 4.6

**Hypothesis:** Astrocyte-specific knockdown of Nkt in MB-associated glia (using alrm-GAL4 or repo-GAL4 restricted to the mushroom body neuropil) will impair 24-hour long-term memory consolidation scores by ≥30% relative to controls in the olfactory aversive conditioning paradigm in 3–5-day-old mated female w1118 flies at 25°C, without reducing immediate recall (3-min post-training performance index), demonstrating a consolidation-specific glial role that is separable from general neuronal support functions. Furthermore, simultaneous glial-specific knockdown of cat and sod1 in the same MB-associated glia will compound this deficit (≥45% LTM reduction) only when combined with Nkt knockdown but not alone at early adult timepoints, indicating that NKT-mediated glial signaling and oxidative stress management are partially independent consolidation-relevant pathways converging at MB-associated astrocytes.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Consolidation-specific glial Nkt requirement — Determine whether astrocyte-specific knockdown of Nkt using alrm-GAL4 impairs 24-hr LTM but not 3-min STM in the olfactory aversive conditioning paradigm, without causing detectable Kenyon cell loss or synaptic structural defects at 3–5 days of age
- Aim 2: Spatial restriction of glial Nkt requirement to MB-associated astrocytes — Compare LTM deficits produced by alrm-GAL4 (astrocyte-specific), repo-GAL4 (pan-glial), and a GAL80-suppressed intersectional strategy confining expression to MB neuropil-adjacent glia, to determine whether the consolidation-relevant glial Nkt requirement is specifically localized to MB-associated astrocytes rather than distributed across all CNS glia
- Aim 3: Epistatic relationship between glial NKT signaling and oxidative stress management in LTM — Test whether combinatorial knockdown of Nkt with cat and sod1 in alrm-GAL4-expressing astrocytes produces additive or synergistic LTM impairment relative to single knockdowns, and quantify ROS levels in MB neuropil glia by DHE staining to determine whether oxidative stress is a convergent downstream effector of NKT pathway disruption

**Approach:** We will use the GAL4/UAS binary system to achieve tissue-restricted knockdown of candidate genes in MB-associated glia. The primary driver will be alrm-GAL4 (astrocyte-specific) crossed to UAS-Nkt-RNAi lines to knock down Nkt selectively in astrocytes; repo-GAL4 (pan-glial) will serve as a broader glial control driver. To isolate MB-associated glial effects from systemic glial effects, we will employ the intersectional Split-GAL4 or GAL80 suppression strategy using 201Y-GAL80 or MB247-GAL80 to confine or exclude MB neuronal expression, ensuring driver activity is enriched at the MB neuropil-associated glia. Memory consolidation will be assessed using the Pavlovian olfactory aversive conditioning assay (electric shock + odor pairing): performance indexes will be measured at 3 min (immediate recall/short-term memory, STM), 1 hr (middle-term memory, MTM), and 24 hr after a single training session or spaced training (long-term memory, LTM). Controls will include: (1) driver-only (alrm-GAL4/+), (2) UAS-RNAi-only (+/UAS-Nkt-RNAi), (3) UAS-mCD8::GFP to confirm driver expression pattern in MB-associated glia by confocal microscopy with nc82 (Bruchpilot) counterstain, and (4) UAS-Dicer2 co-expression to enhance RNAi efficiency. Neuronal support integrity will be assessed by confocal imaging of Kenyon cell morphology (anti-Fasciclin II, anti-Dachshund), synapse density (nc82), and glial coverage (anti-Repo, anti-Alrm) in experimental versus control animals aged 3–5 days (before overt neurodegeneration). A secondary aim will co-express UAS-cat-RNAi and UAS-sod1-RNAi with UAS-Nkt-RNAi under alrm-GAL4 to test for additive or epistatic effects on LTM, with oxidative stress levels quantified by DHE (dihydroethidium) staining and protein carbonylation assays in dissected brains. All experiments will use n ≥ 20 flies per group per trial with ≥5 independent biological replicates; statistical analysis will use one-way ANOVA with Tukey post-hoc correction, significance threshold p < 0.05.

**Expected outcomes:** Aim 1: alrm-GAL4>UAS-Nkt-RNAi flies will show ≥30% reduction in 24-hr LTM performance index compared to both parental controls (p < 0.05, ANOVA + Tukey), with no significant difference in 3-min STM performance index (p > 0.05), demonstrating consolidation specificity. MB morphology (Fasciclin II staining, Kenyon cell number by anti-Dachshund) and glial coverage will be indistinguishable from controls at 3–5 days of age (p > 0.05), ruling out structural neuronal support failure as the explanation. Aim 2: repo-GAL4>UAS-Nkt-RNAi will show a ≥40% LTM reduction (expected to be larger than astrocyte-specific knockdown due to broader glial coverage), providing a dose-response reference. Aim 3: alrm-GAL4>UAS-cat-RNAi and alrm-GAL4>UAS-sod1-RNAi alone will not significantly impair LTM in 3–5-day-old flies (p > 0.05 vs. controls), but triple knockdown (Nkt-RNAi + cat-RNAi + sod1-RNAi in alrm-GAL4) will produce ≥45% LTM reduction, and DHE staining will show ≥1.5-fold increase in ROS signal in MB neuropil-associated glia (p < 0.05), indicating that oxidative homeostasis and NKT signaling are partially additive consolidation-relevant glial functions. All negative results (STM intact, morphology intact) must reach p > 0.1 in equivalence testing to be interpreted as true null.

**Novelty:** This experiment directly addresses three gaps identified in the literature synthesis: (1) No prior study has manipulated glia specifically within the MB-associated neuropil while leaving neuronal support intact to isolate a purely consolidation-specific glial contribution — our astrocyte-restricted, early-timepoint design fills this gap. (2) The downstream signaling role of secreted NKT from astrocytes in gating MB α'β' neuron-dependent consolidation is unresolved; this experiment isolates the astrocyte arm of NKT requirement from its neuronal arm for the first time. (3) Whether glial metabolic functions (oxidative stress management via cat/sod1) and glial signaling functions (NKT secretion) act as independent or convergent consolidation-relevant pathways at MB-associated glia has not been tested — the triple-knockdown combinatorial aim directly addresses this distinction, moving beyond the whole-CNS APOE4 model that cannot isolate MB-circuit-level effects.

**Feasibility:** The Pavlovian olfactory aversive conditioning assay is well-established in Drosophila and can be performed with standard equipment in most fly neuroscience labs. alrm-GAL4 and repo-GAL4 are widely used and available; however, the extent of MB neuropil astrocyte coverage by alrm-GAL4 must be confirmed empirically by GFP reporter staining before committing to this driver — if coverage is insufficient, substitute with NP3233-GAL4 (reported to label MB-associated cortex glia) or a tethered GAL4 approach. A key pitfall is that pan-glial repo-GAL4 drives expression in neurons under some conditions and may produce off-target effects; this must be monitored by immunostaining. RNAi efficiency for Nkt knockdown must be validated by RT-qPCR on sorted glial cells or by in situ hybridization, as Nkt is expressed at potentially low levels. The triple-knockdown experiment risks developmental lethality or non-specific effects; a conditional temporal control using tub-GAL80ts (shift to 29°C at eclosion) is recommended to restrict knockdown to adult stages. Estimated timeline: 3 months for line establishment and driver characterization, 4 months for behavioral experiments (n ≥ 20 per group × 5 replicates × 6 genotypes × 3 memory timepoints), 2 months for imaging and biochemical assays, 3 months for analysis and replication — total approximately 12 months. Potential pitfall: if alrm-GAL4 expression is too sparse at the MB neuropil, the consolidation phenotype may be below detection threshold; power analysis based on a 30% effect size and σ = 10 PI units indicates n = 22 per group at 80% power (α = 0.05).

### Sonnet 5.5

**Hypothesis:** Proposed: In adult flies at 25°C (permissive 18°C for development), astrocyte-specific, adult-restricted knockdown of Nkt (alrm-GAL4; tub-GAL80ts > UAS-Nkt-RNAi) will reduce 24-h aversive olfactory long-term memory after spaced training by at least 30% in performance index relative to genetic controls (p<0.05, n≥12 performance indices per genotype). Immediate memory, Kenyon cell nuclei counts and shock/odor acuity will change by less than 10%, and the LTM deficit will persist when night sleep is pharmacologically normalized, which would indicate a consolidation role for glia separate from sleep loss and neuronal support.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Glial Nkt requirement for LTM — test whether adult-restricted astrocyte, ensheathing-glia, pan-glia or α'β' neuron Nkt knockdown reduces 24-h spaced-training olfactory memory, with immediate memory and sensory acuity as specificity controls.
- Aim 2: Separating sleep from consolidation — measure night sleep in each knockdown genotype and test whether post-training sleep normalization or sleep deprivation alters the LTM deficit.
- Aim 3: Signaling versus metabolic support and timing — assess Kenyon cell number, lobe morphology and lipid staining, and use post-training thermogenetic astrocyte silencing to define a temporal consolidation window.

**Approach:** Mushroom bodies are required for consolidation of short- and long-term courtship memory [10624959], so aversive olfactory spaced-training LTM at 24 h will be the primary readout. Nkt will be knocked down with TARGET (tub-GAL80ts) control, shifting to 29°C for 3 days before training in adult flies only, using astrocyte (alrm-GAL4), ensheathing glia and pan-glia (repo-GAL4) drivers, with α'β' neuron knockdown as a neuronal comparison. Controls are GAL4/+, UAS-RNAi/+, a non-targeting RNAi, and a second independent Nkt RNAi or mutant to guard against off-target effects. Specificity tests include immediate memory, shock and odor acuity, Kenyon cell nuclei counts, mushroom body lobe morphology, and Nile red or BODIPY lipid staining to assess metabolic support. To separate sleep from memory, night sleep will be measured by DAM/video tracking in the same genotypes. Sleep will then be normalized after training (for example with gaboxadol/THIP) or deprived in controls, and LTM re-tested. A post-training thermogenetic silencing window (proposed, UAS-shi-ts in astrocytes, 0–6 h after training) will test whether glial activity during the consolidation window matters independently of developmental or chronic effects.

**Expected outcomes:** Proposed predictions: astrocyte-specific adult Nkt knockdown lowers 24-h spaced-training LTM performance index by ≥30% versus all controls (one-way ANOVA with Tukey post hoc, α=0.05), with unchanged immediate memory, sensory acuity, Kenyon cell count and lobe morphology (<10% difference). Night sleep is expected to be reduced, but pharmacological sleep normalization will not restore LTM beyond 50% of the control-to-knockdown gap if the glial role is separable from sleep. If LTM is restored by sleep normalization, the null alternative (the glial effect is mediated by sleep) is supported. Post-training astrocyte silencing is predicted to reduce LTM by ≥25% when applied 0–6 h after training but not when applied 12–18 h after training, which would define a temporal consolidation window. Lipid staining should not differ by >15% between knockdown and control, arguing against a primarily metabolic cause.

**Novelty:** Nkt is required in astrocytes and in mushroom body α'β' neurons for night sleep [31353186], but no supplied paper tests whether glial manipulation alters mushroom body-dependent long-term memory. Glial APOE4 expression impaired memory and altered lipid metabolism [38537387], but the supplied abstract does not separate metabolic support from signaling. Existing mushroom body consolidation studies [10624959, 24183945] address neuronal requirements only, and none uses temporally restricted glial manipulation to define a glial consolidation window distinct from neuronal support.

**Feasibility:** Estimated 12-15 months: 3 months for stock acquisition, verification of RNAi knockdown efficiency (qPCR, antibody if available) and crossing; 5 months for LTM and sleep behavior; 3 months for histology and lipid staining; 2-4 months for the sleep-rescue and post-training silencing experiments. Pitfalls: the Nkt RNAi lines and drivers require verification, and RNAi knockdown kinetics are slow, so the post-training window depends on shi-ts silencing, which may have non-specific effects on glial physiology and temperature-dependent memory effects. Sleep deprivation or drug treatment may itself affect memory, so sham and drug-only controls are required. Spaced-training LTM has high variance, so adequate sample size and blinded scoring are essential. A negative result would not exclude other glial molecules (the supplied literature does not identify the receptors on Kenyon cells or output neurons).

## 7. When light and temperature cues conflict, how do clock neurons decide which one sets the circadian phase?

PubMed search: `circadian clock neurons light temperature entrainment`, genes none, 8 papers

### Sonnet 4.6

**Hypothesis:** DN1p clock neurons act as the primary integration site for conflicting light and temperature entrainment cues, and when a 6-hour phase-advanced temperature cycle (20°C:29°C, TC) conflicts with a fixed light-dark cycle (LD 12:12, 25°C mean), silencing DN1p output (via UAS-Kir2.1 driven by Clk856-GAL4) will shift the phase of evening locomotor activity by ≥2 hours toward the LD phase (compared to ≤0.5 hours shift in controls), demonstrating that intact DN1p signaling is required for temperature entrainment to override light entrainment under conflict conditions in mated female w1118-background flies maintained at 25°C.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: DN1p silencing under conflict — Test whether chronic silencing of DN1p neurons via Clk856-GAL4/UAS-Kir2.1 eliminates temperature-driven phase advance of evening locomotor activity under a 6-h phase-advanced TC conflicting with fixed LD 12:12, quantified as mean evening peak phase shift ≥2 h in controls vs. ≤0.5 h in silenced flies
- Aim 2: Molecular phase tracking in DN1ps — Determine whether DN1p neurons molecularly track the TC phase (TIM immunostaining peak advanced ≥2 h in TC and conflict conditions vs. LD-only) and whether DN1p calcium activity (CaLexA) correlates with warm-phase timing rather than lights-off timing under conflict
- Aim 3: sLNv vs. DN1p circuit hierarchy — Test whether silencing sLNv PDF neurons (pdf-GAL4/UAS-Kir2.1) produces the opposite behavioral outcome (enhanced TC-driven phase advance, evening peak at ZT9 ± 1 h) compared to DN1p silencing, and whether acute thermogenetic activation of DN1ps (UAS-dTRPA1) during the LD-predicted rest phase is sufficient to phase-advance evening activity by ≥1.5 h

**Approach:** Flies will be entrained for 5 days under standard LD 12:12 at 25°C, then transferred to a conflict protocol in which the LD cycle is held fixed while a TC (20°C:29°C, 12:12, warm phase advanced 6 h relative to lights-off) is applied for 7 days; locomotor activity will be recorded continuously by DAM system. Four genotypes will be compared: (1) Clk856-GAL4/UAS-Kir2.1 (DN1p-silenced), (2) Clk856-GAL4/+ (GAL4 control), (3) +/UAS-Kir2.1 (UAS control), and (4) w1118 wild-type. Evening activity peak phase (ZT referenced to LD lights-off) will be extracted for each fly; population-level phase distributions will be compared by Watson-Williams circular statistics (α=0.05). To confirm that DN1ps receive temperature input, we will perform anti-TIM immunostaining of DN1p neurons in LD-only, TC-only, and conflict conditions (n≥10 brains/condition) and quantify TIM cycling phase as a molecular phase readout. As a complementary circuit dissection, we will use CaLexA-based calcium reporter imaging in DN1ps during the first 24 h of conflict exposure to ask whether DN1p activity correlates with temperature or light phase.

**Expected outcomes:** Under the 6-h phase-advanced TC conflict protocol, wild-type and GAL4/UAS sibling controls should show a partial split of the evening activity peak: a primary peak remaining near LD lights-off (ZT12) and a secondary peak shifting ~3 h earlier toward the TC warm-phase onset (ZT6), consistent with TC partially overriding LD as reported in earlier conflict paradigm literature. DN1p-silenced flies (Clk856-GAL4/UAS-Kir2.1) are predicted to show evening peak phase locked to ZT12 (LD lights-off ± 0.5 h), indicating failure of TC-driven phase advance (mean phase shift ≤0.5 h vs. ≥2 h in controls; Watson-Williams test p<0.05, n≥32 flies/genotype). TIM immunostaining in DN1p neurons is expected to show a phase-advanced TIM peak (~3 h earlier) in TC and conflict conditions compared to LD-only, confirming that DN1ps molecularly track temperature even under conflict. CaLexA signal in DN1ps should be elevated during the warm phase of TC regardless of LD phase, supporting temperature-dominant input to DN1ps. sLNv-silenced flies (pdf-GAL4/UAS-Kir2.1) are predicted to show enhanced phase advance toward TC (evening peak ~ZT9 ± 1 h), suggesting that sLNv PDF signaling normally restrains temperature-driven phase shifts.

**Novelty:** The literature synthesis explicitly identifies that 'the specific circuit-level or molecular mechanisms by which clock neurons resolve light-temperature conflicts remain entirely uncharacterized' and that 'temperature input pathways, their anatomical relationship to light pathways, and the mechanisms that weight one cue over the other under conflict conditions are entirely absent' from available studies. Although [39638801] reveals novel indirect light input pathways and extensive peptidergic connectivity, no study maps corresponding temperature inputs or their overlap with light pathways. Although [28360128] establishes that different clock neuron clusters serve distinct entrainment roles, no study has used targeted silencing to dissect which cluster is causally required for light vs. temperature dominance under explicit conflict. This design directly tests causal circuit hierarchy — not merely correlation — between DN1p activity and cue-conflict resolution, filling a gap unaddressed by any paper in the synthesis.

**Feasibility:** All core transgenic tools (GAL4/UAS, Kir2.1, CaLexA) are established and widely used in circadian biology. The TC conflict paradigm is technically straightforward using Peltier-equipped incubators with programmable ramps; 0.5°C/min ramp rates are standard. The main pitfall is that Clk856-GAL4 drives expression in DN1ps plus a subset of LNds, so observed effects cannot be attributed to DN1ps alone without additional intersectional controls (e.g., split-GAL4 lines specific to DN1ps — these would need to be obtained or generated, adding ~4 months if not available). A second pitfall is lethality or developmental defects with chronic Kir2.1 expression; if observed, temporal control using TARGET (tub-GAL80ts) should be implemented to restrict silencing to adulthood. CaLexA imaging requires ex vivo brain prep and confocal access — standard in most Drosophila neuroscience labs. Estimated timeline: Aim 1 (locomotor behavior) 6 months; Aim 2 (TIM immunostaining + CaLexA) 5 months; Aim 3 (dTRPA1 thermogenetic activation + sLNv controls) 5 months; total ~14–16 months with overlap.

### Sonnet 5.5

**Hypothesis:** Proposed: When a 12h:12h light-dark cycle (LD; 300 lux) is placed in conflict with a 12h:12h 25°C:16°C temperature cycle (TC) that is phase-delayed by 6 h or 12 h, PDF-expressing ventral lateral neurons (LNv) will set the behavioral phase through PDF signaling. In control flies, evening activity peak phase on the first 5 days of free-run (DD, constant 25°C) will lie within ±1.5 h of the light-cycle-predicted phase in ≥70% of individuals, and will otherwise follow the temperature cycle.
