Proposed prediction: Pdf-GAL4-driven RNAi knockdown of Pdf, or Pdf-GAL4-driven ablation/silencing of PDF neurons, will shift the free-running evening phase by ≥3 h toward the temperature cycle and reduce the proportion of light-aligned flies to ≤35% (Fisher's exact test, p<0.01, n≥48 per genotype). Proposed: this light dominance will be accompanied by a ≥3 h divergence in PER oscillation peak phase between LNv/LNd (light-aligned in controls) and DN1p neurons (temperature-aligned in controls), measured by immunostaining at 4-h intervals on day 3 of DD; PDF loss will collapse this divergence to <1.5 h, with all clusters following temperature.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Behavioral cue hierarchy — define free-running evening-peak phase of wild-type flies across aligned, 6 h, and 12 h light-temperature conflicts and across light intensities and temperature amplitudes.
- Aim 2: PDF neuron requirement — test with Pdf RNAi, Kir2.1 silencing, and adult-restricted Gal80ts induction whether PDF signaling is necessary for resolving the conflict.
- Aim 3: Cluster-resolved clock phase — measure PER oscillation phase in LNv, LNd, and DN1p neurons under conflicting cues to test whether clusters diverge and whether PDF loss collapses divergence.

**Approach:** Proposed: Use a conflicting-cue entrainment protocol in which flies are entrained for 6 days to LD with a TC offset by 0 h (aligned), 6 h, or 12 h, then released into DD at constant 25°C. Locomotor rhythms are recorded in DAM monitors to quantify evening-peak phase, period, and rhythm strength (FFT/chi-square). Cell-type-specific manipulations use GAL4/UAS and Gal80ts: Pdf-GAL4 for PDF neurons, plus a DN1p-specific driver and a CRY-expressing neuron driver to be verified, driving UAS-Pdf-RNAi, UAS-Kir2.1 (silencing), and UAS-cry-RNAi. Cluster-specific clock phase is read out by anti-PER/anti-PDF immunostaining at ZT/CT 0, 4, 8, 12, 16, 20 in whole-mount brains, with blinded confocal quantification. Controls include GAL4/+ and UAS/+ parents, aligned-cue conditions, LD-only and TC-only entrainments, and temperature-shift controls (Gal80ts permissive vs. restrictive) to avoid confounding developmental effects. Because Gal80ts-based induction itself uses temperature, TARGET experiments will be paired with constitutive-driver experiments as a cross-check.

**Expected outcomes:** Proposed (not established): (1) Control flies in aligned cues show evening peaks within ±1 h of the common phase; under 6 h or 12 h conflict they show a phase that is predominantly light-aligned (≥70% of flies within ±1.5 h of the LD-predicted phase) with a shift of ≤2 h toward temperature. (2) PDF knockdown, PDF neuron silencing, or cry knockdown in the relevant neurons shifts median free-running phase by ≥3 h toward the TC (one-way ANOVA with Tukey post hoc or Watson-Williams test, p<0.01). (3) In controls, PER peak phase in LNv/LNd differs from DN1p by ≥3 h under 12 h conflict, whereas PDF loss reduces the difference to <1.5 h. Falsification: if PDF manipulation changes phase by <1.5 h and does not alter PER phase divergence, the hypothesis is rejected. If temperature dominates in controls, the hypothesis of light dominance is rejected, though the cluster-weighting question remains testable.

**Novelty:** Per the literature synthesis, no supplied paper tests light versus temperature conflict directly, and which clock neuron subtypes weight temperature versus light is unresolved. The connectome describes indirect light input pathways [39638801], and the PDF role in setting phase of roughly half of clock neurons is described [33065014], but whether PDF signaling mediates resolution of cue conflict is untested. This proposal combines a conflicting-cue protocol with cell-type-specific manipulation and cluster-resolved phase imaging to address those gaps.

**Feasibility:** Estimated timeline of 12-15 months: 2 months for stock verification/acquisition and crosses, 4 months for behavioral conflict-cue screening, 3 months for immunostaining time courses, 3 months for Gal80ts and follow-up, 2 months for analysis. Pitfalls: temperature cycles can directly affect locomotor activity (masking), so phase must be measured in free-running DD at constant temperature; PDF loss alone weakens rhythms, so rhythm strength must be controlled for and arrhythmic flies excluded transparently; driver specificity for DN1p and CRY neurons requires verification; light intensity and temperature amplitude may change which cue dominates, so dose-response conditions may be needed; the baseline assumption of light dominance is itself uncertain.

## 8. How does a growing organ tell the rest of the body that it has not yet reached its final size, and how is that signal turned off?

PubMed search: `organ size developmental timing Dilp8`, genes ['Ilp8', 'Lgr3'], 15 papers

### Sonnet 4.6

**Hypothesis:** The cell-autonomous shutdown of dilp8 expression in the wing imaginal disc at the end of the larval size-adjustment window is controlled by a mechanical/morphogen size-sensing threshold intrinsic to the disc rather than solely by rising systemic ecdysone levels; specifically, loss of disc-intrinsic Hippo pathway activity (as a proxy for reaching final disc size) will maintain dilp8 expression ≥2-fold above wild-type levels at 96 h after egg laying (AEL) in w1118 larvae at 25°C, and will extend the median time to pupariation by ≥8 hours compared to controls, independently of exogenous ecdysone manipulation.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Temporal profiling of disc-intrinsic dilp8-GFP expression — determine whether constitutive Yorkie activation in wing imaginal discs (nub-GAL4>UAS-yki.S168A) prolongs the dilp8-GFP peak beyond the normal size-adjustment window (96–108 h AEL) compared to parental controls, using time-resolved confocal quantification of dissected discs
- Aim 2: Systemic timing and ecdysone independence — establish whether nub-GAL4>UAS-yki-induced dilp8 upregulation causes a ≥8-hour delay in pupariation without a corresponding reduction in larval ecdysone titers, distinguishing disc-autonomous size-sensing from ecdysone-feedback-driven dilp8 regulation using pupariation cohort assays and ecdysone ELISA
- Aim 3: Epistasis with Dilp8 and IP3R/Ca2+ pathway — test whether the Hippo/Yorkie-driven pupariation delay requires Dilp8 secretion (rescue by UAS-dilp8-RNAi) and whether it is IP3R/Ca2+-independent (persistence despite UAS-IP3R-RNAi co-expression), to position Hippo-to-dilp8 signaling as a parallel or convergent input relative to the established Galphaq/IP3R/Ca2+ axis

**Approach:** We will use the GAL4/UAS system to manipulate disc-intrinsic size-sensing pathways (Hippo pathway via UAS-yki overexpression and UAS-wts-RNAi) specifically in wing imaginal discs using nub-GAL4, while simultaneously monitoring dilp8-GFP reporter fluorescence as a real-time readout of dilp8 transcriptional activity. Larvae will be staged precisely by 4-hour egg-collection windows, and disc-specific dilp8-GFP intensity will be quantified by confocal microscopy at 84, 96, 108, and 120 h AEL to capture the normal and perturbed temporal profiles of the dilp8 peak. Pupariation timing will be recorded in cohorts of 30 larvae per vial in triplicate to determine whether disc-autonomous size-sensing manipulation alters developmental timing independently of upstream G alphaQ/IP3R/Ca2+ perturbation. Ecdysone levels will be measured by ELISA from whole-larval homogenates at each time point to distinguish disc-intrinsic repression of dilp8 from ecdysone-driven repression. Controls will include nub-GAL4/+ heterozygotes, UAS-transgene/+ heterozygotes, dilp8-GFP/+ reporters without GAL4, and a positive control using UAS-Galphaq overexpression (known to upregulate dilp8) to validate the assay sensitivity.

**Expected outcomes:** If the hypothesis is correct: (1) nub-GAL4>UAS-yki larvae will show dilp8-GFP fluorescence intensity ≥2-fold above nub-GAL4/+ controls at 96–108 h AEL as measured by mean integrated fluorescence per disc (n ≥ 15 discs per time point, two-tailed t-test, p < 0.05); (2) median time to pupariation in nub-GAL4>UAS-yki cohorts will be delayed by ≥8 hours relative to both parental controls (log-rank test on pupariation curves, p < 0.01, n = 90 larvae per genotype across 3 replicates); (3) larval ecdysone titers at 96 h AEL will not be significantly reduced in nub-GAL4>UAS-yki versus controls (confirming the dilp8 upregulation is not driven by reduced ecdysone), and the pupariation delay will be at least partially rescued by simultaneous dilp8 knockdown (nub-GAL4>UAS-yki + UAS-dilp8-RNAi), with rescue of ≥50% of the delay. If the hypothesis is incorrect and dilp8 shutdown is entirely ecdysone-controlled, nub-GAL4>UAS-yki will not prolong the dilp8-GFP peak, and ecdysone titers will be suppressed in parallel with any pupariation delay.

**Novelty:** Current literature establishes that the dilp8 peak at the end of juvenile growth is triggered and shut off by ecdysone signaling in the epidermis (PMID 36163439) and that disc perturbations (via Galphaq/IP3R/Ca2+) can upregulate dilp8 (PMID 41131630, 36711848). However, a major identified gap in the literature is that 'it is unclear what cell-autonomous signal within a growing organ sets the threshold at which dilp8 expression is turned off — specifically, whether organ-intrinsic size sensors (mechanical, morphogen gradient, or metabolic) directly repress dilp8 transcription or whether this is entirely controlled by rising ecdysone levels.' No published study has directly tested whether disc-intrinsic Hippo pathway growth arrest contributes to dilp8 shutdown independently of systemic ecdysone, nor whether Hippo pathway status in the disc constitutes the organ-side input that, when satisfied, allows ecdysone to terminate dilp8 expression. This experiment also addresses the gap regarding the Ca2+-independent arm of Galpahq-linked size control by testing whether a non-Ca2+ size-sensing pathway (Hippo/Yorkie) independently feeds into dilp8 regulation.

**Feasibility:** All proposed genetic tools (nub-GAL4, UAS-yki.S168A, UAS-wts-RNAi, dilp8-GFP reporter) are well-established in the Drosophila community; BDSC stock numbers cited require verification before ordering. The dilp8-GFP reporter-based assay for temporal profiling has been validated in prior work (PMID 36163439). Ecdysone ELISA from Drosophila larvae is technically demanding but established, requiring ~50 larvae per sample for reliable signal; this may require optimization. A potential pitfall is that strong Yki overexpression via nub-GAL4 causes disc overgrowth and lethality at later stages, which could complicate pupariation scoring — use of a weaker UAS-yki allele or temperature-sensitive GAL80 (tub-GAL80ts at 18°C→29°C shift) is recommended to titrate expression. Another pitfall is that nub-GAL4 is active in the wing disc but low-level expression in other tissues could confound disc-specific interpretations; disc dissection and cell-type-resolved GFP quantification will mitigate this. Estimated timeline: Aim 1 (reporter characterization) 3 months; Aim 2 (pupariation timing and ecdysone measurements) 4 months; Aim 3 (epistasis with dilp8 knockdown and IP3R) 4 months; total ~11–12 months.

### Sonnet 5.5

**Hypothesis:** Proposed: After a transient 24 h pulse of Gαq overexpression in the wing pouch (rn-GAL4 with tub-GAL80ts, shifted to 29°C at ~72 h AEL, then returned to 18°C), the Dilp8-dependent pupariation delay ends because disc dilp8 transcription falls, not because Lgr3 signaling desensitizes or Dilp8 protein is slowly cleared. Specifically, within 12 h of the return to 18°C, wing-disc dilp8 mRNA will fall to ≤1.5-fold of GAL80ts-only controls, and the pupariation delay will be ≥50% shorter than with sustained Gαq expression. If dilp8 is forced to stay on in the pouch after the pulse (UAS-dilp8), the delay will persist at ≥24 h versus controls, showing that Lgr3 signaling is not desensitized.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Kinetics of signal termination — measure dilp8 mRNA, Dilp8 protein and pupariation delay after a 24 h pouch Gαq pulse versus sustained expression to distinguish transcriptional shut-off from clearance
- Aim 2: Receptor-side test — use post-pulse forced dilp8 expression and Lgr3 manipulation to test whether Lgr3 signaling desensitizes, and test whether endogenous Gαq/IP3R is required for dilp8 induction in a physiological local growth-retardation model
- Aim 3: Source separation — use tissue-specific dilp8 knockdown (disc versus epidermis) to test whether the ecdysone-linked epidermal dilp8 peak contributes to terminating the disc-derived delay

**Approach:** A temperature-controlled pulse-chase design uses rn-GAL4 with tub-GAL80ts to drive UAS-Gαq in the wing pouch for a defined 24 h window. Sustained expression, pulse-only, and GAL80ts-only controls are compared. Readouts are pupariation timing (hourly scoring of staged cohorts, n≥100 per genotype, log-rank test), wing-disc dilp8 mRNA (RT-qPCR and HCR/in situ at 0, 6, 12, 24 h after the pulse), Dilp8 protein (tagged or antibody-based reporter, to be verified), and adult wing size. Epistasis tests use the Gαq pulse combined with (a) IP3R RNAi in the pouch, (b) pulsed UAS-dilp8 as a clearance control, (c) Lgr3 knockdown in Lgr3-positive neurons, and (d) epidermal dilp8 knockdown with an epidermal driver, to separate disc and epidermal Dilp8 sources. A separate physiological perturbation, local growth retardation in the pouch, tests whether endogenous Gαq/IP3R is needed for dilp8 induction. Statistical thresholds: Mann-Whitney or log-rank tests with Bonferroni correction, α=0.05.

**Expected outcomes:** Proposed predictions, not established results. (1) The Gαq pulse induces dilp8 mRNA ≥3-fold and a pupariation delay of ≥12 h. After the pulse ends, dilp8 mRNA returns to ≤1.5-fold of controls within 12 h and the delay is ≥50% shorter than with sustained expression (p<0.01). (2) UAS-dilp8 after the pulse maintains a delay ≥24 h, indicating intact Lgr3 responsiveness and arguing against desensitization. (3) IP3R knockdown reduces pulse-induced dilp8 mRNA by ≥50%. (4) If epidermal dilp8 knockdown prolongs the delay by ≥6 h, this would support a role for the ecdysone-linked epidermal peak in terminating the signal. (5) Lgr3 knockdown abolishes the delay. If dilp8 mRNA remains high while the delay ends, transcriptional shut-off is falsified and receptor desensitization or Dilp8 clearance must be favored.

**Novelty:** The shut-off mechanism of the Dilp8 signal is not directly shown in the supplied literature, and the literature synthesis lists as open whether it ends through reduced disc dilp8 transcription, Dilp8 clearance, or Lgr3 desensitization. Gαq overexpression in wing discs triggers Dilp8 secretion and an IP3R-dependent delay [41131630], but that work used sustained overexpression and did not test reversibility. The ecdysone-triggered epidermal dilp8 peak at the end of juvenile growth [36163439] has not been tested as an off switch for the disc-derived signal. This design adds a reversible pulse, separates disc from epidermal dilp8 sources, and tests the receptor side in one assay.

**Feasibility:** Estimated 12-15 months: 3 months for stock acquisition, verification and recombination; 4 months for Aim 1 kinetics; 3 months for Aim 2; 3 months for Aim 3 and analysis. Pitfalls: GAL80ts leakiness at 18°C could cause background delay, so controls and shift-timing calibration are needed. Temperature shifts themselves alter developmental timing, so all genotypes must be shifted in parallel. Dilp8 protein detection may be limited by the absence of validated reagents. Gαq overexpression may cause apoptosis or tissue damage, so cell death markers should be checked to avoid conflating Gαq signaling with injury. An epidermal driver specific enough to avoid disc expression needs validation. Line identities and availability are unconfirmed.

## 9. How do morphogen gradients scale with tissue size so that pattern stays proportional in small and large flies?

PubMed search: `morphogen gradient scaling wing disc`, genes ['dpp'], 15 papers

### Sonnet 4.6

**Hypothesis:** Drosophila wing imaginal disc scaling of the Dpp gradient beyond a critical disc area (>50,000 µm²) relies on endocytic recycling of Dpp (re-exocytosis) as the dominant mechanism, whereas receptor/co-receptor feedback (Tkv/Wit downregulation) dominates in discs <30,000 µm², such that genetic ablation of re-exocytosis (by dominant-negative Rab11 expression) will reduce the Dpp gradient length scale by ≥30% specifically in large (late L3) but not small (early L3) discs in w1118 female larvae at 25°C, while Pentagone null discs will show ≥40% reduction in gradient scaling across all sizes, as quantified by pMad immunofluorescence decay length.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Stage-stratified gradient measurement — Quantify Dpp gradient decay length (λ by pMad immunofluorescence) in wild-type w1118 wing discs across five disc-size classes (early L2 to late L3) to establish the scaling relationship and identify the size threshold beyond which scaling is impaired, providing the quantitative baseline for all subsequent perturbation experiments.
- Aim 2: Mechanistic dissection of recycling vs. receptor-feedback dominance — Compare Dpp gradient scaling in nub-GAL4 > UAS-Rab11DN (recycling block) vs. nub-GAL4 > UAS-tkv-RNAi; UAS-wit-RNAi (receptor feedback block) larvae across small and large disc size classes using temporally controlled GAL80ts induction, to determine which mechanism is stage-dependent and quantify their relative contributions to gradient scaling.
- Aim 3: Epistasis between Pentagone and endocytic recycling — Cross UAS-Rab11DN; nub-GAL4 into pent2 null background and quantify gradient scaling and adult wing patterning proportionality, to determine whether Pent-dependent expansion-repression and re-exocytosis-based recycling act in the same pathway or in parallel, and to characterize the organismal patterning consequences of combined scaling failure.

**Approach:** We will use the GAL4/UAS system to acutely disrupt endocytic recycling by expressing UAS-Rab11DN (dominant-negative Rab11, S25N) under nub-GAL4 to restrict perturbation to the wing pouch, combined with temporal control via tub-GAL80ts to allow conditional expression at defined larval stages (early vs. late L3). Parallel experiments will use pent null mutants (pent2 allele) crossed to UAS-Rab11DN; nub-GAL4 to dissect additive vs. epistatic interactions between the recycling and expansion-repression mechanisms. Receptor feedback will be assessed in a separate arm using UAS-tkv-RNAi and UAS-wit-RNAi driven by nub-GAL4 with staged induction. All discs will be dissected at defined larval weights (as a proxy for disc size), stained for pMad (Dpp signaling readout), GFP-tagged Dpp when available, and Dpp receptors (Tkv), then imaged by confocal microscopy; gradient decay length (λ) will be extracted by exponential curve fitting. Controls include nub-GAL4/+ and UAS-Rab11DN/+ heterozygotes, and Oregon-R wild-type discs. A minimum of n=20 discs per genotype per size class will be analyzed, with statistical comparison by ANOVA with Tukey post-hoc correction (α=0.05).

**Expected outcomes:** In large late-L3 discs (disc area >50,000 µm², corresponding to ~120 h AEL at 25°C), nub-GAL4 > UAS-Rab11DN expression is predicted to reduce the Dpp gradient decay length (λ) by ≥30% relative to nub-GAL4/+ controls (e.g., from ~60 µm to <42 µm), with minimal effect (<10% change in λ) in small early-L3 discs (<30,000 µm²). pent2 null discs are expected to show ≥40% reduction in λ scaling ratio (large/small disc λ ratio) compared to wild-type, replicating prior findings and validating the assay. Combining pent2 with Rab11DN is predicted to produce an additive or synergistic reduction (>50% scaling failure) if both pathways act in parallel, or no additional effect beyond pent2 alone if they are epistatic. UAS-tkv-RNAi discs are expected to show reduced Tkv protein levels (≥50% by immunofluorescence intensity) and partial scaling rescue in pent2 background if receptor feedback is Pent-dependent. Statistical significance threshold: p<0.05 after Tukey correction; effect sizes reported as Cohen's d. Organismal consequence of scaling failure will be assessed by measuring wing vein proportionality (L3/L4 intervein width ratio) in adult flies using ImageJ.

**Novelty:** This experiment directly addresses two identified gaps in the literature: (1) whether the endocytic recycling mechanism (PMID 34937053) and the receptor/co-receptor feedback mechanism (PMID 32574592) are mutually exclusive or act in parallel, and which dominates at different stages of disc growth — explicitly identified as unresolved in the synthesis; and (2) the developmental consequences of exceeding the predicted size limit for scaling failure (PMID 32574592) have not been characterized in naturally large discs. No prior study has used stage-controlled genetic perturbation with quantified disc-size stratification to dissect temporal dominance of these two mechanisms within the same experimental framework. Additionally, the proposed wing vein proportionality readout in adults links molecular gradient scaling to organismal patterning outcome, a connection not previously made quantitatively.

**Feasibility:** All core genetic tools (GAL4/UAS, GAL80ts, RNAi) are standard and well-established in Drosophila. The main technical challenge is precise staging of larvae by age and larval weight to obtain reproducible disc size classes; this is feasible with timed egg-laying collections and gravimetric sorting. pMad immunofluorescence is a validated quantitative readout for Dpp signaling (used in PMID 34937053 and PMID 32574592). The dominant-negative Rab11 approach may have off-target effects on other endocytic cargoes; this will be controlled by examining disc morphology and Wg gradient integrity as a parallel readout. Estimated timeline: stock assembly and validation (2 months), larval staging protocol optimization (1 month), main imaging experiments (4 months), adult wing analysis (1 month), data analysis and statistics (2 months); total ~10 months. Potential pitfall: nub-GAL4 expression domain does not precisely mirror the Dpp source/receiving field boundary — an alternative ap-GAL4 or sal-GAL4 driver may be needed for source-proximal perturbations. UAS transgene dosage effects at 29°C with GAL80ts should be titrated.

### Sonnet 5.5

**Hypothesis:** Proposed (not established): In wing discs of wandering third-instar w1118 larvae at 25°C, where anteroposterior (A-P) pouch length is varied at least 1.5-fold by larval yeast restriction, the pMad gradient decay length will scale with pouch length with a log-log slope of at least 0.7 up to a critical size L*, and the slope will fall below 0.3 above L*; wing-pouch pent knockdown will reduce the sub-L* slope to 0.3 or less, and wing-pouch Rab11 dominant-negative expression will reduce it by at least 50%, with the Rab11 effect persisting after Pent is restored.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Define the scaling range — quantify pMad decay length versus pouch length in wing discs from diet- and temperature-varied larvae and estimate whether a size limit L* exists.
- Aim 2: Test Pent-dependence and range — compare control, pent-knockdown and Pent-restored discs and map Pent range together with Tkv and Dally levels to discriminate expansion-repression from local receptor feedback.
- Aim 3: Test the recycling contribution — inhibit Rab11-dependent recycling and quantify endocytosed Dpp flux and pMad slope, with and without Pent restoration, to test whether recycling acts independently of Pent.

**Approach:** Larvae will be raised on graded yeast concentrations (and, as a second axis, at different temperatures) to produce small and large wing discs. Discs will be stained for pMad and quantified as A-P profiles normalized to the anterior-posterior boundary, and decay length will be fit as a function of pouch length. Pouch-restricted GAL4 (e.g. nub-GAL4 or rn-GAL4, to be verified) with tub-GAL80ts temporal control will drive UAS-pent-RNAi, a Rab11 dominant-negative, or a Pent-overexpression/restoration transgene. Dpp endocytosis and re-exocytosis will be assessed with a fluorescently tagged Dpp (availability to be verified) using a pulse-chase or recycling assay. Pent range will be tested with clones and in situ staining, and Tkv and Dally levels will be measured in the same discs, so that Pent range, receptor levels and recycling flux are quantified together. Controls are GAL4-only, UAS-only, a non-targeting RNAi, and same-diet siblings imaged in parallel. Statistics will use ANCOVA for slope differences with n ≥ 15 discs per size bin.

**Expected outcomes:** Proposed predictions: (1) In control discs, the log-log slope of pMad decay length versus pouch length is at least 0.7 below L* and below 0.3 above L*, with L* estimated by segmented regression and reported with a 95% CI. (2) pent knockdown reduces the sub-L* slope to 0.3 or less (p < 0.05 by ANCOVA interaction). (3) Rab11-DN reduces the slope by at least 50% and an altered endocytosed-Dpp fraction is detected. (4) If Pent acts only locally, as in the receptor-feedback model, Pent restoration will not rescue slope in large discs beyond L*. If scaling is unaffected by both perturbations, or if the slope is the same above and below L*, the hypothesis is falsified.

**Novelty:** Ben-Zvi et al. reported that scaling is abolished when pent is ubiquitously expressed (PMID 21835621). Zhu et al. reported that Pent acts only within a few cell diameters and proposed a receptor/co-receptor feedback model that predicts a size limit to scaling (PMID 32574592). Romanova-Michaelides et al. reported that scaling depends on recycling of endocytosed Dpp, with Pent-modulating binding rates (PMID 34937053). The literature synthesis identifies two gaps: these models have not been reconciled in the same discs, and the size limit has not been tested against natural size variation between small and large flies. The proposed work addresses both by measuring Pent range, receptor levels and recycling flux together across a size series.

**Feasibility:** Estimated timeline is 12-15 months: 3 months for line verification, construction and diet calibration, 5 months for the scaling and perturbation datasets, 4 months for recycling and Pent-range assays, and 3 months for analysis. Pitfalls include diet-induced changes in developmental timing and staging (mitigate by staging at wandering L3 and by hours after egg laying), heterochronic differences in pMad, antibody variability (normalize to internal anterior controls), non-specific RNAi effects (use two independent RNAi lines or a mutant allele), Rab11-DN pleiotropy on general trafficking (use GAL80ts and short induction), and uncertain availability of tagged Dpp lines. Disease relevance is not established in the supplied literature; any link to BMP2/4 biology is inference.

## 10. What keeps developmental timing precise under nutritional or temperature stress, and which genes buffer it?

PubMed search: `developmental timing robustness ecdysone nutrition`, genes none, 6 papers

### Sonnet 4.6

**Hypothesis:** Nutritional restriction (50% standard yeast concentration) and thermal stress (29°C vs. 25°C) will each upregulate miR-305-5p expression in the prothoracic gland (PG) by ≥1.5-fold, leading to ≥30% reduction in Nerfin-1 protein, ≥40% increase in Cyp18a1 mRNA, and a ≥12-hour delay in median pupariation timing in w1118 third-instar larvae compared to unstressed controls — demonstrating that the miR-305-5p/Nerfin-1/Cyp18a1 cascade is an environmentally responsive buffering mechanism rather than a constitutive fine-tuner of ecdysone pulse clearance.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Environmental Responsiveness — Determine whether nutritional restriction (50% yeast) and thermal stress (29°C) each upregulate the miR-305-5p/Nerfin-1/Cyp18a1 cascade in the PG and delay pupariation in a dose- and cascade-dependent manner in w1118 larvae.
- Aim 2: Genetic Rescue — Test whether PG-specific inhibition of miR-305-5p (via UAS-miR-305-5p-sponge driven by phm-GAL4) is sufficient to suppress stress-induced Cyp18a1 upregulation and rescue pupariation timing precision under nutritional or thermal stress.
- Aim 3: Cascade Integration — Determine whether stress-induced miR-305-5p upregulation acts in parallel with or upstream of the Dilp8-mediated growth-maturation coordination axis by co-quantifying Dilp8-lacZ reporter activity and hemolymph ecdysteroid titers across all genotype-by-environment combinations.

**Approach:** The experiment uses a 2×2 factorial design (nutritional status: standard vs. 50% yeast; temperature: 25°C vs. 29°C) in a w1118 wild-type background to quantify cascade gene expression and pupariation timing. Tissue-specific manipulation employs the phm-GAL4 driver (PG-specific) crossed to UAS-miR-305-5p-sponge (miR-305-5p inhibitor/decoy) and UAS-miR-305-5p (overexpressor) to test whether genetic buffering of miR-305-5p is sufficient to rescue stress-induced timing delays. Primary assays include: (1) smFISH/RT-qPCR for miR-305-5p, nerfin-1 mRNA, and cyp18a1 mRNA in dissected PGs at 6-hour intervals from 96–120 h AEL; (2) anti-Nerfin-1 immunofluorescence and western blot of PG lysates; (3) ecdysteroid ELISA (hemolymph) at pupariation onset; and (4) automated pupariation timing assay (n ≥ 100 larvae/condition, four biological replicates). Controls include phm-GAL4/+ and UAS-transgene/+ heterozygotes under all four environmental conditions, and dietary 20E supplementation (1 mg/mL) as a positive-rescue control replicating the published rescue paradigm.

**Expected outcomes:** Primary outcome: Under 50% yeast (nutritional restriction) or 29°C (thermal stress), w1118 larvae will show ≥1.5-fold upregulation of miR-305-5p by RT-qPCR in PG tissue, with corresponding ≥30% reduction in Nerfin-1 protein by western blot and ≥40% increase in cyp18a1 mRNA, associated with ≥12-hour delay in median pupariation (assessed by Kaplan-Meier survival-style timing curves, log-rank p < 0.05, n ≥ 100/condition). Ecdysteroid ELISA is predicted to show ≥25% reduction in peak hemolymph ecdysteroid titer in stressed animals versus controls. Genetic rescue: phm-GAL4 > UAS-miR-305-5p-sponge larvae under stress are predicted to restore median pupariation timing to within 6 hours of unstressed controls and rescue Cyp18a1 mRNA levels to within 20% of baseline. Conversely, phm-GAL4 > UAS-miR-305-5p under non-stress conditions is predicted to delay pupariation by ≥10 hours and deplete ecdysteroid titers, phenocopying environmental stress. Statistical threshold: all pairwise comparisons by two-way ANOVA with Tukey post-hoc, α = 0.05; effect sizes reported as Cohen's d.

**Novelty:** This experiment directly addresses the primary identified gap in the literature: although the miR-305-5p/Nerfin-1/Cyp18a1 cascade has been shown to buffer ecdysone pulse timing under normal conditions (PMID 42267566), it remains entirely unknown whether this cascade is itself responsive to nutritional or thermal inputs — i.e., whether it constitutes an environmentally adaptive buffer or merely a constitutive fine-tuner. No prior study has measured miR-305-5p or Nerfin-1 levels as a function of diet or temperature in the PG. Additionally, the literature identifies that miRNAs such as bantam and let-7 adapt to environmental conditions to maintain developmental timing (PMID 33152557), but the specific environmental regulators of miR-305-5p in larvae remain entirely unresolved. This experiment also addresses a secondary gap by linking the Dilp8 coordination axis to the miR-305-5p cascade readout, testing whether two independently identified buffering systems respond in parallel to the same stressors.

**Feasibility:** All assays (RT-qPCR, western blot, immunofluorescence, ecdysteroid ELISA, pupariation timing) are standard in Drosophila developmental biology laboratories. The phm-GAL4 driver is well-validated for PG-specific expression. The principal technical challenge is obtaining or generating UAS-miR-305-5p and UAS-miR-305-5p-sponge lines; if unavailable from the originating lab (Chen et al. 2026), 6–8 weeks of injection and selection would be required. PG dissection for smFISH at defined larval stages requires expertise but is routine. Estimated timeline: months 1–2, line acquisition/verification and stress protocol optimization; months 3–5, main expression profiling experiments (RT-qPCR, western, IF) across all four environmental conditions; months 4–6, genetic rescue and pupariation timing experiments; months 6–8, ecdysteroid ELISA and Dilp8 reporter quantification; months 8–10, data analysis and replication. Potential pitfalls: (1) miR-305-5p changes in whole larvae may be diluted if only PG-isolated tissue is limiting — single-PG RNA extraction protocols must be optimized; (2) 29°C may produce nonspecific developmental acceleration confounding ecdysone titer measurements — inclusion of a 18°C cold-stress arm as a specificity control is advisable; (3) sponge constructs vary in efficacy — functional validation by Nerfin-1 protein recovery must precede phenotypic interpretation.

### Sonnet 5.5

**Hypothesis:** Proposed: The PG miR-305-5p/Nerfin-1/Cyp18a1 cascade buffers pupariation timing against nutritional and temperature stress, so that PG-specific cyp18a1 knockdown (phm-GAL4>UAS-cyp18a1-RNAi) will increase the stress-induced variance and shift in time to pupariation. Specifically, at 25°C on low-yeast food (proposed 20% of standard yeast) or at 29°C on standard food, controls will show a delay of a given magnitude, and cyp18a1 knockdown will change the median time to pupariation by ≥6 h relative to the control shift in the same stress condition (genotype x environment interaction, two-way ANOVA p<0.05) and increase the coefficient of variation of pupariation time by ≥50%. Under stress, nerfin-1 mRNA is predicted to increase (≥1.5-fold) and cyp18a1 mRNA to decrease (≥30%) in prepupal ring glands relative to 25°C standard food.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Stress responsiveness of the cascade — measure nerfin-1 and cyp18a1 transcripts (and miR-305-5p if feasible) in staged ring glands under low-yeast and 29°C conditions.
- Aim 2: Functional buffering test — determine whether PG cyp18a1 knockdown alters stress-induced shift and variance (CV) in pupariation timing.
- Aim 3: Mechanistic rescue and milestone alignment — test whether dietary 20E and nerfin-1 manipulation rescue or phenocopy timing changes, and assess wing disc patterning alignment at pupariation.

**Approach:** Use the PG-specific driver phm-GAL4 (requires verification of line and stock) to manipulate the miR-305-5p/nerfin-1/cyp18a1 axis in the prothoracic gland, since the supplied literature shows that PG miR-305-5p overexpression delays development in a manner rescued by dietary 20E or cyp18a1 knockdown [42267566]. Larvae are staged at L2-L3 or egg collection and exposed to a 2x3 design: standard food at 25°C (control), low-yeast food at 25°C, and standard food at 29°C. Readouts are time to pupariation (hourly scoring, with CV as a robustness measure), ring gland qRT-PCR for nerfin-1 and cyp18a1 in staged prepupae, and 20E titer by ELISA/LC-MS. Controls include GAL4-only and UAS-only parents, a nerfin-1 knockdown/overexpression arm to test directionality, and 20E food rescue to test whether effects act via hormone clearance. Wing disc pattern alignment at pupariation is a secondary readout, following the milestone concept in [24945255].

**Expected outcomes:** Proposed predictions: (1) Control larvae will show a stress-induced shift in median pupariation time (expected ≥12 h under low yeast or 29°C relative to standard 25°C; magnitude to be determined empirically). (2) PG cyp18a1 knockdown will change that shift by ≥6 h with a significant genotype x diet or genotype x temperature interaction (p<0.05, n≥100 larvae/genotype/condition, 3 biological replicates), and raise CV by ≥50%. (3) Stressed prepupal ring glands will show nerfin-1 up ≥1.5-fold and cyp18a1 down ≥30%. (4) Dietary 20E will normalize the timing defects. Falsification: no significant interaction and no change in CV or in the expression of these genes under stress.

**Novelty:** The literature synthesis identifies as untested whether the miR-305-5p/Nerfin-1/Cyp18a1 cascade [42267566] is modulated by nutritional or temperature stress, and that nutritional stress was not directly tested in the supplied primary studies. This proposal tests the cascade as a stress-buffering module, i.e. variance and genotype x environment interaction, rather than just a timing regulator under standard conditions.

**Feasibility:** Estimated 8-10 months: 2 months for stock acquisition and verification of RNAi efficiency (qRT-PCR), 3-4 months for timing assays, 2 months for expression and 20E measurements, and 1-2 months for analysis. Pitfalls: low-yeast diets also change growth/critical weight and may confound timing; RNAi efficiency and off-target effects (use two independent lines); 29°C may reduce GAL4 activity or viability; ring gland dissection of staged prepupae is low-yield, so pooling is needed; the cascade may not respond to stress at all, which is a valid negative result.

## 11. How do mechanical forces during morphogenesis (e.g., germband extension, dorsal closure) feed back on gene expression?

PubMed search: `mechanical forces morphogenesis gene expression`, genes none, 6 papers

### Sonnet 4.6

**Hypothesis:** Drosophila Piezo channel activity in ectodermal epithelial cells is required for mechano-transcriptional feedback during germband extension, such that epithelial-specific loss of Piezo function (using e22c-GAL4 > UAS-Piezo-RNAi) will reduce strain-induced upregulation of Notch target gene expression (e.g., E(spl)mβ-HLH reporter activity) by ≥40% relative to controls, and will cause a ≥15% reduction in germband extension speed (measured as anterior-posterior body axis elongation rate) in stage 7–9 embryos at 25°C, compared to w1118 sibling controls.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Characterize Piezo expression and spatial distribution in embryonic ectodermal cells during germband extension (stages 7–9) using endogenously tagged Piezo-GFP knock-in or immunofluorescence, to confirm that Piezo is expressed in the relevant tissue and developmental window.
- Aim 2: Test whether ectodermal Piezo knockdown (e22c-GAL4 > UAS-Piezo-RNAi) reduces Notch target gene (E(spl)mβ-HLH) reporter activity and germband extension rate by the predicted thresholds (≥40% and ≥15% respectively) in live stage 7–9 embryos imaged by confocal timelapse microscopy.
- Aim 3: Establish causal directionality of mechanical force → Piezo → Notch transcription by acute laser ablation of actomyosin cables in wild-type and Piezo knockdown embryos, testing whether ablation phenocopies Piezo loss in reducing E(spl) reporter activity and whether Piezo knockdown blocks the transcriptional response to experimentally applied mechanical strain.

**Approach:** We will use the GAL4/UAS system to knock down Piezo specifically in the embryonic ectoderm using e22c-GAL4 (a broad ectodermal driver active during germband extension), crossed to a validated UAS-Piezo-RNAi line, to test whether epithelial Piezo is required for mechano-transcriptional feedback. Notch target gene activation will be monitored using a transcriptional reporter transgene driving a fluorescent protein under the E(spl)mβ-HLH enhancer, quantified by confocal live imaging of staged embryos (stage 7–9). Germband extension kinematics will be quantified by tracking tissue displacement using in vivo timelapse microscopy and cell-tracking software (e.g., Fiji/MTrackJ), measuring the rate and extent of anterior-posterior elongation. Controls will include: (1) w1118 wild-type, (2) e22c-GAL4/+ heterozygotes, (3) UAS-Piezo-RNAi/+ heterozygotes, and (4) e22c-GAL4 > UAS-GFP as a GAL4 expression control. To confirm that the transcriptional effect is mechanosensory rather than a secondary consequence of morphogenetic failure, we will also perform laser ablation of actomyosin cables in control embryos to acutely reduce mechanical strain and measure corresponding changes in E(spl) reporter output.

**Expected outcomes:** If the hypothesis is correct: (1) e22c-GAL4 > UAS-Piezo-RNAi embryos will show ≥40% reduction in E(spl)mβ-HLH-GFP fluorescence intensity (mean pixel intensity per cell, normalized to nuclear marker) in the extending germband at stage 8 compared to e22c-GAL4/+ and UAS-Piezo-RNAi/+ controls (p < 0.01, one-way ANOVA with Tukey post-hoc, n ≥ 20 embryos per genotype, 3 independent experiments). (2) Germband extension rate will be reduced by ≥15% in knockdown embryos relative to w1118 controls (measured as μm/min of tissue displacement, p < 0.05). (3) Laser ablation of actomyosin cables in wild-type embryos will reduce E(spl)mβ reporter activity by ≥25% within 30 minutes post-ablation, confirming that mechanical strain is causally upstream of Notch target transcription. If the hypothesis is incorrect, reporter activity and extension rates will be statistically indistinguishable from controls (effect size < 10%), suggesting that Piezo is dispensable for epithelial mechanotransduction during germband extension and that alternative mechanosensors mediate this feedback.

**Novelty:** This experiment directly addresses two identified literature gaps: (1) the molecular identity of the mechanosensor relaying tissue-scale stress to transcriptional machinery in epithelial cells during germband extension is unknown — Piezo has been characterized exclusively in nociceptive sensory neurons in Drosophila and its role in epithelial morphogenesis is completely uncharacterized in the supplied literature; (2) whether Notch-mechanical crosstalk operates specifically during germband extension has not been established, despite general evidence for Notch-mechanics coupling in patterning contexts. No prior study in the supplied literature tests Piezo function in embryonic ectoderm during germband extension or causally links Piezo-mediated Ca2+ influx to Notch target gene transcription during morphogenesis.

**Feasibility:** Live confocal imaging of Drosophila embryos during germband extension (stage 7–9, approximately 3–4 hours post-fertilization at 25°C) is technically routine. GAL4/UAS knockdown efficiency in the embryonic ectoderm using ectodermal drivers is well established. Key risks include: (1) UAS-Piezo-RNAi lines may show incomplete knockdown — western blot or qRT-PCR validation of knockdown efficiency (≥70% target reduction) should be confirmed before phenotypic analysis; (2) e22c-GAL4 expression onset relative to germband extension onset must be confirmed by GFP reporter staging — if expression is too late, an earlier driver (e.g., mata-GAL4 for maternal expression or 69B-GAL4) should be substituted; (3) the E(spl)mβ-HLH reporter line availability is uncertain and may require construction or substitution with an available E(spl) reporter; (4) laser ablation experiments require specialized equipment (two-photon or UV laser) available in core facilities. Estimated timeline: 3 months for fly line generation and validation, 2 months for live imaging and quantification, 1 month for laser ablation experiments, 2 months for data analysis and replication. Total: approximately 8 months.

### Sonnet 5.5

**Hypothesis:** Proposed: In Drosophila embryos at 25°C during dorsal closure, amnioserosa-specific RNAi knockdown of Piezo will reduce mean nuclear intensity of a Notch-responsive transcriptional reporter in amnioserosa cells by at least 30% relative to driver-only controls, and laser-ablation-induced loss of tissue tension will reduce reporter intensity by at least 30% in control embryos but by less than 10% additionally in Piezo-knockdown embryos (n ≥ 15 embryos per genotype, p < 0.05).

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Piezo requirement — test whether amnioserosa-specific Piezo knockdown lowers Notch-reporter expression and delays dorsal closure, with cDNA rescue and a null-allele comparison.
- Aim 2: Force dependence — test with laser ablation whether acute loss of tension reduces reporter expression in control embryos but not further in Piezo-knockdown embryos.
- Aim 3: Pathway epistasis — test with Notch knockdown and combined perturbations whether Piezo and Notch act in the same pathway to link tension to transcription.

**Approach:** Proposed: an amnioserosa-expressing GAL4 driver will drive UAS-Piezo-RNAi, with driver-only and non-targeting RNAi as controls, in embryos carrying a Notch-responsive nuclear reporter and a myosin marker for live imaging. Embryos will be imaged by confocal microscopy through dorsal closure. Reporter intensity, amnioserosa apical area oscillations, and closure rate will be quantified per cell. Tension will be reduced acutely by targeted UV laser ablation of a small group of amnioserosa cells or a segment of the leading edge, and reporter changes will be measured over the following 30-60 minutes against sham-ablated controls. A Piezo null allele and a Notch knockdown arm will test whether Piezo and Notch act in the same pathway, with a Piezo-RNAi plus UAS-Piezo cDNA rescue to control for off-target effects. Because the supplied literature does not address germband extension, the same readouts will be applied there as an exploratory extension, not as the primary test.

**Expected outcomes:** Predicted, not established: Piezo-RNAi embryos will show at least 30% lower mean nuclear reporter intensity in the amnioserosa than driver-only controls. Dorsal closure will be delayed by at least 20% in the same embryos. Laser ablation will reduce reporter intensity by at least 30% in controls, and Piezo-knockdown embryos will show less than 10% additional reduction. The UAS-Piezo cDNA rescue should restore reporter intensity to within 15% of control. Significance will be tested by mixed-effects models with embryo as a random effect, at p < 0.05. If Piezo knockdown changes neither reporter intensity nor closure kinetics, or if ablation reduces the reporter equally in Piezo-knockdown embryos, the hypothesis is falsified.

**Novelty:** Drosophila Piezo is a mechanically activated channel required in vivo for mechanical nociception, but the only Drosophila Piezo evidence in the supplied literature concerns nociceptive neurons, so whether Piezo transduces morphogenetic forces into gene expression in embryonic epithelia is untested. The supplied dorsal closure paper is a modeling review, and whether its force variables translate into measurable changes in gene expression remains untested. Reviews of Notch and mechanics describe interplay but give no specific molecular mechanism by which tissue tension alters Notch target gene transcription in vivo. This proposal combines acute force perturbation, tissue-specific Piezo loss of function and a transcriptional reporter to address these gaps in the amnioserosa.

**Feasibility:** Estimated timeline is 12-18 months: 3 months for line verification, driver and reporter validation, and crosses; 4-6 months for live imaging and ablation; 3-4 months for rescue and epistasis; 2-3 months for analysis. Pitfalls: Piezo may not be expressed in the amnioserosa, which RNA in situ or a tagged allele should check first. Notch reporter activity in the amnioserosa may be low, in which case an alternative mechanosensitive transcriptional reporter will be needed. RNAi knockdown may be incomplete. Laser ablation causes wound responses that could change gene expression independently of tension, so sham ablation and distance-matched controls are required. Other mechanosensitive channels such as nompc or ppk-family members may compensate. The germband extension extension is exploratory.

## 12. Why do imaginal discs lose their capacity to regenerate late in larval development, and can that capacity be restored?

PubMed search: `imaginal disc regeneration capacity late larval`, genes none, 7 papers

### Sonnet 4.6

**Hypothesis:** Combinatorial restoration of trithorax activity (to reverse epigenetic silencing of damage-responsive enhancers) together with activation of JNK signaling (via knockdown of puckered) will restore regenerative growth capacity in late third-instar (L3) wing imaginal discs by ≥50% relative to age-matched undamaged controls, measured as disc area recovery within 48 h post-ablation at 25°C in w1118 female larvae, whereas either manipulation alone will fail to achieve ≥20% recovery — thereby demonstrating that combinatorial chromatin remodeling plus signaling pathway reactivation is both necessary and sufficient to break the late-larval regenerative barrier.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Temporal mapping of regenerative decline — Quantify disc area recovery, EdU incorporation, pJNK, and dILP8-GFP expression at six larval time points (early L2 through late L3, 12-h intervals) following standardized genetic ablation to precisely define when regenerative competence is lost and establish stage-specific baselines for Aims 2 and 3.
- Aim 2: Combinatorial pathway rescue of late-larval regeneration — Test whether simultaneous rn-GAL4-driven UAS-trx overexpression and UAS-puc-RNAi knockdown restores disc area recovery, EdU index, and dILP8 checkpoint activation in late L3 discs to ≥50% of early L3 levels, compared to single-transgene arms and damage-only controls.
- Aim 3: Chromatin state profiling at damage-responsive enhancers — Use CUT&RUN to measure H3K4me3 and H3K27me3 occupancy at the wingless injury-responsive enhancer and at least two additional damage-responsive loci across early and late L3 stages in wild-type and rn-GAL4 > UAS-trx genotypes, to directly test whether trithorax overexpression reverses stage-dependent epigenetic silencing correlated with restored regenerative competence.

**Approach:** We will use the temperature-sensitive GAL4/UAS system combined with the esg-GAL4 or rotund-GAL4 (rn-GAL4) disc-expressed driver crossed to UAS-trithorax (trx) and UAS-puckered-RNAi to simultaneously upregulate trithorax and suppress the JNK-inhibitor puckered in late L3 wing discs; genetic ablation will be induced by a second independent GAL4 driver (e.g., spalt-GAL80ts/spalt-GAL4 driving UAS-reaper) to damage a defined disc compartment, after which larvae will be shifted to permissive temperature for 48 h recovery. All four experimental arms — (1) trx overexpression alone, (2) puc-RNAi alone, (3) trx + puc-RNAi combinatorial, and (4) damage-only controls with UAS-lacZ — will be assayed in parallel in early L3 (regeneration-competent) and late L3 (regeneration-incompetent) stages to establish stage-specific baselines. Disc regeneration will be quantified by confocal-imaged disc area (µm²), EdU incorporation (S-phase index), and immunostaining for JNK activity (pJNK), Wingless protein, and the dILP8 transcriptional reporter to confirm pathway engagement; chromatin accessibility at known damage-responsive enhancers (e.g., the wg injury enhancer) will be assessed by CUT&RUN for H3K4me3 (a trithorax mark) and H3K27me3 in sorted disc cells from each genotype and stage.

**Expected outcomes:** In early L3 (regeneration-competent) larvae, all genotypes including damage-only controls are expected to show ≥70% disc area recovery and ≥3-fold EdU index increase within 48 h. In late L3 damage-only controls (UAS-lacZ), disc area recovery is expected to be ≤15% with minimal EdU increase, consistent with published regenerative failure. In late L3 larvae carrying rn-GAL4 > UAS-trx alone or rn-GAL4 > UAS-puc-RNAi alone, recovery is predicted to remain ≤25% (consistent with prior failure of single-pathway reactivation). In the combinatorial arm (rn-GAL4 > UAS-trx + UAS-puc-RNAi) in late L3, we predict ≥50% disc area recovery, ≥2-fold EdU increase relative to late L3 controls, pJNK signal restoration to ≥60% of early L3 levels, dILP8-GFP induction ≥2-fold above late L3 controls, and significant gain of H3K4me3 at the wg injury enhancer with reciprocal loss of H3K27me3 (assessed by CUT&RUN). Statistical significance threshold: p < 0.05 by ANOVA with Tukey post-hoc, n ≥ 20 discs per genotype per stage per replicate, three independent biological replicates.

**Novelty:** The central novelty is directly testing whether combinatorial reactivation of trithorax-dependent chromatin remodeling and JNK pathway derepression together — but not either alone — can overcome the late-larval regenerative barrier. The literature synthesis explicitly identifies this as an open question: 'Whether combinatorial reactivation of multiple pathways (e.g., JNK + Wingless + chromatin remodeling) could restore late-larval regenerative capacity has not been established' (gap from pmid 19531351 and 26487779). Smith-Bolton et al. (2009, pmid 19531351) showed that Wingless or Myc activation alone cannot restore late-larval regeneration, and Skinner et al. (2015, pmid 26487779) linked trithorax to JNK/dILP8 axis, but no study has combined trithorax restoration with downstream signaling pathway manipulation. Additionally, CUT&RUN profiling of H3K4me3 and H3K27me3 at specific damage-responsive enhancers across larval stages provides the first direct chromatin-level mechanistic readout of the silencing process identified as a gap by Worley & Hariharan (2022, pmid 34872971).

**Feasibility:** All core GAL4/UAS tools are established in the field; the temperature-sensitive ablation approach is standard and reproducible (pmid 34872971). The primary technical challenge is constructing the intersectional genotype combining independent ablation and rescue drivers without mutual interference — this may require use of split-GAL4 components or orthogonal binary systems (e.g., QF/QUAS for rescue vs. GAL4/UAS for ablation), adding 2–3 months of stock construction. CUT&RUN on sorted disc cells is technically demanding given low cell numbers from larval discs (~10,000 cells/disc); pooling 50–100 discs per replicate and using CUT&RUN rather than ChIP-seq mitigates this. Estimated timeline: 3 months stock construction and validation, 2 months ablation and regeneration assays, 3 months CUT&RUN and imaging, 2 months analysis and repetition = ~10 months total. Potential pitfall: UAS-trx overexpression may cause developmental lethality or disc overgrowth independent of damage; mitigation is to use tubGAL80ts to restrict expression temporally and to include no-damage trx-overexpression controls for viability and disc morphology assessment.

### Sonnet 5.5

**Hypothesis:** Proposed: In wing imaginal discs genetically ablated at late third instar (rn-GAL4 > UAS-egr under tub-GAL80ts control, 25°C to 30°C shift protocol), RNAi knockdown of the Polycomb-group gene Pc in the pouch will raise mean adult wing area by at least 15 percentage points of undamaged-control wing area compared with control RNAi. Co-expression of Pc RNAi with Myc will produce a significantly greater rescue than either manipulation alone (two-way ANOVA interaction p<0.05). Myc overexpression alone will change late-stage regeneration by less than 5 percentage points.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Define the late-larval decline — quantify regenerated adult wing area and JNK, dilp8 and enhancer reporter responses after rn-GAL4>egr ablation at early versus late third instar.
- Aim 2: Test chromatin silencing as a tissue-intrinsic block — determine whether Pc knockdown, alone and with Myc or Wg overexpression, restores late-stage regeneration in a 2x2 factorial design.
- Aim 3: Separate local from systemic limits — compare Pc knockdown, trx heterozygosity and experimentally delayed pupariation (method to be verified) for effects on regenerated wing size and dilp8 expression.

**Approach:** Proposed: Wing pouch cells are ablated with rn-GAL4-driven UAS-egr restricted in time by tub-GAL80ts, with the temperature shift at early (about 72 h AEL) versus late (about 96-108 h AEL) third instar. Regeneration is scored as adult wing area relative to undamaged-genotype controls, together with larval pupariation timing. Pc is the candidate silencing machinery, chosen as an inference because the supplied review does not name the silencer. Candidate machinery and knockdown efficiency must be verified, for example by H3K27me3 staining and a second independent RNAi. Wg and Myc are overexpressed with UAS-wg and UAS-Myc, alone and combined with Pc RNAi, in a 2x2 design. Controls are a neutral RNAi (e.g., UAS-mCherry RNAi), undamaged discs, and early-stage ablation as a positive regeneration control. Mechanistic readouts are a JNK reporter (puc-lacZ or equivalent), a dilp8 reporter, and a reporter for a damage-responsive enhancer, all of which require verification of available lines. A trx heterozygote group serves as a checkpoint-defective comparison.

**Expected outcomes:** Predicted, not established: Late-stage ablation will give lower relative adult wing area than early-stage ablation in controls. Pc RNAi will increase late-stage regenerated wing area by at least 15 percentage points over control RNAi (n≥40 wings per genotype, Bonferroni-corrected p<0.05). Pc RNAi plus Myc will exceed the sum of the single manipulations (interaction p<0.05). Pc RNAi will increase damage-responsive enhancer reporter activity in late-stage discs at least 2-fold over control. If Pc RNAi fails to improve regeneration, or if rescue is seen only when pupariation is also delayed, the tissue-intrinsic silencing model is rejected or limited in favor of a systemic checkpoint contribution.

**Novelty:** Prior work shows that as the animal matures, regenerative ability is lost and is not restored by Wingless or Myc activation [19531351]. A review reports a role for localized silencing of damage-responsive enhancers in limiting regeneration as tissues mature [34872971], but the supplied abstract does not identify the enhancers or the silencing machinery. Trithorax is linked to the JNK/dILP8 checkpoint during regeneration [26487779], yet its contribution to the late-larval decline is untested. This proposal tests a candidate silencer in combination with Wg/Myc activation and separates local competence from systemic timing, which the synthesis identifies as unresolved gaps.

**Feasibility:** Estimated 12-15 months: about 3 months for stock assembly and verification, 4 months for the stage-course and Pc/Myc/Wg 2x2 experiments, 3 months for reporter and immunostaining analyses, and 2-3 months for the systemic-timing experiments and replication. Pitfalls: (1) Pc knockdown may be lethal or cause patterning defects independent of regeneration, so temporal control with GAL80ts and a weak RNAi should be used. (2) Late ablation may shorten the regenerative window in a way that confounds wing-size scoring, so relative area and pupariation timing need to be reported together. (3) Pc may not be the relevant silencer, and other Polycomb-group or chromatin regulators may need screening. (4) rn-GAL4 expression may itself change with stage. (5) Several lines need verification and may need to be constructed.

## 13. How do germline and intestinal stem cell niches adjust stem cell output to diet and tissue demand?

PubMed search: `stem cell niche diet intestinal germline`, genes none, 11 papers

### Sonnet 4.6

**Hypothesis:** In adult mated female w1118 Drosophila melanogaster, a shared systemic nutrient-sensing mechanism coordinately regulates both germline stem cell niche size (cap cell number) and intestinal stem cell proliferation uncoupling from niche signals; specifically, we hypothesize that under high-sugar diet (10% sucrose, 0.5% yeast) for 7 days, (1) cap cell number will decrease by ≥30% relative to holidic standard diet controls, (2) ISC mitotic index (phospho-histone H3-positive cells per midgut) will increase by ≥40% relative to controls, (3) both phenotypes will be suppressed (restored to ≤15% deviation from control) by simultaneous gut-specific knockdown of TOR (using Myo1A-GAL4 > UAS-TorRNAi) and ovary-specific Notch activation (using nos-GAL4 > UAS-Notch-ICD), and (4) tissue-specific rescues will fail to fully suppress the contralateral tissue phenotype, demonstrating that at least one systemic intermediary is required for cross-tissue coordination, under 25°C, 12:12 LD cycle conditions.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Simultaneous phenotypic mapping — Quantify germline niche cap cell number and midgut ISC mitotic index in the same individual w1118 and orco1 females across a dietary time course (standard vs. 10% sucrose diet, days 0/3/7/14) to establish whether the two tissue responses are temporally correlated within individual flies.
- Aim 2: Tissue-autonomous versus systemic rescue — Use a 2×2 tissue-specific GAL4/UAS rescue design (gut TOR knockdown, germline Notch activation, both, neither) with tub-GAL80ts temporal control to determine whether each tissue's diet response can be independently suppressed without affecting the other, thereby testing for a required systemic intermediary.
- Aim 3: Systemic signal identification — Measure hemolymph DILP2 and DILP5 levels by ELISA and perform MARCM clonal lineage tracing of cap-cell-to-escort-cell fate transitions in high-sugar-diet flies to identify a candidate systemic hormonal relay and resolve the cellular mechanism of reversible niche contraction.

**Approach:** Adult mated female flies (day 3 post-eclosion) will be transferred to defined holidic medium or high-sugar medium (10% sucrose, 0.5% yeast) for 0, 3, 7, and 14 days; ovaries will be dissected and stained with anti-Fasciclin III (cap cell marker) and anti-Vasa to quantify cap cell number per germarium by confocal microscopy, while midguts will be stained with anti-phospho-histone H3 (pH3) to quantify ISC mitotic index and with anti-Dl (Delta) to confirm ISC identity. To test tissue-autonomous versus systemic coordination, a 2×2 tissue-specific rescue design will be implemented: (A) gut-specific TOR knockdown (Myo1A-GAL4 > UAS-TorRNAi) to attenuate ISC uncoupling, (B) germline-specific Notch activation (nos-GAL4 > UAS-Notch-ICD) to stabilize cap cell number, (C) both rescues simultaneously, and (D) neither rescue (dietary controls), with all genotypes carrying tub-GAL80ts for temporal control (shift to 29°C at day 0 of dietary treatment). Hemolymph insulin-like peptide (DILP2, DILP5) levels will be measured by ELISA at each time point as a candidate systemic intermediary. For the olfactory axis, orco1 mutants and wild-type controls will be subjected to the same dietary paradigm to determine whether loss of olfactory input exacerbates or decouples the two tissue phenotypes.

**Expected outcomes:** Under high-sugar diet for 7 days: (1) cap cell number per germarium will decrease by ≥30% (from ~8 to ≤5.6 cap cells per germarium) in w1118 controls, consistent with established niche contraction; (2) midgut pH3+ cell count will increase by ≥40% per midgut (from a baseline of ~5 to ≥7 pH3+ cells per midgut), consistent with ISC proliferative uncoupling from niche signals; (3) gut-only TOR knockdown (arm A) will suppress the ISC mitotic phenotype to ≤15% above standard diet but will leave cap cell number unchanged (>25% reduction persists), demonstrating tissue autonomy of the enterocyte TOR mechanism; (4) germline-only Notch activation (arm B) will maintain cap cell number (≤10% reduction) but will not suppress ISC uncoupling, similarly demonstrating tissue autonomy; (5) the dual-rescue arm (C) will suppress both phenotypes to ≤15% of standard diet levels, but hemolymph DILP levels will remain altered, indicating a systemic signal remains perturbed; (6) orco1 mutants will show exacerbated cap cell loss (≥45% reduction) and may decouple the ISC response, indicating olfactory input contributes to the coordination. Statistical thresholds: all comparisons by one-way ANOVA with Tukey post-hoc, α = 0.05, n ≥ 20 females per genotype per time point, three biological replicates.

**Novelty:** This experiment directly addresses the identified gap that 'the relationship between diet-dependent niche size changes in the germline and the parallel diet-dependent uncoupling of ISC proliferation from niche signals in the midgut has not been studied in the same animal or dietary paradigm, so it is unknown whether a shared systemic nutrient-sensing mechanism coordinates both tissues simultaneously.' No prior study has simultaneously quantified germline niche contraction and ISC proliferative uncoupling in the same individual fly under identical dietary conditions, nor tested whether tissue-autonomous rescues (gut TOR, germline Notch) are sufficient to suppress the contralateral tissue's response — a prediction that would definitively establish or refute systemic coordination. Additionally, the inclusion of orco1 mutants tests the further gap that 'it is unknown how olfactory-circuit-derived brain signals are transduced systemically to act on germline cysts' by asking whether olfactory input is also required for the cross-tissue coordinated dietary response.

**Feasibility:** All core assays (anti-FasIII immunostaining of germaria, pH3 staining of midguts, confocal microscopy) are standard in Drosophila biology labs and can be completed by a single trained researcher. The tub-GAL80ts temperature-shift strategy is well-established and eliminates developmental confounds. Primary concerns: (1) nos-GAL4 may drive expression in early germline cells rather than cap/escort cells — if so, tj-GAL4 (traffic jam) should be substituted as it drives expression in escort and cap cells (BDSC stock 36,480, requires verification); (2) Notch-ICD overexpression in the germline can cause tumorous germaria, so low-temperature titration (25°C induction) and short experimental windows (≤7 days) will be used to avoid this; (3) DILP ELISA requires hemolymph pooling from ~50 flies per condition and the assay requires either commercial kit or antibody availability (anti-DILP2 antibody from Tatar lab or equivalent); (4) simultaneous dissection and staining of ovaries and midguts from the same individual requires careful dissection scheduling. Estimated timeline: 6 months — month 1-2: line establishment and crossing scheme validation; month 3-4: dietary phenotype characterization (w1118 and orco1); month 5-6: rescue experiments and DILP measurements. Potential pitfall: if cap cell loss on high-sugar diet is less penetrant than the 30% threshold, the dietary concentration or duration should be increased to 15% sucrose or 14 days based on Bonfini et al. 2021 and 2015 parameters.

### Sonnet 5.5

**Hypothesis:** Proposed: In mated adult w1118-background female flies at 25°C, adult-restricted knockdown of eIF3d1 in niche-responsive cells will blunt the reversible, diet-dependent adjustment of stem cell output in both the ovary and the midgut. After a shift from nutrient-poor to nutrient-rich food, cap cell number per germarium will recover at least 30% less, and the increase in phospho-histone H3-positive intestinal stem cell mitoses per posterior midgut will be at least 30% smaller, than in GAL4-only controls (diet x genotype interaction, two-way ANOVA, p<0.05). Hypothesis rationale: Notch-dependent niche remodelling is expected to require diet-sensitive translation initiation downstream of TOR.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Ovarian niche — test whether adult cap/escort-cell eIF3d1 knockdown reduces diet-dependent recovery of cap cell number and GSC number after refeeding.
- Aim 2: Midgut niche — test whether adult progenitor eIF3d1 knockdown reduces refeeding-induced ISC mitosis and midgut progenitor output.
- Aim 3: Pathway placement — use SUnSET, Notch reporters, and NICD, eIF4E and TOR manipulations to test whether eIF3d1 acts with Notch and TOR in a shared diet-responsive module in both tissues.

**Approach:** Use the temperature-sensitive GAL4 system (tub-GAL80ts) to restrict RNAi to adulthood, so developmental defects are avoided. Drive UAS-eIF3d1-RNAi in the ovarian niche (cap/escort-cell driver, such as bab1-GAL4, to be verified) and in midgut progenitors (esg-GAL4). Cross each driver to a non-targeting or empty-vector control and to a second independent eIF3d1 RNAi line. Subject flies to a diet-shift paradigm: rich food, then 7 days of nutrient-poor food, then 5 days of refeeding. Quantify cap cell number (Lamin C or Bab1 staining), GSC number (Vasa and pMad/1B1), ISC mitoses (pH3), and a Notch reporter. Measure global and tissue-level translation by puromycin incorporation (SUnSET), and test epistasis by expressing NICD or eIF4E in the knockdown background and rapamycin or TOR-RNAi in controls. Score all phenotypes blind, with at least 15 germaria or midguts per condition across 3 biological replicates.

**Expected outcomes:** Proposed predictions: in controls, nutrient-poor food will lower cap cell number and ISC mitoses, and refeeding will restore both. eIF3d1 knockdown is predicted to reduce recovery by at least 30% in each tissue (effect size to be tested with Tukey post hoc tests, alpha 0.05), with reduced SUnSET signal in the targeted cells. NICD or eIF4E co-expression should restore at least half of the lost recovery if eIF3d1 acts downstream of Notch or in the same translational module. Falsification: no diet x genotype interaction (p>=0.05), or a recovery difference below 15%, in either tissue would reject the shared-module hypothesis. A phenotype in only one tissue would indicate tissue-specific logic, which is also informative.

**Novelty:** The supplied papers examine diet-responsive Notch signalling in the ovarian niche and diet-dependent TOR and niche-signal coupling in the midgut separately, and integration across the two niches has not been tested. Niche-activated eIF3d1/eIF4F control of stem cell renewal has been shown in the testis but has not been tested in diet-responsive GSC or ISC niches. This design tests the same nutrient manipulation and the same translational perturbation side by side in both tissues.

**Feasibility:** Estimated timeline is 12-14 months: 2 months for line verification and driver validation, 4 months for ovary experiments, 4 months for midgut experiments, and 2-4 months for epistasis, SUnSET and analysis. Pitfalls: eIF3d1 is a general translation factor, so knockdown could cause non-specific toxicity or lethality, which will be mitigated by temporal restriction and a titrated induction time. Cap cell drivers may not be specific, so expression patterns need validation. Diet effects on feeding rate and body size need to be monitored. Rescue by NICD or eIF4E may be partial.

## 14. Which tissues are rate-limiting for lifespan, and can a tissue-specific intervention extend lifespan as much as a whole-body one?

PubMed search: `tissue-specific lifespan extension`, genes none, 11 papers

### Sonnet 4.6

**Hypothesis:** Fat-body-specific overexpression of dFOXO (using ppl-GAL4 > UAS-dFOXO) will extend median lifespan of mated female w1118 flies by ≥15% relative to whole-body dFOXO overexpression (da-GAL4 > UAS-dFOXO) under standard 25°C, ad libitum SY food conditions, whereas muscle-specific dFOXO overexpression (Mhc-GAL4 > UAS-dFOXO) and gut-specific dFOXO overexpression (NP1-GAL4 > UAS-dFOXO) will each extend median lifespan by <10% relative to w1118 GAL4/+ controls, demonstrating that the fat body is the dominant rate-limiting tissue for IIS/dFOXO-mediated longevity and that its tissue-specific manipulation is sufficient to recapitulate ≥75% of the whole-body intervention effect in the same isogenic background.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Quantitative tissue ranking — Determine which single tissue (fat body, muscle, gut, neuron, or whole-body) yields the greatest median lifespan extension upon dFOXO overexpression in mated female and male w1118 flies under identical 25°C standard conditions, establishing a quantitative rank order of tissue rate-limiting contributions to IIS/dFOXO-mediated longevity.
- Aim 2: Sufficiency benchmarking — Test whether fat-body-specific dFOXO overexpression (ppl-GAL4) is sufficient to recapitulate ≥75% of the whole-body (da-GAL4) median lifespan extension, and determine whether this sufficiency holds across both sexes or is female-specific as predicted by sex-specific IIS and TOR literature.
- Aim 3: Transcriptional mechanism — Identify shared and tissue-specific transcriptional effectors of dFOXO-mediated longevity by RNA-seq of fat body at day 15 and day 40 in fat-body-specific versus whole-body dFOXO overexpression flies, with a focus on ILP (ilp2, ilp5), stress response (4E-BP), and chromatin regulator (Rpd3) targets to define the downstream signaling architecture of rate-limiting tissue control.

**Approach:** We will use the GAL4/UAS binary expression system to overexpress UAS-dFOXO in a panel of tissues (whole-body via da-GAL4, fat body via ppl-GAL4, muscle via Mhc-GAL4, gut enterocytes via NP1-GAL4, and neurons via elav-GAL4) in age-matched mated female and male w1118 flies in the same isogenic background. Each experimental genotype (GAL4/+; UAS-dFOXO/+) will be compared against two controls per tissue driver: the GAL4/+ driver-only control (to detect GAL4 toxicity) and the UAS-dFOXO/+ transgene-only control (to detect leaky UAS expression), with ≥200 flies per genotype across three independent biological replicates (n ≥ 600 per group total). Primary endpoint is median and maximum (90th percentile) lifespan assessed by daily mortality scoring; secondary endpoints include intestinal barrier integrity (Smurf assay at 30, 45, and 60 days), climbing assay for locomotor healthspan (weekly from day 10), and fat body dFOXO nuclear localization confirmed by anti-dFOXO immunofluorescence. In parallel, fat-body RNA-seq at day 15 and day 40 will compare transcriptional signatures of tissue-specific versus whole-body dFOXO overexpression to identify shared versus unique downstream effectors, with particular attention to ilp2 and ilp5 transcript levels as established dFOXO targets.

**Expected outcomes:** Based on prior evidence that dFOXO overexpression in the head fat body extends lifespan under high-yeast conditions, we predict: (1) fat-body-specific dFOXO overexpression (ppl-GAL4) will extend female median lifespan by 15–25% over GAL4/+ controls, approximating ≥75% of the whole-body (da-GAL4) effect; (2) whole-body da-GAL4 > UAS-dFOXO will extend female median lifespan by 20–30% consistent with published benchmarks; (3) muscle-specific and gut-specific overexpression will yield <10% median lifespan extension in females, confirming these tissues as non-rate-limiting for dFOXO-mediated longevity; (4) neuronal overexpression will yield an intermediate effect (10–15%) consistent with CNS IIS literature; (5) ilp2 and ilp5 mRNA will be suppressed ≥1.5-fold in fat-body RNA-seq of ppl-GAL4 > UAS-dFOXO flies versus controls; (6) Smurf assay positivity will be delayed by ≥5 days in fat-body and whole-body dFOXO lines relative to controls. Statistical thresholds: log-rank p < 0.01 for lifespan comparisons with Bonferroni correction for multiple tissue comparisons; effect sizes reported as hazard ratios with 95% CIs. Male cohorts are run in parallel; sex-specific effects will be analyzed separately given documented sex bias in dFOXO and TOR interventions.

**Novelty:** No single study has performed a systematic, head-to-head, same-genetic-background comparison of dFOXO overexpression across fat body, muscle, gut, and neurons alongside a whole-body positive control with quantitative ranking of tissue contributions to lifespan — a gap explicitly identified in the literature synthesis. While dFOXO overexpression in the head fat body has been shown to extend lifespan in a diet-dependent manner, its effect has never been directly benchmarked against whole-body dFOXO overexpression in a single unified experiment with matched controls and multiple pathway-relevant secondary endpoints. Furthermore, the literature identifies that muscle is rate-limiting for Ucp4a-mediated longevity and gut/fat body for GCN2-mediated longevity, but the rate-limiting tissue for dFOXO/IIS-specific longevity across multiple tissues simultaneously has not been determined. The inclusion of adult-onset induction via GAL80ts addresses the unresolved confound of developmental dFOXO activity. Finally, the parallel male cohort directly addresses the identified gap that sex-specific tissue rate-limiting effects are largely unexplored.

**Feasibility:** All GAL4 drivers proposed (ppl, da, Mhc, NP1, elav) are widely used in aging studies and are expected to be available from BDSC, though stock numbers require verification before ordering. The UAS-dFOXO line is well-characterized in the literature; if BDSC availability is uncertain, it can be obtained from collaborating labs (e.g., Partridge, Bhatt, or Tatar labs). The experimental design is technically straightforward using established GAL4/UAS methodology. Primary lifespan assays require 90–100 days per cohort; with three biological replicates staggered by 4 weeks, total lifespan data collection spans approximately 8–10 months. RNA-seq adds 2–3 months of analysis. Potential pitfalls include: (1) GAL4 driver leakiness — mitigated by UAS-only and GAL4-only controls; (2) dFOXO overexpression toxicity at high levels (especially da-GAL4) — mitigated by GAL80ts for adult-onset induction in a parallel arm; (3) ppl-GAL4 may have expression in some non-fat-body tissues — expression pattern should be confirmed by crossing to UAS-GFP reporter and imaging; (4) diet-dependence of dFOXO effects requires standardized SY food preparation across all replicates with batch verification. Estimated total timeline: 14–16 months from line acquisition to manuscript submission. Budget considerations: lifespan assays at scale require dedicated incubator space and fly pushing labor; RNA-seq costs approximately $300–500 per sample (20–24 samples total).

### Sonnet 5.5

**Hypothesis:** Proposed: In a matched-driver comparison using a single UAS-Methioninase insertion in one isogenic background at 25°C, fat body-driven Methioninase expression will recover at least 70% of the median lifespan extension produced by ubiquitous expression in mated females, whereas muscle-, neuron-, gut- or oenocyte-restricted expression will each recover no more than 40%, and no single tissue will fully equal whole-body extension.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Matched-driver lifespan comparison — measure median and maximum lifespan of ubiquitous versus fat body, muscle, neuron, gut and oenocyte Methioninase expression in a single isogenic background, and calculate the fraction of whole-body extension recovered by each tissue.
- Aim 2: Insertion-site and driver controls — use a landing-site-matched control transgene, driver-alone and UAS-alone lines, and adult-restricted induction (GAL80ts or GeneSwitch) to determine how much of each tissue's apparent effect is due to the insertion site, the driver or developmental expression.
- Aim 3: Sex and mechanism — test each tissue driver in males and females for driver x sex interaction, and measure hemolymph methionine and related metabolites to determine whether the rate-limiting tissue acts systemically or cell-autonomously.

**Approach:** Express a single UAS-Methioninase insertion with a ubiquitous driver and in parallel with fat body, muscle, neuron, gut and oenocyte drivers, all in the same isogenic background and on the same standard diet. Adult-restricted expression (tub-GAL80ts temperature shift or GeneSwitch with RU486 and vehicle-fed controls) separates adult effects from developmental effects. Each driver-alone and UAS-alone control is run in parallel, plus a landing-site-matched control transgene (for example UAS-neutral reporter at the same attP site), because the transgene insertion site used for tissue-specific RNAi can itself lengthen lifespan.
