Each genotype is run in both sexes (at least 200 flies per genotype per sex, 10 vials of 20, flies transferred 3 times per week). Median lifespan is analysed by log-rank and Cox models with a driver x sex interaction and Bonferroni correction, and the fraction of whole-body extension recovered is reported with bootstrap 95% CIs. Hemolymph or whole-fly methionine and related metabolites are measured by LC-MS at day 10 and day 30 to test whether the rate-limiting tissue acts through systemic methionine depletion or cell-autonomously.

**Expected outcomes:** Proposed predictions, not established results: ubiquitous Methioninase will extend median lifespan by at least 20% over the matched controls. Fat body expression is predicted to recover at least 70% of this gain, and muscle, neuron, gut and oenocyte expression each no more than 40% (log-rank p < 0.01 after correction). If any single tissue recovers 80% or more, or if fat body recovers under 50%, the hypothesis is falsified. Hemolymph methionine is expected to fall in proportion to lifespan recovery across drivers (Spearman rho > 0.7), which would support a systemic mechanism over a cell-autonomous one. Landing-site control flies are expected to differ from driver-alone controls by under 5% in median lifespan; a larger difference would indicate an insertion-site artefact. A driver x sex interaction with p < 0.05 would indicate that tissue rate-limitation differs between sexes.

**Novelty:** Parkhitko et al. reported that both whole-body and tissue-specific Methioninase expression can dramatically extend lifespan [34588310], but the literature synthesis found that the tissues responsible for Methioninase-mediated extension are not identified in the supplied abstract. No supplied study compares, within one experiment, whole-body versus tissue-specific manipulation of the same gene using matched drivers. This design fills that gap by testing five tissue drivers and a ubiquitous driver side by side with a landing-site-matched control, and it also addresses the untested sex-specificity of tissue-specific interventions.

**Feasibility:** Estimated timeline is 18-24 months: 3 months for line verification, backcrossing and construction of the control transgene; 8-10 months for lifespan cohorts of both sexes, run in two staggered replicates; 3 months for LC-MS metabolomics; 3 months for analysis. Pitfalls: (1) the Methioninase transgene and some drivers may not be available and may need to be rebuilt; (2) some drivers have leaky or ectopic expression, which should be verified with a reporter; (3) Methioninase is a bacterial enzyme and may affect the microbiome or feeding, so food intake (CAFE or dye assay) should be measured; (4) GeneSwitch RU486 can itself affect lifespan, so vehicle and drug controls are required; (5) background effects require backcrossing for at least 6 generations into a common background; (6) enzyme secretion or leakage could mean that the tissue of expression is not the tissue of action, which the hemolymph measurements are designed to detect.

## 15. Which components of dietary restriction (total protein, specific amino acids, sugar ratio) are necessary and sufficient for lifespan extension?

PubMed search: `dietary restriction amino acids lifespan`, genes none, 10 papers

### Sonnet 4.6

**Hypothesis:** Dietary restriction of isoleucine alone (to 10% of holidic diet standard concentration, with all other amino acids and sugar held constant) will extend median lifespan by ≥15% in mated female w1118 Drosophila melanogaster at 25°C compared to flies on a complete holidic diet, while equivalent restriction of total protein (all amino acids reduced to 10% of standard) will produce a smaller or equivalent lifespan extension (≥15%), and this isoleucine-specific lifespan extension will be abolished in females with fat-body-specific TOR hyperactivation (r4-GAL4 > UAS-Rheb) but not in dFOXO null mutants, demonstrating that isoleucine sensing acts through TORC1 rather than IIS to extend lifespan in a sex-specific manner.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Isoleucine sufficiency for lifespan extension — Determine whether restriction of isoleucine alone (to 10% standard on holidic diet) is sufficient to extend median female lifespan by ≥15%, and compare the magnitude to total protein restriction and leucine restriction, to establish amino-acid specificity of the longevity effect.
- Aim 2: Sugar-to-protein ratio independence — Test whether altering dietary sucrose concentration (10% or 200% of standard) while holding all amino acids constant produces significant lifespan changes, and whether caloric compensation via sucrose supplementation during isoleucine restriction negates lifespan extension, to determine whether carbohydrate ratio is an independent or dependent variable in DR-mediated longevity.
- Aim 3: TORC1 vs. IIS pathway requirement — Use fat-body-specific TOR hyperactivation (r4-GAL4>UAS-Rheb) and dFOXO null mutants to causally determine whether isoleucine-restriction-induced lifespan extension requires TORC1 suppression in the fat body and is independent of dFOXO/IIS, and validate pathway engagement by measuring phospho-S6K levels in fat body tissue across dietary conditions.

**Approach:** Female w1118 flies (mated, 25°C, n≥150 per dietary group) will be maintained on chemically defined holidic diets with systematic single-amino-acid restriction: (1) complete holidic diet control, (2) isoleucine restricted to 10% standard, (3) total protein restricted to 10% standard (all amino acids reduced proportionally), (4) leucine restricted to 10% standard (closely related branched-chain amino acid control), (5) isoleucine-restricted diet with sugar (sucrose) increased 2-fold to control for caloric compensation, and (6) sugar-only restriction (sucrose reduced to 10%) keeping amino acids constant. Lifespan assays will be conducted in triplicate cohorts with weekly food changes and blind scoring. Genetic dissection will use fat-body-specific TOR activation (r4-GAL4 > UAS-Rheb) and dFOXO null mutants on isoleucine-restricted vs. complete holidic diets to test pathway requirements. TORC1 activity will be measured by western blot (phospho-S6K/total S6K ratio) in fat body tissue at days 10, 20, and 30. Fecundity will be scored weekly as an independent fitness endpoint to assess reproduction-lifespan trade-offs. All dietary groups will be compared using log-rank tests with Bonferroni correction (α=0.05), and Cox proportional hazards models will assess genotype-by-diet interactions.

**Expected outcomes:** Primary endpoint: Isoleucine restriction (10% standard) will extend median female lifespan by ≥15% relative to complete holidic diet control (e.g., from ~60 days to ≥69 days median survival; log-rank p<0.05). Total protein restriction will also extend lifespan by ≥15%, but the magnitude of isoleucine-specific extension will be statistically non-inferior to total protein restriction (overlapping 95% CIs), supporting sufficiency of isoleucine restriction. Leucine restriction will extend lifespan by <10% (or not significantly), establishing amino-acid specificity. Sugar-only restriction will not significantly extend lifespan (p>0.05), confirming that carbohydrate ratio is not the primary driver. Caloric compensation (isoleucine restricted + 2x sucrose) will not diminish the isoleucine-restriction lifespan benefit (<5% difference from isoleucine restriction alone), confirming a calorie-independent mechanism. TORC1 activity (phospho-S6K/S6K ratio) will be reduced ≥30% in fat body of isoleucine-restricted females relative to controls at day 10. Fat-body TOR hyperactivation (r4-GAL4>UAS-Rheb) will abolish the isoleucine-restriction lifespan extension (median lifespan on ILe-restricted diet not significantly different from complete-diet controls; p>0.05), while dFOXO null flies will retain ≥10% median lifespan extension under isoleucine restriction (p<0.05), confirming TORC1-dependence and IIS-independence. Fecundity on isoleucine-restricted diet will be reduced ≥20% relative to complete diet, demonstrating a reproduction-lifespan trade-off.

**Novelty:** This experiment directly addresses two key gaps identified in the literature synthesis: (1) which specific amino acids are necessary and sufficient for lifespan extension (as opposed to stress resistance) has not been systematically tested — prior work showed isoleucine deprivation confers stress resistance but did not measure lifespan endpoints; (2) no causal pathway linking individual amino acid sensing to longevity has been established — the role of TORC1 vs. IIS in mediating isoleucine-specific (rather than bulk protein) lifespan extension is unresolved. Additionally, this design directly tests whether dietary sugar ratio influences lifespan independently of protein content, a question identified as underexplored in the geometric framework literature. The parallel genetic epistasis design (TORC1 hyperactivation vs. dFOXO null) in the context of single-amino-acid restriction is novel and will causally distinguish pathway requirements that prior genetic studies conducted only in the context of bulk yeast-based dietary restriction.

**Feasibility:** Holidic (chemically defined) diets for Drosophila are established and published; the exact isoleucine-restricted holidic diet formulation requires calibration against published holidic diet recipes (e.g., Piper et al. 2014, Nature Protocols) to confirm palatability and consumption rates before lifespan assays begin (estimated 1 month optimization). Lifespan assays require 3–4 months per complete cohort; with three biological replicates run in parallel, total lifespan data collection is estimated at 4–5 months. Western blotting for phospho-S6K in Drosophila fat body is technically established but requires dissection of ≥20 fat bodies per sample for sufficient protein yield — this is technically demanding but routine. Potential pitfall: isoleucine restriction may reduce food intake non-specifically (dietary aversion), confounding calorie-independent interpretations — this will be controlled by measuring food intake via CAFE assay or dye-based consumption assays in a separate cohort. BDSC stock numbers for r4-GAL4 and UAS-Rheb require verification before ordering. Sex-specific analysis requires male cohorts to be run in parallel (adding ~30% additional workload) to assess whether effects are female-specific as predicted. Total estimated timeline: 8–10 months including optimization, lifespan assays, and molecular endpoint collection.

### Sonnet 5.5

**Hypothesis:** Proposed: In mated female Drosophila melanogaster (w1118 background, 25°C, 12:12 light:dark, measured food intake), lowering dietary isoleucine and methionine together to 25% of the reference concentration on a chemically defined diet, with all other amino acids, sucrose and total energy held constant, will extend median lifespan by ≥15% over the reference diet (log-rank P<0.01) and will reproduce ≥50% of the extension produced by lowering all essential amino acids to 25%. Restoring isoleucine and methionine to reference levels in the low-essential-amino-acid diet will remove ≥50% of that extension. Varying sucrose alone over a 4-fold range at fixed amino acid concentrations will change median lifespan by <5%.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Separate protein, amino acid and sugar axes — map lifespan response surfaces across a total amino acid × sucrose grid on a chemically defined diet with measured intake, and test the prediction that sucrose alone changes median lifespan by <5%.
- Aim 2: Necessity and sufficiency of specific amino acids — test whether reducing Ile and Met (alone and together) is sufficient for ≥50% of the extension from total essential amino acid reduction, and whether their add-back is necessary to remove ≥50% of the extension.
- Aim 3: Genetic requirement for TORC1/4E-BP — test whether Thor loss-of-function or adult fat-body Tor knockdown blocks the amino-acid-driven lifespan extension (genotype × diet interaction).

**Approach:** Proposed: A geometric-framework design on a chemically defined (holidic-type) diet in mated w1118 females, with 150-200 flies per condition across at least 3 independent cohorts. Diet arms: (i) a grid of total amino acid concentration × sucrose concentration, which separates protein, sugar and protein:carbohydrate ratio effects; (ii) single or paired amino acid reduction (Ile, Met, Ile+Met, and the remaining essential amino acids pooled) with compensation for total nitrogen and energy; (iii) add-back arms for necessity and subtractive arms for sufficiency. Food intake will be measured per diet (e.g., CAFE or dye-based consumption) so that any lifespan effect can be separated from altered feeding, and fecundity will be recorded as a covariate. Lifespan will be analysed by log-rank and Cox models with diet as a covariate, and by fitting nutrient response surfaces. In a follow-up arm, Thor (4E-BP) loss-of-function and adult fat-body Tor knockdown flies will be tested on the informative diets to see whether TORC1 pathway components are required for any amino-acid-driven extension, with genotype-matched controls and adult-restricted induction to avoid developmental confounds.

**Expected outcomes:** Proposed predictions, not established results: (1) Median lifespan rises monotonically as total amino acids fall, with sucrose varied 4-fold changing median lifespan by <5% at fixed amino acids. (2) Ile+Met reduction alone gives ≥15% median extension, i.e. ≥50% of the effect of total essential amino acid reduction. (3) Add-back of Ile+Met reduces the DR extension by ≥50%. (4) Intake differences between diets will be <15%, so the effects cannot be attributed to altered consumption. (5) If Thor loss or fat-body Tor knockdown abolishes ≥50% of the amino-acid effect (genotype × diet interaction P<0.05), TORC1/4E-BP is implicated. If the extension persists, it is TORC1/4E-BP-independent. Falsification: if Ile+Met reduction gives <5% extension, or sucrose variation shifts median lifespan by ≥10%, the hypothesis is rejected.

**Novelty:** The literature synthesis reports that specific nutrients, not calories, mediate longevity in Drosophila. This finding is from 21187139. Dietary restriction extended lifespan in the presence and absence of dFOXO, as shown in 18221413. Isoleucine deprivation protected against several stressors and was not replicated by protein restriction in females, as shown in 39106944, but that work measured stress resistance, not lifespan. No supplied paper tests lifespan under single-amino-acid restriction versus total protein restriction in a full geometric-framework design with measured intake. The sugar component and protein:carbohydrate ratio are also not tested for lifespan in the supplied abstracts, and the requirement for TORC1 or amino-acid sensing components in DR lifespan extension is untested. This proposal addresses these gaps together by crossing amino acid identity, total protein and sugar in one lifespan design.

**Feasibility:** Proposed timeline of about 14-18 months: 2-3 months for diet formulation and intake validation, 8-10 months for lifespan cohorts (median lifespan on chemically defined diets may be around 60-80 days; cohorts run in parallel), and 3-4 months for the genetic arm and analysis. Pitfalls: chemically defined diets may give shorter baseline lifespan than yeast-based diets; single amino acid reduction may change feeding (compensatory intake), which is addressed by intake measurement; amino acid interactions (e.g., Ile/Leu/Val antagonism) may complicate add-back; and sex and genetic background effects may limit generality (males and a second background are proposed as extensions). Stock numbers and allele identities must be verified before ordering. The numbers of flies are sized for log-rank detection of a ~10-15% median change; power calculations should be finalized with pilot data.

## 16. How does the gut report nutritional status to the brain to shape food choice, and which enteroendocrine signals carry it?

PubMed search: `enteroendocrine gut brain food choice`, genes none, 15 papers

### Sonnet 4.6

**Hypothesis:** Gut enteroendocrine cell (EEC)-derived NPF signals to brain insulin-producing neurons (IPCs) via NPFR expressed on the two dopaminergic enteric relay neurons, and this relay is necessary and sufficient for macronutrient-specific food choice: specifically, we hypothesize that selective silencing of NPFR in dopaminergic enteric neurons will phenocopy EEC-specific NPF knockdown (≥30% increase in sucrose intake and ≥25% decrease in yeast intake relative to controls in a two-choice capillary feeding assay in mated female w1118 flies at 25°C, 12:12 LD), and that acute optogenetic activation of NPFR-expressing dopaminergic enteric neurons will suppress sucrose intake by ≥30% within 30 minutes of stimulation under the same conditions.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Necessity of the NPFR dopaminergic enteric relay — Determine whether silencing or knocking down NPFR in TH∩NPFR dopaminergic enteric neurons phenocopies EEC-specific NPF depletion in macronutrient-specific food choice (sucrose vs. yeast) in mated female flies using CAFE assay with Kir2.1 and UAS-npfr-RNAi effectors
- Aim 2: Sufficiency of the NPFR dopaminergic enteric relay — Test whether acute optogenetic activation of TH∩NPFR dopaminergic enteric neurons is sufficient to suppress sucrose intake within 30 minutes in freely feeding mated females expressing UAS-CsChrimson, and map CNS axonal projection targets using UAS-mCD8-GFP anatomical analysis
- Aim 3: Downstream IPC calcium dynamics — Establish whether the dopaminergic enteric relay causally drives IPC activity by measuring GCaMP7s calcium responses in dilp2-GAL4 IPCs during gut sucrose perfusion when enteric neurons are simultaneously silenced with Kir2.1 in ex vivo intact gut-to-brain preparations

**Approach:** We will use the split-GAL4 intersectional strategy to isolate the two NPFR-expressing dopaminergic enteric neurons identified in PMID 38664401, crossing a TH-GAL4 (dopaminergic) driver hemidrive with an NPFR-LexA or NPFR-GAL4 hemidrive to restrict expression to the relevant neurons. Food choice will be quantified using the two-choice capillary feeding assay (CAFE) with 5% sucrose vs. 5% yeast extract solutions over 24-hour intervals in individual vials; total intake, macronutrient preference index (PI = [sucrose consumed − yeast consumed] / [sucrose + yeast consumed]), and meal bout frequency/duration will be recorded. For loss-of-function, UAS-Kir2.1 (electrical silencing) and UAS-npfr-RNAi lines will be expressed in NPFR dopaminergic enteric neurons; for gain-of-function, UAS-CsChrimson will enable red-light (620 nm, 20 µW/mm²) optogenetic activation of these neurons in freely feeding flies on all-trans retinal-supplemented food. Controls will include: (1) driver-only crosses (no UAS effector), (2) UAS-only crosses (no driver), (3) UAS-mCD8-GFP crosses for anatomical confirmation of split-GAL4 specificity, and (4) virgin vs. mated female cohorts to assess reproductive-state dependence. Downstream IPC activity will be assessed by in vivo calcium imaging (UAS-GCaMP7s in dilp2-GAL4 IPCs) combined with ex vivo gut-to-brain preparation during nutrient perfusion experiments to determine whether enteric neuron silencing blunts the IPC calcium response to sucrose stimulation. All experiments will use ≥10 biological replicates per genotype per condition; statistical analysis will use one-way ANOVA with Tukey's post hoc correction, α = 0.05.

**Expected outcomes:** Aim 1: Silencing NPFR in TH∩NPFR dopaminergic enteric neurons will increase the sucrose preference index by ≥0.30 units (absolute) relative to driver-only and UAS-only controls (expected PI shift from ~0.0 to ≥+0.30) and decrease yeast intake by ≥25%, measured over 24 h in mated female flies. This result would confirm necessity of the dopaminergic relay. Aim 2: CsChrimson-mediated acute activation of the same neurons (30 min red-light pulse) will reduce sucrose intake by ≥30% relative to non-stimulated retinal-fed controls and non-retinal red-light controls, confirming sufficiency. Aim 3: IPC GCaMP7s calcium responses to gut sucrose perfusion will be reduced by ≥40% (ΔF/F peak amplitude) when the dopaminergic enteric neurons are simultaneously silenced with Kir2.1, compared to Kir2.1-absent controls, establishing the mechanistic link between the enteric relay and IPC activity. Virgin females are predicted to show attenuated effects relative to mated females (consistent with PMID 36344765 sex-specificity findings). All comparisons require p < 0.05 after Tukey correction with n ≥ 10 per group.

**Novelty:** Existing work (PMID 38664401) identified two NPFR-expressing dopaminergic enteric neurons as mediators of the gut NPF-to-brain relay but did not (1) functionally establish their necessity and sufficiency for macronutrient-specific food choice using bidirectional manipulations, (2) map their axonal targets in the CNS, or (3) link their activity causally to downstream IPC calcium dynamics. This experiment fills the gap explicitly identified in the literature synthesis: 'The identity and circuitry of the dopaminergic enteric neurons that relay NPF signals toward the brain have only been partially described; their axonal targets in the CNS and the downstream circuits governing macronutrient-specific food choice remain unmapped.' Additionally, no prior study has combined optogenetic gain-of-function with calcium imaging of IPCs in the same experimental framework to close the circuit from gut EEC → enteric dopaminergic relay → brain IPC.

**Feasibility:** The core behavioral assays (CAFE two-choice) and GAL4/UAS manipulations are standard in Drosophila neuroscience and are achievable in a well-equipped fly lab. The primary technical challenge is the split-GAL4 intersection: if existing NPFR-GAL4 or LexA drivers do not recapitulate the precise two-neuron expression pattern described in PMID 38664401, CRISPR-mediated T2A-GAL4 knock-in at the npfr locus may be required (~3–6 additional months). Ex vivo gut-brain calcium imaging requires intact semi-dissected preparations with nutrient perfusion, which demands training but is established in the field. Estimated timeline: Aim 1 behavioral characterization 3–4 months; Aim 2 optogenetics 2–3 months; Aim 3 calcium imaging 4–6 months; total ~12–18 months. Potential pitfalls: (1) TH-GAL4 may label additional dopaminergic neurons beyond the two enteric neurons, necessitating anatomical validation and possibly additional intersectional specificity; (2) CsChrimson experiments require strict retinal and light-exposure controls to avoid thermal artifacts; (3) mating status must be rigorously controlled across all cohorts given documented sex-specificity of gut NPF signaling (PMID 36344765).

### Sonnet 5.5

**Hypothesis:** Proposed: in mated adult female flies on a w1118 background at 25°C, adult-onset, enteroendocrine-cell-specific NPF overexpression acts through two dissociable, parallel arms. (i) NPFR in dopaminergic enteric neurons accounts for at least 50% of the reduction in total food intake but not for the shift in macronutrient choice. (ii) AKH-producing cells account for at least 50% of the shift in sugar:yeast preference (a ≥30% decrease in sugar preference index) but not for the reduction in total intake. Falsification: if NPFR knockdown in dopaminergic enteric neurons blocks the sugar-preference shift by ≥50%, or if Akh knockdown blocks the total-intake reduction by ≥50%, the parallel-arm model is rejected in favour of a serial pathway.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Define the gut NPF behavioural phenotype — quantify total intake and sugar:yeast preference after adult-onset EEC-specific NPF overexpression in mated females, with GAL80ts and brain-restriction controls.
- Aim 2: Test the NPFR-enteric-dopaminergic-neuron arm — determine how much of the total-intake and macronutrient-choice phenotypes depends on NPFR in dopaminergic enteric neurons.
- Aim 3: Test the AKH arm and epistasis — determine how much of each phenotype depends on AKH-producing cells, and run double manipulations to distinguish parallel from serial pathways.

**Approach:** Use temporally controlled GAL4/UAS (tub-GAL80ts) to overexpress NPF in adult enteroendocrine cells with a gut-restricted driver, and confirm that the driver does not express in the brain. Cross this to cell-type-specific knockdown or manipulation of the candidate downstream arms. For the enteric neuron arm, use NPFR RNAi in dopaminergic neurons with a gut-restricted intersectional strategy (TH-GAL4 combined with a brain-restricting GAL80). For the AKH arm, use Akh-GAL4 or Akh RNAi, with Akh mutants as a secondary test. Feeding is measured in mated females with the FlyPAD or CAFE assay, in two ways: total intake on a single diet, and a two-choice assay (sucrose vs. yeast) that yields a sugar preference index. Controls are UAS-only and GAL4-only genotypes, GAL80ts permissive vs. restrictive temperature, matched age and mating status, and a starvation-state control. Genotype × manipulation interactions are tested by two-way ANOVA, with n ≥ 30 flies per genotype across ≥3 independent replicates.

**Expected outcomes:** Proposed predictions, not established results: (1) Gut NPF overexpression reduces total intake by ≥30% and sugar preference index by ≥30% vs. controls (p<0.05, Bonferroni-corrected). (2) NPFR knockdown in dopaminergic enteric neurons restores ≥50% of total intake but restores <25% of the sugar preference shift. (3) Akh knockdown restores ≥50% of the sugar preference shift but restores <25% of the total intake reduction. A significant genotype × manipulation interaction in each case would support dissociable parallel arms. Double manipulation is predicted to be additive for the sum of the two phenotypes. If instead both knockdowns block both phenotypes, the pathways are serial or converge.

**Novelty:** Gut NPF has been reported to act through AKH to suppress sugar appetite and raise protein intake in PMID 36344765, and separately to inhibit feeding through NPFR-expressing dopaminergic enteric neurons in PMID 38664401. The literature synthesis identifies as unresolved whether these are the same or parallel pathways and how an overall anorexigenic signal produces nutrient-specific choice. No supplied study tests the two arms by epistasis in a single design with separable readouts for total intake and macronutrient choice. This proposal does so.

**Feasibility:** Estimated timeline is 12-14 months: 3 months for line construction, verification and driver validation (including gut restriction); 6 months for feeding assays; 3 months for epistasis and double manipulations; 2 months for analysis. Pitfalls: (a) the intersectional driver for dopaminergic enteric neurons is unverified and may leak or fail; (b) RNAi efficiency must be checked by qPCR or reporter; (c) feeding readouts differ between FlyPAD and CAFE, so key results should be confirmed with both; (d) mating and diet history strongly modulate feeding, so these must be standardised; (e) developmental effects are avoided by GAL80ts but need temperature controls; (f) AKH also affects metabolism and locomotion, which may confound feeding readouts.

## 17. Is age-related intestinal barrier failure a cause of death or a marker of it, and what triggers it?

PubMed search: `intestinal barrier dysfunction aging death`, genes none, 10 papers

### Sonnet 4.6

**Hypothesis:** Age-related intestinal barrier failure is a proximate cause of death rather than merely a marker, such that acute restoration of enterocyte mitochondrial function (via inducible, enterocyte-specific overexpression of BNIP3 after barrier failure has already been detected at day 40 in female w1118 flies at 25°C) will reduce intestinal permeability by ≥40%, suppress systemic AMP expression by ≥30%, restore Akt phosphorylation in the fat body to levels indistinguishable from young (day-10) controls, and extend median lifespan by ≥15% relative to uninduced age-matched controls bearing the same transgenes, as assessed in a blinded, replicated survival assay of ≥150 flies per group across ≥3 independent cohorts.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Temporal causality — Determine whether acute, post-onset induction of enterocyte BNIP3 overexpression (via NP1-GAL4/tub-GAL80ts TARGET system initiated at confirmed day-40 barrier failure) extends median lifespan by ≥15% and reduces intestinal permeability by ≥40% in mated female w1118-background flies at 25°C, directly testing whether intestinal barrier dysfunction is a cause rather than a marker of death.
- Aim 2: Mitochondrial mechanism — Quantify mitophagy flux (mito-QC), enterocyte apoptosis (Dcp-1), and systemic metabolic restoration (fat-body pAkt, AMP mRNA) within 3, 7, and 14 days of BNIP3 induction post-barrier failure to establish the mechanistic sequence linking enterocyte mitochondrial quality control to systemic aging phenotype reversal.
- Aim 3: Cell-non-autonomous axis — Test whether post-failure induction of neuronal BNIP3 (elavC155-GAL4/tub-GAL80ts) initiated at day-40 barrier failure can rescue intestinal permeability and extend lifespan, and compare the magnitude and kinetics of rescue to enterocyte-specific induction to identify the direction and sufficiency of the neuronal–intestinal inter-organ signal.

**Approach:** We will use the TARGET system (tub-GAL80ts combined with NP1-GAL4, which is expressed specifically in adult enterocytes) to achieve temporal control of BNIP3 overexpression: flies are raised at 18°C (GAL80ts active, transgene off) and shifted to 29°C (GAL80ts inactivated, transgene on) only after intestinal barrier failure is confirmed in a sentinel cohort by the Smurf assay (blue food dye leakage) at day 40 post-eclosion. The primary endpoint is median lifespan in GAL80ts-restricted, NP1>UAS-BNIP3 animals compared to three controls: (1) NP1-GAL4 alone shifted to 29°C, (2) UAS-BNIP3 alone shifted to 29°C, and (3) the full genotype maintained at 18°C (transgene off). Secondary endpoints measured in parallel cohorts sacrificed at 0, 3, 7, and 14 days post-induction include: intestinal permeability (Smurf assay scored in individual flies), enterocyte mitophagy flux (mito-QC reporter), enterocyte apoptosis (activated Dcp-1 immunostaining), systemic AMP mRNA levels (Diptericin, Attacin by RT-qPCR from whole carcasses), fat-body Akt phosphorylation (pAkt/total Akt western blot), and ISC proliferation rate (pH3-positive cell counts per midgut). To probe the neuronal–intestinal axis as an independent upstream trigger, a parallel arm will use elavC155-GAL4 combined with GAL80ts to induce BNIP3 specifically in neurons after confirmed barrier failure at day 40, testing whether the cell-non-autonomous rescue reported for constitutive neuronal BNIP3 expression can still operate after barrier failure has occurred. All survival cohorts use mated females, standard cornmeal-molasses diet, 25°C (except the temperature-shift protocol), density of 20 flies per vial, scored every 2 days; statistical analyses use log-rank test with Bonferroni correction for multiple comparisons and Cox proportional hazards to adjust for cohort effects.

**Expected outcomes:** Primary survival outcome: induction of NP1>BNIP3 after confirmed barrier failure at day 40 will extend median lifespan by ≥15% (expected median ~55 days in controls, ~63 days in induced flies; p < 0.01 log-rank, ≥150 flies/group, ≥3 cohorts). Within 7 days of induction: intestinal permeability (Smurf-positive fraction) will decrease from ~60–70% in uninduced day-40 controls to ≤30% in induced flies (≥40% relative reduction). Systemic Diptericin and Attacin mRNA will be suppressed by ≥30% relative to uninduced controls (RT-qPCR, n ≥ 15 flies/timepoint). Fat-body pAkt/total-Akt ratio will increase to within 1.5-fold of day-10 young controls (western blot, n ≥ 5 biological replicates). ISC mitotic index (pH3+ cells/midgut) will decrease from the elevated aged baseline toward young-fly levels (≤5 pH3+ cells/midgut vs. ~15 in aged uninduced controls). If the lifespan extension is absent despite confirmed reduction in intestinal permeability, this would support IBD as a marker rather than a cause. If lifespan extension occurs without full permeability restoration, it would implicate a shared upstream driver. The neuronal BNIP3 arm is expected to show partial but smaller rescue (≤10% lifespan extension) given the indirect nature of the cell-non-autonomous signal, providing an internal specificity control.

**Novelty:** All prior literature establishing that targeting intestinal barrier integrity promotes longevity used constitutive or preventive interventions (S3QELs from early life, lifelong BNIP3 overexpression) rather than post-onset rescue; the gap explicitly identified in the synthesis is that 'a direct rescue experiment post-onset is needed to confirm IBD as a cause rather than a marker' [PMID 37144684, PMID 23236133]. This design is the first proposed to use inducible, tissue-specific BNIP3 overexpression initiated only after spontaneous barrier failure has already occurred in aged animals, directly testing causality rather than prevention. Additionally, while BNIP3-mediated mitophagy in neurons improves intestinal homeostasis [PMID 36213625], the identity of cell-non-autonomous signals is unresolved per the synthesis; the parallel neuronal arm here positions the experiment to detect whether post-failure neuronal induction can still rescue gut phenotypes, addressing the gap in inter-organ signaling. The mechanistic link between complex-III superoxide and Hif-1α was established in dietary (high-nutrient) barrier disruption [PMID 34521156]; whether the same mitochondrial axis underlies spontaneous age-onset barrier failure at normal diet is untested and addressed here.

**Feasibility:** The TARGET system with NP1-GAL4 and tub-GAL80ts is well-established in Drosophila intestinal biology and should present no fundamental technical barriers. The main challenge is constructing double-balanced driver lines (NP1-GAL4; tub-GAL80ts and elav-GAL4; tub-GAL80ts), which requires 3–4 months of standard genetics. Sentinel-cohort Smurf assays to time the temperature shift add logistical complexity but are routine. The 29°C induction temperature independently shortens lifespan (~15–20% reduction relative to 25°C), which is a critical confound; all control genotypes must undergo identical temperature shifts, and a no-transgene w1118 temperature-shift control must be included to partition temperature effects from transgene effects. Estimated total timeline: months 1–4 for line construction and validation; months 5–6 for pilot Smurf-assay timeline calibration and mito-QC confirmation; months 7–14 for primary survival and biochemical cohorts (three independent survival runs of ~2 months each); months 15–18 for mechanistic follow-up (proteomics of gut secretome, candidate inter-organ factor identification). Total n required: ~1,800 flies for survival arms plus ~600 for biochemical endpoints. Potential pitfalls: (1) incomplete GAL80ts repression at 18°C causing leaky baseline BNIP3 expression — validate by RT-qPCR and western blot at 18°C before experiments; (2) temperature-shift stress at day 40 causing acute mortality spike unrelated to transgene — use tightly controlled ramp protocol and large cohort sizes; (3) NP1-GAL4 having some expression in other tissues — confirm specificity by crossing to UAS-GFP and imaging; (4) UAS-BNIP3 line may not be publicly deposited — contact Walker lab directly for transfer.

### Sonnet 5.5

**Hypothesis:** Proposed (not established): In mated female flies on standard diet at 25°C, adult-onset, enterocyte-specific inhibition of complex III superoxide signaling, by S3QEL feeding or enterocyte Hif-1α knockdown started at day 20, will delay median time to Smurf (barrier-failure) conversion by at least 15% and extend median lifespan by at least 10% versus vehicle or control-RNAi flies (log-rank p<0.01). The same interventions started only after an individual fly converts to Smurf will extend median post-Smurf survival by at least 25%. If Smurf delay occurs without lifespan extension, or post-Smurf intervention has no effect, barrier failure is more likely a marker than a cause.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Trigger test — determine whether enterocyte complex III superoxide and Hif-1α contribute to spontaneous age-onset Smurf conversion on standard diet, using day-20 S3QEL feeding and adult-onset enterocyte Hif-1α RNAi.
- Aim 2: Cause versus marker — compare pre-onset versus post-Smurf-onset interventions, and an optional junction-restoring manipulation, for effects on time to Smurf conversion, post-Smurf survival and total lifespan.
- Aim 3: Order of events — measure Dpt and Drs expression, enterocyte apoptosis and microbiota dependence (axenic versus conventional) in pre-Smurf and Smurf flies, to place immune activation and microbiota relative to barrier failure.

**Approach:** Use a longitudinal, individual-fly Smurf assay (blue food dye) in mated females to record the time of barrier failure and the survival that follows it. Drive enterocyte-restricted, adult-inducible manipulations with an enterocyte GeneSwitch or TubGAL80ts-controlled driver to avoid developmental effects. The manipulations are Hif-1α RNAi and a pharmacological S3QEL arm, each with matched controls (control RNAi plus RU486 or temperature shift; vehicle-fed). Intervention timing is a three-way design: from day 20 (pre-failure), from day 40 or at first Smurf conversion (post-onset), and never. Secondary readouts are Dpt and Drs transcript levels by qPCR in dissected guts from pre-Smurf versus Smurf flies, to confirm barrier-linked immune activation. Barrier integrity is confirmed by a second permeability measure, and enterocyte apoptosis is assessed by staining. Axenic versus conventionally reared flies are compared to place microbiota in the sequence of events. Lifespan cohorts of at least 200 flies per arm are used, with Cox regression and a Smurf-state time-varying covariate.

**Expected outcomes:** If barrier failure is causal and driven by enterocyte complex III superoxide in normal aging, then day-20 S3QEL or Hif-1α RNAi will lower the fraction of Smurf flies at day 40 by at least 30% relative to controls and extend median lifespan by at least 10%. Post-Smurf intervention is predicted to lengthen median post-Smurf survival (control roughly several days) by at least 25%. Dpt and Drs induction is predicted to be higher in Smurf than in age-matched non-Smurf flies in all arms, and to be reduced in treated flies mainly through delayed Smurf conversion. Axenic flies are predicted to show a modest delay in Smurf onset (at least 10%); a larger delay would implicate microbiota upstream of failure. Alternative outcomes: Smurf delay without lifespan gain supports 'marker'; no change in Smurf timing supports a trigger other than complex III superoxide in aging on standard diet. Significance threshold: p<0.01 for log-rank tests with Bonferroni correction across arms.

**Novelty:** The supplied literature shows that barrier dysfunction predicts impending death independent of chronological age in individual flies [23236133]. Diet-induced barrier disruption involves enterocyte complex III superoxide, and Hif-1α knockdown in enterocytes also protected on a high-nutrient diet [34521156]. Reviews describe an emerging concept that targeting barrier integrity promotes longevity [37144684]. The identified gaps are that causality is unresolved and that it is untested whether the complex III mechanism operates in normally aged flies on standard diet. This plan addresses both gaps by pairing a temporally staged intervention design (before versus after the first Smurf conversion) with a test of the diet-derived trigger in spontaneous aging.

**Feasibility:** Estimated timeline is 12-14 months: 2 months for line verification, backcrossing and S3QEL dose validation, 6-8 months for lifespan cohorts with daily Smurf scoring, and 2-3 months for qPCR, imaging and analysis. Pitfalls include RU486 or GeneSwitch effects on lifespan (controlled by matched vehicle and control-RNAi arms), temperature shift effects with GAL80ts, and the S3QEL dose and route being unvalidated on standard diet. Post-Smurf lifespans are short (days), so statistical power requires large cohorts and frequent scoring. Smurf conversion may be irreversible, so post-onset interventions may fail to restore the barrier even if the barrier is causal (a false negative); the optional junction-restoration arm partially addresses this. Line identities and stock numbers must be verified before the work begins. Human relevance is not assessed.

## 18. What tissue-level mechanisms produce sex differences in lifespan and in the response to lifespan interventions?

PubMed search: `sex differences lifespan`, genes none, 9 papers

### Sonnet 4.6

**Hypothesis:** Sex-differential activity of dFOXO signaling in the fat body is the primary tissue-level mechanism producing sexually dimorphic lifespan in Drosophila melanogaster, such that fat body-specific overexpression of dFOXO (using ppl-GAL4) will extend median lifespan by ≥20% in mated female w1118 flies but by <10% in mated male w1118 flies under standard 25°C conditions with ad libitum food, and fat body-specific Rpd3 knockdown (RNAi) will recapitulate this sex-biased lifespan extension through upregulation of dFOXO target genes, with a ≥1.5-fold greater transcriptional response in females than males as measured by qRT-PCR of canonical dFOXO targets (4E-BP/thor, InR) in dissected fat bodies.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Sex-specific lifespan effects of fat body dFOXO — Quantify median and maximum lifespan in mated males and mated females carrying ppl-GAL4 > UAS-dFOXO versus controls under ad libitum and dietary restriction conditions at 25°C to determine whether fat body dFOXO overexpression produces a sex-biased lifespan extension of ≥20% in females and <10% in males.
- Aim 2: Sex-specific fat body transcriptional response to dFOXO and Rpd3 manipulation — Measure mRNA levels of dFOXO target genes (thor/4E-BP, InR) and IIS members (ilp2, ilp5) by qRT-PCR in dissected fat bodies from 10-day-old and 30-day-old adult males and females carrying ppl-GAL4 > UAS-dFOXO or ppl-GAL4 > UAS-rpd3-RNAi, to determine whether the transcriptional response to these interventions is ≥1.5-fold greater in females than males.
- Aim 3: Interaction of fat body dFOXO/Rpd3 manipulation with dietary restriction in a sex-specific context — Apply a yeast dilution dietary restriction protocol (1% vs. 0.1% SY) across all genotypes and sexes from Aims 1–2 to test whether DR and fat body dFOXO/Rpd3 manipulation act additively or epistatically on lifespan extension, and whether this interaction is itself sex-dependent, addressing the unresolved question of whether the IIS-independent DR pathway and the Rpd3/dFOXO axis converge differentially in males versus females.

**Approach:** We will use the GAL4/UAS system to achieve fat body-specific manipulation of dFOXO and Rpd3 in both sexes simultaneously, employing ppl-GAL4 (a well-characterized adult fat body driver) crossed to UAS-dFOXO, UAS-rpd3-RNAi, and appropriate controls (UAS-lacZ, GAL4/+ alone). Lifespan assays will be conducted separately for mated males and mated females (n ≥ 150 per sex per genotype across ≥3 independent cohorts) at 25°C on standard yeast-glucose medium, with cohorts maintained at a standardized density of 20 flies per vial to control for density-dependent social effects on lifespan identified in the literature. Molecular endpoints will include qRT-PCR of dFOXO transcriptional targets (thor/4E-BP, InR, ilp2, ilp5) and IIS pathway members in dissected fat bodies from 10-day-old and 30-day-old adults of both sexes, as well as triglyceride and glycogen storage assays to assess sex-specific metabolic consequences. As a secondary aim, dietary restriction (dilution of yeast from 1% to 0.1% SY medium) will be applied to all genotypes and sexes to determine whether the IIS-independent DR pathway interacts additively or epistatically with fat body dFOXO/Rpd3 in a sex-specific manner. All crosses will include w1118 as the genetic background control, and GAL4-only and UAS-only siblings will serve as within-cross controls.

**Expected outcomes:** Primary outcome: ppl-GAL4 > UAS-dFOXO females will show ≥20% extension of median lifespan relative to ppl-GAL4/+ female controls, while males of the same cross will show <10% median lifespan extension, with log-rank test p < 0.01 for the sex-by-genotype interaction. Secondary outcome: ppl-GAL4 > UAS-rpd3-RNAi will produce a similar sex-biased pattern (≥15% female extension, <10% male extension) relative to controls. Molecular outcome: qRT-PCR of fat body tissue from 10-day-old adults will reveal ≥1.5-fold greater induction of thor/4E-BP and ≥1.5-fold greater repression of ilp2 mRNA in dFOXO-overexpressing females compared to males (Student's t-test or Mann-Whitney U, p < 0.05). Metabolic outcome: triglyceride levels in fat bodies of dFOXO-overexpressing females will be elevated ≥1.3-fold relative to controls, consistent with increased energy storage reported downstream of Rpd3/IIS manipulation. DR interaction: dietary restriction will extend lifespan in both sexes and all genotypes, but the additive effect of DR combined with fat body dFOXO overexpression will be significantly greater in females than males (two-way ANOVA interaction term, p < 0.05), consistent with independent but convergent pathways.

**Novelty:** The literature synthesis explicitly identifies as a critical gap that 'no study directly maps which specific tissues drive sexually dimorphic lifespan or differential responses to DR or IIS manipulation in a sex-specific manner in Drosophila' and that 'the sex-specificity of dietary restriction effects on lifespan in Drosophila is not addressed in the provided literature.' While fat body dFOXO overexpression has been shown to extend lifespan and regulate ilp2/ilp5 (PMID 18221413) and Rpd3 has been shown to interact with IIS through dFOXO (PMID 27852975), neither study examined sex as a variable, and neither the sex-specific transcriptional response of fat body dFOXO targets nor the sex-differential efficacy of Rpd3/dFOXO manipulation on lifespan has been characterized. The proposed experiment directly addresses whether the fat body is the tissue mediating sexually dimorphic lifespan, filling the gap between established pathway-level knowledge and unanswered tissue-level sex-specificity.

**Feasibility:** All core GAL4/UAS tools required are either in wide circulation or available from BDSC, though specific stock numbers for ppl-GAL4 and UAS-dFOXO must be verified before ordering. The lifespan assay protocol is standard and well-established; the primary technical challenge is maintaining sufficiently large cohorts (≥150 per sex per genotype, ~6 genotypes = ~1,800 flies minimum per replicate) and ensuring consistent mating status. Fat body dissections for qRT-PCR require practice but are achievable in any Drosophila lab. Estimated timeline: 2 months for stock acquisition and validation of GAL4 expression pattern by GFP reporter; 3–4 months for primary lifespan assays (three independent cohorts staggered); 2 months for molecular assays (qRT-PCR, metabolite measurements); 1 month for DR lifespan assays = approximately 8–9 months total. Potential pitfalls include: (1) ppl-GAL4 may have some activity in non-fat-body tissues (gut, oenocytes) requiring validation by UAS-GFP imaging; (2) dFOXO overexpression may reduce fecundity differentially by sex, requiring tracking of reproductive output as a covariate; (3) sex-by-density interactions (PMID 35290690) require strict vial density standardization; (4) if sex difference is smaller than predicted, power calculations suggest n = 200 per sex per genotype may be needed.

### Sonnet 5.5

**Hypothesis:** Proposed: Adult-onset, fat body-restricted knockdown of Rpd3 (RU486-induced GeneSwitch-GAL4 driving UAS-Rpd3-RNAi) in mated flies on a w1118 background at 25°C on standard diet will extend median lifespan in both sexes. The extension will differ between sexes by at least 10 percentage points (sex-by-genotype interaction in a Cox proportional hazards model, p < 0.05, n >= 150 flies per sex per genotype per treatment). In the more responsive sex, the extension will be reduced by at least 50% by concurrent fat body foxo knockdown. The direction of the sex difference is not known in advance; the hypothesis is falsified if the interaction is below 10 percentage points or not significant.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Sex-stratified lifespan response — measure median and maximum lifespan after adult-onset fat body Rpd3 knockdown in males and females and test the sex-by-genotype interaction under fixed density and mating conditions.
- Aim 2: dfoxo dependence — determine in the responsive sex whether concurrent fat body foxo knockdown reduces the Rpd3-RNAi lifespan extension by at least 50%.
- Aim 3: Tissue-level readouts — quantify sex-specific fat body transcript changes (Rpd3, dfoxo targets, IIS members) and head Ilp2 and Ilp5 expression at day 10 and day 30 to identify which tissue-level changes track the lifespan response.

**Approach:** Use a fat body GeneSwitch-GAL4 driver so that RNAi is induced only in adults with RU486, which avoids developmental confounds. Compare RU486 versus vehicle in each sex, using sibling-matched genotypes (driver/+, UAS/+, driver>UAS) and a control RNAi, with the same density per vial and mated status in both sexes. Males and females are housed in single-sex vials after a fixed 48 h mating window, which keeps density and mating status constant [35290690]. Lifespan is scored every 2-3 days. Parallel cohorts are sampled at day 10 and day 30 for sex-stratified fat body qPCR of Rpd3, foxo targets and IIS readouts, and for Ilp2 and Ilp5 transcript levels in dissected heads. Dependence on dfoxo is tested by combining Rpd3 RNAi with foxo RNAi in the same fat body driver. RU486 itself has sex-dependent effects and must be controlled with driver-only and UAS-only genotypes plus vehicle in each sex.

**Expected outcomes:** Proposed predictions, not established results. (1) Fat body Rpd3 knockdown with RU486 will extend median lifespan by at least 8% in at least one sex relative to both genetic controls and vehicle. (2) The sex-by-genotype interaction will be significant (p < 0.05) with a difference of at least 10 percentage points between sexes. (3) In the responsive sex, adding foxo RNAi will reduce the gain in median lifespan by at least 50%. (4) Fat body qPCR will show sex-different changes in dfoxo target expression after Rpd3 knockdown, with a fold-change difference of at least 1.5 between sexes. If the extension is equal in both sexes and foxo knockdown abolishes it in both, the hypothesis is rejected and the intervention acts through a sex-shared fat body mechanism.

**Novelty:** Woods et al. [27852975] linked rpd3 reduction to IIS downregulation and dfoxo-dependent longevity, and Min et al. [18221413] provided head fat body dFOXO data, but neither abstract reports sex-stratified analyses. The literature synthesis found that no provided paper identifies a tissue-level mechanism for sex differences in lifespan or in the response to lifespan interventions (inference from absence in the provided set). This proposal tests a tissue-restricted, sex-stratified manipulation of a defined aging pathway in the fat body. It also tests the sex-by-genotype interaction and the dfoxo requirement directly, rather than relying on descriptive sex-by-environment effects [35290690] or evolutionary explanations such as Mother's Curse, whose support is patchy [38523670].

**Feasibility:** All components are standard Drosophila methods. Estimated timeline: 2 months for crosses and RNAi validation, 5-6 months for lifespan cohorts (about 3,600 flies for the 2 sexes x 5 genotypes x 2 treatments design, or fewer if the foxo double-knockdown arm is run only in the responsive sex), and 2 months for qPCR and analysis, about 10 months in total. Pitfalls: (a) the identity and fat body specificity of the GeneSwitch driver, and RNAi efficiency, must be verified; (b) RU486 can have sex-dependent toxic or physiological effects, so drug-only controls in each sex are mandatory; (c) the sexes differ in reproductive investment, so mating status must be fixed; (d) background effects require backcrossing to the w1118 background; (e) the Rpd3 effect may be dose-sensitive, so a RU486 dose series may be needed.

## 19. How does the fat body integrate signals from several organs to control insulin-like peptide release from the brain?

PubMed search: `fat body insulin-like peptide secretion`, genes ['Ilp2'], 15 papers

### Sonnet 4.6

**Hypothesis:** Fat body-specific overexpression of tribbles (trbl) suppresses dilp2 transcription in brain IPCs by increasing dFOXO nuclear localization in pericerebral fat body cells, and this suppression is mediated by at least one secreted humoral factor; specifically, we predict that fat body trbl overexpression will reduce dilp2 mRNA levels in IPCs by ≥40% and reduce hemolymph DILP2 protein by ≥35% compared to genetic controls in adult mated female w1118 background flies at 25°C, and that these effects will be abolished when fat body secretion is broadly disrupted by co-expression of dominant-negative Rab1 (Rab1-DN) in the fat body, demonstrating a secretion-dependent inter-organ signaling mechanism.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Quantify trbl-to-IPC signaling — Determine whether fat body-specific trbl overexpression suppresses dilp2 mRNA in brain IPCs and reduces hemolymph DILP2 protein by ≥40% and ≥35%, respectively, in adult mated female flies at days 5, 10, and 20 post-eclosion using RT-qPCR, smFISH, and DILP2 ELISA.
- Aim 2: Test secretion dependence — Determine whether co-expression of dominant-negative Rab1 in the fat body abolishes trbl-induced suppression of brain dilp2 expression and hemolymph DILP2, thereby establishing whether the fat body-to-IPC signal is a secreted humoral factor.
- Aim 3: Assess functional IPC output — Determine whether fat body trbl overexpression reduces IPC calcium activity (GCaMP7s ΔF/F by ≥25%) and downstream whole-body pAKT/total AKT ratios (by ≥30%) compared to genetic controls, linking fat body trbl signaling to functional suppression of brain IPC activity and systemic IIS.

**Approach:** We will use the fat body-specific r4-GAL4 driver to overexpress UAS-trbl and/or UAS-dFOXO transgenes in adult female flies, then quantify dilp2 mRNA in dissected brain IPCs by smFISH and RT-qPCR, and DILP2 protein in hemolymph by ELISA, at days 5, 10, and 20 post-eclosion. To test whether the fat body signal is secreted rather than contact-dependent, we will co-express a dominant-negative vesicle trafficking transgene (UAS-Rab1-DN) alongside UAS-trbl in the fat body using r4-GAL4, predicting rescue of brain dilp2 levels if secretion is required. As controls, we will use r4-GAL4/+ and UAS-trbl/+ heterozygotes, w1118 wild-type, and a trbl RNAi knockdown line to confirm specificity. Downstream IPC activity will be assessed by calcium imaging (UAS-GCaMP7s expressed under dilp2-GAL4) and by measuring InR pathway activity (pAKT/total AKT ratio by western blot) in whole-fly lysates. All assays will be performed on mated females reared on standard cornmeal-yeast medium at 25°C, 60% humidity, with n ≥ 30 flies per genotype per time point, and statistical analysis by one-way ANOVA with Tukey post-hoc correction.

**Expected outcomes:** Aim 1: Fat body-specific trbl overexpression (r4-GAL4 > UAS-trbl) will reduce dilp2 mRNA in IPCs by ≥40% (measured by RT-qPCR, normalized to rp49) and hemolymph DILP2 protein by ≥35% (ELISA) compared to r4-GAL4/+ and UAS-trbl/+ controls at day 10, consistent with published phenotypic data (PMID 37083954). Aim 2: Co-expression of UAS-Rab1-DN in the fat body will rescue dilp2 mRNA and DILP2 hemolymph levels to within 15% of control values, supporting the secreted-factor hypothesis; failure to rescue would support a contact-dependent or neural relay mechanism. Aim 3: IPC calcium activity (GCaMP7s ΔF/F) will be reduced by ≥25% in r4-GAL4 > UAS-trbl flies compared to controls under fed conditions, and pAKT/total AKT ratios in whole-fly lysates will decrease by ≥30%, confirming functional suppression of downstream IIS. Statistical thresholds: p < 0.05 after Tukey correction, with n ≥ 30 flies per genotype per time point across ≥3 independent biological replicates.

**Novelty:** The literature establishes that fat body dFOXO activation suppresses brain dilp2 expression (PMID 15175753) and that fat body trbl overexpression reduces circulating DILP2 (PMID 37083954), but the identified gap is that 'the upstream fat body sensor and downstream secreted factor linking fat body trbl activity to brain IPC dilp2 expression has not been identified' and 'whether dFOXO-driven fat body signals are secreted factors or require direct cell contact would clarify the mechanistic basis of this non-autonomous regulation.' This experiment directly tests the secretion-dependence of the trbl-to-brain-IPC signaling axis using a Rab1-DN strategy, which has not been reported in the context of fat body trbl or dFOXO-to-IPC communication, and combines live calcium imaging of IPCs with hemolymph DILP2 quantification to distinguish transcriptional from secretory control — addressing the additional gap that 'how nutritional signals differentially regulate DILP protein levels versus dilp transcript levels in IPCs is unclear.'

**Feasibility:** All core transgenic components (r4-GAL4, UAS-trbl, UAS-dFOXO, GCaMP reporters, w1118) are established Drosophila tools, though specific stock numbers for several lines require verification at BDSC before ordering. The DILP2 hemolymph ELISA protocol has been validated in prior published work (PMID 37083954). The primary technical challenge is the Rab1-DN secretion block, which may have pleiotropic effects on fat body cell viability; fat body cell death must be assessed by TUNEL or caspase staining as a quality control. smFISH on dissected brains is technically demanding and requires optimization for dilp2 probe sets; RT-qPCR on pooled dissected brain tissue (≥20 brains per replicate) is a more tractable alternative for transcript quantification. GCaMP calcium imaging of IPCs requires live-brain preparation expertise and may have low throughput. Estimated timeline: line verification and crossing scheme — months 1–2; assay optimization (ELISA, RT-qPCR, smFISH) — months 2–4; Aim 1 data collection — months 3–6; Aim 2 (Rab1-DN crosses and assays) — months 5–9; Aim 3 (calcium imaging and pAKT) — months 7–12. Total estimated timeline: 12–14 months. Potential confound: r4-GAL4 may have expression outside the fat body in some developmental stages; adult-restricted expression using the GeneSwitch system (r4-GS-GAL4 with RU486 induction) should be considered as an alternative to limit developmental effects.

### Sonnet 5.5

**Hypothesis:** Proposed (not established): In 7-day-old mated female flies at 25°C on standard diet, adult-onset, fat body-specific dFOXO activation for 5 days will reduce DILP2 release from the insulin-producing cells (IPCs). Release is read out as a ≥30% lower hemolymph-to-IPC DILP2 ratio and a ≥30% smaller glucose- or depolarisation-evoked IPC GCaMP response than genotype-matched uninduced controls. The ratio change is predicted to exceed the accompanying change in dilp2 mRNA, so that at least part of the effect reflects release control rather than transcription alone. Fat body-specific Dilp6 knockdown is predicted to reverse ≥50% of the ratio reduction, which would make fat body Dilp6 a candidate relay.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Separate release from transcription — in adult fat body foxo and trbl manipulations, quantify IPC DILP2 retention, hemolymph DILP2, dilp2/3/5 mRNA and IPC calcium responses in fed and starved mated females.
- Aim 2: Test Dilp6 as a fat body relay — determine whether fat body dilp6 knockdown reverses the foxo-induced reduction in IPC DILP2 release and calcium activity.
- Aim 3: Test multi-organ integration and sex dependence — combine fat body manipulation with a second organ manipulation (gut or AKH axis) and compare additive versus non-additive IPC output in females and males.

**Approach:** Use a temperature-inducible fat body driver (tub-GAL80ts with a fat body GAL4; the specific driver requires verification) to express UAS-foxo or UAS-trbl in the adult fat body only, which avoids developmental confounds. Measure IPC release with an epitope-tagged DILP2 reporter: IPC retention by immunostaining and circulating DILP2 by hemolymph ELISA or dot assay, alongside dilp2/3/5 qPCR to separate transcriptional from release effects. Perform ex vivo or in vivo IPC GCaMP imaging (dilp2-GAL4 > UAS-GCaMP) with glucose or KCl stimulation after fat body manipulation. Test the Dilp6 relay hypothesis by combining fat body foxo activation with fat body dilp6 RNAi. Test integration by adding a second organ-specific manipulation (a gut or AKH-axis manipulation, lines to be selected and verified) and comparing additive versus non-additive IPC responses. Controls include uninduced (18°C) animals, driver-only and UAS-only genotypes, a UAS-lacZ or UAS-GFP control, both sexes, and fed versus 24 h starved states.

**Expected outcomes:** Proposed predictions: (1) Fat body foxo activation lowers the hemolymph/IPC DILP2 ratio by ≥30% (n≥10 per group, ≥3 biological replicates, two-way ANOVA with Tukey post-hoc, p<0.05), with a smaller percentage change in dilp2 mRNA. (2) IPC GCaMP peak ΔF/F0 is reduced by ≥30%. (3) trbl overexpression phenocopies this at ≥50% of the foxo effect size. (4) Fat body dilp6 RNAi restores ≥50% of the ratio and calcium responses. If dilp6 RNAi restores <20%, the Dilp6 relay hypothesis is rejected and other humoral candidates are indicated. (5) Starvation and fat body manipulation are expected to interact non-additively if the IPCs weigh multiple inputs, whereas a purely additive outcome would argue for independent inputs. (6) Effect sizes in males are predicted to differ from those in females.

**Novelty:** Fat body dFOXO activation lowers dilp2 expression [15175753] and fat body trbl overexpression lowers DILP2 levels [37083954], but neither study measured IPC peptide release. Fat body Dilp6 is active in the hungry state while IPC Dilp2 acts in the sated state [37847673]. Whether Dilp6 feeds back on IPC Dilp2 release has not been tested. Combined organ-specific manipulations with IPC calcium imaging are lacking, and Diet-dependent dilp changes were detected in females but not males [33990225]. This plan pairs release-specific assays with fat body manipulations, a Dilp6 epistasis test, and sex comparison.

**Feasibility:** Estimated timeline is 12-15 months: 3 months for stock acquisition and verification, recombination and GAL80ts crosses; 4 months for Aim 1 release assays; 3 months for the Dilp6 epistasis and calcium imaging in Aim 2; 3-5 months for the multi-organ and sex comparison in Aim 3. Pitfalls: hemolymph volumes from adults are small and require pooling; fat body drivers may show leaky expression in other tissues (check with GFP reporters); dilp2-GAL4 and the fat body GAL4 compete for GAL4 use, so a binary LexA system may be needed; GCaMP responses vary with feeding state and need standardised timing; and long-term fat body dFOXO activation may cause systemic stress, so lifespan and feeding (CAFE) controls are required. The reagent availability (tagged dilp2, RNAi, drivers) is not documented in the supplied literature and needs verification.

## 20. How does reproduction trade off against somatic maintenance, and which signal from the germline sets that balance?

PubMed search: `reproduction lifespan trade-off germline`, genes none, 8 papers

### Sonnet 4.6

**Hypothesis:** Germline-derived Imp-L2 acts as a secreted endocrine signal that is released into the hemolymph and taken up by fat body somatic tissue, where it suppresses IIS and activates dFOXO-dependent somatic maintenance programs; specifically, we propose that (1) germline-restricted overexpression of Imp-L2 (using nanos-GAL4 > UAS-Imp-L2) will increase median lifespan by ≥15% and increase 10-day fecundity by ≥20% relative to GAL4-only and UAS-only controls in mated female w1118 flies at 25°C on standard cornmeal-yeast medium, and (2) this dual benefit will be abolished (lifespan and fecundity returned to control levels, ≤5% difference) when dFOXO is simultaneously knocked down specifically in the fat body (r4-GAL4 > UAS-dFOXO-RNAi in a nanos-GAL4 > UAS-Imp-L2 background), demonstrating that somatic fat body dFOXO is a required downstream effector of the germline Imp-L2 signal.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Germline Imp-L2 overexpression — quantify the effect of germline-specific UAS-Imp-L2 expression (nanos-GAL4) on median lifespan and 10-day fecundity in mated female w1118 flies relative to GAL4-only and UAS-only parental controls at 25°C
- Aim 2: Endocrine secretion — determine whether Imp-L2 expressed in the germline is detectable as a secreted protein in hemolymph and in fat body tissue, and whether fat body dFOXO nuclear localization and transcriptional targets (Thor/4EBP) are upregulated in Imp-L2-overexpressing females
- Aim 3: dFOXO epistasis — test whether fat body-specific dFOXO knockdown (r4-GAL4 > UAS-dfoxo-RNAi) is sufficient to abolish the lifespan and fecundity benefits conferred by germline Imp-L2 overexpression, establishing dFOXO as a required somatic effector of the germline signal

**Approach:** We will use the GAL4/UAS binary expression system with tissue-specific drivers to (1) overexpress or knock down Imp-L2 exclusively in the germline using nanos-GAL4, and (2) independently manipulate dFOXO in the adult fat body using r4-GAL4, exploiting the temperature-sensitive GAL80ts (tubulin-GAL80ts) to restrict transgene expression to adult stages and avoid developmental confounds. Lifespan assays (n ≥ 150 females per genotype, three independent cohorts) and daily fecundity counts (eggs laid per female per day, n ≥ 30 females for 10 days) will be the primary endpoints. To test whether Imp-L2 is secreted into hemolymph, we will perform hemolymph western blots and co-immunoprecipitation from fat body lysates in flies expressing HA-tagged UAS-Imp-L2::HA under nanos-GAL4, comparing signal in gonad-intact versus germline-ablated (bam-RNAi) females. Fat body dFOXO nuclear localization and target gene expression (4EBP/Thor, InR) will be assessed by immunofluorescence and qRT-PCR to confirm pathway engagement. All genotypes will include matched GAL4-only and UAS-only parental controls raised simultaneously under identical husbandry conditions (25°C, 12:12 LD, standard cornmeal-molasses-yeast medium, 10 females per vial).

**Expected outcomes:** Aim 1: nanos-GAL4 > UAS-Imp-L2 females will show ≥15% increase in median lifespan and ≥20% increase in cumulative 10-day fecundity versus both parental controls (log-rank p < 0.01 for lifespan; Mann-Whitney U p < 0.05 for fecundity; n ≥ 150/genotype). Aim 2: HA-tagged Imp-L2 will be detectable by western blot in hemolymph of nanos-GAL4 > UAS-Imp-L2::HA females but not in germline-ablated controls, and co-immunoprecipitation from fat body lysates will pull down Imp-L2::HA, consistent with a secreted endocrine mechanism; dFOXO nuclear localization in fat body cells will increase ≥1.5-fold (fluorescence intensity ratio nuclear:cytoplasmic) and Thor/4EBP mRNA will increase ≥2-fold by qRT-PCR in Imp-L2-overexpressing versus control flies. Aim 3: Simultaneous fat body dFOXO knockdown (r4-GAL4 > UAS-dfoxo-RNAi in the nanos-GAL4 > UAS-Imp-L2 background) will reduce median lifespan and 10-day fecundity to within ≤5% of w1118 parental controls, demonstrating epistasis; fat body dFOXO overexpression alone (r4-GAL4 > UAS-dfoxo) will extend median lifespan ≥10% consistent with published IIS/dFOXO fat body data. Statistical analysis: Kaplan-Meier survival curves with log-rank test, Cox proportional hazards for covariates, Bonferroni correction for multiple comparisons.

**Novelty:** This experiment directly addresses three gaps identified in the literature synthesis: (1) it tests the unresolved mechanism by which germline Imp-L2 bypasses the canonical trade-off by asking whether its effects are mediated through fat body dFOXO, a relationship that has not been examined (gap: 'The relationship between epigenetic regulators (Rpd3/HDAC1) and germline-derived longevity signals has not been examined'); (2) it explicitly tests whether Imp-L2 acts via a secreted endocrine versus paracrine mechanism, resolving the open question of 'whether the germline signal acts cell-autonomously within the gonad or is secreted to influence somatic tissues systemically'; and (3) it combines germline-specific and fat body-specific genetic manipulations in a single epistasis framework, which has not been attempted for Imp-L2 based on the supplied literature. The finding that DR extends lifespan independently of dFOXO (PMID 18221413) makes it non-obvious whether Imp-L2's longevity signal converges on dFOXO, adding mechanistic novelty.

**Feasibility:** All core GAL4/UAS tools are established Drosophila techniques with well-validated tissue-specific drivers. The primary technical risk is generating the UAS-Imp-L2 and UAS-Imp-L2::HA lines if not already available from the Noh et al. 2025 group; this requires 3-4 months for cloning, injection, and stock establishment. A second risk is potential leaky expression of nanos-GAL4 in somatic tissues, which must be verified by GFP reporter assay before committing to lifespan experiments. The triple-genotype epistasis cross (nanos-GAL4 > UAS-Imp-L2; r4-GAL4 > UAS-dfoxo-RNAi) requires careful balancer chromosome management and genotype verification by PCR. Estimated timeline: months 1-4, line acquisition and verification; months 5-7, Aim 1 lifespan and fecundity assays; months 6-9, Aim 2 biochemical secretion assays; months 8-14, Aim 3 epistasis lifespan assays (lifespan experiments require ~90 days per cohort). Total estimated duration: 14-16 months. Key pitfall: if nanos-GAL4 has somatic activity, conclusions about germline-specific Imp-L2 will be confounded; mitigation is to use an alternative germline driver (e.g., MTD-GAL4 or bam-GAL4) in parallel.

### Sonnet 5.5

**Hypothesis:** Proposed: In mated w1118-background female Drosophila at 25°C on standard diet, germline-restricted knockdown of Imp-L2 will reduce median lifespan by at least 10% and 20-day cumulative fecundity by at least 25% relative to driver-only and RNAi-only controls. It will also lower hemolymph Imp-L2 by at least 30% and reduce somatic foxo target-gene expression (Thor/4E-BP) by at least 30%. The lifespan effect will be non-additive with fat-body foxo knockdown (genotype interaction p<0.05), and the Imp-L2 knockdown × dietary amino acid interaction on lifespan will also be significant (p<0.05).

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Germline Imp-L2 and the trade-off — quantify fecundity and lifespan after germline-specific Imp-L2 knockdown on standard and amino-acid-varied diets, alongside sterile-germline controls.
- Aim 2: Signal route — determine whether germline Imp-L2 is detectable in hemolymph and whether knockdown lowers circulating levels, versus acting only within the ovary.
- Aim 3: Somatic foxo dependence — measure fat-body foxo targets and ilp2/ilp5 expression after germline Imp-L2 knockdown and test epistasis with fat-body foxo knockdown.

**Approach:** Use a germline-specific GAL4 driver (e.g., nos-GAL4 or an equivalent ovary driver, to be verified) to express UAS-Imp-L2 RNAi in female germline cells, with driver-only, RNAi-only and wild-type controls in a common w1118 background. Fecundity (daily eggs per female, 20 days) and lifespan (at least 150 mated females per genotype per diet, 3 replicate cohorts, log-rank and Cox models) are measured on standard food and on diets differing in amino acid or yeast content. A genetically sterile control (e.g., ovo^D1 or a bam-mutant germline-less female, to be verified) separates the effect of Imp-L2 loss from the effect of losing fecundity. Hemolymph and ovary Imp-L2 are quantified by an epitope-tagged Imp-L2 or antibody-based method (to be verified), and somatic ilp2, ilp5, Thor and foxo activity are measured by qRT-PCR and a foxo reporter in fat body. Epistasis is tested by combining germline Imp-L2 knockdown with fat-body foxo knockdown, using a two-driver GAL4/LexA or GAL4/RNAi-independent strategy to be designed.

**Expected outcomes:** Proposed predictions: germline Imp-L2 knockdown reduces 20-day cumulative fecundity by at least 25% and median lifespan by at least 10% (log-rank p<0.05 after multiple-testing correction). Hemolymph Imp-L2 falls by at least 30% if the signal is secreted; no hemolymph change with intact local ovarian phenotypes would favor a cell-autonomous or indirect mechanism. The sterile-germline control will show whether lifespan loss tracks reduced egg production or Imp-L2 specifically. Fat-body foxo knockdown combined with germline Imp-L2 knockdown yields no additional lifespan reduction if foxo mediates the effect, or an additive reduction if it does not; either outcome is informative. A significant genotype × diet interaction (p<0.05) would indicate that germline Imp-L2 modulates the diet response.

**Novelty:** Germline Imp-L2 raises both fecundity and lifespan in females, apparently independently of IGF signaling, but its route to the soma is unidentified (40103616). The synthesis identifies four untested gaps: secreted versus cell-autonomous action, comparison against sterile-germline controls, interaction with amino acid manipulation (29129276), and involvement of foxo/ilp expression given conflicting evidence from dietary restriction (18221413) and rpd3 work (27852975). This design tests all four in one framework, using germline Imp-L2 as a candidate signal setting the balance rather than assuming a simple competitive trade-off.

**Feasibility:** Estimated 12-15 months: 2-3 months for line verification and crosses, 3-4 months for fecundity and lifespan cohorts on standard diet, 3-4 months for diet-interaction cohorts, 2-3 months for hemolymph and qRT-PCR assays, and 2-3 months for the epistasis cohorts. Pitfalls include RNAi knockdown efficiency in the germline, possible germline developmental defects confounding adult phenotypes (use of temperature-sensitive or adult-restricted induction may be needed), RNAi off-target effects (use two independent RNAi lines), hemolymph Imp-L2 detection sensitivity, microbiome and larval density effects on lifespan, and the lack of data on males, which are not covered here.

## 21. Which genetic modifiers suppress neurodegeneration across fly models of tau, alpha-synuclein and TDP-43, and which act in all three?

PubMed search: `neurodegeneration genetic modifiers tau TDP-43 synuclein`, genes ['tau'], 15 papers

### Sonnet 4.6

**Hypothesis:** Overexpression of Hsp70 (UAS-Hsp70) pan-neuronally (elav-GAL4) will suppress neurodegeneration by ≥30% (measured as ommatidial disorganization score in the eye) and improve climbing speed by ≥25% relative to disease-model controls in all three Drosophila proteinopathy models — human tau (UAS-tauV337M), human alpha-synuclein (UAS-αSyn-A53T), and human TDP-43 (UAS-TDP-43) — tested in a w1118 background at 25°C, thereby identifying Hsp70 as a pan-proteinopathy suppressor; and a secondary panel of five candidate synaptic/Ca2+-homeostasis genes identified from single-cell transcriptomics (including EB1 and dTau) will suppress neurodegeneration in ≥2 of 3 models by ≥20%, establishing partial cross-model suppressor activity.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Hsp70 as pan-proteinopathy suppressor — Test whether UAS-Hsp70 co-expression with elav-GAL4 and GMR-GAL4 suppresses neurodegeneration (eye disorganization ≥30%, climbing speed improvement ≥25%) in tau-V337M, alpha-synuclein-A53T, and TDP-43 fly models simultaneously
- Aim 2: Synaptic/Ca2+ homeostasis modifiers across models — Screen five candidate synaptic and Ca2+-homeostasis gene RNAi lines (including EB1 and dTau, derived from PMID 36948206) for suppression of neurodegeneration in all three proteinopathy models, identifying modifiers active in ≥2 of 3 models
- Aim 3: Behavioral and lifespan cross-validation — Validate top cross-model suppressors from Aims 1-2 using longitudinal climbing (RING assay, days 1, 10, 20) and lifespan assays (elav-GAL4 background) to determine whether morphological suppression translates to functional neurological rescue across proteinopathy models

**Approach:** Three established Drosophila eye-degeneration and behavioral models will be built using the GAL4/UAS system driven by elav-GAL4 (pan-neuronal) for behavioral assays and GMR-GAL4 (eye-specific) for morphological screens: (1) UAS-tauV337M, (2) UAS-αSyn-A53T, and (3) UAS-TDP-43WT or UAS-TDP-43G298S. Each disease model will be crossed to UAS-Hsp70 (co-expression via UAS-Hsp70; elav-GAL4 or GMR-GAL4) and to UAS-RNAi lines targeting five candidate synaptic/Ca2+-homeostasis genes (EB1, dTau, and three additional top candidates from PMID 36948206 to be selected after full-text review). Primary readouts will be: (a) quantitative scoring of external eye roughness and deep pseudopupil integrity at day 10 post-eclosion (GMR-GAL4 crosses), (b) negative geotaxis climbing assay (RING assay, 10-day and 20-day time points, elav-GAL4 crosses), and (c) lifespan analysis. Controls will include: disease model alone (GAL4 + UAS-disease gene), UAS-Hsp70 or UAS-RNAi alone (no disease transgene), GAL4 alone, and w1118 wild-type. All experiments will use ≥100 flies per genotype per assay replicated across ≥3 independent biological replicates with the experimenter blinded to genotype during scoring.

**Expected outcomes:** Aim 1: Hsp70 overexpression will reduce ommatidial disorganization (GMR-GAL4 crosses, day 10) by ≥30% vs. disease-model controls in the alpha-synuclein model (consistent with PMID 12149445), and by ≥30% in the tau model and TDP-43 model (proposed, unverified). A ≥25% improvement in 10-day climbing speed (RING assay, elav-GAL4 crosses) is expected in all three models. Failure to reach these thresholds in any model will indicate model-specific limits of Hsp70 suppression. Aim 2: At least 2 of 5 candidate synaptic/Ca2+ homeostasis gene RNAi lines will suppress neurodegeneration by ≥20% in ≥2 of 3 disease models; genes that suppress all three at ≥20% will be designated pan-proteinopathy modifiers. Aim 3: Lifespan extension of ≥10% over disease-model controls is expected for Hsp70 co-expression in elav-GAL4-driven tau and TDP-43 models. Statistical analysis will use one-way ANOVA with Tukey post-hoc correction (p < 0.05) for eye scores and climbing assays, and log-rank test for lifespan (p < 0.05, n ≥ 100 per group). All negative results are interpretable as evidence against Hsp70 universality.

**Novelty:** The literature synthesis explicitly identifies that 'no single gene has been demonstrated in the provided literature to suppress neurodegeneration across all three models (tau, alpha-synuclein, AND TDP-43) simultaneously' and that 'Hsp70 suppresses polyglutamine and Parkinson's disease (alpha-synuclein) phenotypes in Drosophila, but its functional effect on tau and TDP-43 toxicity specifically has not been addressed in the provided literature.'
