Additionally, 'synapse- and Ca2+ homeostasis-related genes identified as tau toxicity modifiers have not been tested in alpha-synuclein or TDP-43 fly models,' and 'functional cross-validation of modifier effects on behavioral phenotypes across tau, alpha-synuclein, and TDP-43 models is lacking.' This study directly fills these gaps by performing the first systematic, side-by-side cross-model validation of Hsp70 and synaptic/Ca2+-homeostasis modifiers in all three proteinopathy fly models using both morphological and behavioral endpoints.

**Feasibility:** All three disease models (tau, alpha-synuclein, TDP-43) are well-established in Drosophila and have been used in multiple labs; the GAL4/UAS system is routine. The primary technical risk is sourcing the exact UAS-tauV337M, UAS-αSyn-A53T, and UAS-TDP-43 lines with confirmed expression levels — these must be obtained from originating labs or verified BDSC stocks before crosses begin. A key pitfall is variation in transgene insertion site and expression level across lines, which could confound cross-model comparisons; UAS-GFP controls should be included to verify GAL4 driver activity. Temperature sensitivity of GAL4 expression (25°C standard) must be maintained consistently. Blind scoring of eye roughness requires training and inter-rater reliability checks. Estimated timeline: 3 months for line acquisition and verification; 4 months for cross setup and F1 collection; 4 months for behavioral assays and lifespan; 2 months for data analysis and histology — total approximately 13 months. The secondary candidate panel (synaptic/Ca2+ genes) requires full-text review of PMID 36948206 to finalize gene selection, which is a prerequisite before ordering RNAi lines.

### Sonnet 5.5

**Hypothesis:** Proposed (not established): In a single-driver, single-readout parallel comparison, neuronal overexpression of Hsp70 (e.g., Hsp70Aa/Hsp70Ab) will suppress neurodegeneration in all three models (human tau, human alpha-synuclein, human TDP-43) at 25°C. Specifically, in the GMR-GAL4 eye model, Hsp70 co-expression will reduce the ommatidial degeneration score by ≥30% relative to UAS-LacZ (or UAS-GFP) co-expression controls (n≥15 eyes per genotype, ≥3 independent crosses, Bonferroni-corrected p<0.01), and in the elav-GAL4 adult model it will improve day-30 climbing performance by ≥25%. In contrast, RNAi knockdown of the tau enhancers HNRNPA2B1 and MEPCE orthologs will modify tau toxicity but will change alpha-synuclein and TDP-43 toxicity by <15% (i.e., be tau-selective). This hypothesis will be rejected if Hsp70 suppresses fewer than three models by ≥30% or if the tau-enhancer orthologs shift all three models by ≥15% in the same direction.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Harmonized models — establish matched GMR-GAL4 and elav-GAL4 tau, alpha-synuclein and TDP-43 fly models with quantified, blinded degeneration readouts and equalized transgene expression.
- Aim 2: Hsp70 cross-model test — determine whether Hsp70 overexpression suppresses degeneration by ≥30% in each of the three models independent of changes in transgene protein level.
- Aim 3: Tau-enhancer specificity — test whether knockdown of HNRNPA2B1 and MEPCE orthologs and selected synaptic/Ca2+ homeostasis modifiers is tau-selective or also modifies alpha-synuclein and TDP-43 toxicity by ≥15%.

**Approach:** Use the GAL4/UAS system with a matched design: the same driver (GMR-GAL4 for the eye, elav-GAL4 for pan-neuronal adult phenotypes), the same temperature (25°C), the same genetic background, and the same blinded quantitative readouts (external eye degeneration score or eye-area quantification; day-30 negative geotaxis; TUNEL or vacuole counting in brain sections) for UAS-tau, UAS-alpha-synuclein and UAS-TDP-43. Because tau modifier eye-screen protocols are available (Dourlen et al. 2024), they will be adapted as the readout framework. Candidate modifiers are Hsp70 (a suppressor in polyglutamine and Parkinson's disease fly models; Bonini et al. 2002), the fly orthologs of HNRNPA2B1 and MEPCE (tau enhancers; Leventhal et al. 2025) and synaptic/Ca2+ homeostasis genes (tau modifiers; Praschberger et al. 2023). Candidates will be tested by overexpression or RNAi, with UAS-LacZ/GFP and a UAS-RNAi control (e.g., mCherry RNAi or white RNAi) for dosage-matched GAL4 titration. Transgene expression (western blot or qPCR) will be measured to rule out modifier effects on proteinopathy transgene levels, and a modifier will be classed as 'pan-proteinopathy' only if it passes the threshold in all three models with the same sign.

**Expected outcomes:** Proposed predictions: (1) each proteinopathy transgene will produce a reproducible eye or climbing phenotype in the matched system. (2) Hsp70 co-expression will lower the degeneration score ≥30% in all three eye models and improve day-30 climbing ≥25% (one-way ANOVA with Tukey/Bonferroni, p<0.01) with no reduction in transgene protein level >20%; reduction in transgene level would classify the effect as expression-mediated. (3) HNRNPA2B1- and MEPCE-ortholog knockdown will alter tau toxicity (direction to be determined, because the screen reported them as tau enhancers) but change alpha-synuclein and TDP-43 phenotypes by <15%, indicating tau selectivity. (4) If Hsp70 fails in one model (e.g., TDP-43), that is a meaningful negative result showing partial overlap only. Overall, we expect 0 to 2 modifiers among those tested to meet the all-three criterion.

**Novelty:** The supplied literature contains no TDP-43 fly model or modifier data, and only Praschberger et al. (2023) compares alpha-synuclein and tau in the same system. Hsp70 is reported to suppress polyglutamine and Parkinson's disease phenotypes (Bonini et al. 2002), but its effect on tau, alpha-synuclein-specific or TDP-43 toxicity is not shown in the supplied abstracts, and HNRNPA2B1/MEPCE have not been reported to be tested outside tau (Leventhal et al. 2025). Existing screens use non-comparable readouts (Yang et al. 2023; Dourlen et al. 2024). This design directly fills these gaps with a harmonized three-model, one-driver, one-readout comparison.

**Feasibility:** Estimated 12-15 months: months 1-3 line acquisition, verification and recombination/standardization of transgene strength; months 3-7 eye screen of candidates across the three models; months 7-12 elav-GAL4 climbing and histology of hits, plus expression controls; months 12-15 replication and analysis. Pitfalls: UAS-transgene insertion site and strength differences will confound cross-model comparison (mitigate by using same-landing-site lines or adjusting expression and quantifying protein); GAL4 titration by multiple UAS elements (controlled by dosage-matched UAS controls); eye phenotypes may be too severe or mild for TDP-43 (tune temperature or driver); ortholog assignment for MEPCE and HNRNPA2B1 requires verification; RNAi off-target effects require two independent lines; eye phenotypes may not reflect adult neuronal degeneration (hence the elav validation).

## 22. Can human variants of uncertain significance be classified at scale in humanized flies, and how well do fly results agree with clinical data?

PubMed search: `humanized fly variants of uncertain significance`, genes none, 4 papers

### Sonnet 4.6

**Hypothesis:** A standardized two-pronged GAL4/UAS humanization pipeline applied to ≥50 human VUS across ≥5 genes (MRTFB, ABCA1, ABCA7, PICALM, and one additional AD-risk gene) with established ClinVar pathogenicity adjudications will achieve ≥80% concordance between fly-based functional classifications (pathogenic vs. benign) and clinical ground-truth labels, as measured by sensitivity and specificity of wing morphology rescue scores, viability indices, and neuronal lipid droplet formation assays under 25°C standard conditions in w1118 background flies.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: VUS Panel Curation and Transgene Generation — Curate ≥50 ClinVar-adjudicated coding missense variants (P/LP and B/LB balanced) across MRTFB, ABCA1, ABCA7, PICALM, and VPS35, and generate matched UAS-human-reference and UAS-human-variant transgenic fly lines at identical attP landing sites via PhiC31 integration.
- Aim 2: Blinded Phenotypic Classification and Concordance Benchmarking — Apply the two-pronged rescue/overexpression assay to all variant lines using tissue-appropriate GAL4 drivers, score viability and morphology endpoints blinded to ClinVar status, and compute sensitivity, specificity, PPV, and overall concordance of fly classifications against ClinVar ground truth.
- Aim 3: Multi-Assay Integration with Neuronal Lipid Droplet Readout — Apply the ROS-induced neuronal lipid droplet formation assay (adapted from PMID 34949639) to ABCA1, ABCA7, and PICALM variant lines, quantify lipid droplet number per neuron, and determine whether adding this pathway-relevant endpoint improves concordance beyond morphology/viability alone.

**Approach:** We propose a benchmarking study using the two-pronged UAS/GAL4 rescue-plus-overexpression workflow (Harnish et al. 2019, PMID 31498321) applied to a curated panel of ≥50 coding missense VUS drawn from ClinVar with known Pathogenic (P), Likely Pathogenic (LP), Benign (B), or Likely Benign (LB) interpretations, spanning MRTFB, ABCA1, ABCA7, PICALM, and VPS35. For each gene, the endogenous fly ortholog will be replaced via T2A-GAL4 knock-in or MiMIC insertion with a UAS-human-reference or UAS-human-variant cDNA transgene; viability/rescue of lethal or morphological null phenotype (wing, eye, or neuronal) will be the primary readout. As a secondary assay, a ROS-induced neuronal lipid droplet formation assay (adapted from Moulton et al. 2021, PMID 34949639) will be applied to humanized lines for ABCA1, ABCA7, and PICALM to provide a pathway-relevant, quantitative neuronal endpoint. All variant classification calls from flies will be made blinded to ClinVar status by two independent scorers, and concordance (sensitivity, specificity, positive predictive value) will be computed against ClinVar P/LP vs. B/LB ground truth using a pre-registered analysis plan. Appropriate controls include wildtype w1118, null-ortholog (rescue-negative), and human-reference-transgene lines for each gene.

**Expected outcomes:** Primary outcome: ≥80% overall concordance (sensitivity ≥75%, specificity ≥80%) between fly-based classifications and ClinVar P/LP vs. B/LB ground truth across ≥50 VUS. We predict that rescue-positive (viability restored to ≥70% of wildtype) and morphology-normal variants will classify as B/LB, while rescue-negative or morphology-disrupted variants will classify as P/LP. For the lipid droplet assay, we predict that variants in ABCA1, ABCA7, and PICALM classified as P/LP will reduce glial lipid droplet number by ≥30% relative to reference-transgene controls under ROS induction, consistent with the loss-of-function effects documented in PMID 34949639. Inter-rater reliability between blinded scorers should reach κ ≥ 0.75. Discordant cases (fly P/LP but ClinVar B/LB, or vice versa) will be catalogued as a secondary outcome to identify gene-specific or assay-specific failure modes. Statistical thresholds: Fisher's exact test for concordance, p < 0.05; 95% confidence intervals reported for sensitivity and specificity.

**Novelty:** The literature synthesis explicitly identifies the absence of quantitative concordance data between fly VUS classifications and clinical/ClinVar adjudications as the primary unaddressed gap: 'No quantitative concordance data between fly VUS classifications and clinical/ClinVar adjudications are reported in the provided literature — the scale and accuracy of agreement between fly functional results and established clinical classifications remains unmeasured.' No paper in the synthesis reports sensitivity, specificity, or PPV of fly-based calls against a clinical ground truth at scale. Additionally, the synthesis notes that throughput and scalability benchmarks are entirely absent from the provided papers. This study is the first proposed systematic, blinded benchmarking of the two-pronged humanization pipeline across multiple genes and ≥50 variants with pre-registered concordance metrics, directly addressing both gaps. Furthermore, the integration of a pathway-relevant neuronal lipid droplet assay for AD-risk gene VUS (extending PMID 34949639) alongside rescue/morphology endpoints provides a multi-assay classification framework not present in any cited work.

**Feasibility:** The two-pronged GAL4/UAS humanization workflow is technically established (PMID 31498321) and the Drosophila genetic toolkit for gene replacement is mature (PMID 29951825). Key technical challenges include: (1) generating ≥50 site-directed variant transgenes via PhiC31 integration into matched attP sites — estimated 6–8 months for cloning, injection, and line establishment; (2) identifying ≥50 ClinVar-adjudicated P/LP and B/LB coding missense VUS with sufficient balance across the 5 target genes — feasible given ClinVar database scale but requires careful curation to avoid VUS-labeled entries; (3) ensuring sufficient fly-human orthology for humanization to be informative — MRTFB, ABCA1, PICALM, and VPS35 have well-documented fly orthologs, but conservation scores should be confirmed via MARRVEL before committing to a gene. Potential pitfalls: low penetrance of rescue phenotypes for some variants may require scoring ≥100 adult flies per genotype; the lipid droplet assay requires confocal imaging and is lower-throughput than wing scoring. Estimated overall timeline: 18–24 months (6–8 months transgene generation, 6 months phenotypic scoring, 4 months data analysis and concordance calculation). Cost is dominated by injection services (~$300–500 per line) and confocal imaging time.

### Sonnet 5.5

**Hypothesis:** Proposed: In a blinded, pre-specified benchmark of 72 ClinVar-classified missense variants (36 pathogenic/likely pathogenic and 36 benign/likely benign) across 6 genes with fly orthologs, a two-assay humanized Drosophila pipeline will classify variants as functionally abnormal or normal with sensitivity ≥80% and specificity ≥90% against clinical classification (Cohen's kappa ≥0.6). The pipeline combines T2A-GAL4 rescue of ortholog loss with GAL4/UAS overexpression of human cDNA, and each variant is compared with the reference allele at a single attP site. Rescue-only scoring will show ≥25 percentage points lower sensitivity than the combined pipeline for gain-of-function variants (as for MRTFB), which will make the combined readout necessary for scalable VUS classification.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Build and validate the humanized pipeline — generate T2A-GAL4 and UAS-human reference lines for 6 genes, confirm that reference cDNA rescues the null phenotype, and fix assay thresholds before unblinding.
- Aim 2: Measure concordance with clinical classification — blind-test 72 ClinVar P/LP and B/LB variants and estimate sensitivity, specificity, and kappa, stratified by gene, mechanism, and assay mode (rescue-only vs overexpression vs combined).
- Aim 3: Prospectively classify VUS — test ~30 VUS in the validated genes, issue fly-based calls before clinical review, and compare them with subsequent clinical reclassification or segregation evidence.

**Approach:** For each of 6 genes (to be selected for a fly ortholog with a scorable null phenotype, e.g. lethality or a viable morphological defect, and ≥10 ClinVar P/LP plus ≥10 B/LB missense variants; selection requires verification), a T2A-GAL4 gene-replacement or knock-in allele will be generated or sourced, and UAS-human cDNA reference and variant constructs will be integrated at the same attP landing site to equalize expression. Assay 1 is rescue of the T2A-GAL4 loss-of-function phenotype by reference versus variant cDNA, scored as fraction of expected eclosion, lifespan, or climbing as appropriate. Assay 2 is overexpression with a ubiquitous driver and a tissue-restricted driver (e.g. a wing-pouch or neuronal driver), scored for wing or eye morphology, viability, or locomotion, including MRTFB-like gain-of-function readouts. Variants will be coded and scored blind, with transgene expression verified by western blot or qPCR, and with reference cDNA, empty-vector, and known-null variant controls in every batch. Thresholds for 'abnormal' will be fixed before unblinding as >2 SD from the reference-allele distribution with Benjamini-Hochberg-corrected p<0.05. Concordance will be reported as sensitivity, specificity, and kappa with 95% Wilson confidence intervals, stratified by gene, mechanism (LoF vs GoF), and assay.

**Expected outcomes:** Proposed predictions: (1) Combined-pipeline sensitivity ≥80% and specificity ≥90% against ClinVar P/LP versus B/LB, with the lower 95% CI bound for specificity >75%, and kappa ≥0.6. (2) Rescue-only sensitivity will be ≥25 percentage points lower than the combined pipeline for variants acting through gain-of-function, while the two modes will perform similarly for loss-of-function variants. (3) Between-batch reproducibility of reference-allele phenotypes will give CV <20%, and inter-line (independent insertion) agreement for variant calls will be ≥90%. (4) Of ~30 prospectively tested VUS, 30-50% are expected to score abnormal, and fly calls will be compared with later clinical reclassification or segregation evidence. Falsification: sensitivity <60% or specificity <80%, or no sensitivity difference between rescue-only and combined scoring, would reject the hypothesis.

**Novelty:** The literature synthesis shows that no supplied paper quantifies concordance between fly assay outcomes and clinical variant classification across a variant set. The MRTFB study covers only two variants [37013900], the UDN protocol allows any scorable phenotype without reported false-positive or false-negative rates [31498321], and humanization resources are described without throughput or cost estimates [29951825]. This proposal supplies a blinded benchmark with defined error rates, a direct comparison of rescue-only versus rescue-plus-overexpression readouts, and a prospective VUS test.

**Feasibility:** Estimated timeline is 24 months: months 1-4 gene selection, T2A-GAL4 allele acquisition, and cloning; months 4-12 transgenesis of ~72 benchmark lines (~12 per gene); months 10-18 blinded phenotyping; months 16-24 VUS testing and analysis. Pitfalls include fly-human ortholog divergence (some genes will not be rescuable by human cDNA, so a reference-rescue pass criterion is required before a gene enters the benchmark), overexpression artifacts from non-physiological dosage, a ClinVar benchmark biased toward well-studied variants, hypomorphic variants close to the assay detection limit, and the cost and throughput of generating many transgenic lines, which have not been quantified in the supplied literature. Noncoding variants and variant-variant interactions are out of scope. The clinical concordance claim applies only to missense variants in genes meeting the rescue criterion.

## 23. Why are some neuron types selectively vulnerable in fly Parkinson's models while their neighbors survive?

PubMed search: `dopaminergic neuron selective vulnerability Parkinson`, genes none, 12 papers

### Sonnet 4.6

**Hypothesis:** Vulnerable dopaminergic neuron clusters (PPL1) in parkin-null Drosophila melanogaster express significantly higher levels of the voltage-gated calcium channel cacophony (cac) and lower levels of the ER-stress regulator TMBIM6 ortholog compared to resistant DA neuron clusters (PPM1/2), and that cluster-specific RNAi knockdown of cac in PPL1 neurons will reduce mitochondrial H2O2 accumulation by ≥40% and rescue ≥30% of PPL1 neuron number in 30-day-old parkin-null flies at 25°C relative to parkin-null controls, while concomitant DA-neuron-specific overexpression of the TMBIM6 ortholog will independently rescue ≥25% of PPL1 neuron number under the same conditions.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Cluster-resolved transcriptomics — Use single-nucleus RNA sequencing of dissected adult brains from parkin-null and w1118 control flies at day 10 and day 30 to identify genes (including cac and the TMBIM6 ortholog CG9775) differentially expressed between PPL1 (vulnerable) and PPM1/2 (resistant) DA neuron clusters
- Aim 2: Causal role of cacophony in cluster-specific oxidative stress — Test whether TH-GAL4-driven RNAi knockdown of cac in parkin-null flies reduces mitochondrial H2O2 accumulation (measured by mito-roGFP2-Orp1 ratiometric imaging) in PPL1 neurons by ≥40% and rescues ≥30% of PPL1 neuron number at day 30 relative to parkin-null controls
- Aim 3: TMBIM6 ortholog overexpression as ER-stress neuroprotection — Determine whether DA-neuron-specific overexpression of the proposed TMBIM6 fly ortholog (CG9775) in parkin-null flies rescues ≥25% of PPL1 DA neuron number and improves negative geotaxis climbing index by ≥20% at day 30, and whether RNAi knockdown of CG9775 exacerbates PPL1 loss in otherwise wild-type flies

**Approach:** We will use the GAL4/UAS system to achieve cluster-restricted transgene expression in Drosophila DA neurons. First, single-nucleus or single-cell RNA sequencing of dissected adult fly brains from parkin-null (park25/park25) and w1118 control flies at day 10 and day 30 will be performed to quantify differential expression of cac, the TMBIM6 fly ortholog (CG9775, requiring verification), antioxidant genes (sod1, cat), and ER-stress effectors (Ire1, Xbp1) across all annotated DA neuron clusters (PPL1, PPL2, PPM1/2, PPM3, PAL). Second, TH-GAL4-driven RNAi of cac (UAS-cac-RNAi) specifically in DA neurons of parkin-null flies will be combined with the mitochondrial H2O2 sensor UAS-mito-roGFP2-Orp1 to measure real-time redox status in PPL1 versus PPM1/2 clusters by confocal imaging at day 10 and day 30. Third, DA-neuron-specific overexpression of UAS-CG9775 (TMBIM6 ortholog) in parkin-null flies will be assessed for PPL1 neuron survival by TH immunostaining. All experiments include w1118, park25/park25; TH-GAL4/+, and UAS transgene/+ heterozygous controls; locomotor performance (negative geotaxis) will serve as a functional readout alongside neuronal counts.

**Expected outcomes:** Aim 1 (snRNA-seq): PPL1 DA neurons from parkin-null flies at day 30 are predicted to show ≥2-fold higher cac mRNA and ≤0.5-fold TMBIM6 ortholog mRNA relative to PPM1/2 neurons in the same brain (adjusted p < 0.05, DESeq2). Aim 2 (cac RNAi + redox imaging): TH-GAL4>UAS-cac-RNAi in park25 flies is predicted to reduce mito-roGFP2-Orp1 oxidation ratio in PPL1 neurons by ≥40% and rescue PPL1 neuron count from ~50% loss (expected in parkin-null controls at day 30 based on PMID 36290790) to ≤25% loss (≥30% rescue, n ≥ 20 brains/genotype, ANOVA with Tukey post-hoc, p < 0.05). Aim 3 (TMBIM6 OE): TH-GAL4>UAS-CG9775 in park25 flies is predicted to rescue PPL1 neuron number by ≥25% relative to park25; TH-GAL4/+ controls at day 30 (p < 0.05). Locomotor climbing index is predicted to improve by ≥20% in rescued genotypes relative to parkin-null controls at day 30.

**Novelty:** This experiment directly addresses three unresolved gaps identified in the literature synthesis: (1) The molecular basis for differential redox buffering between adjacent vulnerable (PPL1) and resistant (PPM1/2) DA neuron clusters in parkin-null flies is unknown (Gap 1 from synthesis, PMID 36290790); no study has directly compared cac expression levels across DA neuron subtypes within the same fly brain, nor tested whether cluster-specific cac knockdown is sufficient to rescue PPL1 neurons in a genetic PD model (Gap 2, PMID 25445363). (2) The TMBIM6/IRE1α axis has been implicated in DA neuron survival but it is unknown whether ER stress is differentially activated between vulnerable and resistant DA neuron subtypes in the same fly brain (Gap 4, PMID 41932887). (3) No prior study has combined cluster-resolved transcriptomics (snRNA-seq) with real-time mitochondrial redox imaging and subtype-specific genetic intervention in the same parkin-null model to causally link calcium channel expression levels to cluster-specific oxidative stress and degeneration.

**Feasibility:** TH-GAL4-driven tools for DA-neuron-specific transgene expression in Drosophila are well established; parkin-null flies with documented PPL1-selective loss are available. The mito-roGFP2-Orp1 sensor is validated for in vivo Drosophila redox imaging. Potential pitfalls include: (1) TH-GAL4 may not provide sufficient cluster-specificity to distinguish PPL1 from PPM1/2 — a split-GAL4 or intersectional approach (e.g., PPL1-specific GAL4 driver, availability requiring verification at BDSC) may be needed; (2) CG9775 as TMBIM6 ortholog requires bioinformatic confirmation (BLASTP/OrthoFinder analysis) before UAS line construction; (3) snRNA-seq of Drosophila adult brain requires optimization for cell dissociation and sufficient DA neuron capture given their small number (~120 total per brain); (4) parkin-null flies have reduced viability requiring larger starting populations. Estimated timeline: months 1–4 snRNA-seq profiling and data analysis; months 3–8 cac RNAi redox imaging experiments; months 6–14 TMBIM6 ortholog UAS line validation and overexpression rescue experiments; months 12–18 integration and replication. Total estimated timeline: 18 months.

### Sonnet 5.5

**Hypothesis:** Proposed (not established): in park null (park25/park25) flies at 25°C, the vulnerable dopaminergic cluster has higher cac-dependent cytosolic Ca2+ load than a non-degenerating dopaminergic cluster, and this Ca2+ load acts upstream of sustained mitochondrial H2O2 elevation and neuron loss. Specifically, (a) baseline and depolarization-evoked GCaMP signal will be ≥30% higher in vulnerable than in resistant neurons of park nulls; (b) cac RNAi restricted to dopaminergic neurons will reduce mitochondrial H2O2 (roGFP2-Orp1 oxidation) in the vulnerable cluster by ≥30% and rescue ≥50% of neuron loss at day 30; and (c) mitochondria-targeted H2O2 scavenging will rescue neuron loss without lowering cac-dependent Ca2+ signals.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Cluster-resolved Ca2+ and redox profiling — measure GCaMP and mito-roGFP2 signals in vulnerable vs resistant dopaminergic clusters of park nulls and controls at days 5, 15 and 30.
- Aim 2: Necessity of cac — test whether dopaminergic-neuron-specific cac knockdown lowers mitochondrial H2O2 and rescues neuron loss and climbing in park nulls.
- Aim 3: Causal order of Ca2+ and H2O2 — test whether mitochondrial H2O2 scavenging rescues degeneration without altering cac-dependent Ca2+ signals, and whether combined manipulation is additive.

**Approach:** Use park25 homozygous and heterozygous control flies (allele identity to be verified) with GAL4/UAS tools. Cluster-resolved drivers (TH-GAL4 as a pan-dopaminergic driver, plus split-GAL4 lines for the vulnerable and resistant clusters, which require verification) will express cytosolic GCaMP, mito-roGFP2-Orp1 or mito-roGFP2-Grx1 sensors, and cac RNAi or a mitochondrial H2O2 scavenger. Ex vivo whole-brain imaging will measure Ca2+ and redox signals separately in the vulnerable and resistant clusters at days 5, 15 and 30. Neuron survival will be scored by anti-TH immunostaining with blinded cluster counts, and climbing (negative geotaxis) will be measured as a behavioral readout. Controls are park/+ siblings, driver-only and UAS-only genotypes, a second non-overlapping cac RNAi, and a non-targeting RNAi. Epistasis will be tested by comparing cac knockdown, mitochondrial scavenger expression, and both together in park nulls, in both sexes, with sex-stratified analysis.

**Expected outcomes:** Proposed predictions: park nulls will show ≥30% higher Ca2+ signal in the vulnerable cluster than in the resistant cluster (p<0.05, mixed-effects model, n≥10 brains per genotype and age). cac RNAi will reduce vulnerable-cluster mito-roGFP2-Orp1 oxidation by ≥30% and increase surviving neuron counts by ≥50% of the park-null deficit at day 30. The mitochondrial scavenger will improve survival without changing GCaMP signals, which would place H2O2 downstream of Ca2+. If cac knockdown fails to change H2O2, or if the vulnerable cluster does not show higher Ca2+ load, the hypothesis is falsified. A scavenger that rescues survival while cac knockdown does not would indicate a Ca2+-independent source of H2O2.

**Novelty:** Parkin-null flies show sustained mitochondrial H2O2 elevation in vulnerable but not neighboring non-degenerating dopaminergic neurons [36290790], and cacophony-mutant dopaminergic neurons resist MPP+ toxicity in primary cultures [25445363]. The literature synthesis notes that it is not known whether cac-dependent Ca2+ load differs between vulnerable and non-degenerating clusters in vivo, and that causality of H2O2 elevation remains unresolved. This plan tests the two mechanisms together in intact park mutants with cluster-resolved readouts.

**Feasibility:** Estimated 14-18 months: 3 months for line acquisition, verification and cluster driver validation; 5 months for imaging (Aim 1); 6 months for knockdown and rescue with aging to day 30 (Aims 2-3); 2-3 months for analysis. Pitfalls: ex vivo Ca2+ imaging may not reflect in vivo activity; roGFP sensors need calibration with DTT and diamide; RNAi efficiency and off-target effects need two independent lines; cac is essential, so strong knockdown could cause developmental or behavioral confounds (consider temporal control with tub-GAL80ts); park nulls may have modest neuron loss and age-dependent variability, requiring adequate power. Cluster-specific drivers may not exist for every cluster of interest.

## 24. How does neuronal activity level interact with mitochondrial quality control (PINK1/parkin) to determine neuron survival?

PubMed search: `PINK1 parkin neuronal activity mitophagy`, genes ['Pink1', 'park'], 15 papers

### Sonnet 4.6

**Hypothesis:** Increasing neuronal electrical activity in identified Drosophila dopaminergic (DA) neurons by ≥2-fold above baseline (achieved via dTrpA1 thermogenetic activation at 29°C) will upregulate PINK1/Parkin-dependent mitophagy flux by ≥1.5-fold (measured by a mito-Keima reporter signal ratio) and reduce DA neuron number by ≥25% within 20 days in Pink1 null (Pink1B9) flies, whereas wild-type flies subjected to identical activity elevation will show no significant DA neuron loss (≤5%), demonstrating that physiological increases in neuronal firing rate quantitatively engage PINK1/Parkin-dependent mitophagy and that this pathway is required to buffer activity-driven mitochondrial stress in vivo.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Quantify mitophagy flux as a function of neuronal activity level — determine whether thermogenetic activation of DA neurons via TH-GAL4>dTrpA1 at 29°C increases mito-Keima reporter ratio by ≥1.5-fold in a dose- and time-dependent manner in wild-type (w1118) flies, establishing a causal link between physiological firing rate elevation and mitophagy induction in identified neurons in vivo.
- Aim 2: Test PINK1 requirement for activity-induced mitophagy and neuroprotection — compare DA neuron survival (PPL1 anti-TH counts), phospho-ubiquitin accumulation, and climbing performance between TH-GAL4>dTrpA1 Pink1B9 null and isogenic wild-type flies at days 5, 10, and 20 post-eclosion under matched thermogenetic activation, to determine whether PINK1 is necessary to buffer activity-driven mitochondrial stress and prevent DA neuron loss.
- Aim 3: Genetic epistasis through CISD to confirm mechanistic axis — test whether neuron-specific knockdown of Cisd (TH-GAL4>Cisd-RNAi) suppresses activity-induced DA neuron degeneration and restores mitophagy flux in Pink1B9 mutants subjected to dTrpA1 activation, validating the CISD/IP3R/ER-calcium pathway as a downstream mediator connecting elevated neuronal activity to PINK1/Parkin-dependent mitochondrial quality control.

**Approach:** The experiment uses the GAL4/UAS system to co-express the thermogenetic activator UAS-dTrpA1 (for graded neuronal depolarization at 29°C vs. permissive 22°C control), UAS-mito-Keima (ratiometric mitophagy reporter), and UAS-mCD8::GFP (DA neuron morphology marker) under TH-GAL4 (dopaminergic neuron-specific driver) in Pink1B9 null mutant and isogenic wild-type (w1118) backgrounds. Three activity tiers will be established: baseline (22°C, TH-GAL4 driving reporters only), moderate activity (29°C for 4h/day), and sustained high activity (29°C continuous). Mitophagy flux will be quantified by confocal imaging of mito-Keima 561/440 nm excitation ratios in DA neuron soma and axonal fields of the protocerebral posterior lateral (PPL1) cluster at days 5, 10, and 20 post-eclosion. DA neuron survival will be scored by anti-TH immunohistochemistry and GFP-positive cell counts. Phospho-ubiquitin (Ser65) immunostaining will serve as an orthogonal readout of endogenous PINK1 activity. Genetic epistasis controls include Pink1B9; UAS-dTrpA1/TH-GAL4 (no reporter), UAS-dTrpA1/TH-GAL4 in wild-type, and static temperature controls at 22°C for all genotypes. Locomotor performance (negative geotaxis climbing assay, 10 trials/cohort, n≥50 flies/group) will serve as a functional correlate of DA neuron integrity.

**Expected outcomes:** In wild-type flies, TH-GAL4>dTrpA1 activation at 29°C will increase mito-Keima 561/440 ratio by ≥1.5-fold relative to 22°C controls at day 10, indicating upregulated mitophagy flux; DA neuron number in PPL1 cluster (expected ~12 neurons/hemisphere at baseline) will decrease by ≤5% (not significant, p>0.05 by one-way ANOVA with Tukey correction). In Pink1B9 mutants under identical thermogenetic activation, mito-Keima ratio will be lower than wild-type activated controls (failed mitophagy flux), phospho-ubiquitin puncta will accumulate (≥2-fold increase over wild-type), and DA neuron loss will reach ≥25% by day 20 (p<0.01). Locomotor performance (climbing index) will decline by ≥30% in activated Pink1B9 flies versus activated wild-type by day 15 (p<0.01). Pink1B9 flies at 22°C (unstimulated) will show intermediate phenotypes consistent with published baseline degeneration. Genetic suppression by co-expression of Cisd-RNAi in TH-GAL4 neurons of activated Pink1B9 flies is predicted to rescue DA neuron loss to ≤10% and restore climbing index by ≥50%, providing mechanistic validation of the CISD-IP3R axis. All group sizes: n≥20 brains/timepoint for histology, n≥50 flies/cohort for climbing assay; statistical threshold α=0.05 with Bonferroni correction for multiple comparisons.

**Novelty:** This design directly addresses the primary gap identified in the literature synthesis: the causal direction between neuronal firing rate and PINK1/Parkin mitophagy activation has not been experimentally established. While it is known that pathological calcium overload (constitutively active TRP channels) engages PINK1/Parkin mitophagy, and that phospho-ubiquitin is disproportionately high in DA neurons, whether graded physiological increases in electrical activity per se — independent of pathological calcium overload — quantitatively upregulate mitophagy flux in a PINK1-dependent manner in identified neurons in vivo has not been tested. The experimental design also begins to address the second gap: why DA neurons are selectively vulnerable, by providing a controlled activity-titration paradigm that can dissociate activity-dependent mitophagy demand from constitutive PINK1 expression differences. The use of mito-Keima as a continuous flux reporter in TH-positive neurons in a thermogenetically controlled firing paradigm is a novel methodological combination not present in cited literature.

**Feasibility:** All genetic tools (TH-GAL4, UAS-dTrpA1, Pink1B9, UAS-mCD8::GFP) are established Drosophila reagents; the primary technical challenge is the availability of a TH-GAL4-compatible UAS-mito-Keima line, which may require generation or transfer from a collaborating laboratory (estimated 2–3 months for line establishment and validation). Temperature-shift protocols using dTrpA1 are routine. Key pitfalls: (1) dTrpA1 expression in DA neurons may cause developmental lethality or early adult death at sustained 29°C — a daily 4h activation regimen should mitigate this and will be tested in pilot experiments during months 1–2; (2) mito-Keima imaging requires live tissue or rapid fixation with specific excitation settings — confocal protocols must be optimized and standardized across time points; (3) Pink1B9 flies have reduced viability and fecundity, requiring larger crossing schemes; (4) PPL1 cluster neuron counting requires blinded scoring with anti-TH antibody and confocal z-stacks — inter-rater reliability must be established. Estimated timeline: months 1–3 line construction and validation; months 4–6 pilot dose-response and temperature titration; months 7–14 main experimental cohorts with three activity tiers, three genotypes, three time points; months 15–18 epistasis experiments with Cisd-RNAi and data analysis. Total estimated duration: 18 months.

### Sonnet 5.5

**Hypothesis:** Proposed (not established): Neuronal activity level sets how strongly dopaminergic (DA) neuron survival depends on PINK1/Parkin. In Pink1B9 null males, chronic thermogenetic activation of TH-GAL4-positive neurons (UAS-TrpA1, 29°C for 12 h/day from day 3) will cause at least 30% greater loss of PPL1-cluster DA neurons at day 25 than in Pink1B9 males with the same genotype kept at 22°C. Chronic Kir2.1 silencing (with tub-GAL80ts restricting expression to adults) will reduce PPL1 neuron loss in Pink1B9 males by at least 30% relative to Pink1B9 controls without Kir2.1. DA-neuron-specific knockdown of Cisd or of the IP3 receptor (Itpr) will suppress the activity-dependent excess loss by at least 50%.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Activity-dependent survival — test whether TrpA1-driven activation increases, and Kir2.1 silencing decreases, PPL1 DA neuron loss and motor decline in Pink1B9 (and park) mutants relative to matched controls.
- Aim 2: Mitophagy and phospho-Ub readout — quantify mito-QC/mt-Keima and phospho-Ub signal in DA neurons under activation and silencing, to test whether activity drives a PINK1/Parkin-dependent mitophagy response.
- Aim 3: Calcium and Cisd/IP3R mechanism — use GCaMP imaging and DA-specific Cisd or Itpr RNAi to test whether activity-evoked ER calcium release and Cisd accumulation mediate the activity-dependent loss in Pink1B9 neurons.

**Approach:** Use a 2 x 3 design crossing genotype (Pink1B9 vs. Pink1B9/+ heterozygous controls on an isogenic background) with activity state (TrpA1 activation, Kir2.1 silencing, GAL4-only or UAS-only baseline). TH-GAL4 drives expression in DA neurons. Adult-restricted expression uses tub-GAL80ts, with 29°C-matched and 22°C-matched controls for every genotype to separate temperature effects from activation effects. Primary endpoints are the number of TH-immunoreactive PPL1 neurons at days 10, 25 and 40, and climbing performance. Mitophagy is quantified with a mito-QC or mt-Keima reporter in DA neurons, together with phospho-Ub immunostaining. Activity-evoked calcium is measured by GCaMP imaging in dissected brains or explants. Rescue arms add UAS-Cisd RNAi or UAS-Itpr RNAi in the Pink1B9 TrpA1 background. Parkin (park) mutants are used to test whether the findings generalize beyond Pink1. Analysis is blind to genotype, with at least 10 brains per group and 3 independent crosses. Counts are compared by two-way ANOVA with Tukey post-hoc tests, using alpha = 0.05.

**Expected outcomes:** Proposed predictions: (1) At 22°C, Pink1B9 males lose a baseline fraction of PPL1 neurons. TrpA1 activation at 29°C increases this loss by at least 30% over temperature-matched Pink1B9 controls without activation (p < 0.05, two-way ANOVA genotype x activity interaction). (2) Kir2.1 silencing reduces loss by at least 30%. (3) Mito-QC puncta and phospho-Ub signal are elevated by activation in controls, and the Pink1B9 mutant fails to show this adaptive mitophagy response. (4) Activity-evoked calcium transients are larger in Pink1B9 neurons, and Itpr or Cisd knockdown reduces them and suppresses at least 50% of the activity-dependent excess neuron loss. Falsification: if the genotype x activity interaction is not significant (p ≥ 0.05) or the effect is under 15%, the hypothesis of activity-dependent PINK1/Parkin requirement is rejected for this paradigm.

**Novelty:** Huang et al. (PMID 27054334) showed that PINK1 and Parkin protect against constitutively active TRP channel-induced retinal degeneration, but that model involves constitutive calcium influx rather than graded physiological activity. Ham et al. (PMID 37626046) showed that loss of PINK1/Parkin increases IP3R-mediated ER calcium release through CISD1/Cisd. Martinez et al. (PMID 38273330) reported that Cisd accumulates in Pink1/parkin mutants and blocks mitophagy. No supplied study manipulates neuronal firing or excitability in Pink1/parkin mutant Drosophila neurons and measures mitophagy and survival, so the activity-dependence of the PINK1/parkin requirement is untested. Imai et al. (PMID 32035987) noted stronger phospho-ubiquitin signals in DA neurons than other neurons, and whether this reflects higher intrinsic activity is also untested.

**Feasibility:** Estimated timeline is 12-16 months: 3 months for line construction and validation, 6 months for aging and survival cohorts (to day 40), and 3-4 months for imaging and analysis. Pitfalls: (a) 29°C alone may stress Pink1 mutants, so every genotype needs temperature-matched controls and a TrpA1-independent heat control. (b) Pink1B9 flies have baseline phenotypes (flight muscle defects, reduced viability), so the isogenic background must be controlled. (c) TH-GAL4 also marks some non-DA cells, so confirm with anti-TH counts. (d) Adult DA neuron loss in Pink1 mutants is modest, so a power analysis from pilot data is needed and the effect sizes in the predictions are assumptions to be validated. (e) RNAi efficiency for Cisd and Itpr must be verified, and Itpr knockdown may itself be toxic. (f) TrpA1 raises calcium and firing but is not physiological firing, so the findings should be confirmed with a second manipulation such as optogenetics or sensory-driven activity.

## 25. Which tumor-secreted factors are necessary for wasting of distant tissues in fly cachexia models?

PubMed search: `tumor cachexia wasting secreted factors`, genes none, 10 papers

### Sonnet 4.6

**Hypothesis:** In the RasV12/scrib Drosophila tumor cachexia model, simultaneous loss of tumor-secreted Upd3 and eiger will reduce fat body triglyceride loss by ≥50% and muscle detachment frequency by ≥40% compared to tumors retaining both factors, whereas single knockdown of either Upd3 or eiger alone will each rescue these phenotypes by <30%, demonstrating that these two secreted factors act in parallel, non-redundant pathways to drive systemic wasting in mated female w1118 background flies at 25°C.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Necessity mapping — Determine whether tumor-secreted Upd3 and eiger are individually necessary for fat body wasting and muscle detachment in the RasV12/scrib cachexia model by quantifying TAG content, Pepck1/Pdk mRNA, systemic glucose, and muscle detachment frequency in single-knockdown animals versus full-tumor controls.
- Aim 2: Combinatorial epistasis — Determine whether simultaneous tumor-specific knockdown of Upd3 and eiger produces additive, synergistic, or sub-additive rescue of wasting phenotypes to establish whether these factors act in parallel, sequential, or redundant pathways driving systemic wasting.
- Aim 3: Tissue-autonomy validation — Confirm that fat body JAK/STAT signaling (via Stat92E RNAi under r4-GAL4) is the obligate downstream effector of tumor Upd3 in the gluconeogenic wasting axis, and that this is separable from eiger-mediated systemic insulin restriction as measured by fat body InR target gene expression (4EBP, Thor) and S6K phosphorylation.

**Approach:** Eye-antennal disc tumors will be induced using the GMR-GAL4 driver to express UAS-RasV12 in a scrib RNAi background (GMR>RasV12, scrib-IR), which is an established fly cachexia model. Within this tumor genotype, Upd3 and eiger will be knocked down individually and in combination using UAS-RNAi transgenes co-expressed under GMR-GAL4, allowing tumor-specific depletion of each secreted factor while leaving host tissues intact. Wasting endpoints will be quantified at day 10 post-eclosion: fat body lipid content by Nile Red staining and TAG colorimetric assay, muscle detachment scored by phalloidin staining of indirect flight muscles and thoracic wall muscles, systemic glucose levels by colorimetric assay, and fat body Pepck1/Pdk mRNA levels by RT-qPCR as molecular readouts of the gluconeogenic axis. Survival assays (n ≥ 150 females per genotype, three biological replicates) will assess whether rescue of wasting markers translates to lifespan extension. Controls will include: (1) GMR-GAL4 driver alone (no tumor), (2) GMR>RasV12/scrib-IR with UAS-luciferase RNAi as a specificity control, and (3) fat body-specific (r4-GAL4) rescue of JAK/STAT signaling to confirm tissue-autonomy of Upd3 signaling downstream of the tumor.

**Expected outcomes:** If the hypothesis is correct: (1) Single Upd3 knockdown in tumors will reduce fat body Pepck1 and Pdk mRNA by ≥60% and restore systemic glucose toward control levels (within 20% of tumor-free flies) but rescue muscle detachment by <30%; (2) Single eiger knockdown will reduce muscle detachment frequency by <30% and partially restore fat body TAG content (<30% rescue) by relieving systemic insulin restriction, with minimal effect on gluconeogenic gene expression; (3) Double Upd3/eiger knockdown will reduce fat body TAG loss by ≥50% and muscle detachment by ≥40% relative to RasV12/scrib tumors expressing control RNAi, demonstrating synergy consistent with parallel pathways; (4) Survival will be extended by ≥15% in double knockdown animals relative to full-tumor controls. Statistical analysis: one-way ANOVA with Tukey post-hoc correction, α = 0.05, n ≥ 30 per genotype per biochemical assay, n ≥ 150 per genotype for survival. If double knockdown does not exceed additive rescue of single knockdowns, this would indicate sequential rather than parallel action.

**Novelty:** The literature synthesis identifies as an explicit gap that 'the relationship between tumor-derived eiger (TNF-α)-mediated systemic insulin restriction and Upd3-mediated gluconeogenic reprogramming in the same cachexia context is unexplored; it is unknown whether these are parallel, sequential, or redundant mechanisms of systemic wasting.' No published study has directly tested whether Upd3 and eiger function in the same tumor model with combinatorial genetic loss-of-function to determine epistasis or pathway independence. Additionally, the synthesis notes that 'a systematic comparison of which specific secreted molecules are necessary versus sufficient across multiple tumor genotypes has not been completed,' and this design directly addresses necessity by genetic ablation of each factor in the RasV12/scrib background. This distinguishes the proposed work from Lodge et al. (2021), which focused on Mmp1 in an epithelial overgrowth model, and from Liu et al. (2025), which focused on Upd3/gluconeogenesis without examining eiger interaction.

**Feasibility:** The GMR>RasV12/scrib cachexia model is well-established and produces robust wasting phenotypes by day 10 post-eclosion, providing a reliable experimental window. The primary technical challenge is generating and validating the double UAS-Upd3-IR; UAS-eiger-IR recombinant chromosome, which requires 3–4 months of stock construction and verification by RT-qPCR of knockdown efficiency in dissected eye discs. A potential pitfall is GAL4 dilution when driving multiple UAS transgenes simultaneously, which will be controlled by including UAS-luciferase-IR as a filler transgene in single-knockdown arms. Fat body dissection quality is variable and requires consistent technique; investigators should be blinded to genotype during dissection and quantification. Mated females must be collected within 24 hours of eclosion and maintained at controlled density (20 per vial). Estimated timeline: months 1–4, stock construction and validation; months 5–8, wasting endpoint assays; months 9–12, survival assays and downstream mechanistic experiments. The project is technically feasible within 12 months with one trained fly researcher.

### Sonnet 5.5

**Hypothesis:** Proposed: In mated female flies at 25°C, tumor-specific knockdown of Mmp1 will restore at least 50% of the tumor-induced loss of fat-body triglyceride and of muscle integrity in an eye-antennal disc RasV12 scrib tumor model but less than 20% in a gut ykiAct tumor model, whereas tumor-specific upd3 knockdown will show the reverse pattern, and combined Mmp1 and upd3 knockdown will restore significantly more (p<0.05) than either single knockdown in each model.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Mmp1 necessity across tumor models - test whether tumor-specific Mmp1 knockdown rescues fat-body and muscle wasting in eye-disc RasV12 scrib and gut ykiAct tumor models at matched tumor burden.
- Aim 2: Upd3 necessity across tumor models - test whether tumor-specific upd3 knockdown rescues fat-body triglyceride and muscle loss and normalizes fat-body Pepck1 and Pdk expression in both models.
- Aim 3: Additivity of Mmp1 and upd3 loss - test whether combined tumor-specific Mmp1 and upd3 knockdown gives greater rescue than either alone, indicating parallel rather than sequential action.

**Approach:** Two tumor models will be compared side by side: an eye-antennal disc RasV12 scrib tumor model and a gut ykiAct tumor model driven by a temperature-inducible enterocyte or progenitor GAL4 (exact driver and tumor genotypes to be verified). Tumor-specific RNAi against Mmp1 and upd3, singly and combined, is driven by the same tumor driver, with UAS-lacZ or UAS-mCherry RNAi controls to equalize UAS dosage. Distant-tissue wasting is scored at matched tumor burden (GFP-positive tumor volume or area) using fat-body triglyceride normalized to protein, Nile Red or BODIPY staining of fat-body lipid droplets, phalloidin staining of thoracic or abdominal muscle, and climbing assays. Fat-body Pepck1 and Pdk transcripts are measured by qPCR as a readout of the Upd3 axis. Controls include tumor-free flies, tumor flies with control RNAi, and RNAi-only flies, and knockdown efficiency in tumor tissue is confirmed by qPCR.

**Expected outcomes:** Predicted, not established: in the eye-disc model, Mmp1 knockdown will restore at least 50% of the deficit in fat-body triglyceride and muscle integrity relative to tumor-control flies, with no more than 20% restoration in the gut ykiAct model. upd3 knockdown is predicted to restore at least 50% of the deficit and normalize fat-body Pepck1 and Pdk transcripts in the gut ykiAct model, but less than 20% in the eye-disc model. Combined knockdown is predicted to give a significantly larger rescue than either single knockdown (two-way ANOVA with interaction test, p<0.05, n≥10 animals per genotype across at least 3 independent biological replicates). If either factor rescues at least 50% in both models, or neither rescues in either model, the model-dependence prediction is falsified. Tumor size must not differ by more than 20% between knockdown and control groups, so that rescue cannot be attributed to reduced burden.

**Novelty:** Mmp1 necessity has been shown in an eye-antennal disc tumor model (PMID 34473940) and Upd3 necessity for fat-body Pepck1 and Pdk induction in a cachexia model (PMID 40275022). Reviews report that different fly tumor models yield both common and distinct cachectic factors (PMID 35319749). The supplied literature does not test either factor across multiple tumor models in parallel, nor whether Mmp1 and Upd3 act additively when lost together. This design addresses those gaps in a single controlled comparison with tumor-burden matching.

**Feasibility:** Estimated timeline is 10-12 months: 2 months for stock acquisition, verification and RNAi validation, 4 months for the single-knockdown experiments in both models, 2 months for combined knockdown, and 2-4 months for replication and analysis. Pitfalls include variable tumor burden between genotypes (to be matched or used as a covariate), RNAi off-target effects (use two independent lines), larval lethality or pupariation delay in the eye-disc model, and combined RNAi diluting GAL4 activity (mitigated by UAS dosage controls). The gut model needs temperature-shift timing optimization. The exact tumor genotypes and all stock identities must be verified before starting. Eiger/TNF is not tested here and remains an unresolved candidate.

## 26. What converts benign overgrowth into invasive growth when an oncogene is combined with loss of cell polarity?

PubMed search: `scribble Ras tumor invasion`, genes ['scrib', 'Ras85D'], 15 papers

### Sonnet 4.6

**Hypothesis:** Ets21c overexpression in the posterior compartment of Drosophila larval wing imaginal discs (using hh-GAL4) will be sufficient to substitute for scrib loss and cooperate with UAS-RasV12 to produce invasive tumors (defined by MMP1 upregulation ≥3-fold over RasV12 alone by immunofluorescence intensity, tumor mass escaping the disc basement membrane in ≥40% of larvae, and Dilp8 upregulation ≥2-fold) within 10 days at 25°C in a w1118 background, and that this invasive phenotype will be suppressed ≥60% by simultaneous knockdown of Fos (UAS-Fos-RNAi) or Dorsal (UAS-dl-RNAi), demonstrating that the JNK–Fos–Ets21c transcriptional axis and the NF-κB/Dorsal axis are both required downstream of polarity-independent oncogenic cooperation

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Ets21c sufficiency — Test whether overexpression of Ets21c in hh-GAL4>UAS-RasV12;UAS-Ets21c wing discs is sufficient to substitute for scrib loss and produce quantifiable invasive tumor phenotypes (MMP1 ≥3-fold, basement membrane breach ≥40%, Dilp8 ≥2-fold) compared to RasV12 alone and the classical RasV12;scrib-RNAi positive control
- Aim 2: JNK–Fos axis requirement — Determine whether Fos knockdown (UAS-Fos-RNAi) in the RasV12;Ets21c background suppresses invasion ≥60% (MMP1, basement membrane breach), establishing Fos as a required effector of the Ets21c-driven invasive program and confirming the JNK–Fos–Ets21c transcriptional hierarchy in a polarity-independent context
- Aim 3: NF-κB/Dorsal axis requirement and isoform distribution — Determine whether Dorsal knockdown (UAS-dl-RNAi) independently suppresses invasion ≥50% in RasV12;Ets21c tumors, and whether DorsalA and DorsalB show highest immunofluorescence intensity in invasive versus non-invasive tumor subpopulations, resolving the mechanistic contribution of NF-κB isoforms to invasion downstream of RasV12

**Approach:** Using the GAL4/UAS system, hh-GAL4 will drive co-expression of UAS-RasV12 with UAS-Ets21c in the posterior compartment of larval wing imaginal discs, using the anterior compartment as an internal wild-type control; GFP (UAS-GFP) will mark clones throughout. Tumor invasiveness will be scored by confocal immunofluorescence for MMP1, Dilp8, E-cadherin (loss of epithelial integrity), and phalloidin (basement membrane breach), alongside Dorsal and Cactus localization in tumor subpopulations. Epistasis experiments will use UAS-Fos-RNAi and UAS-dl-RNAi (targeting Dorsal/NF-κB) to dissect the relative requirement of the JNK–Fos–Ets21c axis versus the Toll–NF-κB–Dorsal–Chinmo axis in driving the invasive phenotype. Controls will include: hh-GAL4/UAS-GFP (wild-type), hh-GAL4/UAS-RasV12 alone (benign overgrowth), hh-GAL4/UAS-Ets21c alone (polarity-independent TF overexpression), and hh-GAL4 driving UAS-scrib-RNAi;UAS-RasV12 (the classical invasive model). A minimum of n=30 discs per genotype will be analyzed; statistical comparison will use one-way ANOVA with Tukey post-hoc test, with p<0.05 as significance threshold.

**Expected outcomes:** Aim 1: hh-GAL4>UAS-RasV12;UAS-Ets21c discs will show ≥3-fold increase in MMP1 immunofluorescence intensity relative to hh-GAL4>UAS-RasV12 alone, basement membrane breach in ≥40% of larvae (scored by confocal z-stack), and ≥2-fold Dilp8 upregulation by qRT-PCR, recapitulating the invasive phenotype of classical RasV12;scrib-/- tumors. Aim 2: Co-expression of UAS-Fos-RNAi in the RasV12;Ets21c background will suppress MMP1 upregulation and basement membrane breach by ≥60%, confirming Fos as a required downstream effector of the JNK-driven invasive transcriptional cascade. Aim 3: Co-expression of UAS-dl-RNAi will independently suppress the invasive phenotype by ≥50% (basement membrane breach frequency and MMP1 levels), and DorsalA/DorsalB immunofluorescence will show highest intensity in invasive subpopulations of RasV12;Ets21c tumors compared to non-invasive RasV12-alone controls, replicating the heterogeneous Dorsal distribution reported in classical tumors. If Fos-RNAi and dl-RNAi each provide only partial rescue (~50-60%), combined double knockdown is predicted to suppress invasion synergistically to <15% of controls, indicating parallel pathway architecture.

**Novelty:** This experiment directly addresses two identified gaps in the literature: (1) The gap that 'the relative contributions and epistatic relationships among the multiple cooperating signaling axes (JNK, NF-κB/Toll, Hippo/Yki, JAK/STAT, MAPK) in the same tumor cell have not been fully resolved; it is unknown whether these pathways act in parallel, in series, or in a context-dependent hierarchy.' By combining Ets21c overexpression (activating the JNK–Fos–Ets21c axis cell-autonomously without polarity loss) with RasV12 and then performing individual versus combined knockdown of Fos and Dorsal, this design resolves the parallel-versus-serial architecture of the JNK–NF-κB axes in the same genetic background. (2) The gap that 'the differential roles of distinct NF-κB isoforms (DorsalA vs. DorsalB) and their cell-population-specific distribution in invasive versus non-invasive tumor subpopulations have been identified but their mechanistic contributions to invasion are not yet resolved.' Using Ets21c to decouple scrib loss from Ras activation allows attribution of Dorsal isoform induction specifically to the RasV12 input (as established by PMID 40294135), while Ets21c provides a clean oncogenic transcription factor input independent of upstream polarity scaffolding. Additionally, the proposed Ets21c-substitution approach tests the sufficiency of a single transcription factor to bypass the entire polarity-loss requirement, a causal mechanistic question not addressed by prior RNAi screens in the classical scrib-/- model.

**Feasibility:** All GAL4/UAS components are standard and the wing disc system is among the most experimentally tractable Drosophila tissues. The hh-GAL4 driver provides strong, spatially controlled expression with a built-in internal control (anterior compartment). Major potential pitfalls: (1) Lethality — co-overexpression of RasV12 and Ets21c may cause larval lethality before disc analysis; mitigation: use a temperature-sensitive GAL80 (tub-GAL80ts) to restrict expression to late L3 by shifting to 29°C for 48h. (2) UAS-Ets21c line availability: the Külshammer et al. 2015 (PMID 26398940) line must be obtained from the originating lab or re-generated by cloning; this adds 2-3 months if re-cloning is required. (3) Incomplete RNAi knockdown: confirm >80% knockdown by qRT-PCR before interpreting epistasis results; use two independent RNAi lines for each target. (4) MMP1 antibody lot variability: include a positive control (hh-GAL4>scrib-RNAi;RasV12) in every staining batch. Estimated timeline: 6-9 months total (2 months line establishment and crosses, 2 months primary phenotype characterization, 3 months epistasis experiments and quantification, 1 month data analysis and validation). The experiment is technically feasible in a standard Drosophila genetics laboratory with confocal microscopy access.

### Sonnet 5.5

**Hypothesis:** Proposed: In eye-antennal disc clones, the invasive switch in RasV12 scrib-RNAi tissue requires aPKC activity upstream of JNK-dependent Ets21c induction. Specifically, in RasV12; scrib-RNAi clones at 25°C, (a) co-expression of an aPKC-inhibiting transgene (e.g., aPKC-RNAi, line requiring verification) will reduce Ets21c reporter-positive clone area and Mmp1 immunofluorescence by ≥40% relative to RasV12; scrib-RNAi controls, and (b) will reduce the fraction of wandering third-instar larvae with ventral nerve cord (VNC) invasion/dissemination from ≥50% to ≤20%. (c) Restoring Ets21c (UAS-Ets21c) in the aPKC-inhibited background will rescue invasion to ≥60% of control levels. This is a proposed hypothesis, not an established result; significance threshold p<0.05 (Fisher's exact test for incidence, Mann-Whitney for intensity/area, n≥25 discs/larvae per genotype).

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: aPKC requirement — test whether aPKC inhibition in RasV12; scrib-RNAi clones reduces Mmp1, JNK reporter activity, Ets21c induction, and VNC invasion relative to controls.
- Aim 2: Ets21c sufficiency epistasis — test whether UAS-Ets21c rescues invasion in aPKC-inhibited RasV12; scrib-RNAi clones and whether bsk-DN blocks the rescue or the baseline phenotype.
- Aim 3: Autonomy — compare clones where RasV12 and scrib-RNAi are in the same cells versus adjacent populations, with aPKC or Ets21c manipulation in only one population, to quantify cell-autonomous versus non-autonomous contributions to invasion.

**Approach:** Use MARCM or flip-out (ey-FLP) clonal systems in the eye-antennal disc to generate GFP-marked clones of RasV12 + scrib-RNAi (scrib loss-of-function), with or without aPKC knockdown or an aPKC-inhibitory construct. Readouts at day 6-7 after egg laying: clone volume, Mmp1 immunostaining, an Ets21c transcriptional reporter or Ets21c antibody/in situ (reagent requiring verification), phospho-JNK/TRE-reporter (JNK activity), and VNC invasion scoring. Epistasis: add UAS-bsk-DN to confirm JNK dependence, and UAS-Ets21c to test whether Ets21c can rescue invasion downstream of aPKC inhibition. Controls: RasV12 alone (benign overgrowth), scrib-RNAi alone, GFP/lacZ or white-RNAi, and UAS-dosage controls (UAS-lacZ) to balance GAL4 titration. Include a mosaic-versus-whole-tissue comparison (clones versus adjacent-clone RasV12 and scrib- populations) in Aim 3 to separate cell-autonomous from non-autonomous contributions, addressing a gap in the synthesis.

**Expected outcomes:** Proposed predictions: RasV12 alone yields benign overgrowth with low Mmp1 and <10% VNC invasion; RasV12; scrib-RNAi yields high Mmp1, JNK reporter activation, and ≥50% invasion. aPKC inhibition is predicted to reduce Mmp1 and Ets21c reporter signal by ≥40% and invasion to ≤20%, with partial reduction in clone size (consistent with aPKC enhancing overgrowth), and UAS-Ets21c is predicted to restore invasion to ≥60% of control. If aPKC inhibition does not reduce Ets21c/Mmp1 (<15% change) while bsk-DN does, the hypothesis that aPKC feeds into the JNK-Ets21c switch is falsified and scrib loss activates JNK via an aPKC-independent route. In mosaic tests, a non-autonomous contribution would appear as invasion/Mmp1 induction in RasV12 cells adjacent to scrib- cells.

**Novelty:** The synthesis states that how scrib loss mechanistically activates JNK and Ets21c, and whether aPKC mislocalization feeds in, is unresolved, with testing of Ets21c induction in aPKC-inhibited scrib mutants proposed to address it. Prior work shows JNK becomes an overgrowth/invasion driver with Ras and that aPKC enhances overgrowth (PMID 19778415), and defines a Fos > Ets21c/Ftz-F1 hierarchy where Ets21c can substitute for polarity loss (PMID 26398940). This plan connects these findings by testing aPKC → JNK → Ets21c epistasis quantitatively and by separating autonomous from non-autonomous contributions (PMID 20072127), which is a gap flagged in the synthesis.

**Feasibility:** Estimated 9-12 months: 2-3 months stock assembly and validation of aPKC knockdown and Ets21c reagents; 4-5 months for clonal experiments and imaging; 2-3 months for mosaic experiments and analysis. Pitfalls: aPKC knockdown can itself disrupt polarity or cause cell death/clone loss (survivorship bias in clone recovery); GAL4 dilution with multiple UAS constructs; variability in VNC invasion scoring (use blinded scoring); RNAi off-target effects (use two independent lines); lack of confirmed Ets21c reporter reagents. Human relevance is not tested here and remains inferred.

## 27. Can flies predict which approved drugs rescue phenotypes of a specific patient variant, and how often do those predictions hold in patients?

PubMed search: `patient variant drug screening fly model`, genes none, 6 papers

### Sonnet 4.6

**Hypothesis:** Drosophila melanogaster lines expressing ≥2 patient-specific variants from a defined cohort of ≥10 rare-disease or cancer patients will correctly predict clinically beneficial approved-drug responses (defined as ≥stable disease or ≥50% seizure reduction or equivalent primary endpoint) in the matched human patient at a rate significantly greater than chance (>30% positive predictive value, compared to a historical chance baseline of ~10%), as assessed prospectively across patients enrolled at a single center under standardized 25°C screening conditions within a 36-month study window.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Variant Modeling and Phenotype Validation — Generate and validate ≥10 patient-specific UAS-transgene fly lines (≥3 per disease arm: PKD2, KCNT1, oncogene) carrying exact patient missense variants, confirm a robust, quantifiable, variant-dependent phenotype in the appropriate tissue-specific GAL4 background, and establish that phenotype is rescued by at least one positive control compound where a literature precedent exists (e.g., chloroquine for TRPP2-D511V lines)
- Aim 2: Approved-Drug Screen and Hit Identification — Screen ≥80 FDA-approved compounds at two concentrations in each validated patient fly line using a blinded 96-well survival/locomotion assay format, identify per-patient drug hits defined as ≥20% phenotypic rescue with p<0.05, and rank hits by rescue magnitude and drug safety profile for clinical prioritization
- Aim 3: Prospective Clinical Correlation — Report top fly-predicted drug candidates to treating physicians for each enrolled patient, prospectively collect clinical outcome data at 6 and 12 months using pre-specified disease-appropriate endpoints (RECIST 1.1, seizure frequency, eGFR), and calculate positive predictive value, sensitivity, and specificity of fly-screen predictions relative to clinical outcome in a blinded analysis

**Approach:** For each of ≥10 enrolled patients harboring a specific characterized variant in one of three index genes (PKD2/amo, KCNT1/kcnt1, or a tumor-specific oncogenic driver analogous to the ACC model), a patient-specific UAS-transgene line will be generated carrying the exact human missense or gain-of-function variant via site-directed mutagenesis of a wild-type UAS-cDNA backbone, followed by PhiC31 integrase-mediated insertion at a defined attP landing site to control for position effects. Transgenic flies will be crossed to a tissue-appropriate GAL4 driver (elav-GAL4 for neuronal KCNT1 models, nanos-GAL4 or nephrocyte-specific Dot-GAL4 for PKD2 models, and en-GAL4/ptc-GAL4 for tumor suppressor contexts) to generate a primary phenotype (locomotor defect, wing vein disruption, or lethality assay) that is quantified in a blinded manner. A curated library of ≥80 FDA-approved drugs (drawn from existing approved-drug repurposing sets such as the NIH Clinical Collection) will be fed at two concentrations (1 µM and 10 µM in 5% sucrose/yeast food) in 96-well-format survival or locomotion assays, with at least three biological replicates per drug per patient line; hits are defined as drugs restoring phenotype to within 20% of the wild-type control mean with p<0.05 (one-way ANOVA with Dunnett correction). All patient lines are screened in parallel with a positive control (a previously validated rescue combination where known, e.g., chloroquine for TRPP2-D511V flies) and a vehicle-only negative control. Top fly-predicted drug candidates (≥2 hits per patient) are reported to the treating physician as candidate repurposing options, and patient clinical outcome at 6 and 12 months is assessed using pre-specified endpoints (RECIST 1.1 for tumors, seizure diary for KCNT1, eGFR/cyst volume for PKD2), with outcome assessors blinded to fly results until the 12-month lock.

**Expected outcomes:** Primary endpoint: Across ≥10 patient lines, the fly platform will identify at least one phenotype-rescuing approved drug in ≥70% of lines (≥7/10), with a positive predictive value for clinical benefit (stable disease or better at 6 months) of ≥30% (≥3 of the first 10 drug-patient matches tested clinically). Secondary endpoints: (1) Mean phenotypic rescue magnitude in hit compounds will be ≥20% improvement over vehicle control (p<0.05, ANOVA with Dunnett correction); (2) Concordance between fly hits and clinical benefit will be significantly greater than a randomized drug-assignment comparator (chi-square or Fisher's exact, p<0.05 one-tailed); (3) At least one patient in each disease category (renal, epilepsy, cancer) will show measurable clinical benefit attributable to a fly-predicted drug not previously prescribed for that variant. Negative outcome: If <2 patients (out of 10) show clinical benefit from fly-predicted drugs, this would be consistent with the null hypothesis that fly screens do not outperform chance and would prompt reassessment of phenotypic endpoints.

**Novelty:** No published systematic dataset exists reporting the hit rate of fly-predicted drug rescues that subsequently hold in the matched human patient — the adenoid cystic carcinoma case (PMID 33733072) is a single n=1 report, and it is unknown whether the fly platform predicts clinical benefit more often than chance across a patient cohort. This study directly addresses that gap by prospectively testing fly predictions in a multi-disease cohort of ≥10 patients under standardized conditions. Additionally, the TRPP2(D511V) chloroquine rescue was validated in Drosophila (PMID 26924047) but never tested in a human PKD2 D511V patient or mammalian knock-in model; this plan includes PKD2 patients as one of the three index disease arms, providing the first prospective clinical test of that fly-predicted drug. Finally, existing reviews note that KCNT1 and POLG Drosophila models do not fully recapitulate the human condition (PMID 40544226, PMID 41199776), so this study will empirically determine which fly phenotypic endpoints (locomotion, lifespan, electrophysiology) correlate with drug response in patients, filling the gap in validated translational biomarkers.

**Feasibility:** Transgene generation via PhiC31 insertion is routine and takes approximately 3-4 months per patient line including sequence verification and balancer establishment. The 96-well drug feeding format has been validated for survival and locomotion assays in published high-throughput fly studies. The main technical risk is that some patient variants (frameshift, splice-site, or dominant-negative) may not produce a robust, drug-sensitive phenotype using UAS-cDNA overexpression; to mitigate this, only missense gain-of-function or dominant-negative missense variants will be accepted for the initial cohort, consistent with variant types that have been successfully modeled (PMID 33733072, PMID 26924047). Clinical translation requires IRB/ethics approval and willing treating physicians; this is a prerequisite and may limit enrollment rate. Patient recruitment for rare diseases (especially PKD2 D511V specifically) may be slow; a 36-month window with rolling enrollment is planned, targeting 3-4 patients per disease arm. A limitation is that tumor models may require somatic mosaic expression strategies rather than simple UAS overexpression, adding complexity for cancer patients. Estimated timeline: months 1-4 for line generation and validation; months 5-10 for drug screening; months 10-36 for clinical follow-up and outcome assessment. Total cost estimated at $250,000-$400,000 USD depending on patient cohort size and clinical data collection infrastructure.

### Sonnet 5.5

**Hypothesis:** Proposed (not established): In Drosophila models of patient-derived variants (an Alk gain-of-function variant and a Pkd2/TRPP2 D511V-equivalent variant), a standardized rescue assay applied to a blinded panel of ≥40 approved drugs per model will identify rescuers (≥30% rescue of a quantitative phenotype, Benjamini-Hochberg FDR <0.05, with a monotonic dose-response in ≥3 doses) with ≥70% positive predictive value and ≥70% negative predictive value against a pre-registered reference standard of published mammalian or clinical drug responses for the same variant or pathway. Rescue calls that also pass a measured-drug-exposure check (detectable drug in fly tissue by LC-MS) will have a higher concordance than calls without that check, by ≥15 percentage points. The 70% thresholds and the 15-point difference are arbitrary proposed thresholds. If PPV or NPV falls below 50% (the lower bound of the 95% CI must exclude 70%), the hypothesis is rejected.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Validate variant models and positive controls — establish blinded quantitative rescue assays for the Alk and Pkd2 D511V-equivalent fly models, confirming that brigatinib and chloroquine, respectively, rescue at ≥30%.
- Aim 2: Benchmark fly predictions — screen ≥40 approved drugs plus negative controls per model and compute PPV, NPV, sensitivity and specificity with 95% CIs against a pre-registered reference standard.
- Aim 3: Quantify pharmacokinetic confounders — use LC-MS tissue exposure to test whether exposure-verified calls have ≥15 points higher concordance and to classify false negatives; add an exploratory Polg model and a prospective N-of-1 registry protocol.

**Approach:** Build or obtain fly lines expressing patient-derived variants. Use UAS-driven human or ortholog variant transgenes with a tissue-specific GAL4 (for example, an eye or renal-tubule-relevant driver) paired with a quantitative, blinded readout. Candidate readouts are eye-size or tumor-like overgrowth for Alk and a TRPP2 protein-level or tubule phenotype for Pkd2; the exact assays require verification. Screen each model against an approved-drug library plus clinically inactive negative-control drugs, using a ≥3-point dose series, randomized vial positions, and experimenters blinded to genotype and drug. Include wild-type-allele and vehicle controls and a positive-control drug for each model (brigatinib for Alk, chloroquine for Pkd2 D511V). Compare fly calls against a reference standard fixed before the screen, then compute PPV, NPV, sensitivity, specificity and exact binomial 95% CIs. Measure drug uptake by LC-MS in a subset to separate pharmacokinetic false negatives from true biological non-rescue. Include a Polg disease-variant model as an exploratory stress test, because POLG models are reported to be of limited quality. Design a prospective registry protocol (N-of-1 with predefined response criteria) for any fly-nominated drug that clinicians elect to use.

**Expected outcomes:** Proposed predictions: (1) Positive controls (brigatinib for Alk, chloroquine for Pkd2 D511V) will rescue at ≥30% with FDR <0.05; failure to recover them invalidates that assay. (2) The fly hit rate will be roughly 5-15% of the library per model. (3) PPV and NPV against the reference standard will each be ≥70%, with 95% CI lower bounds >50%. (4) At least a third of fly false negatives will be explained by low tissue exposure on LC-MS. (5) The exploratory Polg model will show lower concordance than the Alk and Pkd2 models. All tests will be two-sided with α=0.05 after multiple-testing correction, using ≥3 independent biological replicates of ≥30 flies per dose.

**Novelty:** The literature synthesis shows that chloroquine improved the phenotype of flies expressing a TRPP2(D511V) ortholog without patient data, and that one adenoid cystic carcinoma patient received a fly-predicted cocktail with about 12 months of stabilization. No provided study reports a denominator of fly-predicted rescues, so the concordance rate cannot be estimated. The proposal addresses this gap by pre-registering a reference standard and reporting PPV and NPV, including negative-control drugs and fly false negatives, which no provided paper assesses. It also tests whether measuring drug exposure improves prediction, a pharmacokinetic confounder the synthesis identifies as unaddressed.

**Feasibility:** Estimated timeline is 18-24 months: 3 months for line construction and assay validation, 6-9 months for screening and dose-response, 3 months for LC-MS exposure checks, and 3-6 months for analysis and registry protocol and ethics preparation. The main pitfalls are that the reference standard is limited by sparse published patient data, so many comparisons will use mammalian data instead of clinical outcomes, which the paper must state. Other risks are that variant transgene expression levels may cause artifacts, that drug solubility and feeding variability may produce false negatives, and that the library size limits power. Direct patient concordance cannot be measured within this project; the prospective registry is a proposal and requires clinical partners and ethical approval.

## 28. How does the gut tell commensal bacteria from pathogens when both carry similar microbial patterns?

PubMed search: `gut immunity commensal pathogen discrimination`, genes none, 9 papers

### Sonnet 4.6

**Hypothesis:** Bacterial-derived uracil is both necessary and sufficient to shift gut epithelial immune signaling from commensal-tolerant (low Duox-ROS, low IMD-AMP) to pathogen-discriminating (high Duox-ROS, high IMD-AMP) mode in Drosophila melanogaster adult females: specifically, (1) germ-free w1118 flies orally colonized with uracil-secreting commensal Lactobacillus plantarum engineered to overexcrete uracil will display ≥2-fold elevation in gut ROS levels (measured by luminol assay), ≥1.5-fold increase in Diptericin-GFP reporter fluorescence, and ≥30% reduction in viable commensal CFU relative to wild-type L. plantarum-colonized controls within 5 days at 25°C; and (2) germ-free flies orally challenged with the pathogen Erwinia carunculovora (a standard gut pathogen) in the presence of exogenous uracil-degrading enzyme uricase will show ≥50% reduction in gut ROS and ≥40% reduction in pathogen-driven ISC proliferation (assessed by phospho-H3 staining) compared to vehicle-treated pathogen controls, demonstrating that uracil is the primary discrimination signal upstream of Duox activation.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Uracil Sufficiency — Test whether uracil overproduction by a commensal bacterium is sufficient to trigger pathogen-level Duox-ROS and AMP responses and suppress commensal colonization in germ-free w1118 females
- Aim 2: Uracil Necessity — Test whether enzymatic depletion of luminal uracil by dietary uricase is sufficient to suppress Duox-ROS activation, reduce ISC proliferation, and impair pathogen clearance during Ecc15 oral infection in germ-free w1118 females
- Aim 3: Duox Pathway Dependency — Confirm that the uracil-dependent discrimination phenotypes identified in Aims 1 and 2 are specifically mediated through enterocyte Duox activity using tissue-specific Myo1A-GAL4-driven Duox-RNAi and catalase overexpression controls

**Approach:** We will use a two-arm experimental design in adult mated female w1118 flies reared under germ-free (axenic) conditions on autoclaved holidic medium at 25°C, 12h:12h light:dark cycle.
