Arm 1 tests uracil sufficiency: germ-free flies will be colonized by feeding with standard wild-type Lactobacillus plantarum (WCFS1) or an isogenic uracil-oversecreting strain (pyrE/pyrF deletion mutant, which accumulates uracil due to impaired pyrimidine salvage, constructed in-house or sourced from collaborators) at 10^8 CFU/mL for 24h; gut ROS will be quantified by luminol chemiluminescence assay and DHE staining, AMP induction by a Diptericin-GFP transcriptional reporter line, commensal load by CFU plating, and ISC proliferation by phospho-histone H3 immunostaining. Arm 2 tests uracil necessity: standard pathogen Erwinia carunculovora (Ecc15) oral infection at 10^9 CFU/mL will be performed with simultaneous administration of recombinant uricase (50 U/mL in food) or vehicle control (PBS) to enzymatically degrade luminal uracil; endpoints are identical to Arm 1 plus survival assay. All genotypes will include UAS-Duox-RNAi × esg-GAL4 and UAS-Duox-RNAi × Myo1A-GAL4 crosses as tissue-specific controls to confirm Duox pathway dependency; a catalase-overexpression line (UAS-Cat × Myo1A-GAL4) will serve as ROS-quench positive control. Sample sizes: ≥100 flies per condition per replicate, ≥3 biological replicates; statistical analysis by ANOVA with Tukey post-hoc and survival analysis by log-rank test, significance threshold p<0.05.

**Expected outcomes:** Arm 1 — Uracil sufficiency: Flies colonized with uracil-oversecreting L. plantarum pyrE mutant will show ≥2-fold higher luminol signal and ≥2-fold higher DHE fluorescence in midgut relative to wild-type L. plantarum colonized controls; Diptericin-GFP reporter fluorescence will be ≥1.5-fold elevated; viable commensal CFU will be reduced by ≥30%; phospho-H3-positive cells will increase by ≥1.5-fold, consistent with damage-induced ISC proliferation. Arm 2 — Uracil necessity: Uricase-treated Ecc15-infected flies will show ≥50% reduction in luminol signal and DHE fluorescence vs. PBS-treated Ecc15 controls; phospho-H3-positive ISCs will decrease by ≥40%; pathogen CFU in gut will increase by ≥2-fold (due to impaired clearance), and 7-day survival will decrease by ≥20 percentage points, confirming that uracil sensing is required for productive pathogen elimination. Negative controls (germ-free uncolonized flies) will show baseline ROS and AMP reporter levels. Duox-RNAi in enterocytes will abrogate ROS differences between uracil-high and uracil-low conditions, confirming Duox pathway dependency. All primary endpoints will be analyzed at day 5 post-colonization/infection; significance threshold p<0.05 with ≥3 independent biological replicates.

**Novelty:** This experiment directly addresses two gaps explicitly identified in the literature synthesis: (1) while uracil has been shown to trigger Duox-ROS activation, the provided literature does not establish whether uracil is sufficient to convert a commensal into an immune-activating entity in vivo, nor whether uracil removal is sufficient to protect a pathogen from clearance — this causal bidirectional test has not been performed; (2) the provided literature does not clarify how upstream recognition events are transduced into pathway-specific activation when commensals and pathogens share MAMPs such as peptidoglycan — by manipulating a single metabolic variable (uracil) while holding MAMP content constant (same bacterial species backbone or same pathogen strain), we isolate the metabolic discrimination signal from MAMP-based recognition, directly testing the metabolic-cue hypothesis in a controlled genetic system. No paper in the synthesis has used uricase delivery to ablate luminal uracil during pathogen infection or used an isogenic uracil-oversecreting commensal to test sufficiency in germ-free Drosophila.

**Feasibility:** Germ-free fly rearing and oral colonization with defined bacterial strains are well-established protocols in Drosophila gut immunology labs. Luminol and DHE ROS assays, phospho-H3 immunostaining, and CFU plating are standard. The main technical challenge is generating or obtaining the L. plantarum pyrE/pyrF uracil-oversecreting mutant — this requires bacterial genetics expertise or collaboration; alternatively, exogenous uracil supplementation (e.g., 1-10 mM uracil added to food) could serve as a surrogate in a pilot experiment to validate the system before committing to mutant generation. Uricase is commercially available (Sigma-Aldrich) and has been used in Drosophila food supplementation studies, though gut stability and delivery efficiency will need to be empirically validated with a uracil-measurement pilot. Estimated timeline: months 1-3, axenic line establishment and bacterial mutant generation/validation; months 4-6, Arm 1 (sufficiency) experiments; months 7-9, Arm 2 (necessity/uricase) experiments; months 10-12, Duox-RNAi mechanistic controls and data analysis. Potential pitfalls include: uricase instability in gut lumen reducing effective uracil degradation (mitigated by dose-response pilot); uracil-oversecreting bacteria having reduced fitness affecting colonization efficiency (mitigated by CFU normalization at inoculation); non-specific ROS sources confounding luminol assay (mitigated by catalase control arm).

### Sonnet 5.5

**Hypothesis:** Proposed (not established): in germ-free adult mated female flies mono-associated at matched CFU, the pathogen Erwinia carotovora carotovora 15 (Ecc15) will elicit at least 2-fold higher Duox-dependent intestinal ROS than the commensal Lactiplantibacillus plantarum at 24 h, and this difference will track bacterial uracil release rather than receptor-level imd activation. Adding uracil to L. plantarum-colonized flies to match Ecc15 gut uracil levels will raise ROS to at least 70% of the Ecc15 level, and enterocyte-specific Duox RNAi will reduce that uracil-driven ROS increase by at least 50%.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Quantify discrimination signals — measure gut uracil, Duox-dependent ROS and ISC mitoses at 6, 24 and 48 h in germ-free flies mono-associated at matched CFU with L. plantarum or Ecc15.
- Aim 2: Test necessity of Duox-ROS and imd — compare enterocyte Duox RNAi and imd-pathway loss of function to determine whether the commensal-pathogen ROS difference depends on Duox and is independent of imd-mediated receptor-level signaling.
- Aim 3: Test sufficiency of uracil — supplement L. plantarum-colonized flies with uracil to match Ecc15 gut levels and determine whether this is sufficient to raise ROS and ISC proliferation, with and without enterocyte Duox RNAi.

**Approach:** Germ-free, mated female flies of a single defined background (w1118 or an isogenic control matching the GAL4/UAS lines) are mono-associated at 25°C with L. plantarum or Ecc15 at matched CFU. Ecc15 and L. plantarum are my proposed representatives of a pathogen and a commensal and are not taken from the supplied literature. Readouts at 6, 24 and 48 h are: (a) luminal and culture-supernatant uracil by LC-MS; (b) gut ROS by a ROS-sensitive dye or probe; (c) ISC mitoses by anti-phospho-histone H3 counts; (d) Diptericin and Duox transcripts by qPCR; (e) bacterial load by CFU. Causality is tested with enterocyte-specific Duox RNAi (myo1A-GAL4, tub-GAL80ts), with imd pathway loss of function (e.g., a Relish null or enterocyte RNAi) to separate receptor-level from metabolite-level discrimination, and with uracil supplementation of commensal-colonized flies. Controls are germ-free flies, heat-killed bacteria, GAL4-only and UAS-only genotypes, and a non-targeting RNAi. Tests use at least 3 biological replicates and 15 guts per group per replicate.

**Expected outcomes:** Predicted (proposed): (1) Ecc15-colonized guts show at least 2-fold higher uracil and at least 2-fold higher ROS than L. plantarum guts at matched CFU (p<0.05, two-way ANOVA with post hoc correction). (2) Duox RNAi reduces the Ecc15 ROS signal to within 25% of germ-free levels. (3) Uracil supplementation of L. plantarum flies raises ROS to at least 70% of the Ecc15 level and raises pH3+ ISC counts by at least 1.5-fold. (4) The imd-pathway mutant retains the ROS difference between the two bacteria, which would support metabolite-level rather than receptor-level discrimination. Falsification: if uracil levels are not at least 1.5-fold different, if uracil supplementation fails to raise ROS above 40% of the Ecc15 level, or if Duox RNAi leaves ROS unchanged, the uracil-Duox-ROS discrimination model is rejected for this strain pair.

**Novelty:** Literature reports that bacterial uracil and uracil-modulated ROS contribute to host-microbe interactions [24997434], but the synthesis identifies that it is not shown whether uracil release differs between commensals and pathogens in situ. It also notes that receptor-level discrimination is not addressed in the supplied abstracts. This plan directly compares uracil output, Duox-ROS and imd dependence across a commensal and a pathogen at matched dose, which targets those gaps. Reviews state that the pathway induced depends on infection or pathogen type [38193088] and that both microbiota and pathogens induce immune and stress signaling in enterocytes [40745139], so a quantitative threshold model is a reasonable proposal to test.

**Feasibility:** Estimated timeline is 10-12 months: 2 months for germ-free derivation, strain preparation and verification of fly lines; 3 months for Aim 1; 3 months for Aim 2; 3 months for Aim 3 and statistics. Pitfalls: gut uracil is difficult to measure at low luminal volumes, so pooled guts and isotope-labeled standards are needed. Ecc15 and L. plantarum differ in many ways beyond uracil, so a single strain pair is confounded, and a second pair or uracil-biosynthesis mutants should be added if feasible. ROS probes can be non-specific. Matching CFU may fail because the strains colonize at different densities, so CFU must be measured at each time point. GAL80ts induction can leak at 18°C. The RNAi lines are unverified and need efficiency validation by qPCR.

## 29. Which microbial metabolites mediate the microbiome's effects on fly development rate, lifespan and behavior?

PubMed search: `microbiota metabolites development lifespan`, genes none, 15 papers

### Sonnet 4.6

**Hypothesis:** Bacterially derived acetic acid and polyamines (putrescine + spermidine) act through independent, non-overlapping signaling axes in the adult Drosophila intestine — acetic acid via JAK/STAT in intestinal stem cells and polyamines via suppression of TotM/Halo stress genes — such that gnotobiotic w1118 female flies (25°C, standard diet) mono-colonized with wild-type Acetobacter persici will show ≥15% median lifespan extension and ≥30% reduction in esg+ ISC count relative to germ-free controls, mono-colonization with polyamine-producing Lactobacillus plantarum will show ≥15% median lifespan extension and ≥40% reduction in TotM/Halo mRNA relative to germ-free controls, and simultaneous ablation of JAK/STAT signaling in ISCs (esg-GAL4 > UAS-domN) will abolish the Acetobacter-mediated but not the polyamine-mediated lifespan benefit, while spermidine-pathway genetic blockade (nos-GAL4 > UAS-spdsSp RNAi) will abolish the polyamine-mediated but not the Acetobacter-mediated lifespan benefit, demonstrating pathway independence.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Metabolite Sufficiency — Determine whether exogenous acetic acid and spermidine are each individually sufficient to recapitulate the full lifespan, ISC homeostasis, developmental rate, and behavioral phenotypes observed with bacterial colonization in germ-free w1118 flies on polyamine-free and acetate-free holidic medium
- Aim 2: Pathway Independence Epistasis — Test whether JAK/STAT blockade in ISCs (esg-GAL4 > UAS-domN) selectively abolishes the Acetobacter persici-mediated lifespan benefit while leaving the L. plantarum polyamine-mediated benefit intact, and conversely whether gut-specific spermidine synthase RNAi (nos-GAL4 > UAS-Spds-RNAi) selectively abolishes the polyamine-mediated lifespan benefit while leaving the acetic acid-mediated benefit intact
- Aim 3: Behavioral Metabolite Mapping — Quantify geotaxis climbing performance, open-field locomotion, and sleep/activity rhythms at days 10, 30, and 50 in GF flies supplemented with acetic acid, spermidine, both, or neither, to provide the first causal single-metabolite-to-behavior map in Drosophila melanogaster

**Approach:** Gnotobiotic flies will be generated by standard bleach-dechorionation of w1118 embryos and rearing on axenic holidic medium (amino-acid-defined, polyamine-free, acetate-free) in individually vented vials. Four bacterial colonization conditions will be established: germ-free (GF), mono-colonized with wild-type Acetobacter persici (AP-WT), mono-colonized with polyamine-biosynthesis-deficient A. persici (AP-ΔspeB, generated by lambda-Red recombineering to delete the agmatinase gene), and mono-colonized with L. plantarum WCFS1 WT vs. its speB/speE double-deletion mutant (LP-Δpoly) to provide the polyamine-null control. Pathway-specific epistasis will be tested by crossing esg-GAL4 (ISC-specific driver) to UAS-domN (dominant-negative Dome/JAK receptor) to block JAK/STAT in ISCs while maintaining systemic signaling, and by using nos-GAL4 > UAS-Spds-RNAi to knock down spermidine synthase in the gut epithelium; all GAL4/UAS experiments will include UAS-only and GAL4-only sibling controls. Primary endpoints are (1) median and maximum lifespan (Kaplan-Meier, log-rank test, n ≥ 150 females per condition across three independent cohorts), (2) larval development rate (egg-to-adult timing on restrictive 0.25× yeast holidic medium, n ≥ 100 larvae per condition), (3) esg+ ISC cell count by confocal imaging of dissected midguts stained with anti-Esg antibody (n ≥ 20 guts per condition), (4) RT-qPCR of TotM, Halo, STAT92E target genes (Socs36E, upd3), ilp2, ilp5, and dfoxo in whole gut and fat body, and (5) open-field locomotion and geotaxis climbing assays at days 10, 30, and 50 to capture behavioral endpoints. Metabolite supplementation rescue experiments will add 5 mM sodium acetate or 100 µM spermidine to axenic holidic medium of GF flies to confirm metabolite sufficiency independent of bacterial colonization.

**Expected outcomes:** Aim 1 (metabolite sufficiency): GF flies supplemented with 5 mM sodium acetate will show ≥15% extension of median lifespan and ≥30% reduction in esg+ ISC count vs. unsupplemented GF controls (log-rank p < 0.01); GF flies supplemented with 100 µM spermidine will show ≥15% median lifespan extension and ≥40% reduction in TotM and Halo mRNA (RT-qPCR, fold-change vs. GF, p < 0.01 by one-way ANOVA with Tukey correction); larval development on 0.25× yeast medium will be accelerated by ≥10% (hours to pupation) by acetate but not by spermidine supplementation, consistent with the acetic acid-specific developmental signaling axis. Aim 2 (pathway independence): esg-GAL4 > UAS-domN flies mono-colonized with A. persici WT will lose the lifespan benefit of Acetobacter colonization (median lifespan not significantly different from GF controls, log-rank p > 0.05) but will retain ≥80% of the lifespan benefit conferred by L. plantarum WT colonization; conversely, nos-GAL4 > UAS-Spds-RNAi flies will lose ≥80% of the L. plantarum WT lifespan benefit but retain the A. persici WT lifespan benefit, with corresponding RT-qPCR showing STAT target gene expression rescued in Spds-RNAi flies colonized with A. persici. Aim 3 (behavior): Geotaxis climbing speed at day 30 will be ≥25% higher in spermidine-supplemented or L. plantarum WT-colonized flies vs. GF controls (one-way ANOVA p < 0.05, n ≥ 60 per condition), providing the first metabolite-specific behavioral quantification; acetate supplementation will not significantly improve climbing speed (p > 0.05), dissociating the behavioral from the ISC-homeostasis endpoint.

**Novelty:** This experiment directly addresses three identified gaps in the literature: (1) it tests for the first time whether the acetic acid-JAK/STAT-ISC axis and the polyamine-TotM/Halo axis are independent or coordinated, a question explicitly flagged as unresolved in the synthesis; (2) it provides mechanistic dissection of which specific metabolite mediates which specific behavior in Drosophila, identified as 'a major empirical gap' because no paper in the synthesis links a single metabolite causally to a behavioral endpoint; (3) it uses gnotobiotic rescue and bacterial biosynthetic mutants to establish causal directionality, addressing the critique that the metabolic disorder–dysbiosis cascade in diet-induced aging studies is inferred from correlative data rather than gnotobiotic rescue experiments. Additionally, the epistasis design using dominant-negative JAK/STAT combined with spermidine synthase RNAi has not been reported in the polyamine or acetic acid literature synthesized here.

**Feasibility:** Gnotobiotic Drosophila methodology is well established and the holidic medium formulation is published; polyamine-free holidic medium can be prepared by omitting putrescine from the standard recipe. Generation of A. persici speB deletion mutants by lambda-Red recombineering is feasible within 2–3 months but requires Acetobacter-optimized electroporation protocols that may need troubleshooting — an alternative is to use commercially available polyamine biosynthesis inhibitor DFMO (difluoromethylornithine) supplemented to bacterial cultures as a faster fallback. The esg-GAL4 > UAS-domN combination has precedent for blocking JAK/STAT in ISCs without lethality, but dominant-negative expression level must be titrated to avoid gut barrier failure that would confound lifespan results; single-copy UAS insertions and 18°C rearing during development with shift to 25°C in adults can mitigate over-expression toxicity. Total estimated timeline: bacterial mutant construction and validation (3 months), fly line verification and gnotobiotic setup (2 months), Aim 1 lifespan + developmental assays (4 months), Aim 2 epistasis lifespan assays (4 months, overlapping with Aim 1), Aim 3 behavioral assays embedded within lifespan cohorts (concurrent); total ~12–14 months. Key pitfall: if A. persici colonization efficiency in axenic flies is variable, 16S rRNA qPCR on individual fly guts must be used to confirm colonization at experiment start and end, and non-colonized individuals must be excluded post-hoc with pre-specified criteria.

### Sonnet 5.5

**Hypothesis:** Proposed: In gnotobiotic w1118 flies reared at 25°C on a holidic or defined diet, monocolonization with an E. coli strain that synthesizes polyamines (e.g., spermidine/putrescine) versus an isogenic polyamine-biosynthesis-deficient mutant will, in a metabolite-specific manner, (a) shorten egg-to-pupariation time by ≥8% (about ≥6 h on a ~5-day baseline), (b) extend median adult lifespan by ≥10%, and (c) increase age-matched (day 30) negative-geotaxis climbing performance by ≥15%. Dietary add-back of physiological-range polyamines to flies carrying the mutant will rescue ≥50% of each effect. Fat-body/gut-restricted knockdown of TotM or Halo is predicted to phenocopy the lifespan and climbing effect of bacterial polyamines (≥50% of the difference) without further extension in polyamine-colonized flies (non-additivity). Acetic acid from Acetobacter persici is predicted to be the dominant driver of the developmental-rate effect but not the lifespan or climbing effect, and polyamines the converse, testing whether one metabolite acts across traits or different metabolites mediate different traits.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Cross-trait metabolite mapping — in gnotobiotic w1118 flies, use a polyamine-producing vs. non-producing E. coli by A. persici presence/absence design, with chemical add-back, to quantify effects of polyamines and acetic acid on development time, lifespan and age-related climbing.
- Aim 2: Host mediators of polyamine effects — test whether gut- or fat-body-restricted adult-onset knockdown of TotM and Halo reproduces the bacterial polyamine effect on lifespan and climbing and shows non-additivity with polyamine-producing colonization.
- Aim 3: Pathway positioning of acetic acid and polyamines — test with qPCR/genetic manipulation whether the metabolites act through JAK/STAT (Stat92E), insulin-like signaling (ilp2, ilp5, foxo) or neither, including a microbe-by-diet (dietary restriction) interaction.

**Approach:** Axenic (bleached-embryo) w1118 flies will be re-associated with defined single-species or paired communities: wild-type E. coli, an isogenic E. coli polyamine-biosynthesis mutant (strains from the Shimokawa et al. study, to be requested/verified), wild-type Acetobacter persici, and an A. persici acetate-production-impaired mutant or acetate-depleted condition (to be constructed or verified). A 2x2 design (polyamine-producing vs. non-producing E. coli x A. persici present vs. absent) plus chemical add-back (spermidine, putrescine, acetic acid at titrated doses) on axenic flies will separate the contribution of each metabolite across three phenotypes: developmental timing (egg-to-pupa and egg-to-adult, scored every 4-6 h, ≥6 vials of 30 embryos per condition), lifespan (≥200 mated females per condition, 25°C, 12:12 LD, tipped every 2-3 days, log-rank and Cox models with vial as random effect), and behavior (RING-assay negative geotaxis at days 10/30/50 and, as an additional exploratory readout, locomotor activity in DAM monitors). Host mechanism will be tested using tissue-specific RNAi (e.g., gut driver and fat-body driver, with drivers and RNAi lines to be verified) against TotM and Halo, alongside ilp2/ilp5, foxo and Stat92E readouts by qPCR and, if justified, reporter lines, with microbial load (CFU) and metabolite levels (LC-MS of polyamines, GC/LC-MS of short-chain fatty acids in fly and diet) measured to exclude colonization differences. Controls include driver-only and UAS-only lines, heat-killed bacteria, and matched CFU across colonization groups.

**Expected outcomes:** Proposed predictions, not established results: (1) Polyamine-producing E. coli will extend median lifespan ≥10% relative to the mutant (log-rank p<0.01, Bonferroni-adjusted), consistent with prior lifespan data [42720309]; this will be repeated in two independent biological replicates. (2) Polyamine add-back to mutant-colonized flies will recover ≥50% of the lifespan difference. (3) A. persici/acetic acid will accelerate development ≥8% but have a smaller (<5%) lifespan effect than polyamines; if acetic acid also extends lifespan, this will be consistent with prior ISC work [41814352]. (4) Day-30 climbing index will be ≥15% higher with polyamines; if not, the metabolite acts on lifespan without a healthspan benefit. (5) TotM or Halo knockdown in the gut/fat body will reduce the colonization-dependent lifespan gap by ≥50%; no change would falsify the TotM/Halo-mediator model. Metabolite-specific dissociation (polyamines vs. acetate across traits) will be assessed by a genotype/colonization-by-treatment interaction term with p<0.05. A null result for developmental timing with polyamines would falsify a single-metabolite, multi-trait model.

**Novelty:** The supplied literature shows polyamines extend lifespan in gnotobiotic flies but measured lifespan only [42720309], and acetic acid is named as a larval-growth metabolite [26827889] and ISC modulator in aging [41814352] without cross-context testing. Gaps identified: no test of whether polyamines affect development or behavior, no host genetic test of TotM/Halo as mediators, no direct comparison of metabolites across development, lifespan and behavior in one gnotobiotic design, and little direct evidence linking defined microbial metabolites to behavior. This plan addresses these simultaneously with a factorial metabolite-by-trait design.

**Feasibility:** Estimated 14-18 months: months 1-3 strain acquisition/verification, axenic setup and CFU/metabolite method validation; months 3-7 development and metabolite add-back assays; months 4-14 lifespan cohorts (run in parallel, ~3-4 months each for median, longer for max) and behavior; months 8-16 RNAi and molecular readouts; months 16-18 analysis. Pitfalls: availability of the polyamine mutant and an acetate-deficient A. persici (may need construction or substitution by chemical add-back); polyamine mutant may have growth differences affecting nutrition (control via CFU, diet-nutrient measurement, heat-killed bacteria); polyamines in yeast/food could mask effects (use defined diet and measure dietary polyamines); acetic acid is toxic at high doses (dose titration); lifespan requires large cohorts and axenic maintenance to prevent contamination (periodic 16S/plating checks); RNAi efficiency and off-target effects need qPCR verification and a second independent RNAi line.

## 30. What is the cost of immune activation for reproduction and lifespan, and which tissue pays it?

PubMed search: `immune activation cost reproduction lifespan`, genes none, 15 papers

### Sonnet 4.6

**Hypothesis:** Fat body-specific constitutive activation of the IMD pathway (via fat body-targeted overexpression of a dominant-active PGRP-LC transgene using Lsp2-GAL4) will reduce fecundity by ≥30% (eggs laid per female per day) and shorten median lifespan by ≥20% in mated w1118 females at 25°C on standard cornmeal-agar medium, compared to GAL4-only and UAS-only controls, while ovary-specific IMD activation (using nos-GAL4) will produce ≤10% fecundity reduction and ≤5% lifespan reduction under identical conditions — demonstrating that the fat body, not the ovary, is the primary tissue bearing the reproductive and longevity cost of immune activation.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Tissue-restricted cost mapping — determine whether constitutive fat body IMD activation (Lsp2-GAL4 × UAS-PGRP-LC) reduces fecundity by ≥30% and median lifespan by ≥20% in mated females compared to ovary-specific (nos-GAL4) and hemocyte-specific (Hml-GAL4) IMD activation and driver/transgene-only controls
- Aim 2: Resource diversion quantification — measure fat body triglyceride and glycogen stores, and hemolymph AMP activity, in fat body-activated versus tissue-control females to test whether reproductive cost correlates with measurable diversion of energy reserves from vitellogenesis to immune effector synthesis
- Aim 3: Energetic rescue test — determine whether high-yeast dietary supplementation rescues ≥50% of the fecundity deficit in fat body-activated females, distinguishing resource-competition from immune-toxicity as the mechanistic basis of the reproductive cost of fat body immune activation

**Approach:** We will use the GAL4/UAS binary expression system to constitutively activate IMD signaling in a tissue-restricted manner: (1) Fat body-specific activation using Lsp2-GAL4 to drive UAS-PGRP-LC (dominant-active isoform) expression, representing the primary AMP-secreting organ; (2) Ovary/germline-specific activation using nos-GAL4 as a tissue compartment control; (3) Hemocyte-specific activation using Hml-GAL4 as a third tissue comparator. All experimental lines will be crossed to the UAS-PGRP-LC line and compared to parental GAL4-only and UAS-only controls, as well as to a ubiquitous-driver positive control (Act5C-GAL4 × UAS-PGRP-LC). Mated females will be collected within 24 h of eclosion, housed individually with w1118 males (refreshed every 3 days), and assessed for: (a) daily egg-laying rate and egg-to-adult survivorship over 30 days, (b) adult lifespan (n ≥ 120 per genotype, three independent replicates), (c) hemolymph AMP activity by colony-forming unit assay against E. coli K12, (d) fat body triglyceride and glycogen content by colorimetric assay to measure resource diversion from energy stores, and (e) RT-qPCR of Drosomycin, Defensin, and Attacin in dissected fat bodies and ovaries to confirm tissue-restricted transgene effect. To validate that the reproductive cost is resource-mediated rather than inflammation-mediated, a subset of fat body-activated females will receive a high-yeast diet (5% yeast w/v) to test whether caloric supplementation rescues fecundity.

**Expected outcomes:** Primary outcome: Fat body-specific PGRP-LC overexpression (Lsp2-GAL4 × UAS-PGRP-LC) females will lay ≥30% fewer eggs per day compared to both GAL4-only and UAS-only controls (two-way ANOVA with Tukey post-hoc, p < 0.05, n ≥ 120 per genotype per replicate, 3 replicates), and will show ≥20% reduction in median lifespan (log-rank test, p < 0.05). Ovary-specific activation (nos-GAL4 × UAS-PGRP-LC) will produce ≤10% fecundity reduction and ≤5% lifespan reduction, statistically indistinguishable from controls (p > 0.05). Fat body triglyceride levels in Lsp2-GAL4 × UAS-PGRP-LC females will be reduced by ≥25% relative to controls, indicating resource diversion from vitellogenesis. Hemolymph AMP activity will be elevated ≥2-fold in fat body-activated flies compared to controls (CFU assay). High-yeast diet supplementation will rescue ≥50% of the fecundity deficit in fat body-activated females, confirming a resource-allocation rather than inflammation-toxicity mechanism. RT-qPCR will confirm Drosomycin upregulation ≥5-fold in fat body but not ovary of experimental females.

**Novelty:** This experiment directly addresses the primary gap identified in the literature synthesis: 'the specific tissue(s) responsible for paying the reproductive cost of immune activation remain unresolved — while the fat body is the primary AMP-secreting organ, it is unknown whether the reproductive cost is borne locally (e.g., ovarian resource depletion) or systemically via fat body resource diversion from vitellogenesis to immune effector production.' Prior work established that IMD/Toll activation impairs reproduction and lifespan at the whole-organism level, and that the fat body is the primary humoral immune organ, but no study has used tissue-restricted constitutive IMD activation with parallel tissue compartment controls to causally assign the reproductive cost to a specific organ. The dietary rescue arm additionally tests whether the cost is energetic (resource competition between AMP synthesis and vitellogenesis) or inflammatory (immune effector toxicity to reproductive tissues), a distinction not addressed in prior dose-response or transcriptomic studies.

**Feasibility:** The GAL4/UAS approach is well-established in Drosophila and all components are genetically tractable. Key technical risks: (1) Lsp2-GAL4 drives expression in late larval fat body; adult fat body expression must be verified by GFP reporter — if expression is insufficient in adults, the r4-GAL4 or CG-GAL4 drivers (which are more broadly expressed in adult fat body) should be substituted; (2) UAS-PGRP-LC overexpression may cause lethality if ubiquitous — this is why tissue-restricted drivers are used and why Act5C-GAL4 is included as a positive/lethality control; (3) nos-GAL4 has some expression outside the germline in adults — somatic expression should be verified by GFP reporter; (4) Lifespan assays require strict standardization of mating, density, food, and temperature. Estimated timeline: 3 months for line acquisition and verification (GFP reporter crosses); 2 months for experimental cross setup and genotype confirmation; 3 months for concurrent lifespan and fecundity assays; 2 months for biochemical assays (triglyceride, CFU, RT-qPCR) and data analysis. Total: approximately 10–12 months. The dietary rescue experiment can run concurrently with the main lifespan cohort. Statistical power: based on published lifespan variance in w1118 (σ ≈ 8 days, median ~55 days), n = 120 per group gives 90% power to detect a 20% lifespan reduction at α = 0.05.

### Sonnet 5.5

**Hypothesis:** Proposed: In mated w1118-background female Drosophila at 25°C, adult-restricted Imd pathway activation (UAS-PGRP-LCa under tub-GAL80ts control) in the fat body will shorten median lifespan by at least 20% and reduce offspring egg-to-adult survivorship by at least 15%, without changing 7-day cumulative egg output by more than 10%. Equivalent activation in the ovary, gut or muscle will each change median lifespan by less than 10%. The fat body is therefore predicted to be the tissue that pays the cost of immune activation.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Tissue-resolved lifespan and fecundity cost — compare adult-onset Imd activation in fat body, ovary, gut and muscle for median lifespan and 7-day egg output in mated and virgin females.
- Aim 2: Mechanism of reduced offspring survivorship — use ovary versus fat body activation, reciprocal crosses to wild-type males, ovary apoptosis and ovariole assays, and AMP transcript measurement in eggs to separate germline damage from maternal provisioning.
- Aim 3: Insulin/FOXO mediation of the fat body cost — measure fat body TAG, ilp2/ilp5 and FOXO-target expression after fat body Imd activation, and test rescue by foxo knockdown.

**Approach:** Use the TARGET system (tub-GAL80ts; tissue-GAL4 > UAS-PGRP-LCa, with UAS-imd as an independent activator) to switch on Imd signaling only in adults, by shifting from 18°C to 29°C after eclosion and mating. Tissue drivers will target fat body, ovary (somatic follicle/gonad cells and germline in separate crosses), gut enterocytes or progenitors, and muscle, with brain/neurons as an optional extra arm; each driver's specificity must be verified. Controls are GAL4-only and UAS-only genotypes, each with tub-GAL80ts and shifted to 29°C, plus permissive-temperature (18°C) siblings and a sham/heat-killed-bacteria comparison. Readouts are lifespan (at least 150 females per genotype, log-rank test with Bonferroni correction), daily egg counts, egg-to-adult survivorship of offspring, Imd target (Diptericin, Attacin) qPCR to confirm tissue-restricted activation, and tissue-resolved TAG, ovariole and apoptosis readouts. Virgin and mated cohorts will be compared to test whether mating-associated immune suppression interacts with activation cost.

**Expected outcomes:** Predicted, not established: fat body-restricted Imd activation will reduce median lifespan by 20% or more (log-rank p<0.01 versus both parental controls) and reduce offspring egg-to-adult survivorship by 15% or more, while cumulative 7-day egg number stays within ±10% of controls (equivalence test). Ovary, gut and muscle activation will each shift median lifespan by less than 10%. Ovary-restricted activation may reduce offspring survivorship, which would separate a germline or provisioning cost from a fat body systemic cost. Fat body activation is expected to lower fat body and whole-body TAG by 20% or more and reduce ilp2/ilp5 or foxo-target readouts. foxo knockdown is predicted to rescue at least 50% of the lifespan loss. Virgin females are predicted to show a smaller lifespan cost than mated females. If no tissue shows an effect above these thresholds, or if the effect appears equally in all tissues, the single-tissue-payer hypothesis is rejected.

**Novelty:** Prior infection reduces egg-to-adult survivorship but not egg number in mated females (39965650), and a single mating persistently suppresses immune defense (35728669). However, the supplied papers measure these costs at the whole-organism level and none identifies which tissue pays them. Immune elicitation was dose-dependent for reproductive success but not survival (24731072), and the supplied literature contains no test of tissue-restricted or genetically induced chronic immune activation on lifespan or offspring survivorship. The proposed work fills the tissue-resolution and lifespan gaps by combining adult-restricted, tissue-specific Imd activation with lifespan, fecundity, offspring-survival and metabolic readouts. It also tests whether insulin/FOXO signaling mediates the cost, which the synthesis lists as untested for Sex Peptide-linked immune suppression.

**Feasibility:** Estimated timeline is 12-15 months: 2-3 months for line construction and driver validation, 6-7 months for lifespan cohorts run in parallel (egg and offspring assays in the first 4 weeks), and 3-4 months for qPCR, TAG and ovary analyses. Pitfalls include: leaky GAL4 expression at 18°C; driver expression outside the intended tissue (for example, hemocytes with some fat body drivers, or fat body expression from some gut drivers); heat-shift effects on lifespan, which need matched controls; constitutive PGRP-LCa causing toxicity or gut dysbiosis; and variation in microbiota or Wolbachia status across stocks. Offspring survivorship scoring needs standardized egg density and food. Stock availability and exact driver identities must be verified before starting.

## 31. Through what molecular mechanism does Wolbachia protect its host against RNA viruses?

PubMed search: `Wolbachia antiviral protection`, genes none, 6 papers

### Sonnet 4.6

**Hypothesis:** The JAK-STAT innate immune pathway is the primary molecular mechanism required for Wolbachia-conferred antiviral protection in Drosophila melanogaster: specifically, flies carrying Wolbachia (wMel strain) and a loss-of-function mutation in hopscotch (hop, the JAK-STAT pathway kinase) will show ≥80% loss of Wolbachia-conferred survival benefit compared to Wolbachia-carrying wild-type controls following systemic injection of Drosophila C virus (DCV) at 25°C developmental temperature, while RNAi knockdown of the competing cellular resource hypothesis effectors (fatty acid synthase, FASN) will produce <30% loss of protection, thereby establishing JAK-STAT activation as necessary and lipid competition as insufficient to explain the protection phenotype.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: JAK-STAT epistasis — Determine whether disruption of the JAK-STAT pathway (hopscotch, STAT92E) abolishes Wolbachia-conferred survival benefit and viral restriction following DCV infection at 25°C in mated female w1118 flies.
- Aim 2: Comparative immune pathway screen — Test whether Toll (spätzle), Imd, or antiviral RNAi (Dicer-2) pathways are individually necessary for Wolbachia antiviral protection using the same DCV survival and RT-qPCR titer assays, to identify the primary or redundant immune effectors.
- Aim 3: Resource competition hypothesis test — Determine whether knockdown of fatty acid synthase (FASN) or dihydroorotate dehydrogenase (DHODH) in Wolbachia-positive flies phenocopies loss of protection, thereby testing the cellular resource competition hypothesis in direct comparison with the immune activation results from Aims 1 and 2.

**Approach:** We will use a genetic epistasis strategy in w1118 Drosophila melanogaster to test whether specific innate immune pathway components (JAK-STAT via hopscotch, Toll via spätzle, Imd via imd, and RNAi via Dicer-2) are individually necessary for Wolbachia (wMel)-conferred antiviral protection against DCV. Each immune pathway will be disrupted using validated loss-of-function mutants or tissue-specific UAS-RNAi lines driven by ubiquitous da-GAL4 or Act5C-GAL4, crossed into Wolbachia-positive and Wolbachia-negative (tetracycline-cured) isogenic backgrounds. The primary endpoint will be survival following intrathoracic injection of DCV (standardized dose of 10^3 TCID50 per fly) in 3–5 day old mated females reared at 25°C throughout development, with viral titer (RT-qPCR) measured at 2, 4, and 6 days post-infection as a secondary endpoint to distinguish resistance from tolerance mechanisms. To test the cellular resource competition hypothesis in parallel, we will knock down FASN (fatty acid synthase) and DHODH (dihydroorotate dehydrogenase, for nucleotide competition) using UAS-RNAi under Act5C-GAL4 in Wolbachia-positive backgrounds and measure the same survival and titer endpoints. All experiments will include four genotype classes: (1) Wolbachia+/immune pathway intact, (2) Wolbachia+/immune pathway disrupted, (3) Wolbachia−/immune pathway intact, and (4) Wolbachia−/immune pathway disrupted, with ≥150 flies per group across three independent biological replicates.

**Expected outcomes:** If JAK-STAT is the primary mechanism: (1) Wolbachia-positive hop mutant or hop-RNAi flies will show median survival indistinguishable from Wolbachia-negative controls (≤10% survival difference, versus ≥40% benefit in Wolbachia+/intact-JAK-STAT flies) after DCV injection at 25°C; (2) viral titers in Wolbachia-positive hop-disrupted flies will be ≥5-fold higher than in Wolbachia-positive hop-intact flies by day 4 post-infection (RT-qPCR); (3) FASN-RNAi and DHODH-RNAi knockdown in Wolbachia-positive flies will reduce survival benefit by <30%, suggesting lipid/nucleotide competition is not the primary mechanism. If instead RNAi (Dcr-2) disruption abolishes protection, this implicates small RNA antiviral immunity as the mechanism. If no single pathway disruption eliminates protection, this would support redundancy among mechanisms. Statistical thresholds: log-rank test for survival curves (p < 0.01 after Bonferroni correction for multiple pathway comparisons), one-way ANOVA with Tukey HSD for titer data (p < 0.05), with effect sizes reported as hazard ratios and fold-change in titer.

**Novelty:** The literature synthesis explicitly identifies that 'the precise molecular mechanism — whether host immunity activation, cellular resource competition, or another pathway — has not been resolved' and that 'no specific Drosophila immune effectors have been named as mechanistically necessary' for Wolbachia-conferred antiviral protection. No prior study in the supplied literature has used genetic epistasis in Drosophila to test which specific immune pathway (JAK-STAT, Toll, Imd, or RNAi) is individually necessary for protection, nor has any study directly compared the immune activation and resource competition hypotheses in a single controlled experiment. Additionally, the observation that 'protection can occur with or without detectable reduction in virus titer' remains mechanistically unexplained; our parallel titer and survival measurements across immune-disrupted genotypes will distinguish resistance (reduced titer) from tolerance (normal titer, improved survival) as mechanistic outcomes for each pathway.

**Feasibility:** All genetic tools required (GAL4/UAS system, loss-of-function alleles for hop, spz, imd, Dcr-2, STAT92E, and TRiP RNAi lines) are established in Drosophila and most are available from BDSC, though specific stock numbers must be verified before ordering. The DCV infection protocol via intrathoracic injection is standard and well-described in the literature. The main technical pitfall is that hop null alleles cause lethality; hemizygous males or RNAi-mediated knockdown with Act5C-GAL4 will be used to circumvent this. A second pitfall is incomplete Wolbachia elimination by tetracycline; Wolbachia-negative status must be confirmed by diagnostic PCR before experiments. Wolbachia density may vary with temperature, so all flies must be maintained at 25°C throughout development to match the conditions under which protection is established. A third pitfall is GAL4-driver background effects on immunity; Act5C-GAL4 and da-GAL4 controls without UAS transgene must be included. Estimated timeline: 3 months for line generation and Wolbachia status verification; 4 months for survival and titer experiments across all pathway knockdowns; 2 months for data analysis and validation. Total: approximately 9 months for a complete dataset.

### Sonnet 5.5

**Hypothesis:** Proposed: Wolbachia (wMel) protection of adult w1118 female flies against Drosophila C virus (DCV) at 25°C is independent of Imd, Toll and siRNA signalling and instead depends on competition for host cholesterol; protection will persist in Relish, MyD88 and Dcr-2 mutants (Wolbachia+ vs Wolbachia- hazard ratio for death ≤0.5 in each), and 10-fold dietary cholesterol supplementation will reduce the protective effect (log hazard ratio) by ≥50%.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Immune pathway dependence — test whether Wolbachia-mediated survival after DCV infection persists in Relish, MyD88 and Dcr-2 mutants.
- Aim 2: Cholesterol and lipid competition — test whether altering dietary cholesterol changes protection, DCV titre and host lipid content in Wolbachia+ vs Wolbachia- flies.
- Aim 3: Temperature and density determinants — test whether loss of protection at 18°C relative to 25°C is associated with differences in Wolbachia density, tissue distribution or host lipid state.

**Approach:** Use isogenic Wolbachia-infected (wMel) and tetracycline-cured w1118 lines, with gut microbiota restored in cured lines and cured lines allowed several generations to recover. Cross each background into null or loss-of-function mutants of Relish (Imd), MyD88 (Toll) and Dcr-2 (siRNA), with a wild-type control. Raise flies at 25°C, infect 3–5-day-old mated females by intrathoracic DCV injection (a titration of doses plus a buffer-injected control), and score daily survival, with viral RNA by RT-qPCR at 0, 2 and 4 days post-infection. Next, rear flies on defined diets with standard or 10-fold cholesterol (and a lipid-depleted diet), and measure survival, DCV titre, Wolbachia density (qPCR) and whole-body and tissue cholesterol or lipid content (e.g. mass-spectrometry or filipin staining) in Wolbachia+ vs Wolbachia- flies. Finally, compare 18°C vs 25°C developmental temperature, which affects protection, to test whether loss of protection tracks Wolbachia density, tissue distribution or host lipid state. The Wolbachia density and temperature work follows the reported correlation and temperature dependence. Controls: mock injection, cured and infected genotype-matched siblings, matched Wolbachia density across genotypes, and reporting of survival and viral titre separately because protection can be titre-independent.

**Expected outcomes:** Proposed predictions, not established results: (1) In wild-type flies, Wolbachia+ flies show significantly longer survival than Wolbachia- flies after DCV injection (log-rank p<0.05, n≥100 flies per group over ≥3 biological replicates). (2) In Relish, MyD88 and Dcr-2 mutants, the Wolbachia+ vs Wolbachia- hazard ratio remains ≤0.5, so that ≥50% of wild-type protection is retained; mutants may be more susceptible overall. (3) On 10-fold cholesterol diets, protection (log hazard ratio) falls by ≥50% relative to standard diet (diet x Wolbachia interaction p<0.05), and Wolbachia+ flies show ≥20% lower cholesterol or lipid availability than Wolbachia- flies. (4) At 18°C, protection is lost, which will be compared with Wolbachia density and lipid measures. Falsification: if protection falls by >50% in any immune mutant, or cholesterol supplementation does not change protection, the resource-competition hypothesis is rejected (or only partly supported).

**Novelty:** The mechanism is unresolved and two candidate mechanisms are named: activation of host immunity or competition with the virus for cellular resources (PMID 33584729). Protection can occur with or without a detectable reduction in virus titer (PMID 38006555). Protection depends on developmental temperature (PMID 34488458). Protection correlates with Wolbachia density in larvae and adults (PMID 26407882). The supplied primary abstracts do not directly test immune-pathway mutants against host lipid availability in the same Wolbachia-DCV system, and this design addresses that gap with a factorial test.

**Feasibility:** Estimated timeline is 12–15 months: 2–3 months for generating and validating the Wolbachia+/- mutant lines (including cure verification and microbiota restoration), 3–4 months for the immune-mutant survival assays, 3–4 months for the diet and lipid assays, and 2–3 months for the temperature comparison and analysis. Pitfalls include differences in Wolbachia density between mutant backgrounds (which must be measured and matched), background effects of mutations and incomplete isogenization, altered feeding or fecundity on modified diets, the difficulty of accurately measuring tissue-level cholesterol, and DCV dose dependence. Cholesterol supplementation may affect fly physiology independently of Wolbachia, and the diet x Wolbachia interaction term addresses this. Stock numbers and the availability of specific mutant alleles must be verified before starting.

## 32. Does the fly immune system show trained-immunity-like memory, and if so, in which cells is it stored?

PubMed search: `innate immune memory priming`, genes none, 13 papers

### Sonnet 4.6

**Hypothesis:** Trained immunity memory in Drosophila melanogaster is stored independently and additively in gut enterocytes and circulating hemocytes, such that selective ablation of trained immunity in gut cells alone (via gut-specific PGRP-SC overexpression using NP1-GAL4) or in hemocytes alone (via hemocyte-specific ROS quenching using hml-GAL4>UAS-cat) will each reduce secondary-infection survival by ≥30% relative to fully trained wild-type w1118 flies, and simultaneous ablation of both compartments will reduce survival by ≥55% (approximating naïve controls), all measured at 7 days post-secondary oral challenge with live Pseudomonas entomophila (OD600 = 0.1) in mated 10-day-old female flies at 25°C, 12:12 h LD.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Establish a unified bacterial trained immunity protocol — Optimize and validate a single experimental system using heat-killed E. coli priming followed by live P. entomophila secondary challenge in w1118 flies, confirming memory-associated survival benefit (≥30% improved survival) and molecular markers (H3K9me3 at PGRP-SC loci in gut; elevated ROS in hemocytes) in the same cohort of flies.
- Aim 2: Dissect the independent contribution of gut versus hemocyte compartments — Use NP1-GAL4>UAS-PGRP-SC (gut ablation) and hml-GAL4>UAS-cat (hemocyte ablation) single-compartment disruptions to quantify the proportional reduction in trained immunity survival benefit, AMP induction, and bacterial clearance attributable to each tissue independently.
- Aim 3: Determine whether gut and hemocyte compartments together account for total trained immunity memory — Test the double-compartment ablation genotype (NP1-GAL4; hml-GAL4 > UAS-PGRP-SC; UAS-cat) to determine whether combined disruption recapitulates the naïve phenotype, establishing whether additional unidentified compartments (e.g., fat body) must also contribute to complete immune memory.

**Approach:** Flies will be trained by feeding heat-killed Escherichia coli (gram-negative, OD600 = 10, 24 h exposure) or sterile sucrose (naïve control), following published priming protocols (PMID 42904035; PMID 33176146). After a 5-day rest period to allow epigenetic consolidation, secondary challenge will be delivered by oral infection with live Pseudomonas entomophila. Tissue-specific blockade of memory storage will be achieved using the binary GAL4/UAS system: NP1-GAL4 drives gut-enterocyte-specific transgene expression, hml-GAL4 drives hemocyte-specific expression, and a combined NP1-GAL4; hml-GAL4 intersectional cross will target both compartments simultaneously. In each compartment, trained immunity will be disrupted by two complementary effectors: (1) UAS-PGRP-SC overexpression to restore the negative Imd regulator suppressed by H3K9me3 in the gut, and (2) UAS-cat (catalase) overexpression to quench hemocyte H2O2 and block ROS-dependent upd-3 induction. Survival (Kaplan–Meier, log-rank test, n ≥ 100 flies per genotype per condition, ≥3 independent biological replicates), antimicrobial peptide transcript levels (RT-qPCR for Diptericin and Drosomycin at 6 h post-secondary challenge), and bacterial load (CFU from homogenized flies at 24 h) will be measured as primary endpoints. Epigenetic memory will be confirmed by ChIP-qPCR for H3K9me3 at the PGRP-SC locus in dissected midguts, and hemocyte ROS status will be quantified by DHE fluorescence flow cytometry. All crosses include UAS-only and GAL4-only sibling controls to account for transgene insertion effects.

**Expected outcomes:** Aim 1: Fully trained w1118 flies will show ≥50% survival at 7 days post-secondary P. entomophila challenge versus ≤20% in naïve controls (log-rank p < 0.001). ChIP-qPCR will confirm ≥2-fold enrichment of H3K9me3 at PGRP-SC loci in trained midguts versus naïve midguts. DHE flow cytometry will show ≥1.5-fold elevated ROS in trained hemocytes versus naïve. Aim 2: NP1-GAL4 > UAS-PGRP-SC trained flies will survive at ≤35% (≥30% reduction from wild-type trained), with abolished H3K9me3 enrichment at PGRP-SC loci but intact hemocyte ROS elevation, and reduced Diptericin induction (≤50% of wild-type trained levels). hml-GAL4 > UAS-cat trained flies will survive at ≤35%, with intact gut H3K9me3 but suppressed hemocyte ROS (≤naïve levels), and reduced upd-3 transcript. Aim 3: Double-compartment ablation (NP1-GAL4; hml-GAL4 > UAS-PGRP-SC; UAS-cat) will reduce survival to ≤20%, statistically indistinguishable from naïve controls (log-rank p > 0.05 vs. naïve; p < 0.001 vs. fully trained), confirming that the two compartments together account for the majority of trained immunity memory storage. Bacterial load (CFU) will inversely correlate with survival outcomes across all genotypes.

**Novelty:** No published study has directly compared the relative and combined contributions of gut enterocytes and hemocytes to trained immunity memory storage within a single controlled experimental system. The literature synthesis explicitly identifies this as the primary unresolved gap: 'both hemocytes and gut cells have been implicated as sites of trained immunity memory storage, but no study has directly compared or dissected their relative contributions in the same experimental system' (gap 1). Prior studies addressed each compartment in isolation — PMID 42904035 focused exclusively on gut H3K9me3/PGRP-SC mechanisms, and PMID 33176146 focused exclusively on hemocyte ROS/upd-3 mechanisms — using different priming stimuli (bacterial feeding vs. sterile injury) and different challenge protocols, making cross-study comparison impossible. This proposed experiment is the first to use a unified bacterial priming stimulus, a common secondary challenge, and tissue-specific genetic dissection in the same genetic background to quantitatively partition trained immunity memory between compartments.

**Feasibility:** All core GAL4 drivers (NP1-GAL4, hml-GAL4) and UAS-cat are established Drosophila tools with well-documented tissue specificity; however, BDSC stock numbers provided require verification at the time of ordering. The UAS-PGRP-SC line must be requested from the Deng/Luo laboratory (PMID 42904035) or re-generated by cloning PGRP-SC CDS into pUAST-attB and injecting into attP landing sites (e.g., BDSC #24749), adding approximately 3 months if re-generation is necessary. Double-driver and double-effector lines require 3–4 generations of crossing (approximately 2–3 months). ChIP-qPCR from dissected Drosophila midgut is technically demanding given low cell numbers; pooling ≥50 midguts per replicate and using validated anti-H3K9me3 antibodies (e.g., Abcam ab8898) is recommended. DHE-based FACS from hemolymph requires immediate processing after bleeding to avoid ROS quenching; this is established but requires optimization of bleeding technique. Total estimated timeline: 12–15 months (2–3 months line construction/acquisition, 2 months optimization, 6 months primary survival and molecular experiments, 2 months data analysis and replication). Key pitfalls: (1) NP1-GAL4 leaky expression in other tissues must be confirmed by GFP reporter; (2) hml-GAL4 may not capture all hemocyte subpopulations (crystal cells, lamellocytes) — plasmatocyte-dominant results should be interpreted accordingly; (3) UAS-cat overexpression may have non-specific metabolic effects requiring careful UAS-only controls.

### Sonnet 5.5

**Hypothesis:** Proposed (not established): in adult mated female w1118 flies at 25°C, the survival benefit of heat-killed Gram-negative priming is stored cell-autonomously in enterocytes through H3K9me3-linked repression of PGRP-SC, whereas the benefit of sterile-injury training depends on hemocytes. Specifically, (i) enterocyte-restricted restoration of PGRP-SC during the priming window will reduce the priming-induced survival benefit by at least 50% at 7 days post-priming, (ii) hemocyte ablation will reduce the sterile-injury benefit by at least 50% but the heat-killed priming benefit by less than 20%, giving a double dissociation between the two training routes.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Gut cell-type storage — test whether enterocyte-restricted PGRP-SC restoration or Su(var)3-9 knockdown during the priming window abolishes the heat-killed priming survival benefit, and map H3K9me3 at PGRP-SC in enterocytes.
- Aim 2: Hemocyte dependence — test whether conditional hemocyte ablation removes the sterile-injury training benefit while sparing the heat-killed priming benefit.
- Aim 3: Persistence and specificity — measure the duration of both memory types (days 3, 7, 14 post-priming) and test cross-protection against a Gram-positive bacterium, with PGRP-SC transcript and H3K9me3 tracked over time.

**Approach:** Prior work reports that heat-killed Gram-negative priming improves survival on reinfection and involves gut H3K9me3-mediated repression of PGRP-SC (PMID 42904035). Prior work also reports that sterile injury activates hemocytes and protects flies on later infection (PMID 33176146). We propose a 2x2 design in which flies receive either heat-killed Gram-negative bacteria, sterile injury (PBS puncture), or a naive/PBS control, and are rested 7 days before challenge with a live Gram-negative pathogen. The priming bacterial species, dose and challenge pathogen will be matched to the published protocol and require verification. Tissue-specific perturbations use temperature-sensitive GAL4/GAL80ts control: an enterocyte driver with UAS-PGRP-SC (or a PGRP-SC-restoring construct) induced only during priming, an enterocyte driver with UAS-Su(var)3-9 RNAi to test H3K9me3 dependence, and a hemocyte driver with a UAS pro-apoptotic transgene (rpr or hid) for ablation. Readouts are survival (log-rank test and Cox hazard ratios, n ≥ 150 flies per group across ≥3 biological replicates), bacterial load (CFU at 24 h after challenge), PGRP-SC transcript (RT-qPCR), and H3K9me3 CUT&Tag or ChIP-qPCR at the PGRP-SC locus in isolated enterocytes. Controls are GAL4/+ and UAS/+ parental genotypes, unprimed flies of every genotype, PBS-injured flies, and non-induced (18°C) flies. The priming effect is computed within each genotype (primed vs. its own unprimed control) so that baseline susceptibility differences, especially in hemocyte-ablated flies, do not confound the result.

**Expected outcomes:** If the hypothesis is correct: (1) heat-killed priming in control flies increases median survival after live challenge by at least 20% over unprimed flies (log-rank p < 0.05, hazard ratio < 0.7), and the sterile-injury benefit is detectable at a comparable or smaller magnitude; (2) primed control gut shows at least 1.5-fold higher H3K9me3 enrichment at PGRP-SC and at least 30% lower PGRP-SC transcript than unprimed controls; (3) enterocyte-specific PGRP-SC restoration or Su(var)3-9 knockdown reduces the priming benefit by at least 50% (genotype x priming interaction p < 0.05 in Cox model) and increases CFU at 24 h relative to primed controls; (4) hemocyte ablation reduces the sterile-injury benefit by at least 50% with less than 20% change in the heat-killed priming benefit. Falsification: if enterocyte perturbation changes the priming benefit by less than 20%, if hemocyte ablation removes the heat-killed priming benefit, or if the two training routes show equivalent dependence on both tissues, the proposed double dissociation is rejected.

**Novelty:** The synthesis identifies that cell-type resolution of gut memory is unknown, that whether hemocytes store memory is untested, and that the gut PGRP-SC/H3K9me3 mechanism (PMID 42904035) and the hemocyte ROS/JAK-STAT/Toll mechanism (PMID 33176146) have not been compared in the same assay. This proposal tests both priming regimes side by side with tissue-restricted, temporally controlled perturbations, and maps the H3K9me3 mark to specific gut cell types, which to our knowledge has not been done in the supplied literature.

**Feasibility:** Estimated timeline is 12-15 months: 2-3 months for line construction, validation and recombination (including any UAS-PGRP-SC cloning); 4-5 months for survival and CFU experiments; 3-4 months for enterocyte isolation, CUT&Tag/ChIP-qPCR and RT-qPCR; 2 months for analysis. Pitfalls: GAL80ts leakiness at 18°C; hemocyte ablation lowers baseline immune competence, so the primed-versus-unprimed effect should be compared within genotype; PGRP-SC overexpression may alter general IMD tone independent of memory (needs a PBS/unprimed control per genotype); H3K9me3 signal from sorted enterocytes may be low and require pooled guts; gut turnover could dilute the mark in ISC-derived lineages, which is a testable limitation. Exact priming and challenge bacteria and doses should be verified from the original protocol before starting.

## 33. How do antiviral RNAi and inducible immune pathways divide the work of controlling infection across tissues?

PubMed search: `antiviral RNAi immune pathways`, genes ['Dcr-2'], 15 papers

### Sonnet 4.6

**Hypothesis:** Toll-9-mediated upregulation of Dicer-2 is tissue-autonomous and functionally non-redundant: hemocyte-specific loss of Toll-9 (using Hml-GAL4 > UAS-Toll-9-RNAi) will impair systemic but not local antiviral RNAi, reducing whole-fly viral siRNA levels by ≥40% and increasing DCV titer by ≥10-fold at 5 days post-infection (dpi) compared to controls, whereas fat-body-specific Toll-9 knockdown (r4-GAL4 > UAS-Toll-9-RNAi) will impair local fat-body antiviral responses but leave hemocyte-derived exosomal siRNA output intact, predicting a ≥5-fold but <10-fold increase in DCV titer, in mated female w1118 flies at 25°C under standard conditions.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Tissue-specific Toll-9 requirement for systemic antiviral RNAi — Determine whether hemocyte-specific Toll-9 knockdown selectively impairs systemic vsiRNA dissemination and whole-fly DCV control compared to fat-body- or gut-specific knockdown, using vsiRNA deep sequencing and TCID50 assays at 5 dpi.
- Aim 2: Toll-9-dependent exosomal vsiRNA loading in hemocytes — Quantify and compare exosomal small RNA cargo from hemolymph of hemocyte-specific Toll-9 knockdown versus control flies at 5 dpi to test whether Toll-9 is required for vsiRNA packaging into hemocyte-secreted exosomes.
- Aim 3: Tissue-by-tissue additive contribution mapping — Combine pairwise tissue-specific Toll-9 knockdowns (hemocyte + fat body, hemocyte + gut) and compare survival and viral titer outcomes to single-tissue knockdowns, to determine whether tissue contributions are additive or synergistic and to establish a quantitative tissue-resolution map of Toll-9-dependent antiviral defense.

**Approach:** We will use the GAL4/UAS system to achieve tissue-specific knockdown of Toll-9 (and, as a comparator, Dcr-2) individually in hemocytes (Hml-GAL4), fat body (r4-GAL4), and gut epithelium (NP1-GAL4 [Myo1A-GAL4]), then orally and systemically challenge adult mated female flies with Drosophila C virus (DCV) at a defined infectious dose, harvesting tissues at 3, 5, and 7 dpi. Antiviral outcomes will be assessed by: (1) tissue-dissected small RNA deep sequencing to quantify virus-derived siRNA (vsiRNA) abundance and 5'-nucleotide bias per tissue; (2) RT-qPCR of viral RNA (positive and negative strand) as a proxy for replication; (3) TCID50 plaque assay on whole-fly and dissected-tissue lysates; (4) survival assays with n ≥ 100 flies per genotype across three independent biological replicates. Key controls include: UAS-Toll-9-RNAi/+ (no GAL4), GAL4/+ (no UAS), UAS-Dcr-2-RNAi positive controls, and a ubiquitous knockdown arm (da-GAL4) to establish maximum phenotype magnitude. Exosome fractions will be isolated from hemolymph at 5 dpi by differential ultracentrifugation, and their small RNA cargo will be profiled to determine whether hemocyte Toll-9 loss reduces exosomal vsiRNA loading specifically.

**Expected outcomes:** Hemocyte-specific Toll-9 knockdown (Hml-GAL4 > UAS-Toll-9-RNAi) is predicted to: (1) reduce whole-fly vsiRNA abundance by ≥40% relative to GAL4/+ controls by deep sequencing at 5 dpi; (2) increase DCV titer by ≥10-fold (≥1 log10 TCID50) versus controls; (3) reduce exosomal vsiRNA content in hemolymph fractions by ≥50%; (4) reduce median survival by ≥3 days (log-rank p < 0.01, n ≥ 100/genotype). Fat-body-specific Toll-9 knockdown (r4-GAL4 > UAS-Toll-9-RNAi) is predicted to increase DCV titer by ≥5-fold but <10-fold, with exosomal vsiRNA content reduced by <20%, indicating fat body Toll-9 contributes locally but not to the systemic signal. Gut-specific knockdown (NP1-GAL4 > UAS-Toll-9-RNAi) following oral DCV challenge is predicted to show earliest viral titer increases (3 dpi) with a ≥5-fold increase in gut-dissected viral RNA, but a smaller whole-fly survival phenotype (≤2 day median reduction). All tissue knockdowns combined should phenocopy da-GAL4 > UAS-Toll-9-RNAi. Statistical thresholds: ANOVA with Tukey post-hoc for titer/siRNA data; log-rank for survival; p < 0.05 with Bonferroni correction across tissue comparisons.

**Novelty:** This experiment directly addresses two gaps explicitly identified in the literature synthesis: (1) 'It is unclear how Toll-9 endosomal dsRNA sensing is coordinated across tissues in vivo — whether Toll-9 signaling in specific cell types (e.g., hemocytes vs. fat body) is required for systemic versus local antiviral states, and how its induction of Dicer-2 is spatially regulated, remains unresolved' (PMID 40366172); and (2) 'The identity, composition, and sorting mechanism of the systemic RNAi signal (e.g., exosomal siRNAs) remain incompletely characterized — it is unknown which specific signals trigger hemocyte exosome secretion and how recipient non-immune cells take up and utilize viral siRNAs to mount protection' (PMIDs 23684730, 39926592). Prior work established Toll-9 function in S2 cells and whole flies (PMID 40366172) and hemocyte exosome secretion at the organismal level (PMID 39926592), but no study has systematically dissected per-tissue Toll-9 requirements for systemic versus local RNAi deployment. The proposed mechanistic link — that hemocyte Toll-9 is specifically required to license exosomal vsiRNA loading — is a novel proposed causal connection not previously tested.

**Feasibility:** All core genetic tools (GAL4 drivers, TRiP RNAi lines) are standard in the field; DCV is a well-characterized natural pathogen with established infection protocols. Key technical risks: (1) Hml-GAL4 expression may not cover all hemocyte subtypes uniformly — this should be validated with UAS-mCD8::GFP imaging before infection experiments; (2) exosome isolation from Drosophila hemolymph yields small volumes requiring pooling of ≥50 flies per prep, increasing biological replication effort; (3) TRiP line stock numbers for Toll-9-RNAi require verification against current BDSC holdings, as Toll-9 is a recently characterized gene (PMID 40366172, 2025) and a validated TRiP line may not yet be deposited — alternative: generate UAS-Toll-9-RNAi by microinjection using published Toll-9 sequence or obtain from authors of PMID 40366172. Estimated timeline: 6 months total — Month 1–2: stock verification, driver pattern confirmation, DCV stock titration; Month 3–4: survival assays and viral titer measurements; Month 5–6: small RNA deep sequencing, exosome isolation, and data analysis. Potential confound: GAL4 leakiness in non-target tissues; use of tissue-specific GAL4 lines with documented specificity and inclusion of GAL4/+ controls at every step is essential.

### Sonnet 5.5

**Hypothesis:** Proposed: In adult w1118-background Drosophila melanogaster infected by intrathoracic injection of Drosophila C virus (DCV; 1000 TCID50, 25°C), tissue-specific RNAi knockdown of Dcr-2 in hemocytes (hmlΔ-GAL4 or equivalent) will raise whole-fly DCV titer at 3 days post-infection (dpi) by at least 10-fold relative to controls and reduce survival, whereas fat-body-specific Toll-9 knockdown will raise titer by at least 3-fold and will be accompanied by at least a 30% reduction in infection-induced Dcr-2 transcript in fat body. Fat-body Dcr-2 knockdown is predicted to raise titer by at least 10-fold, and Toll-9 knockdown in hemocytes is predicted to have less than a 2-fold effect. Together these outcomes would indicate that the RNAi effector (Dcr-2) is required in both hemocytes and fat body, while the inducible Toll-9 input is required mainly in fat body (proposed, not established).

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Tissue-resolved requirement of RNAi — Compare DCV titer and survival after adult-restricted Dcr-2 or AGO2 knockdown in hemocytes, fat body, gut and neurons.
- Aim 2: Tissue-specific role of Toll-9 and classical inducible pathways — Test Toll-9, spz/Toll and imd/PGRP-LC knockdown in the same tissues and infection, and measure whether AMP induction tracks viral load.
- Aim 3: Toll-9 to Dcr-2 hierarchy and systemic signal — Use Dcr-2 overexpression epistasis and tissue-resolved viral siRNA sequencing to test whether Toll-9 acts upstream of Dcr-2 and whether hemocyte Dcr-2 shapes distal tissue siRNA levels.

**Approach:** Use the GAL4/UAS system with tissue-restricted drivers (hemocyte, fat body, gut enterocyte, pan-neuronal) to express UAS-RNAi against Dcr-2, AGO2, Toll-9, and, as comparison inducible-pathway nodes, spz/Toll and imd/PGRP-LC, with a temperature-sensitive tub-GAL80ts to restrict knockdown to adulthood and avoid developmental confounds. Infect adult females with DCV by injection (with PBS/mock injection controls) and measure survival, DCV RNA by RT-qPCR in dissected tissues and whole animals at 0, 1, 3 and 5 dpi, and viral siRNA accumulation by small-RNA sequencing in selected genotypes. Controls include driver-only, UAS-only, and non-targeting/mCherry RNAi, with at least two independent RNAi lines per gene where possible and knockdown efficiency confirmed by RT-qPCR. Rescue and epistasis tests (fat-body Dcr-2 overexpression in Toll-9 knockdown flies) will test whether Toll-9 acts upstream of Dcr-2 in vivo. A parallel Imd/Toll antimicrobial peptide readout (e.g., Diptericin, Drosomycin RT-qPCR) will test whether classical AMP outputs change in viral infection.

**Expected outcomes:** Proposed predictions: (1) Hemocyte and fat-body Dcr-2 or AGO2 knockdown increases DCV RNA at 3 dpi by at least 10-fold versus controls (log2 difference, Welch t-test or ANOVA with Dunnett's correction, p<0.05, n≥5 pools of 5 flies per genotype, 3 biological replicates) and shortens median survival by at least 30% (log-rank p<0.05). (2) Fat-body Toll-9 knockdown increases titer at least 3-fold and reduces infection-induced Dcr-2 mRNA by at least 30%, while hemocyte Toll-9 knockdown has less than a 2-fold effect. (3) Fat-body Dcr-2 overexpression partially restores titer in Toll-9-knockdown flies, reducing it by at least 50% of the excess. (4) Spz/imd/PGRP-LC knockdowns change DCV titer by less than 2-fold, with AMP induction not predicted to correlate with viral load. (5) Hemocyte Dcr-2 knockdown is predicted to alter viral siRNA abundance in distal tissue (reduction of at least 30%), consistent with a systemic signal. Failure of these thresholds would falsify the specific division-of-labor model.

**Novelty:** The literature synthesis notes that no supplied study directly compares RNAi and inducible-pathway (Toll/Imd/JAK-STAT) contributions to viral control in the same infection across fat body, gut, hemocytes and brain. It also notes that tissue-specific Toll-9 requirements and whether Toll-9 acts upstream of Dcr-2 in vivo are untested (Toll-9 effects were shown in S2 cells and with an insertion allele [40366172]), and that the requirement of hemocyte-derived RNAi signals for distal tissue protection is unresolved [39926592][23684730]. This proposal addresses those gaps with a single-virus, multi-tissue, conditional knockdown comparison.

**Feasibility:** Estimated timeline 12-15 months: 2-3 months stock acquisition and verification of RNAi efficiency and driver specificity, 4-5 months for infection/titer/survival screens, 3 months for small-RNA sequencing and tissue dissections, 2-3 months for epistasis/rescue and analysis. Pitfalls: driver leakiness or off-target expression (e.g., fat body drivers in hemocytes), incomplete knockdown of Dcr-2, which is already low-abundance, RNAi line off-target effects, DCV-injection variability (control by titrating inoculum and including 0 dpi titers), and tissue dissection contamination by circulating hemocytes. Gut and brain contributions may be small after injection and may require oral or alternate routes. All specified lines must be verified for availability before work begins.

## 34. Which features of enhancer sequence (motif grammar, spacing, affinity) best predict where and when a gene is expressed?

PubMed search: `enhancer grammar motif transcription`, genes none, 2 papers

### Sonnet 4.6

**Hypothesis:** Systematic alteration of inter-site spacing between Dorsal (Dl) and Zelda (Zld) binding sites within the t48 enhancer by ±5 bp, ±10 bp, and ±20 bp increments will shift the ventral expression boundary of t48 by ≥10% of embryo circumference and alter the timing of transcriptional activation by ≥5 minutes (measured by MS2-MCP live imaging at 25°C in w1118 background mated female embryos), demonstrating that inter-site spacing is an independent, quantitatively predictive parameter of enhancer output separable from binding site affinity, and that spacing rules identified at t48 will generalize to at least one tissue-specific enhancer (eve stripe 2) active at a later developmental stage (stage 5–7).

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Spacing-boundary mapping — Determine quantitatively how systematic ±5, ±10, and ±20 bp alterations in Dl–Zld and Dl–Dl inter-site spacing within the t48 enhancer shift the ventral expression boundary and transcriptional kinetic parameters (onset time, burst frequency, amplitude) measured by MS2-MCP live imaging in nc14 embryos.
- Aim 2: Generalization testing — Test whether spacing grammar rules identified at t48 apply to the eve stripe 2 enhancer (Bcd–Dl and Hb–Hb motif pairs) by introducing equivalent spacing variants and measuring stripe position, onset timing, and bursting parameters at developmental stages 5–7.
- Aim 3: Quantitative modeling — Use the spacing–output data from Aims 1 and 2 to build and validate a quantitative predictive model of enhancer output as a function of inter-site spacing, testing whether a helical-periodicity (~10 bp) signature is detectable and whether the model predicts outputs of held-out variant constructs with ≤15% error.

**Approach:** We will use site-directed mutagenesis to generate a series of t48 enhancer variants in which the spacing between the two strongest Dl sites, between Dl and Zld sites, and between paired Zld sites is systematically varied (insertions/deletions of 5, 10, 20 bp neutral sequence) while holding binding site sequence and affinity constant, cloned upstream of an MS2-tagged lacZ reporter in the pBDP vector for PhiC31-mediated integration at the attP40 landing site to ensure positional equivalence across constructs. All reporter lines will be crossed into the MCP-GFP; His2Av-RFP stock for live single-molecule fluorescence imaging of nascent transcription in nc13–14 embryos, enabling measurement of transcriptional onset time, bursting frequency, burst duration, and amplitude as a function of dorsoventral position. As a second aim, analogous spacing variants will be introduced into a minimal eve stripe 2 enhancer to test whether spacing rules generalize beyond early DV patterning. Controls include the wild-type t48 enhancer reporter, affinity-null Dl-site mutants (established in PMID 32541043), and an unrelated spacing-scrambled enhancer that preserves dinucleotide composition. Statistical comparisons will use mixed-effects models with embryo as a random factor, with significance threshold p < 0.05 and effect size (Cohen's d) ≥ 0.5 considered biologically meaningful.

**Expected outcomes:** Aim 1: We expect a monotonic relationship between Dl–Zld inter-site spacing deviation from wild-type and the timing of transcriptional onset (measured in minutes post-nc14 initiation), with ±20 bp variants showing ≥5-minute delays or advances in onset and ≥15% shift in the ventral expression boundary relative to wild-type (assessed as fraction of DV axis circumference expressing MS2 signal above threshold). We expect bursting frequency to be the primary parameter altered (as established for affinity changes in PMID 32541043), while burst amplitude and duration remain within 20% of wild-type values. Aim 2: We expect that eve stripe 2 spacing variants (Bcd–Dl ±10 bp) will similarly shift stripe position by ≥1 cell diameter (≥5 μm) and alter onset timing by ≥3 minutes, demonstrating generalization of spacing grammar to AP patterning and later stages. Aim 3: Computational modeling of spacing–output relationships across all variants will produce a quantitative predictive function; if spacing effects are helical-repeat-dependent, we predict a ~10 bp periodicity in boundary shifts corresponding to one B-DNA helical turn.

**Novelty:** The literature synthesis identifies as an explicit gap that 'it remains unclear how the spatial arrangement (spacing and orientation) of binding sites within an enhancer quantitatively controls transcriptional output beyond pairwise linkages — specifically, whether there are general rules governing multi-site grammar that extend beyond the enhancers studied.' PMID 32541043 established affinity as a quantitative control parameter but did not independently vary spacing while holding affinity constant. PMID 19651877 identified conserved pairwise linkages at genome scale but did not test whether the spacing distance per se (rather than mere co-occurrence) is causally required. No study in the provided literature has used MS2 live imaging to quantitatively disentangle spacing from affinity as independent enhancer grammar parameters, nor has any study tested whether grammar rules from early DV patterning enhancers generalize to later-stage or AP-axis enhancers — another identified gap.

**Feasibility:** All proposed mutagenesis is standard (QuikChange or Gibson assembly); attP40 PhiC31 injection is routine at most fly facilities. MS2-MCP live imaging is an established technique in the field (Garcia lab protocols available). The main technical risk is that small spacing changes (±5 bp) may have effects below the detection threshold of live imaging (~1–2 min temporal resolution, ~2–3 cell spatial resolution); larger spacing changes (±20 bp) may disrupt local DNA structure beyond spacing per se, potentially confounding results — this is addressed by including dinucleotide-composition-matched neutral spacer sequences. The generalization test (eve stripe 2) assumes the minimal stripe 2 enhancer is well-characterized, which is supported by published literature though not directly cited in the synthesis. Estimated timeline: mutagenesis and cloning (2 months), injection and line establishment (3 months), live imaging and data analysis (4 months), computational modeling (2 months), totaling approximately 11–12 months. A potential pitfall is lethality or sterility of dl or zld hypomorph crosses, which can be mitigated by using maternal-zygotic heterozygotes or inducible knockdown.

### Sonnet 5.5
