**Hypothesis:** Proposed: In the early Drosophila embryo at 25°C, Dorsal (Dl) binding-site affinity changes in a synthetic minimal enhancer will shift the dorsoventral expression boundary and activation timing (mainly through burst frequency) more than a comparable change in Zelda (Zld) site affinity. Zld site changes will alter amplitude more than Dl changes do. Specifically, a 2-fold-or-greater reduction in Dl site affinity will delay median activation time by ≥2 min and narrow the boundary by ≥10% embryo width. Equivalent Zld site weakening will reduce mean MS2 burst amplitude by ≥25% with a smaller shift in burst frequency. By contrast, changing Dl-Dl spacing by a half helical turn (~5 bp) at constant affinity will change boundary position or burst frequency by <half the effect of the affinity change. Together, affinity and site identity/arrangement are proposed to explain >60% of variance in timing, frequency and amplitude across 4 enhancer backbones in a cross-validated model.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Affinity series — quantify how graded Dl and Zld site affinities change activation timing, burst frequency, amplitude and boundary position in the t48 enhancer and three additional enhancers.
- Aim 2: Spacing and arrangement — test causally whether constant-affinity spacing, orientation and order variants of Bicoid-Dorsal and Dl-Zld pairs alter transcriptional dynamics.
- Aim 3: Predictive model and mechanism — fit a cross-validated model ranking affinity, spacing and grammar features, and test whether Zld sites potentiate Dl occupancy.

**Approach:** Use MS2/MCP-GFP live imaging in embryos carrying enhancer-MS2 reporters integrated at one common landing site. Test the t48 enhancer (the system studied in [32541043]) plus 3 additional early enhancers chosen to contain Dl, Zld, Bicoid, Hunchback, Caudal or Twist sites (the linked-site combinations described in [19651877]). For each enhancer, build a matrix of variants: (i) point-mutation series of Dl and Zld sites giving graded affinity; (ii) spacing variants of Bicoid-Dorsal or Dl-Zld pairs, shifted by 5 and 10 bp with affinity held constant; (iii) site-order or orientation swaps. Quantify per-nucleus activation time, burst frequency, burst duration, amplitude and boundary position (≥8 embryos per construct), and fit a regularized regression or kinetic model to compare the predictive power of affinity, spacing and grammar features. Test the proposed Zld potentiation of Dl recruitment with Dl-GFP imaging or ChIP-qPCR on sorted-stage embryos for wild-type versus Zld-site-mutant reporters. Controls: wild-type enhancer-MS2, a no-enhancer MS2 reporter, an a-priori scrambled-site control, and a landing-site-matched reporter in each experiment.

**Expected outcomes:** Proposed, not established: Dl affinity reduction delays activation by ≥2 min and lowers burst frequency by ≥30%, with amplitude changing by <15%. Zld affinity reduction lowers amplitude by ≥25% and delays timing. Spacing shifts of 5-10 bp change timing or frequency in ≥1 enhancer, but by less than the affinity effect (p<0.05, mixed-effects model with embryo as a random factor). A combined affinity + site-identity + arrangement model achieves cross-validated R² >0.6 on held-out variants, and affinity alone explains less than the full model. Dl-GFP occupancy at the Zld-mutant reporter is expected to be ≥30% lower than wild type. Falsification: if spacing effects equal or exceed affinity effects, or the full model gives R² <0.4, the hypothesis is rejected.

**Novelty:** Addresses gaps in the synthesis: Keller et al. [32541043] dissect only the t48 enhancer, so generalization of Dl (timing/frequency) versus Zld (timing/amplitude) effects is unknown. Papatsenko et al. [19651877] describe linked-site grammar from sequence analysis but lack causal tests of spacing and orientation. No supplied work integrates grammar with bursting parameters in one predictive model, and Zld potentiation of Dl recruitment is only hypothesized. This design compares affinity, spacing and arrangement directly across enhancers.

**Feasibility:** Timeline ~18 months: months 1-4 design and cloning of ~30-40 variants; 4-8 transgenesis (outsourced injection); 8-14 live imaging and analysis; 14-18 modeling and Dl occupancy assays. Pitfalls: position effects (mitigated by one landing site), limited temporal resolution of MS2 for fast bursts, the difficulty of changing spacing without creating or destroying cryptic sites (check with PWM scans), batch and temperature variation, and the challenge of Dl-GFP ChIP or imaging sensitivity. Dl occupancy tests are exploratory. Human orthologs and disease relevance are unverified.

## 35. Can sequence-to-expression models trained on fly data correctly predict the in vivo effect of noncoding variants?

PubMed search: `sequence to expression model enhancer variants`, genes none, 10 papers

### Sonnet 4.6

**Hypothesis:** A CoNSEPT-class sequence-to-expression model retrained on Drosophila melanogaster embryonic enhancers genome-wide will correctly predict the direction of allelic expression change for ≥70% of individually validated causal eQTL variants from Cannavò et al. (PMID 28024300), while prediction accuracy will drop to ≤50% (near chance) for variants residing within enhancers harboring opposing-effect allelic interactions (buffered enhancers), quantified as area under the ROC curve (AUC) across ≥200 tested variants at 25°C in stage 5–10 embryos.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Model retraining and in silico benchmarking — Retrain a CoNSEPT-class model on genome-wide Drosophila embryonic STARR-seq data and compute predicted allelic expression deltas for all causal eQTL variants from Cannavò et al. (PMID 28024300), computing AUC for direction-of-effect prediction separately for buffered vs. unbuffered enhancer variants.
- Aim 2: In vivo reporter validation of model predictions — Generate phiC31-integrated GFP reporter lines carrying reference and alternative allele sequences for 20–30 selected eQTL variants spanning buffered and unbuffered enhancers, and quantify allele-specific GFP expression by imaging and smFISH in stage 5–10 w1118 embryos to establish in vivo ground truth.
- Aim 3: Mechanistic dissection of buffering as a model failure mode — Computationally and experimentally test whether introducing both opposing variants simultaneously into the same reporter construct recapitulates the buffered (near-neutral) expression phenotype, and whether the thermodynamic model component of CoNSEPT can be extended with a pairwise interaction term to partially recover predictive accuracy for buffered enhancers.

**Approach:** We will use a two-arm benchmarking and reporter-validation strategy. Arm 1 (computational): We will retrain a CoNSEPT-style CNN and thermodynamic model on a genome-wide set of Drosophila developmental enhancers using STARR-seq and ChIP-nexus datasets as training signal, then score each causal eQTL variant from Cannavò et al. 2017 (PMID 28024300) by computing the predicted expression delta between reference and alternative allele sequences in a ±500 bp window. Model predictions will be benchmarked against the measured allelic expression ratios from the published eQTL dataset. Variants will be stratified into 'buffered' (enhancers with ≥2 variants with opposing effect directions) and 'unbuffered' groups, and AUC for direction prediction computed independently for each group. Arm 2 (in vivo reporter validation): For a selected subset of 20–30 eQTL variants spanning both buffered and unbuffered enhancers, we will construct attB-site-integrated reporter transgenes (pBPGUw backbone) carrying reference vs. alternative allele sequences upstream of a minimal hsp70 promoter driving GFP, inserted at the same genomic landing site (e.g., VK37 or 86Fb) to control for position effects. Quantitative GFP fluorescence and smFISH will be measured in stage 5–10 embryos. Controls will include empty vector reporters, reporters with scrambled enhancer sequence, and reporters carrying known-functional Dorsal and Twist binding site mutations. All experiments use w1118 as the host genetic background. A chi-square test and logistic regression will assess whether buffering status is a significant predictor of model failure.

**Expected outcomes:** We expect the retrained sequence model to achieve AUC ≥0.70 for direction-of-effect prediction on unbuffered eQTL variants (single causal SNP per enhancer), consistent with the CoNSEPT model's demonstrated capacity to learn enhancer grammar (PMID 34508359). For buffered enhancers (those with ≥2 opposing-effect variants per Cannavò et al. PMID 28024300), we predict AUC will fall to 0.45–0.55, indistinguishable from random. Reporter assays are expected to validate at least 80% of computationally predicted direction calls for unbuffered variants (GFP signal ratio reference/alternative allele differing by ≥1.5-fold, p<0.05 by t-test after Bonferroni correction). Buffered-enhancer reporters are expected to show <1.3-fold difference per individual variant, confirming that opposing variants cancel in vivo. A statistically significant interaction between buffering status and model accuracy (logistic regression, p<0.01) would confirm the central hypothesis.

**Novelty:** This experiment directly addresses the primary gap identified in the literature synthesis: no published study has benchmarked sequence-to-expression model predictions against in vivo allelic expression measurements for specific causal noncoding variants (gap 1). Critically, it introduces the first systematic test of whether allelic buffering — extensive opposing-effect interactions within enhancers uncovered by Cannavò et al. (PMID 28024300, gap 2) — is a predictable failure mode for CNN and thermodynamic models. The CoNSEPT model (PMID 34508359) was evaluated only on neuroectodermal enhancers and was not tested on natural eQTL variants; our design extends it to genome-wide developmental enhancers and natural variant alleles, addressing gap 4. The use of phiC31-integrated reporters at fixed landing sites to validate model predictions in native embryonic context rather than in silico is novel relative to the reporter-only approach in Takano-Shimizu-Kouno et al. (PMID 29951825).

**Feasibility:** The computational retraining component requires existing STARR-seq and ChIP-nexus Drosophila embryo datasets (publicly available from modENCODE/ENCODE), GPUs, and approximately 2–3 months. The eQTL variant list and allelic effect measurements are published (PMID 28024300), making benchmarking straightforward. Reporter construction via Gibson assembly and phiC31 integration is routine; 20–30 constructs per arm at ~6–8 weeks per injection-selection cycle gives a 4–5 month wet-lab timeline. Potential pitfalls include: (1) the published eQTL dataset may not contain sufficient per-variant effect size information to separate buffered from unbuffered classes cleanly — mitigation is to contact the originating lab for raw allelic counts; (2) CoNSEPT's original architecture may require substantial modification to accept variable-length input — mitigation is to use a sliding-window or dilated CNN; (3) GFP reporters may not recapitulate endogenous chromatin context — mitigation is to include smFISH as an orthogonal readout. Estimated total timeline: 10–12 months. Primary statistical risk: insufficient power if fewer than 200 variants pass quality filters; power calculation suggests 200 variants gives 85% power to detect AUC difference of 0.20 at α=0.05.

### Sonnet 5.5

**Hypothesis:** Proposed: Sequence-to-expression models (the thermodynamics-based model and the CoNSEPT CNN from PMID 34508359) trained only on Drosophila neuroectodermal enhancer data will predict the direction of effect of natural noncoding variants in embryonic enhancers (benchmark from PMID 28024300) with ≥70% accuracy (binomial test vs 50% chance, p<0.05), but will predict effect magnitude only weakly (Spearman rho ≤0.4 between predicted and measured allelic expression ratio). Accuracy for compensatory opposite-effect variant pairs within the same enhancer will be at least 20 percentage points lower than for single variants, and the CNN will outperform the thermodynamic model on held-out variants by ≥0.1 in Spearman rho. Predictions for a prospective set of ≥20 CRISPR-introduced or reporter-based variants, tested in stage 5 embryos at 25°C, will match the retrospective benchmark within 15 percentage points of directional accuracy.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Retrospective benchmark — score natural embryonic eQTL variants with thermodynamic and CoNSEPT models and compare direction and magnitude with measured effects
- Aim 2: Prospective in vivo validation — test model-predicted effects of single and compensatory-pair variants using single-site integrated reporters in stage 5 embryos
- Aim 3: Failure-mode analysis — compare model classes on epistatic pairs and out-of-domain (non-neuroectodermal) enhancers to identify where predictions break down

**Approach:** Proposed: Aim 1 is a retrospective benchmark. Both model classes are trained on the neuroectodermal enhancer set used in PMID 34508359, and variants from the embryonic eQTL maps in PMID 28024300 that fall in enhancers with matching TF inputs are scored in silico. Direction and magnitude are compared with the measured allelic effects, with permutation-based null models and enhancer-level cross-validation. Aim 2 is a prospective in vivo test. Reference and variant alleles of selected enhancers, including single variants and compensatory pairs, are cloned into a lacZ or MS2/fluorescent reporter integrated at a single attP landing site (phiC31) to control position effects. Expression is quantified in stage 5 embryos by in situ hybridization or live imaging, with reference-allele reporters as controls and a scrambled-enhancer reporter as a negative control. Aim 3 tests the models' limits by stratifying errors for compensatory pairs and for variants outside the training tissue. Fly Cell Atlas data (PMID 35239393) could be used to assess generalization, though this is a proposal and not an established use.

**Expected outcomes:** Proposed: (1) Directional accuracy of ≥70% on the retrospective benchmark for both models, with magnitude correlation of rho ≤0.4. (2) Accuracy on compensatory opposite-effect pairs ≥20 percentage points lower than on single variants, indicating poor capture of epistasis. (3) CNN rho exceeding thermodynamic model rho by ≥0.1 on held-out enhancers, assessed by bootstrap 95% CI excluding zero. (4) Prospective reporter results with directional accuracy within 15 percentage points of the retrospective result, with ≥8 embryos per genotype and Benjamini-Hochberg FDR <0.05. Falsification: directional accuracy ≤55%, or no model difference, would reject the hypothesis.

**Novelty:** The supplied literature indicates that no study tests whether the thermodynamic or CNN models of PMID 34508359 predict the in vivo effect of natural or engineered noncoding variants. It also indicates that the embryonic eQTL variants of PMID 28024300 have not been used as a benchmark for sequence-to-expression models, and that it is unknown which model class best predicts held-out variant effects as opposed to fitting data. This proposal addresses these gaps and explicitly tests compensatory allelic pairs as a probe of epistasis.

**Feasibility:** Estimated 18 months: months 1-4 model training and retrospective benchmarking; 5-14 cloning, transgenesis and embryo imaging; 15-18 analysis. Pitfalls: the eQTL variants may lie in enhancers outside the neuroectodermal training domain, limiting the usable variant number; allele-specific effects in reporters may differ from endogenous context; effect sizes may be small and require quantitative imaging; the models may need retraining to include the relevant TF inputs. Variants affecting post-transcriptional regulation (e.g., ARE-mediated decay) are out of scope for enhancer models and must be excluded or analyzed separately. Stock numbers need verification.

## 36. How do young (de novo) genes become essential, and how many of them are required for viability or fertility?

PubMed search: `de novo genes essential fertility`, genes none, 11 papers

### Sonnet 4.6

**Hypothesis:** A comprehensive, tissue-stratified CRISPR-based loss-of-function screen of all annotated Drosophila melanogaster de novo genes (estimated ≥200 candidates) will reveal that ≥15% are essential for male fertility, ≥5% are essential for female fertility, and ≥2% are essential for viability in somatic tissues, under standard 25°C conditions in a w1118 background; furthermore, de novo genes regulated by achintya and/or vismay will be disproportionately enriched (≥2-fold over chance) among the fertility-essential subset relative to de novo genes lacking these transcription factor binding sites in their promoters.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Genome-wide CRISPR fertility screen — Generate and phenotype CRISPR loss-of-function alleles for ≥200 annotated D. melanogaster de novo genes to quantify the proportion essential for male fertility, female fertility, and somatic viability under standard 25°C w1118 conditions.
- Aim 2: Cellular and developmental phenotype classification — For all genes causing fertility or viability defects in Aim 1, use stage-specific GAL4 drivers, phase-contrast and confocal microscopy, and DAPI/protamine staining to classify the developmental stage and cellular mechanism of failure (meiotic arrest, spermatid chromatin defects, motility failure, or somatic lethality).
- Aim 3: Transcription factor regulatory enrichment analysis — Generate endogenous GFP-tagged achintya and vismay knock-in lines, perform ChIP-seq in testes, and test whether de novo genes bearing achintya/vismay binding sites in their promoters are disproportionately enriched among the fertility-essential genes identified in Aim 1, using Fisher's exact test with a significance threshold of p<0.05.

**Approach:** We will compile a genome-wide list of Drosophila melanogaster de novo genes (defined as genes with no detectable homolog outside Drosophila by reciprocal BLAST and synteny analysis) from published catalogs and FlyBase annotation, targeting ≥200 candidates. For each candidate, we will generate a CRISPR/Cas9 frameshift or deletion allele using two independent sgRNAs injected into w1118 embryos (or using available TRiP CRISPR lines from BDSC), confirm loss-of-function by Sanger sequencing, and balance over CyO or TM3,Sb to maintain homozygous-lethal alleles. Fertility assays will be conducted by crossing homozygous or transheterozygous knockout males (or females) to w1118 partners and counting progeny over 10 days (n≥30 crosses per genotype); male fertility will be supplemented by phase-contrast microscopy of seminal vesicle contents for sperm motility and morphology. Viability will be assessed by Mendelian ratio of homozygous adults from balanced heterozygous parents (n≥200 adults scored per line). Tissue-specific requirements will be resolved by generating UAS-RNAi lines (or using existing BDSC/VDRC RNAi stocks) driven by nanos-GAL4 (germline), bam-GAL4 (spermatogonia/spermatocytes), c135-GAL4 (early spermatocytes), αTub85E-GAL4 (late spermatids), da-GAL4 (ubiquitous somatic), and Act5c-GAL4 (broad somatic) drivers, with UAS-Dcr-2 co-expression to enhance knockdown. For the transcription factor regulatory hypothesis, ChIP-seq data from achintya and vismay (from PMID 40659874 or generated de novo using GFP-tagged endogenous knock-ins) will be used to classify each de novo gene as a direct achintya/vismay target or non-target, and Fisher's exact test will determine whether essential genes are enriched among targets.

**Expected outcomes:** Primary screen (CRISPR knockouts, n≥200 de novo genes): We predict ≥30 genes (≥15%) will cause complete or severe male sterility (≤10% progeny relative to w1118 controls, p<0.01 by two-tailed t-test with Bonferroni correction), ≥10 genes (≥5%) will cause female sterility, and ≥4 genes (≥2%) will show homozygous lethality (Mendelian ratio <0.33 of expected homozygotes, p<0.01). Microscopy of sterile males will classify phenotypes into: (a) failed spermatogenesis (no sperm in seminal vesicles), (b) motility defects (immotile sperm), (c) meiotic arrest, or (d) chromatin condensation failure (analogous to atlas). For the transcription factor hypothesis: Fisher's exact test will show ≥2-fold enrichment of fertility-essential genes among achintya/vismay ChIP-seq targets (p<0.05), supporting co-regulation as a mechanism of de novo gene network integration. If the null hypothesis holds (de novo genes are rarely essential somatically and TF targeting is random), we expect <2% somatic lethality and no significant enrichment among TF targets.

**Novelty:** This experiment directly addresses three major gaps identified in the literature synthesis: (1) Prior screens covered only 11 (PMID 28104747) and >40 (PMID 34478447) candidates, leaving the majority of de novo genes uncharacterized — this proposal is the first to attempt genome-wide coverage of all annotated de novo genes; (2) No prior screen has systematically tested de novo genes for somatic viability requirements — all published screens focused exclusively on male reproductive tissues; (3) While achintya and vismay were identified as preferential regulators of de novo genes (PMID 40659874), no study has directly tested whether their transcriptional targets are disproportionately essential, linking regulatory architecture to phenotypic essentiality for the first time.

**Feasibility:** The primary technical challenge is generating and validating ≥200 independent CRISPR knockout alleles, which at a rate of 10–15 lines per month requires approximately 15–20 months for line generation alone; a phased approach prioritizing testis-expressed candidates (using FlyAtlas2 or modENCODE expression data) in the first phase (~80 genes, 6 months) is recommended. Many de novo genes may lack existing RNAi or CRISPR reagents at BDSC/VDRC, necessitating de novo sgRNA design and injection for the majority of candidates — this is feasible but labor-intensive. Off-target CRISPR effects must be controlled by (a) two independent alleles per gene, (b) rescue with a genomic transgene for any essential gene, and (c) whole-genome sequencing of 10% of lines. The achintya/vismay ChIP-seq requires GFP knock-ins that are not currently available and will add 3–4 months. Estimated total timeline: 24–30 months. Potential pitfall: many true de novo genes may have redundant paralogs or may be expressed at levels too low for effective RNAi; CRISPR null alleles are preferred over RNAi for this reason. Genetic background effects (e.g., the w[XD1] background required for Dox/nmy phenotypes per PMID 37289846) must be considered when interpreting sex-ratio or meiotic drive phenotypes.

### Sonnet 5.5

**Hypothesis:** Proposed: In a non-redundant panel of ≥50 putative de novo D. melanogaster genes that includes goddard, saturn and atlas, CRISPR null mutants will show that ≤5% of the genes are required for viability at 25°C, while ≥10% are required for fertility in at least one sex. This fertility fraction would exceed the roughly 6% (3 of ~51) male-fertility hit rate seen in testis-specific RNAi screens. Proposed secondary prediction: knockdown of the transcription factors achintya or vismay in the testis will reduce transcript levels of at least one of goddard, saturn or atlas by ≥50%.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Unified candidate panel — merge the putative de novo gene sets from the goddard/saturn and atlas screens into a non-redundant list of ≥50 genes with uniform de novo criteria, and generate sequence-verified CRISPR null alleles for each.
- Aim 2: Viability and fertility essentiality rates — measure egg-to-adult viability and male and female fertility for each null, confirm hits with ubiquitous and testis-specific RNAi and genomic rescue, and test the predicted ≤5% viability and ≥10% fertility essentiality fractions.
- Aim 3: Transcription-factor dependency — knock down achintya and vismay in the testis and test by RT-qPCR whether expression of goddard, saturn and atlas falls by ≥50%.

**Approach:** Merge the candidate sets from the goddard/saturn screen and the atlas screen into one non-redundant list, and apply uniform de novo criteria (no annotated protein similarity, restricted phylogenetic distribution, noncoding syntenic sequence where traceable). For each gene, generate a CRISPR/Cas9 null (deletion or frameshift, with the marker verified by sequencing) in an isogenic w1118 background. Score egg-to-adult viability (≥100 embryos per genotype, expected Mendelian ratio from heterozygous crosses), male fertility and female fertility (single-pair matings, ≥15 pairs per sex per genotype, progeny counts over 7 days), with sterile mutants examined by testis or ovary and sperm cytology. Use ubiquitous (tub-GAL4>UAS-RNAi) and testis-specific (e.g. bam-GAL4>UAS-RNAi) knockdown as an orthogonal check, with GAL4-only and UAS-only controls. Genetic rescue with a genomic transgene is required for each hit. Separately, knock down achintya and vismay in the testis and measure goddard, saturn and atlas expression by RT-qPCR, with the GAL4-only genotype as control.

**Expected outcomes:** Proposed predictions, not established results: (1) At most 3 of ≥50 genes (≤5%) will show reduced viability, defined as <50% of the expected Mendelian class with p<0.05 by chi-square. (2) At least 5 of ≥50 genes (≥10%) will show fertility defects, defined as ≥80% reduction in progeny per pair versus w1118 (Mann-Whitney, FDR<0.05) in males or females. (3) goddard, saturn and atlas nulls will reproduce male sterility. (4) At least one additional gene will show a female-specific or non-testis phenotype missed by testis-specific RNAi. (5) Knockdown of achintya or vismay will reduce transcript levels of at least one of the three known fertility genes by ≥50% (n≥3 biological replicates, p<0.05). Falsification: if >5% of the genes are viability-essential, or if the fertility fraction is ≤6%, the hypothesis is rejected.

**Novelty:** No supplied study estimates how many de novo genes are required for viability, because the screens only scored male fertility. Only about 3 hits (goddard, saturn, atlas) came from roughly 51 screened candidates, and it is unclear whether the two candidate sets overlap. This study would combine the candidate sets under uniform criteria, use null alleles instead of testis-specific RNAi, and score viability and both sexes. The regulatory work on achintya and vismay does not show whether these transcription factors are required for the fertility function of specific de novo genes, which Aim 3 would address. Goddard and saturn could not be traced to a specific noncoding ancestral sequence, so the ancestral-locus question remains open and is outside the scope of this plan.

**Feasibility:** Estimated timeline is 14 months: months 1-2 for candidate curation and gRNA design; months 3-8 for CRISPR mutant generation and validation; months 6-11 for viability and fertility assays; months 9-12 for the RNAi and transcription-factor experiments; months 12-14 for rescue of hits and analysis. Pitfalls: some candidates overlap or are nested in other genes or enhancers, so deletions may disrupt neighboring loci, which makes rescue and sequence-verified alleles essential. Small ORFs are hard to target, and RNAi off-target effects can produce false viability hits. Maternal contribution can mask zygotic lethality. Sterility may be a secondary effect of poor health. A panel of ~50 genes requires substantial crossing effort. Definitions of de novo origin vary, so the ≥50 gene panel must be verified for provenance and overlap, and no line should be assumed available until verified.

## 37. What happens to transposable element control in somatic tissues with age, and does it contribute to aging?

PubMed search: `transposable elements aging somatic`, genes none, 12 papers

### Sonnet 4.6

**Hypothesis:** Age-dependent decline in Hinfp protein expression in the Drosophila somatic fat body (beginning at day 30 post-eclosion at 25°C) causes ≥2-fold upregulation of retrotransposons 412 and Roo by day 45, and fat-body-specific transgenic restoration of Hinfp in aged w1118 mated female flies will suppress this TE reactivation by ≥50%, reduce γH2Av DNA-damage foci by ≥40%, suppress age-related upregulation of immune/antimicrobial peptide genes (e.g., Diptericin, Attacin) by ≥30%, and extend median lifespan by ≥15% relative to age-matched UAS-Hinfp/+ and r4-GAL4/+ controls under standard 25°C, 12:12 LD conditions.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Age trajectory of Hinfp — Quantify Hinfp mRNA and protein levels in dissected fat bodies of mated female w1118 flies at days 10, 20, 30, 45, and 60 post-eclosion, and correlate with RT-qPCR measurements of 412, Roo, Copia, and gypsy TE expression at the same timepoints to establish whether Hinfp decline precedes or coincides with TE reactivation
- Aim 2: Causal rescue — Test whether fat-body-specific Hinfp overexpression (r4-GAL4 > UAS-Hinfp) from eclosion suppresses age-related TE reactivation, DNA damage (γH2Av foci), and innate immune gene expression (RNA-seq) in day-45 mated females, and extends median lifespan by ≥15% relative to parental controls
- Aim 3: Sufficiency of Hinfp loss — Determine whether fat-body-specific Hinfp knockdown (r4-GAL4 > UAS-Hinfp-RNAi) in young flies (day 10–20) is sufficient to recapitulate aging-associated TE upregulation, DNA damage, immune gene induction, and lifespan shortening, and test whether concurrent UAS-Histone1 co-expression rescues these defects to dissect the Hinfp→Histone1 epistatic axis

**Approach:** We will use the GAL4/UAS system to drive fat-body-specific (r4-GAL4) overexpression of Hinfp throughout adulthood in mated female w1118 flies, and separately use RNAi to knock down Hinfp in the fat body to phenocopy natural age-related decline. First, we will establish the age trajectory of endogenous Hinfp protein (western blot, immunofluorescence) and mRNA (RT-qPCR) in dissected fat bodies at days 10, 20, 30, 45, and 60 post-eclosion. Second, we will measure TE expression (RT-qPCR and small RNA-seq targeting 412, Roo, Copia, gypsy, and mdg1) and DNA damage (γH2Av immunostaining, neutral comet assay) in the same age series. Third, lifespan assays (n ≥ 200 flies per genotype, three independent cohorts) will be performed for r4-GAL4 > UAS-Hinfp versus both heterozygous parental controls and a UAS-lacZ transgenic control to confirm GAL4 dosage effects. Fourth, transcriptomic profiling (bulk RNA-seq, ≥3 biological replicates) of fat bodies from day-45 r4-GAL4 > UAS-Hinfp and control flies will identify downstream immune, proteolytic, and DNA-damage-response gene changes. All crosses and assays will include w1118 isogenic background controls, temperature-controlled incubators, and mated females maintained at standard density (20 flies per vial).

**Expected outcomes:** Primary outcome: Hinfp mRNA and protein in fat body will decline ≥1.5-fold between day 10 and day 45 (RT-qPCR, western blot; significance threshold p < 0.05, Student's t-test with Bonferroni correction across timepoints). TE expression (412, Roo) will increase ≥2-fold in wild-type fat body by day 45 relative to day 10. Fat-body-specific Hinfp overexpression (r4-GAL4 > UAS-Hinfp) will suppress day-45 TE upregulation by ≥50% relative to both parental controls (p < 0.05). γH2Av foci per fat-body nucleus will be reduced ≥40% in r4-GAL4 > UAS-Hinfp flies at day 45 (confocal immunofluorescence, ≥50 nuclei per genotype per replicate, n = 3 biological replicates). Antimicrobial peptide gene expression (Diptericin, Attacin) will be reduced ≥30% in Hinfp-overexpressing fat bodies (RNA-seq, DESeq2 analysis, FDR < 0.05). Median lifespan will extend ≥15% in r4-GAL4 > UAS-Hinfp females relative to both parental controls (log-rank test, p < 0.05, n ≥ 200 per genotype). Conversely, r4-GAL4 > UAS-Hinfp-RNAi flies will show premature TE upregulation by day 20 and lifespan reduction ≥15% relative to controls, confirming the sufficiency of fat-body Hinfp loss.

**Novelty:** This experiment directly tests the untested causal hypothesis that Hinfp expression declines with age in somatic tissues and that this decline is a proximate upstream driver of age-related TE reactivation. The literature synthesis explicitly flags that 'whether the activity or expression of Hinfp or linker histones declines with normal aging (thereby explaining age-related TE derepression) has not been tested directly' (identified gap #5). Additionally, while TE knockdown extends lifespan (PMID 37084379) and Hinfp loss causes TE derepression and aging phenotypes (PMID 34620709), the causal chain linking Hinfp decline → TE reactivation → immune signaling → lifespan shortening has not been assembled in a single tissue-specific in vivo experiment. The experiment also addresses gap #4 — the unresolved identity of downstream effectors (innate immune signaling) connecting TE expression to lifespan shortening — through transcriptome profiling of immune gene expression in fat bodies with manipulated Hinfp levels.

**Feasibility:** GAL4/UAS technology in adult fat body is well-established and r4-GAL4 provides robust adult fat-body expression with minimal developmental confounds. The primary technical risk is the UAS-Hinfp line, which must be generated or obtained from the authors of PMID 34620709; generating it via attP40 injection is standard and takes approximately 3–4 months. A TRiP RNAi line for Hinfp may exist but requires verification against the current BDSC database before ordering. Western blot quantification of endogenous Hinfp across age requires a validated anti-Drosophila-Hinfp antibody, which may need to be commissioned or validated — this is a potential 2–3 month bottleneck. The lifespan assay requires strict standardization of mating, density, food, and temperature; at least three independent biological replicates are needed for statistical power (estimated 6–8 months). RNA-seq on dissected fat bodies from aged flies is technically demanding due to tissue fragility and RNA quality; TRIzol extraction with immediate snap-freezing is recommended. Total estimated timeline: 18–24 months from line acquisition/generation through data analysis. Potential pitfall: if Hinfp overexpression in the fat body causes developmental or metabolic defects, a temporal induction system (e.g., tubGAL80ts; r4-GAL4) should be substituted to restrict overexpression to post-eclosion adulthood.

### Sonnet 5.5

**Hypothesis:** Proposed: adult-onset RNAi knockdown of the retrotransposon 412 restricted to the fat body will extend median lifespan by at least 10% (log-rank p<0.05, n≥200 flies per genotype) in mated female flies at 25°C on standard medium relative to genotype-matched vehicle controls, whereas the same knockdown restricted to neurons will extend median lifespan by less than 5%, and the fat-body extension will occur without a change in 412 genomic copy number in aged fat body DNA.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Tissue-restricted TE knockdown and lifespan — test whether adult-onset 412 (and Roo) RNAi in fat body versus neurons extends median lifespan in mated females by at least 10% in a tissue-dependent manner
- Aim 2: Expression versus insertion mechanism — determine whether lifespan extension from TE knockdown occurs without changes in genomic copy number, and whether it is accompanied by reduced γH2Av DNA damage, lower antimicrobial peptide expression, or sensitivity to reverse transcriptase inhibition
- Aim 3: Hinfp/His1 age-dependent decline and rescue — measure Hinfp and His1 levels across age in fat body and test whether adult-onset Hinfp overexpression reduces TE transcripts and extends lifespan in wild-type flies

**Approach:** Use the GeneSwitch (RU486-inducible) system so knockdown is induced only in adulthood: a fat body GS driver versus a pan-neuronal GS driver, each crossed to UAS-412 RNAi, with a UAS-Roo RNAi arm, a non-targeting RNAi control, and vehicle-fed (no RU486) siblings of every genotype. Prior work showed that knockdown of 412 and Roo extends lifespan, but it did not test tissue-restricted manipulation (PMID 37084379). Lifespan will be scored in mated females at 25°C in the same genetic background, with a minimum of 200 flies per genotype and treatment, and compared by log-rank test with Bonferroni correction. In a second cohort, collected at days 10, 30 and 50, we will measure 412 and Roo transcripts by RT-qPCR, genomic copy number by qPCR/ddPCR on dissected fat body or head DNA, γH2Av foci as a DNA-damage readout, and antimicrobial peptide transcripts as an inflammation readout. To separate expression from insertion, a subset will be fed a reverse-transcriptase inhibitor (proposed; dose and fly-compatibility require verification). A third arm will test whether Hinfp or His1 transcript or protein declines with age in fat body, and whether adult-onset Hinfp overexpression lowers 412 transcript and extends lifespan.

**Expected outcomes:** Proposed predictions, not established results: (1) Fat body 412 knockdown will increase median lifespan by 10-20% over vehicle-fed controls (log-rank p<0.05), while neuronal knockdown will give less than 5% extension. (2) Aged (day 50) fat body will show at least a 2-fold increase in 412 transcript relative to day 10 in controls, and RNAi will reduce 412 transcript by at least 60%. (3) 412 genomic copy number will not differ by more than 10% between knockdown and control or between young and old flies. (4) Knockdown will reduce γH2Av foci and antimicrobial peptide transcripts by at least 30% at day 50. (5) Hinfp or His1 levels will decline with age in fat body, and adult Hinfp overexpression will reduce 412 transcripts and extend median lifespan by at least 8%. A lifespan extension below 5% in the fat body arm, or one equally strong in the neuronal arm, would falsify the tissue-restricted hypothesis.

**Novelty:** The literature synthesis identified that Schneider et al. used knockdown of only two TEs (412, Roo) and did not test other families or tissue-restricted manipulation (PMID 37084379). This plan fills that gap by comparing tissue-restricted, adult-onset knockdown in fat body and neurons. It also addresses the unresolved question of how TE expression without increased insertion shortens lifespan, by pairing lifespan with copy-number, DNA-damage and inflammation readouts. Finally, it tests whether Hinfp/Histone1-dependent repression declines with age and whether restoring it extends lifespan in wild-type flies, which the supplied abstracts did not show (PMID 34620709).

**Feasibility:** Estimated timeline is 12-15 months: 2 months for line acquisition, RNAi validation and RU486 dose titration; 4-5 months for lifespan cohorts; 3 months for molecular time courses; 3 months for the Hinfp arm and analysis. Main pitfalls: (a) RNAi knockdown of high-copy TEs may be incomplete or may have off-target effects, so at least two independent RNAi constructs should be used if available; (b) RU486 itself can affect lifespan and feeding, hence vehicle controls of every genotype; (c) GeneSwitch drivers can be leaky and tissue specificity must be verified; (d) results are strain- and TE-family-dependent (PMID 31014230), so the effect may not generalize beyond the chosen background; (e) the reverse-transcriptase inhibitor arm requires dose and toxicity validation; (f) in-house construction of a UAS-Hinfp line may be needed.

## 38. Why does disrupting 3D genome structure often have modest effects on gene expression in flies, and when does it matter?

PubMed search: `3D genome topology gene expression`, genes none, 4 papers

### Sonnet 4.6

**Hypothesis:** Disrupting the CTCF/cohesin-anchored chromatin loop between the paralogous Abd-B and Ubx Hox gene loci in Drosophila melanogaster will produce gene expression changes (≥1.5-fold in at least one paralog) only in developmental contexts (stage 10–14 embryo abdominal segments) where a single non-redundant loop mediates cross-regulation, whereas in larval wing disc tissue where redundant enhancer contacts exist, disruption will produce <20% change in steady-state mRNA levels; furthermore, transcriptional bursting frequency (not amplitude) will be the primary parameter altered upon loop disruption, detectable by single-molecule fluorescence in situ hybridization (smFISH) as a ≥25% reduction in the fraction of actively transcribing nuclei in affected segments at 25°C.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Loop anchor deletion — Generate and validate CRISPR-Cas9 lines carrying precise deletion of the Abd-B/Ubx inter-locus tethering element and confirm loop disruption by 4C-seq in embryonic and larval tissues without altering flanking gene or enhancer sequences
- Aim 2: Context-dependent expression consequences — Quantify steady-state Abd-B and Ubx mRNA levels by RT-qPCR in stage 10–14 embryonic abdominal segments versus third-instar larval wing discs in loop-anchor deletion versus control lines to test whether expression effects are tissue-context-dependent (≥1.5-fold in embryo, <20% in disc)
- Aim 3: Transcriptional bursting dynamics — Use smFISH with intronic probes in stage 12 embryonic abdominal segments to decompose any expression change into burst frequency, amplitude, and duration components, testing the prediction that burst frequency is preferentially reduced (≥25%) while amplitude is preserved (<25% change) upon loop disruption

**Approach:** We will use CRISPR-Cas9 to delete or invert the loop anchor sequences (tethering element/insulator sequences) between Abd-B and Ubx without altering coding or known enhancer sequences, generating anchor-mutant fly lines verified by 4C-seq or Hi-C to confirm loop disruption. GAL4/UAS-based tissue-specific expression of a dCas9-KRAB transcriptional repressor fused to guide RNAs targeting loop anchor sequences will provide an orthogonal, reversible approach to test loop disruption in specific tissues (abdominal neuroblasts via elav-GAL4, wing disc cells via nub-GAL4, and fat body via Lsp2-GAL4 as a non-expressing control). Quantitative RT-qPCR and smFISH with intronic probes will measure both steady-state mRNA levels and transcriptional bursting parameters (burst frequency, amplitude, and duration) in dissected tissues from stage 10–14 embryos and third-instar larval wing discs. Controls will include isogenic w1118 flies, loop-anchor-intact CRISPR lines with synonymous edits, and GAL4 lines without UAS-dCas9-KRAB to account for GAL4 toxicity. 4C-seq from Abd-B and Ubx viewpoints will confirm loop loss in each tissue context.

**Expected outcomes:** In stage 10–14 embryonic abdominal segments (A5–A8), loop-anchor deletion will reduce Abd-B mRNA levels by ≥1.5-fold and alter Ubx expression by ≥1.5-fold relative to w1118 controls (p<0.05, Mann-Whitney U, n≥20 embryos per genotype), with smFISH revealing ≥25% reduction in burst frequency (fraction of nuclei with nascent transcript foci) but <25% change in burst amplitude (foci intensity per transcribing nucleus). In third-instar larval wing discs (nub-GAL4 territory), the same loop disruption will produce <20% change in Abd-B or Ubx mRNA levels by RT-qPCR (not significant at p>0.05), consistent with regulatory redundancy buffering the perturbation. 4C-seq will confirm ≥50% reduction in inter-locus contact frequency at the anchor in mutant versus control lines. The synonymous-edit control lines and Lsp2-GAL4-driven dCas9-KRAB lines will show no significant expression changes in any tissue (p>0.05), validating specificity.

**Novelty:** This experiment directly addresses three unresolved gaps identified in the literature synthesis: (1) it tests which molecular features — specifically insulator/tethering element sequences at loop anchors — determine whether loop disruption is buffered versus consequential, a distinction explicitly flagged as unresolved; (2) it dissects the developmental-context dependence of a constitutive loop's function by comparing embryonic versus larval disc tissues using the same genetic perturbation, addressing the gap that 'the molecular logic governing how the same architectural scaffold is repurposed in different developmental contexts remains undefined'; and (3) it resolves how transcriptional bursting dynamics (frequency, amplitude, duration) are specifically altered by loop disruption rather than measuring only steady-state mRNA, addressing the gap that bursting parameters 'are reviewed but not experimentally resolved for loop perturbation experiments.' No prior study has simultaneously measured bursting parameters and steady-state expression across multiple developmental tissue contexts for the same loop disruption in Drosophila.

**Feasibility:** CRISPR-Cas9 deletions in Drosophila are routine and achievable within 4–6 months including line establishment and 4C-seq validation. smFISH on Drosophila embryos and larval discs is technically established and compatible with intronic nascent-transcript probes for bursting analysis; the primary challenge is designing probes that distinguish Abd-B and Ubx transcripts given paralogy — this requires careful probe design and validation (~2 months). The dCas9-KRAB approach depends on achieving sufficient repression of the anchor without off-target effects, which may require optimization of sgRNA design and validation by ChIP-seq for KRAB occupancy (~3 months). A potential pitfall is that the loop anchor may overlap with cryptic enhancer sequences not annotated in current databases, making it difficult to separate loop disruption from enhancer disruption — this is mitigated by the synonymous-edit control and by restricting deletions to CTCF motif cores identified by existing ChIP-seq data. Total estimated timeline: 18–24 months from line generation to final analysis. Budget consideration: 4C-seq from multiple viewpoints and tissues represents the largest cost (~$2,000–4,000 per sample). Statistical power analysis: n=20 embryos per genotype provides >80% power to detect a 1.5-fold mRNA difference assuming CV=30% based on published Drosophila smFISH datasets.

### Sonnet 5.5

**Hypothesis:** Proposed: in Drosophila melanogaster embryos at 25°C, deleting one anchor of a gene-gene chromatin loop will change bulk mRNA of the looped genes by less than 1.5-fold, yet will raise cell-to-cell variability of nascent transcription (coefficient of variation) by at least 25% through altered burst frequency. Removing a redundant enhancer in the same background will reduce expression at least 2-fold more than expected from the product of the two single perturbations (synergy, p<0.05).

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Quantify bulk effects of loop anchor loss. Delete one anchor at 2-3 embryonic gene-gene loops, confirm loop loss by Micro-C or 4C, and measure bulk mRNA changes in the looped genes (prediction: under 1.5-fold).
- Aim 2: Test whether modest mean changes hide altered transcriptional dynamics. Use endogenous MS2 live imaging to compare burst frequency, amplitude, duration and cell-to-cell CV between WT and ΔA embryos.
- Aim 3: Test redundancy buffering and context dependence. Combine ΔA with deletion of a redundant enhancer to test for synergy, and compare ΔA effects across at least two stages or tissues.

**Approach:** Proposed: pick 2-3 gene-gene loops from the embryo loop set characterized in the Pollex et al. study (38157845), preferring loops where one anchor can be deleted without removing a coding or promoter sequence. Use CRISPR/Cas9 to make precise anchor deletions (ΔA) and, in a second step, deletions of a candidate redundant enhancer or shadow enhancer (ΔE) of one looped gene, giving WT, ΔA, ΔE and ΔAΔE homozygous lines. Measure bulk expression by RNA-seq and RT-qPCR in staged embryos, and nascent transcription by endogenous MS2 knock-in with MCP-GFP live imaging, quantifying burst frequency, amplitude, duration and cell-to-cell CV. Run the same ΔA comparison in at least two later tissues or stages, using smFISH or MS2, to test whether loop function is context-dependent. Controls: isogenic parental line, a size-matched deletion of non-anchor sequence next to the anchor, and Hi-C or Micro-C or 4C-seq on ΔA embryos to confirm that the loop is actually lost. Synergy is tested with a multiplicative model on log-scale expression across at least 3 biological replicates and at least 20 embryos per genotype for imaging.

**Expected outcomes:** Proposed predictions, not established results: (1) ΔA gives bulk log2 fold-change within ±0.58 (under 1.5-fold) for looped genes, with at least 80% of tested loops in that range. (2) Despite the modest mean effect, ΔA raises CV of nascent transcription by at least 25% or changes burst frequency by at least 20% relative to WT (Benjamini-Hochberg q<0.05). (3) ΔAΔE reduces expression at least 2-fold below the multiplicative expectation from ΔA and ΔE. (4) At least one loop shows a different sign or magnitude of ΔA effect between stages or tissues. Falsification: if ΔA has no significant effect on variability or burst parameters and ΔAΔE is not synergistic, the redundancy and noise-buffering account is rejected for the tested loops.

**Novelty:** Pollex et al. showed that breaking a gene-gene loop attenuates or enhances expression of related genes in embryos, but only three loops were dissected, so it is unknown whether the pattern generalizes. The Deng et al. review indicates that multiple simultaneous enhancer-promoter interactions can act cooperatively or competitively to maintain mRNA levels, but the supplied literature does not quantify redundancy buffering. This plan combines anchor deletion with removal of a redundant enhancer, and adds live burst-level readouts, to test whether modest bulk effects mask changes in variability.

**Feasibility:** Proposed timeline of about 12-15 months. Months 1-4: select loops, design guides and generate ΔA, ΔE and MS2 knock-ins. Months 5-8: build the double mutants and verify loop loss by Micro-C or 4C. Months 9-13: live imaging, RNA-seq and statistical modeling. Pitfalls: anchor deletions may remove cryptic regulatory sequence, which the adjacent-deletion control addresses. MS2 knock-ins may perturb expression, so tagged WT should be compared with untagged WT. Embryo-to-embryo variability requires adequate replication. Redundant enhancers may be hard to identify without prior mapping. Human disease relevance is not addressed by this design.

## 39. How does the dosage compensation complex find the X chromosome so specifically, and what happens when targeting is partial?

PubMed search: `dosage compensation MSL complex X chromosome`, genes ['msl-2'], 15 papers

### Sonnet 4.6

**Hypothesis:** The MOF-mediated acetylation cycle of MSL-3 (acetylation by MOF, deacetylation by RPD3) drives stepwise spreading of the MSL complex from MRE nucleation sites to active X-linked genes in a temporally ordered manner; specifically, expression of an acetylation-mimetic MSL-3 (K→Q substitution at the MOF target lysine) will increase MSL complex spreading to ectopic autosomal loci by ≥2-fold relative to wild-type MSL-3, while an acetylation-dead MSL-3 (K→R substitution) will reduce spreading to distal X-linked genes (>50 kb from the nearest MRE) by ≥50% compared to wild-type controls, as measured by ChIP-seq occupancy of MSL-2 and H4K16ac in adult male w1118 flies at 25°C under standard conditions.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Spreading versus nucleation — Determine whether MSL-3 acetylation state differentially affects MSL complex occupancy at MRE-proximal nucleation sites versus MRE-distal spreading targets across the X chromosome by ChIP-seq in males expressing acetylation-mimetic (K→Q) or acetylation-dead (K→R) MSL-3 in an msl-3 null background
- Aim 2: roX RNA binding in vivo — Test whether MSL-3 acetylation state controls roX1 and roX2 RNA association in vivo using RIP-qPCR from adult male flies expressing MSL-3[WT], MSL-3[K→Q], or MSL-3[K→R], quantifying roX co-immunoprecipitation efficiency for each variant
- Aim 3: Functional compensation output — Assess whether perturbation of MSL-3 acetylation cycling (via K→Q, K→R transgenes, or rpd3 RNAi) causes proportional changes in X-linked gene expression and male viability, using RNA-seq X:autosome ratios stratified by genomic distance from MREs and eclosion rate assays

**Approach:** We will use the GAL4/UAS system to express UAS-msl-3[WT], UAS-msl-3[K→Q] (acetylation-mimetic), and UAS-msl-3[K→R] (acetylation-dead) transgenes in an msl-3 null background using the ubiquitous da-GAL4 driver, allowing controlled replacement of endogenous MSL-3 with defined acetylation-state variants. ChIP-seq with anti-MSL-2, anti-MSL-3, and anti-H4K16ac antibodies will map complex occupancy genome-wide in adult males of each genotype, comparing binding at MRE-proximal (<10 kb), intermediate (10–50 kb), and distal (>50 kb) X-linked loci, and on autosomes. roX RNA co-immunoprecipitation (RIP) assays will quantify the MSL-3 variant interaction with roX1 and roX2, directly testing whether acetylation status controls roX RNA binding as proposed. RNA-seq on the same genotypes will assess X-linked gene expression levels and dosage compensation ratios (X:autosome expression) to link spreading defects to functional compensation outcomes. Controls include w1118 males (wild-type), msl-3 null males rescued with UAS-msl-3[WT] (positive), and unrescued msl-3 null males (negative lethality control confirming null phenotype).

**Expected outcomes:** 1. ChIP-seq: In UAS-msl-3[K→Q] males, MSL-2 and H4K16ac occupancy at autosomal loci will increase ≥2-fold over wild-type, and X-linked loci >50 kb from MREs will show ≥1.5-fold increased occupancy; in UAS-msl-3[K→R] males, MSL-2 occupancy at MRE-distal X loci will decrease ≥50% relative to UAS-msl-3[WT] rescue while MRE-proximal occupancy is maintained (≤20% reduction), demonstrating that acetylation is specifically required for spreading rather than nucleation. 2. RIP assay: MSL-3[K→Q] will co-immunoprecipitate ≥2-fold more roX1/roX2 RNA than MSL-3[K→R], confirming that acetylation status controls roX RNA binding in vivo. 3. RNA-seq: UAS-msl-3[K→R] males will show a reduced X:autosome expression ratio (falling from the expected ~1.0 in males toward ~0.5) specifically for genes >50 kb from MREs (predicted ≥30% reduction in compensation index at distal loci), with statistical significance at FDR < 0.05. 4. rpd3 RNAi males will phenocopy UAS-msl-3[K→Q] spreading patterns, providing independent validation. 5. Viability assays: UAS-msl-3[K→R] males in an msl-3 null background will show reduced eclosion rates relative to UAS-msl-3[WT] rescue (predicted ≥50% reduction), reflecting incomplete dosage compensation.

**Novelty:** This experiment directly addresses the identified gap that 'the interplay between MSL-3 acetylation status (controlled by MOF and RPD3) and roX RNA binding as a dynamic regulatory mechanism for spreading has been proposed but not tested in vivo with temporal resolution' and that 'whether acetylation cycles drive stepwise spreading or merely modulate complex stability remains an open question.' Prior work established that MOF acetylates MSL-3, that RPD3 can deacetylate it, and that acetylation status correlates with roX2 binding in vitro, but no study has used separation-of-function acetylation-mimetic and acetylation-dead MSL-3 transgenes to dissect the spatial consequence of acetylation state on genome-wide MSL spreading versus MRE nucleation in vivo. Additionally, coupling these transgenes to genome-wide ChIP-seq allows quantitative mapping of the boundary between nucleation-dependent and spreading-dependent occupancy, which has not previously been tested functionally.

**Feasibility:** All molecular biology tools (site-directed mutagenesis, pUAST-attB injection, ChIP-seq, RIP, RNA-seq) are standard in Drosophila chromatin biology labs. The main technical risk is that the precise lysine targeted by MOF in MSL-3 must be confirmed from published biochemical data before mutagenesis; the literature confirms a single lysine residue adjacent to one of the MSL-3 chromodomains is targeted, but the specific residue number should be verified from PMID 12769850 before transgene construction. A second risk is that ubiquitous expression of constitutively acetylated MSL-3 may cause dominant lethality if ectopic autosomal spreading is severe; this can be mitigated by using temperature-sensitive GAL4 systems or a weaker tissue-specific driver (e.g., arm-GAL4) as an alternative. Estimated timeline: transgene design and generation (months 1–4), fly line validation and crossing scheme establishment (months 5–7), ChIP-seq and RNA-seq experiments with biological triplicates (months 8–12), data analysis and viability assays (months 13–15); total approximately 15 months. Lethality of msl-3 null males requires balancer chromosome maintenance and careful genotyping of experimental cohorts.

### Sonnet 5.5

**Hypothesis:** Proposed: Reducing MSL2 to about 50% of wild-type level in male third-instar larvae will cause non-uniform loss of MSL3 occupancy on the X. Occupancy at high-affinity MRE-rich entry sites will fall by ≤15%, and occupancy at weakly bound, MRE-poor active genes will fall by ≥40%. The X-to-autosome expression ratio of active genes that lose occupancy will fall by ≥10%. X-linked genes in repressive chromatin domains will stay within 5% of control expression.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Graded MSL2 loss in males — measure how X-wide MSL3 and H4K16ac occupancy decline at entry sites versus weak spreading-dependent sites at 75%, 50% and 25% MSL2
- Aim 2: Buffering in repressive domains — test whether X-linked genes in repressive chromatin domains remain compensated while active genes with reduced occupancy lose expression under partial MSL targeting
- Aim 3: Graded ectopic MSL2 in females — define the dose-response of X occupancy, its dependence on roX dosage, and viability when MSL targeting is leaky

**Approach:** Graded msl-2 knockdown will be produced in males with a ubiquitous GAL4 driving a UAS-msl-2 RNAi construct, titrated by rearing temperature (18, 25 and 29°C). Knockdown will be calibrated by qPCR and western blot to about 75%, 50% and 25% of control MSL2 (proposed, requires verification). MSL3 (or MSL1) and H4K16ac occupancy will be profiled by ChIP-seq or CUT&Tag with an exogenous spike-in for absolute normalization. Occupancy changes will be compared between entry sites (MRE-rich), weak/spreading-dependent active genes, and genes in repressive domains defined from published Hi-C/TAD annotations. Expression will be measured by RNA-seq with ERCC spike-ins in 3 biological replicates per condition. Controls are an RNAi against an unrelated gene (e.g., mCherry) and a driver-only line, plus females as a negative control for MSL binding. As a complementary test of the rheostat model, graded UAS-msl-2 expression lacking its 3' UTR will be driven in females (proposed) to define the dose-response of X occupancy, roX dependence and viability.

**Expected outcomes:** Proposed predictions: (1) MSL2 knockdown to about 50% reduces MSL3 ChIP signal at MRE-rich entry sites by ≤15% but at weak active-gene sites by ≥40% (FDR <0.05, n=3, two-sided Wilcoxon test of site classes). (2) Median log2 X/autosome expression falls by ≥0.14 (~10%) at 50% MSL2 for genes with reduced occupancy, and by a larger amount at 25% MSL2. (3) X-linked genes in repressive domains change by <5% in expression despite low MSL occupancy. (4) In females, a threshold of ectopic MSL2 exists above which X occupancy and reduced viability appear, with a ≥20% viability decrease at the highest expression level. Falsification: uniform proportional loss across site classes, or equal expression loss in repressive-domain genes, would reject the hypothesis.

**Novelty:** The synthesis identifies that the phenotypic consequences of partial or intermediate MSL targeting are not defined, that the rheostat model for MSL abundance is only correlative, and that how non-canonical compensation in repressive domains buffers poorly MSL-occupied regions is unclear. This plan titrates MSL2 in vivo and measures occupancy and expression together, stratified by chromatin state. It directly tests the quantitative coupling between MRE/CXC nucleation and roX/acetylation-dependent spreading, which the literature lists as unresolved.

**Feasibility:** Estimated timeline is 12-15 months: 2 months for line validation and knockdown calibration, 5 months for ChIP/CUT&Tag and RNA-seq in males, 3 months for the female titration and viability work, and 3 months for analysis. Pitfalls: RNAi knockdown may not be graded smoothly, temperature itself alters expression (mitigated by matched-temperature controls), strong knockdown may be lethal in males, and whole-larva tissue mixing may blur tissue-specific rheostat effects. Ectopic msl-2 transgene availability is unverified, and the chromatin-state classification depends on published TAD maps being applicable to the stage used.

## 40. What does cell competition actually measure as cell fitness, and how do loser cells sense they are losing?

PubMed search: `cell competition loser cell elimination`, genes none, 6 papers

### Sonnet 4.6

**Hypothesis:** In Drosophila melanogaster wing imaginal disc epithelial cell competition, Xrp1-mediated eIF2α phosphorylation and NF-κB/Relish activation represent two parallel, non-redundant loser-sensing axes that converge to trigger apoptotic elimination: knocking out both Xrp1 and Rel simultaneously in Minute heterozygous (M/+) loser clones will rescue loser-cell survival by ≥60% compared to single knockouts (each of which rescues ≤35%), as measured by clone area ratio (loser/winner) in third-instar wing discs at 25°C, with statistical significance at p < 0.01 (ANOVA with post-hoc Tukey correction, n ≥ 15 discs per genotype).

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Pathway Independence Assessment — Determine whether Xrp1-RNAi and Rel-RNAi each independently and partially rescue Minute loser-clone survival in wing imaginal discs, establishing individual contributions of each axis to loser elimination
- Aim 2: Convergence Epistasis Test — Quantify whether simultaneous double knockdown of Xrp1 and Rel in Minute loser clones produces rescue of clone survival greater than additive single-knockdown effects, distinguishing parallel-convergent from fully redundant pathway architectures
- Aim 3: Apoptotic Commitment Threshold — Define the relationship between phospho-eIF2α levels, Rel nuclear localization, and Dcp-1-marked apoptotic commitment within individual loser clones across genotypes, to determine whether the two axes must both be active to cross the apoptotic elimination threshold

**Approach:** We will use the MARCM (Mosaic Analysis with a Repressible Cell Marker) system combined with the GAL4/UAS system to generate GFP-marked clones homozygous for a Minute mutation (RpS17, a ribosomal protein gene haploinsufficiency that generates canonical losers) in the wing imaginal disc, while simultaneously depleting Xrp1, Rel, or both via UAS-RNAi transgenes driven by act-GAL4 within the clone. Clone induction will be performed by heat-shock FLP recombinase at 48–72 h AEL, and discs will be dissected from wandering third-instar larvae (~120 h AEL). Primary readouts will be: (1) clone area ratio (GFP+ loser clone area / GFP− winner area) by confocal microscopy and ImageJ quantification; (2) apoptotic index within loser clones by anti-cleaved Dcp-1 immunostaining; (3) phospho-eIF2α (Ser51) levels by immunofluorescence; and (4) Rel nuclear translocation as a proxy for NF-κB activation. Controls will include: wild-type clones (no Minute), single-knockdown clones (Xrp1-RNAi only; Rel-RNAi only), and RNAi-only clones without the Minute background. Epistasis will be assessed by comparing the double-knockdown rescue magnitude to expected additive rescue under independence.

**Expected outcomes:** If the hypothesis is correct: (1) Single Xrp1-RNAi in Minute clones will rescue clone area ratio by 20–35% relative to Minute alone (p < 0.05), and reduce phospho-eIF2α immunostaining intensity by ≥50% within clones; (2) Single Rel-RNAi in Minute clones will rescue clone area ratio by 20–35% (p < 0.05) and reduce Dcp-1+ apoptotic cells by ≥40%; (3) Double Xrp1-RNAi + Rel-RNAi in Minute clones will rescue clone area ratio by ≥60% (p < 0.01) and reduce Dcp-1+ apoptotic index by ≥70%, exceeding the additive expectation of two independent partial rescues, indicating convergence on the same elimination program. If the two pathways are fully redundant (alternative outcome), double knockdown rescue will equal the sum of single rescues (~60–70%), with no synergistic effect. If they are fully independent of apoptosis, neither rescue will affect Dcp-1 staining. Statistical analysis: one-way ANOVA with Tukey post-hoc correction; minimum n = 15 wing discs per genotype; experiments repeated ≥3 independent biological replicates.

**Novelty:** The identified gap states that 'the relationship between the NF-κB/Toll pathway (identified in testis stem cell competition) and the eIF2α/Xrp1 translational stress pathway (identified in epithelial competition) has not been established — it is unknown whether these are parallel, convergent, or context-specific mechanisms for loser sensing.' This experiment directly addresses that gap by: (1) testing both pathways simultaneously in the same tissue (wing imaginal disc epithelium) using the same canonical loser model (Minute), which has not previously been done; (2) providing the first quantitative epistasis analysis between these two molecular axes; and (3) defining whether convergence or independence determines the apoptotic threshold for loser elimination, addressing the further gap that 'the threshold at which reduced protein synthesis transitions a cell from stressed-but-viable to committed loser has not been quantitatively defined.'

**Feasibility:** MARCM-based epithelial competition experiments are well-established in the Drosophila field, and the Minute/+ loser system is a gold-standard model. RNAi knockdown efficiency in imaginal disc clones using act-GAL4 within MARCM is routinely achievable. Key potential pitfalls include: (1) incomplete knockdown by single RNAi lines, which will be mitigated by validating knockdown efficiency via immunostaining and/or RT-qPCR on sorted GFP+ cells; (2) the Rel-RNAi phenotype may be subtle in epithelial (non-stem-cell) tissue if Rel plays a minor role outside the testis niche, which would itself be an informative result; (3) generating the double-UAS recombinant may require 2–3 months of fly genetics. Antibodies for phospho-eIF2α (Ser51; Cell Signaling Technology) and cleaved Dcp-1 (Cell Signaling Technology) are commercially available and validated in Drosophila. Estimated timeline: 3 months for line generation and validation, 4 months for main experiments, 2 months for analysis and repetition — total approximately 9 months. No specialized equipment beyond standard confocal microscopy is required.

### Sonnet 5.5

**Hypothesis:** Proposed: In the adult Drosophila testis, clonal activation of Rel in germline stem cells (GSCs) lowers the clone's relative protein synthesis (OPP incorporation) by at least 25% compared with neighbouring wild-type GSCs at 7 days after clone induction. This reduction depends on Xrp1 and on the presence of wild-type neighbours. Xrp1 knockdown in Rel-activated clones is predicted to at least double their GSC clone retention at 14 days after clone induction relative to Rel-activated clones with control RNAi. Activating Rel in all germline cells at the same level is predicted to reduce OPP signal by less than 10% relative to controls (p<0.05 thresholds, 25°C).

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Translation state of Rel-activated clones — measure OPP, p-eIF2α and Xrp1 reporter in Rel-activated versus neutral GSC clones relative to neighbouring GSCs
- Aim 2: Xrp1 epistasis — test whether Xrp1 knockdown rescues translation and niche retention of Rel-activated clones at 2, 7 and 14 days after clone induction
- Aim 3: Neighbour dependence — compare mosaic Rel activation with germline-wide Rel activation to test whether the translation and Xrp1 responses require wild-type neighbours

**Approach:** Use FLP-out (hs-flp) clonal induction in adult males to create marked GSC clones that express a constitutively active Rel, with or without Xrp1 RNAi, and compare them with neutral marked clones. Measure relative translation by ex vivo OPP incorporation and anti-phospho-eIF2α staining, and measure Xrp1 induction with an Xrp1 reporter, comparing clone to adjacent GSCs within the same niche. Measure clone retention at the niche (hub-contacting GSC clones per testis) at 2, 7 and 14 days after clone induction. To test neighbour dependence, use a temporally controlled germline-wide driver (nos-GAL4 with tub-GAL80ts) to activate Rel in all germline cells and quantify OPP, p-eIF2α and Xrp1 reporter, then compare with the mosaic condition. Controls are neutral FLP-out clones, a non-targeting or mCherry RNAi, a Toll/Rel loss-of-function rescue check, and a GADD34-type eIF2α-phosphatase epistasis arm if a suitable line is verified.

**Expected outcomes:** Proposed predictions: (1) Rel-activated GSC clones show at least 25% lower OPP signal than neighbouring GSCs, higher p-eIF2α and higher Xrp1 reporter signal, with n of at least 60 niches per genotype and p<0.05 by Mann-Whitney test. (2) Xrp1 RNAi restores OPP to within 10% of neighbours and at least doubles clone retention at 14 days after clone induction, compared with control RNAi. (3) Germline-wide Rel activation causes a smaller (<10%) OPP reduction than the mosaic condition, which would support relative sensing. Falsification: if OPP is unchanged in Rel clones, or Xrp1 knockdown does not alter retention, Rel and Xrp1 act in parallel rather than convergently. If germline-wide Rel activation reduces OPP as strongly as mosaic activation, the effect is cell-autonomous stress rather than relative sensing.

**Novelty:** No supplied paper defines a single measurable fitness variable, and the relationship between Rel/Toll-driven loser elimination in the testis and Xrp1/eIF2α-driven protein synthesis reduction is unknown. This plan tests directly whether Rel activation reduces translation through Xrp1, and whether this depends on wild-type neighbours, which addresses two gaps in the synthesis: convergence of loser pathways and how relative difference is sensed. It also asks whether Rel displaces neutral cells by lowering a fitness-like state (translation) rather than by altering niche adhesion directly.

**Feasibility:** Estimated timeline is 10-14 months: 2-3 months for line verification, construction and validation of Xrp1 RNAi and reporters; 4-5 months for mosaic OPP, p-eIF2α and retention time courses; 3-4 months for germline-wide and epistasis experiments and for analysis. Pitfalls: OPP incorporation in small GSCs gives noisy signal, so normalising each clone to adjacent GSCs in the same niche is needed. Xrp1 RNAi may have off-target effects, so a second independent RNAi or mutant allele should be used. Rel activation may also change niche adhesion independently of translation, so a retention effect alone cannot distinguish the two. Heat-shock clone induction may affect germline health, so no-heat-shock controls are included. The testis-specific role of Xrp1 is not established in the supplied literature and may be absent, which would itself be informative.

## 41. How is apical-basal polarity maintained during rapid proliferation in growing epithelia?

PubMed search: `apical basal polarity epithelial proliferation`, genes ['scrib', 'dlg1'], 15 papers

### Sonnet 4.6

**Hypothesis:** During rapid proliferation in the Drosophila larval wing disc epithelium, the Scrib/Dlg basolateral module is re-recruited to daughter cell membranes within ≤15 minutes of cytokinesis completion, and acute, division-synchronized loss of Dlg function (via auxin-inducible degron) will cause ≥30% of post-mitotic cell pairs to display mislocalized Bazooka (apical marker) or fragmented adherens junctions within 20 minutes of mitotic exit, measurable by live imaging in w1118 background under 25°C standard conditions, without triggering Hippo/JNK-dependent cell competition elimination within the 60-minute observation window.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Temporal mapping — Determine the kinetics and sequential order of Dlg (basolateral) and Baz (apical) re-establishment on daughter cell membranes following cytokinesis in wing disc live imaging using endogenously tagged alleles
- Aim 2: Acute perturbation — Test whether auxin-induced Dlg degradation specifically during the cytokinesis window causes quantifiable apical polarity errors in daughter cells, and whether these errors are sufficient to activate Hippo/JNK cell competition signaling within 60 minutes
- Aim 3: Error accumulation — Determine whether repeated, successive cell divisions under sustained Rho-actin perturbation (RhoGEF2 overexpression) cause progressive, cumulative polarity errors that eventually cross a threshold for JNK-dependent cell competition elimination

**Approach:** We will use a dual-strategy approach combining live confocal imaging of endogenously tagged polarity proteins with acute, temporally controlled protein degradation. First, CRISPR-knock-in lines will be used to tag endogenous Dlg with AID (auxin-inducible degron) and mNeonGreen, and endogenous Baz with mCherry, in the same fly line, allowing simultaneous visualization of basolateral and apical markers in real time. Larval wing disc explants will be imaged by spinning-disc confocal microscopy; divisions will be identified by chromatin morphology (using H2B-iRFP), and the temporal sequence of Dlg re-recruitment and Baz re-establishment on daughter cell membranes will be quantified per division event. Acute Dlg degradation will be induced by adding auxin (500 µM indole-3-acetic acid) to the culture medium at defined times relative to cytokinesis (identified from live imaging), and polarity reassembly failures will be scored as mislocalization of Baz beyond a 2-µm apical zone or discontinuous E-cadherin (Arm) signal at daughter-cell junctions. Controls will include: (1) no-auxin vehicle controls, (2) UAS-GFP::dlg rescue in the AID background driven by nub-GAL4 to confirm specificity, and (3) fixed endpoint immunostaining for activated JNK (anti-pJNK) and Hippo pathway output (anti-pYki/Yorkie) to confirm absence of competition signaling during the acute window. A secondary aim will use UAS-RhoGEF2 (overexpression to perturb Rho-actin signaling) driven by act-GAL4 with a temperature-sensitive GAL80 (tub-GAL80ts) to transiently elevate RhoGEF2 during proliferative phases and score polarity error accumulation across 3-5 successive divisions by tracking clonal descendants.

**Expected outcomes:** Aim 1: Live imaging will reveal that endogenous Dlg::AID-mNeonGreen is re-recruited to nascent daughter cell lateral membranes within 10–20 minutes of cytokinesis (anaphase onset to membrane abscission), and Baz::mCherry apical re-restriction will lag by 5–10 minutes, establishing the sequential order of polarity re-establishment. N ≥ 50 division events from ≥10 discs. Aim 2: Auxin-induced Dlg degradation within a 20-minute window around cytokinesis will result in ≥30% of daughter cell pairs displaying Baz mislocalization (lateral or unpolarized signal) and/or discontinuous Arm::GFP at nascent junctions, compared to <5% in vehicle-only controls (chi-square test, p < 0.01). No significant increase in pJNK or pYki signal in affected cells within 60 minutes post-cytokinesis (two-sample t-test, p > 0.05), indicating that cell competition surveillance is not rapidly activated by transient division-associated polarity errors. Aim 3: Three to five successive divisions under RhoGEF2 overexpression will cause progressive accumulation of polarity errors (>50% of 4th-generation daughter cells with detectable Baz mislocalization vs. <10% in controls), and this will correlate with delayed but eventually activated JNK signaling (elevated pJNK by 120 minutes), suggesting that polarity error accumulation — rather than single-division errors — triggers competition surveillance.

**Novelty:** This experimental plan directly addresses the primary gap identified in the literature synthesis: 'the molecular sequence linking mitotic exit to polarity complex reassembly is not defined.' No published study has used temporally controlled, division-synchronized acute degradation of an endogenous polarity component to dissect the kinetics and sequence of polarity re-establishment on daughter cells in real time in the wing disc. Additionally, the plan tests the unresolved question of whether the Ptp10D/Hippo/JNK cell competition surveillance mechanism — shown to eliminate clonal polarity-deficient cells — is activated by transient, single-division polarity asymmetries in otherwise wild-type proliferating epithelium, a gap explicitly identified in the synthesis. Finally, the successive-division RhoGEF2 experiment addresses the gap regarding whether polarity errors accumulate across rapid successive divisions before triggering tissue-scale surveillance.

**Feasibility:** The major technical challenge is generating the AID-tagged endogenous dlg CRISPR knock-in line; this is feasible with established Drosophila CRISPR protocols (6–9 months including validation). The AID system has been demonstrated in Drosophila and the degron approach is technically established, though OsTIR1 expression levels and degradation efficiency in wing disc tissue require empirical optimization. Wing disc live imaging is technically demanding but routinely performed in multiple labs with established ex vivo culture protocols. The spinning-disc confocal acquisition rate (1 frame per 2 minutes) may miss very rapid polarity events; higher-frequency imaging (30-second intervals) should be tested. Total estimated timeline: 24–30 months. Potential pitfalls: (1) AID degron may be leaky or incomplete, requiring titration of auxin concentration; (2) CRISPR knock-in may affect Dlg function — homozygous viability of the tagged line must be confirmed before experiments; (3) wing disc explant culture conditions (duration, temperature) must be standardized to prevent culture-induced artifacts in polarity; (4) if degradation is too slow, division events may complete before sufficient Dlg depletion — inducible temperature-shift or optogenetic degradation systems may be needed as alternatives.

### Sonnet 5.5

**Hypothesis:** Proposed (not established): In the larval wing disc pouch, acute (24 h) tub-GAL80ts-controlled scrib knockdown will raise the fraction of cytokinesis events with delayed apical E-cadherin junction re-establishment (>20 min after completion of furrow ingression) from an assumed control level of ≤10% to ≥30%, and this defect will precede detectable basolateral aPKC mislocalization in non-dividing cells; CycE overexpression will increase the frequency of delayed junction re-establishment by ≥1.5-fold relative to scrib knockdown alone (25-29°C, mixed-sex wandering third-instar larvae, n≥150 divisions from ≥8 discs per genotype).

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Junction re-assembly kinetics — quantify timing of E-cadherin and Crb restoration after cytokinesis in control versus acute scrib-depleted wing pouch cells using live imaging.
- Aim 2: Proliferative load dependence — test whether CycE-driven increase in division rate enhances junction re-assembly delays and basolateral aPKC mislocalization in scrib-depleted tissue, with apoptosis and JNK activity monitored.
