- Aim 3: Compensation by integrin adhesion — test whether knockdown of an integrin subunit in scrib-depleted pouch cells worsens re-assembly delays, probing a secondary polarity pathway in proliferating epithelia.

**Approach:** Background premise: Scrib and Dlg direct initial assembly and positioning of adherens junctions during polarity establishment, which suggests that they may also be needed to re-assemble junctions after each division. Proposed approach: use nub-GAL4 with tub-GAL80ts to drive scrib RNAi in the wing pouch in a temporally restricted way (shift to the restrictive temperature for 24 h before imaging). Live ex vivo disc imaging with endogenously tagged E-cadherin and Crb reporters will score the timing of junction re-establishment, apical Crb restoration and aPKC localization for each cytokinesis event. Division rate will be raised by co-expressing UAS-CycE. Controls are a non-targeting RNAi (e.g., UAS-mCherry RNAi or UAS-lacZ), a second independent scrib RNAi or a scrib allele to exclude off-target effects, GAL80ts-permissive (18°C) discs, and fixed-tissue staining for Dlg, aPKC and cleaved Dcp-1 to separate polarity defects from apoptosis and cell competition. Both the scrib RNAi/CycE and non-dividing-cell comparisons will be analyzed with blinded scoring and mixed-effects statistics (disc as random factor, p<0.05).

**Expected outcomes:** Predicted (proposed): control discs restore E-cadherin junctions within ~20 min in ≥90% of divisions. Acute scrib knockdown is predicted to increase delayed re-establishment to ≥30% of divisions, with transient mislocalization of Crb or aPKC in daughter cells, while neighboring non-dividing cells keep normal apical-basal polarity at 24 h. Adding CycE overexpression is predicted to raise the frequency of delayed re-establishment ≥1.5-fold above scrib RNAi alone and to increase the fraction of discs showing basolateral aPKC at 48 h. If delayed re-establishment stays ≤15% in scrib knockdown and CycE has no effect (two-sided p>0.05, power 0.8 for the stated effect), the hypothesis is rejected. Integrin knockdown in the scrib background is predicted to increase delays further if integrin-based adhesion compensates.

**Novelty:** The synthesis identifies this gap: No supplied paper directly measures polarity protein turnover, trafficking or localization dynamics across successive rapid cell divisions in growing imaginal discs. It also notes that coupling of Scrib-dependent spindle orientation and tricellular junction formation to junction remodeling after division is undefined. The proposal tests this at single-division resolution with acute, stage-restricted loss of function, which also helps separate polarity initiation from maintenance (the dlg-1 discs of Abbott et al. initially retained polarity yet overgrew, while later work places Scrib/Dlg in polarity initiation).

**Feasibility:** Estimated timeline 12-15 months: 3 months for line construction, verification and RNAi validation; 4-5 months for live imaging and quantification (Aim 1); 3 months for CycE experiments (Aim 2); 3 months for integrin compensation tests (Aim 3). Pitfalls: ex vivo live imaging of wing discs is limited in duration and can perturb division; RNAi efficiency at 29°C must be verified; scrib loss can trigger JNK-mediated apoptosis or competition that removes defective cells and biases the surviving population (mitigate with p35 or Dcp-1 monitoring); reporter lines may not exist in required combinations and need verification; the control and effect-size values are assumptions that need pilot data.

## 42. What decides whether a stressed cell dies or triggers compensatory proliferation in its neighbors?

PubMed search: `apoptosis induced compensatory proliferation`, genes none, 11 papers

### Sonnet 4.6

**Hypothesis:** Myo1D acts as a molecular rheostat that maintains Dronc activity in DARE cells below the effector caspase activation threshold by physically limiting Dark-independent apoptosome assembly; genetic reduction of Myo1D by 50% (one copy removed in a Myo1D heterozygous null background) in irradiated w1118 third-instar wing imaginal discs will convert ≥30% of DARE cells (identified by sub-lethal Dronc reporter activity) into committed apoptotic cells (TUNEL-positive, effector caspase reporter-positive) within 24 hours post-irradiation (40 Gy), and will simultaneously reduce compensatory BrdU incorporation in neighboring cells by ≥40% compared to irradiated wild-type controls, under 25°C standard laboratory conditions.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Quantify Myo1D dosage threshold — determine the minimum reduction in Myo1D levels (heterozygous null, 25% RNAi knockdown, 50% RNAi knockdown, full knockdown) that converts DARE cells from effector-caspase-low to effector-caspase-high in irradiated wing discs, establishing a dose-response curve for the Myo1D caspase-buffering rheostat.
- Aim 2: Measure AiP output — quantify BrdU and pH3-positive neighboring-cell proliferation at each Myo1D dosage level from Aim 1 to determine whether DARE-to-apoptosis conversion directly and proportionally reduces compensatory proliferation in adjacent cells, establishing a causal link between Myo1D-gated DARE survival and AiP.
- Aim 3: Epistasis and rescue — test whether co-expression of p35 (effector caspase block) rescues AiP in Myo1D-reduced discs and whether re-expression of UAS-Myo1D in the null heterozygous background fully restores both DARE survival and neighboring BrdU incorporation, confirming that Myo1D acts upstream of effector caspase activation to gate the death-vs-proliferation decision.

**Approach:** We will use the GAL4/UAS system to manipulate Myo1D levels with tissue-specific precision in wing imaginal discs, combining apoptosis reporters (UAS-Apoliner or SCAT3 FRET-based effector caspase sensor), a Dronc activity reporter (UAS-GFP-Dronc cleavage sensor), and proliferation markers (anti-BrdU, anti-pH3). Wing discs from wandering third-instar larvae will be irradiated at 40 Gy using an X-ray source, then cultured ex vivo for 6, 12, and 24 hours before fixation and confocal imaging. Controls will include unirradiated discs, irradiated GAL4-only and UAS-only sibling controls, and a positive control in which full Myo1D knockdown via RNAi is expected to abolish DARE survival entirely. To distinguish DARE from NARE cells, we will co-stain for active Dronc (using antibody to cleaved Dronc or the GFP reporter) alongside effector caspase activity (SCAT3 or anti-cleaved DrICE); DARE cells are defined as Dronc-active/effector-caspase-low and NARE as both-negative. Quantitative confocal image analysis will score the fraction of Dronc-reporter-positive cells that cross into effector caspase positivity and the BrdU incorporation index in immediately adjacent (non-reporter-positive) cells across ≥20 discs per genotype per timepoint.

**Expected outcomes:** Primary outcome: In irradiated wing discs with 50% Myo1D dosage reduction (Myo1D[1]/+ or ap-GAL4>UAS-Myo1D-RNAi at 25°C), ≥30% of Dronc-reporter-positive (DARE) cells will become doubly positive for effector caspase reporter (SCAT3 FRET ratio shift) and TUNEL at 24 hours post-40 Gy irradiation, compared to <5% conversion in irradiated wild-type controls (Fisher's exact test, p<0.01, n≥20 discs/genotype). Secondary outcome: BrdU incorporation index in cells immediately adjacent to former DARE positions will decrease by ≥40% in Myo1D-reduced discs versus wild-type irradiated controls (Student's t-test, p<0.05). Epistasis prediction: Co-expression of p35 (UAS-p35) in the Myo1D-reduced background should block DARE-to-apoptosis conversion and partially rescue neighboring-cell BrdU incorporation, confirming that effector caspase activation is the downstream mediator. Rescue prediction: Reintroduction of UAS-Myo1D in the heterozygous null background should restore DARE survival (effector caspase conversion rate returning to <10%) and restore BrdU index to within 20% of wild-type irradiated levels. Negative control: Unirradiated discs of all genotypes should show <2% Dronc-reporter-positive cells and no increase in TUNEL, confirming irradiation-dependence of DARE induction.

**Novelty:** The literature identifies Myo1D as necessary for DARE survival by preventing effector caspase activation but explicitly states that 'how Myo1D senses or limits caspase activity at the mechanistic level is unresolved' (identified gap 2, PMID 41345088). No prior study has experimentally titrated Myo1D levels to define a quantitative threshold below which DARE cells convert to committed apoptosis, nor has any study measured the direct consequence of this conversion on neighboring-cell compensatory proliferation in the same disc. Furthermore, while the DARE/NARE distinction is established, the molecular logic by which a single gene (Myo1D) gates the binary death-vs-AiP decision in a dose-dependent manner has not been tested. This directly addresses the overarching gap: 'the precise molecular switch that determines whether Dronc activity in a stressed cell tips toward committed apoptosis versus AiP signaling without cell death remains unknown' (identified gap 1, PMIDs 41345088, 28688927, 18774295).

**Feasibility:** All core genetic tools (irradiation, wing disc culture, BrdU labeling, TUNEL, confocal imaging) are standard in Drosophila developmental biology laboratories. The main technical risk is the availability of validated Dronc activity reporters and SCAT3 lines; if not available from stock centers, SCAT3 has been published and can be requested from originating labs (~2-month delay). The Myo1D null allele is referenced in PMID 41345088 but its BDSC stock number is not confirmed in the literature provided and must be verified or requested from the Braun/Bergmann labs. Ex vivo disc culture after 40 Gy irradiation is established but requires optimization of culture media and timing (potential 1-month optimization phase). Estimated total timeline: 14-18 months (2 months line acquisition/verification, 2 months optimization of irradiation and reporter conditions, 6 months primary experimental replication across three independent biological replicates per aim, 2 months epistasis and rescue experiments, 2 months data analysis and preparation). Key pitfalls: (1) Incomplete penetrance of Myo1D heterozygosity may require use of stronger RNAi, which could introduce off-target effects — addressed by using both approaches and requiring concordant results; (2) DARE cells are rare and may require automated image analysis pipelines for unbiased quantification; (3) ex vivo culture conditions may not fully recapitulate in vivo regeneration — larval recovery experiments at 72 hours post-irradiation will be run in parallel as an in vivo validation.

### Sonnet 5.5

**Hypothesis:** Proposed: In wandering third-instar wing imaginal discs at 25°C, undead-cell apoptosis-induced proliferation (AiP; en-GAL4-driven UAS-hid + UAS-p35 in the posterior compartment) will raise Yki activity, read out by the ex-lacZ reporter, by at least 1.5-fold in neighboring anterior cells within ~10 cell diameters of the A/P boundary, relative to p35-only controls. This increase will depend on Dronc and DUOX, because it will be reduced by at least 50% by posterior Dronc RNAi or Duox RNAi. Knockdown of yki in the anterior compartment will reduce AiP-associated anterior compartment overgrowth (anterior/total area ratio) by at least 30% without reducing Dcp-1 staining in the posterior compartment (p<0.05, n≥15 discs per genotype).

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Yki activation by undead cells — quantify Yki reporter activity and proliferation in anterior neighbors of en>hid,p35 posterior cells versus controls.
- Aim 2: Yki requirement in responding cells — test with the dual LexA/GAL4 system whether anterior yki knockdown suppresses non-autonomous overgrowth without altering posterior caspase activity.
- Aim 3: Upstream sender signals — test by epistasis whether Dronc, DUOX/ROS and JNK in the posterior sender cells are required for the Yki response.

**Approach:** Undead-cell AiP will be induced in the posterior compartment with en-GAL4 driving UAS-hid and UAS-p35, with tub-GAL80ts for temporal control. Controls are UAS-p35 alone, UAS-hid alone and UAS-GFP. Yki activity in anterior neighbors will be read out with ex-lacZ and a diap1 reporter, alongside anti-Dcp-1, anti-phospho-histone H3, and Wg and Dpp reporters. Epistasis will use UAS-RNAi against Dronc and Duox, UAS-Catalase and UAS-bsk-DN expressed in the posterior (sender) cells to test whether the Dronc-ROS/Ca2+-DUOX-JNK axis is required for the Yki response. Necessity of Yki in responding cells will be tested with a separate binary system, in which a LexA driver in the posterior compartment drives hid/p35 and ci-GAL4 drives yki RNAi in the anterior compartment. Readouts are compartment area ratios, pH3 counts and reporter intensity, with blinded scoring.

**Expected outcomes:** Proposed predictions: (1) en>hid,p35 discs will show at least 1.5-fold higher ex-lacZ intensity in anterior cells near the boundary than en>p35 controls, along with increased pH3 counts and anterior area ratio. (2) Dronc RNAi, Duox RNAi, Catalase or bsk-DN in the posterior compartment will reduce the Yki reporter induction by at least 50% and the pH3 increase. (3) Anterior yki knockdown will reduce overgrowth by at least 30% while posterior Dcp-1 staining remains unchanged. If the Yki reporter is unchanged, or yki knockdown does not affect anterior overgrowth, the hypothesis is falsified. Statistical tests are ANOVA with post-hoc correction at p<0.05.

**Novelty:** The synthesis identifies that the intersection of Hippo/Yki with caspase/JNK/ROS-driven AiP is not addressed in the provided literature and could be tested by genetic epistasis in wing discs. It also leaves open whether the Dronc-ROS-Ca2+ axis is shared across contexts. This plan tests whether Yki acts as a non-autonomous responder downstream of the Dronc-DUOX axis and whether it helps decide between death and compensatory growth in neighbors.

**Feasibility:** Estimated 10-14 months: 3 months for stock construction and validation of reporters and RNAi, 4 months for Aim 1, 3 months for Aim 2 and 3 months for Aim 3. Pitfalls: undead-cell tissues can show strong JNK-driven overgrowth and developmental delay, so staging must be controlled. ex-lacZ also responds to non-Hippo inputs, so a second Yki readout (diap1 reporter) is included. RNAi efficiency and off-target effects need validation, and the dual LexA/GAL4 system may leak and requires controls. Compartment-boundary effects could confound the A/P area ratio, so total disc size will also be measured.

## 43. How is spindle orientation coupled to fate determinant segregation in neuroblasts, and what happens when the coupling is weakened?

PubMed search: `neuroblast asymmetric division spindle orientation`, genes ['insc', 'mud'], 15 papers

### Sonnet 4.6

**Hypothesis:** Hts/Adducin-mediated liquid-liquid phase separation of the Pins/Mud complex is required for robust coupling of spindle orientation to fate determinant segregation in Drosophila larval neuroblasts; specifically, neuroblasts expressing a phase-separation-deficient Hts variant (hts-ΔLLPS) will show ≥40% spindle misorientation (>30° deviation from apical-basal axis) compared with ≤15% in controls, ≥35% symmetric co-segregation of Miranda and Prospero between daughter cells, and a ≥2-fold increase in type-II neuroblast clone size within 7 days of clone induction at 25°C in mated female larvae, while cortical Baz/aPKC polarity remains intact in ≥80% of mitotic neuroblasts.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Condensate integrity and spindle orientation — Determine whether Hts LLPS activity is required for alignment of the mitotic spindle with the apical-basal polarity axis in larval neuroblasts by comparing spindle angle distributions in hts-null, UAS-hts-ΔLLPS, and UAS-hts-WT rescue neuroblasts using fixed confocal imaging of α-tubulin/Pins/Baz in third-instar larval brain wholemounts
- Aim 2: Fate determinant segregation fidelity — Test whether loss of Hts-driven Pins/Mud condensate assembly is sufficient to cause symmetric co-segregation of Miranda and Prospero fate determinants at anaphase, quantifying segregation ratios in wor-GAL4-driven hts-ΔLLPS vs. control neuroblasts using dual-color live or fixed imaging
- Aim 3: Tumor suppression output — Assess whether weakened spindle-fate coupling caused by hts-ΔLLPS expression produces neuroblast overproliferation in vivo using MARCM clonal analysis, measuring clone cell number and Deadpan-positive neuroblast number per clone at 7 days after clone induction

**Approach:** We will generate a UAS-hts-ΔLLPS transgene encoding Hts with mutations in the predicted phase-separation-driving domain (informed by the Pins/Mud condensate data from PMID 37655159), alongside a UAS-hts-WT rescue control, and express each using the neuroblast-specific worniu-GAL4 driver (wor-GAL4) in an hts-null or strong hypomorphic background to test whether Hts phase-separation activity is required for spindle orientation coupling. Spindle orientation will be quantified by measuring the angle between the metaphase spindle axis (anti-α-tubulin) and the apical Pins/Baz crescent in fixed third-instar larval brain wholemounts using confocal microscopy and automated angle measurement (≥50 mitotic neuroblasts per genotype, ≥3 biological replicates). Fate determinant segregation will be assessed by imaging Miranda-GFP and Pros-RFP co-segregation at anaphase/telophase, scoring symmetric vs. asymmetric segregation; cortical polarity integrity will be confirmed by Baz and aPKC immunostaining. MARCM clone analysis using FLP/FRT-based clonal induction in wor-GAL4 larvae will quantify neuroblast overproliferation as clone cell number and Deadpan-positive neuroblast number per clone at 7 days after clone induction (ACI). Liquid-droplet properties of endogenous Hts/Pins/Mud complexes will be assessed in S2 cells by fluorescence recovery after photobleaching (FRAP) of GFP-Mud puncta in the presence and absence of co-expressed Hts-ΔLLPS to confirm dominant-negative or loss-of-function condensate effects before in vivo deployment.

**Expected outcomes:** Aim 1: hts-null neuroblasts or neuroblasts expressing UAS-hts-ΔLLPS via wor-GAL4 will show ≥40% of mitotic neuroblasts with spindle angles >30° from the apical-basal axis (vs. ≤15% in wor-GAL4/+; hts/+ controls and ≤15% in UAS-hts-WT rescue), with Pins and Baz apical crescents remaining intact in ≥80% of cells, confirming spindle-polarity decoupling without cortical polarity loss. Aim 2: Miranda-GFP and Pros-RFP will co-segregate symmetrically (both markers in equal or near-equal proportions to both daughter cells, defined as <1.5-fold ratio) in ≥35% of anaphase neuroblasts in the hts-ΔLLPS condition vs. ≤10% in controls; this frequency should be significantly lower than in mud[4] mutants (expected ≥60%), indicating partial rather than complete decoupling. Aim 3: MARCM clones in hts-null or hts-ΔLLPS backgrounds will contain ≥2-fold more Deadpan-positive neuroblasts and ≥2-fold more total cells per clone at 7 days ACI compared with wild-type clones (p<0.05, Mann-Whitney U test, n≥20 clones per genotype, ≥3 animals). FRAP of GFP-Mud puncta in S2 cells will show ≥2-fold higher mobile fraction in hts-ΔLLPS co-expression compared with hts-WT co-expression (t½ <30 s vs. >60 s), confirming loss of condensate stability.

**Novelty:** This experimental plan directly addresses two identified gaps in the literature synthesis: (1) It is unknown whether Hts/Adducin-mediated phase separation of the Pins/Mud complex is regulated functionally during mitosis and how liquid-like condensate properties are converted into stable cortical asymmetry sufficient for robust spindle capture [gap 2 from synthesis; PMID 37655159]. (2) The precise mechanism by which spindle misorientation leads to symmetric fate determinant segregation — specifically whether this is a passive geometric consequence of spindle misalignment or whether condensate integrity at the cortex actively tethers fate determinants — has not been resolved [gap 3 from synthesis; PMID 16740476, 16648843]. By genetically separating Hts LLPS activity from total Hts protein levels using a structure-informed ΔLLPS allele expressed in an otherwise normal polarity context, this design provides the first in vivo test of whether condensate assembly per se (rather than Hts protein presence) is the instructive molecular step coupling spindle orientation to fate determinant segregation and tumor suppression in neuroblasts.

**Feasibility:** The core genetic tools (wor-GAL4, mud and pins loss-of-function alleles, MARCM components) are established and widely available, reducing technical risk. The primary novel requirement is generation of UAS-hts-ΔLLPS transgenic flies, which requires prior biochemical domain mapping to identify the minimal LLPS-driving region of Hts in the Pins/Mud context (data partially provided by PMID 37655159 but specific residues must be confirmed); this mapping phase adds approximately 3-4 months. Overall timeline estimate: domain mapping and transgene design (months 1-4), transgenesis and validation in S2 cells (months 4-8), in vivo neuroblast characterization and fixed imaging (months 8-16), MARCM clonal analysis (months 12-18), data analysis and validation (months 18-20). Potential pitfalls include: (a) Hts-ΔLLPS mutations may affect Mud binding independently of phase separation, necessitating binding assays to decouple these activities; (b) partial redundancy with other condensate-promoting factors may buffer the in vivo phenotype, requiring double mutant or dominant-negative strategies; (c) maternal Hts deposition may complicate null analysis, favoring the conditional UAS-hts-ΔLLPS approach; (d) FRAP measurements in vivo in neuroblasts are technically challenging and may require S2 cell validation as a surrogate. The experimental design is feasible within a 20-month window for a team of 2-3 researchers.

### Sonnet 5.5

**Hypothesis:** Proposed: In third-instar larval central-brain type I neuroblasts at 25°C, graded Mud depletion by worniu-GAL4-driven RNAi will increase the fraction of metaphase spindles misaligned by more than 30 degrees from the apical Pins crescent axis in a dose-dependent manner. Symmetric Miranda partitioning at telophase will track spindle angle rather than genotype: at least 50% of neuroblasts with spindle angles above 60 degrees will partition Miranda symmetrically, versus fewer than 10% of neuroblasts with angles of 30 degrees or less. Apical Pins/aPKC crescents will remain intact in at least 90% of metaphase neuroblasts in every genotype. Hts knockdown is predicted to phenocopy a partial Mud reduction on the same angle-to-segregation curve.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Graded coupling versus spindle angle — quantify how graded Mud knockdown shifts metaphase spindle angle relative to the apical crescent while apical polarity stays intact.
- Aim 2: Angle-dependence of determinant segregation — test by live imaging and logistic regression whether telophase Miranda, Pros and Brat partitioning depends on spindle angle rather than genotype, and compare with polarity perturbations (insc or baz).
- Aim 3: Hts contribution and tissue consequences — determine whether Hts knockdown phenocopies partial Mud loss on the same angle-to-segregation curve, and measure resulting neuroblast overproliferation.

**Approach:** Use worniu-GAL4 with tub-GAL80ts for neuroblast-specific, temporally controlled knockdown. A series of Mud RNAi lines or temperature and induction regimes will give graded depletion, and Hts RNAi will be tested in parallel. Live imaging of intact larval brain explants will use a Miranda reporter plus a spindle/centrosome marker (e.g., a tubulin or Jupiter reporter) and an apical polarity marker (Pins or aPKC). From these movies, per-cell metaphase spindle angle relative to the apical crescent axis, apical crescent integrity, telophase Miranda partition ratio, and daughter-cell size ratio will be measured. Controls are driver-only and non-targeting RNAi (e.g., mCherry or white RNAi) flies, plus a polarity-perturbation comparison (e.g., insc or baz knockdown) to distinguish orientation-dependent from polarity-dependent segregation failures. The per-cell relationship between spindle angle and segregation outcome will then be compared across genotypes by logistic regression with genotype as a covariate. Fixed-sample analysis of Mira, Pros and Brat will confirm the live-imaging results, and neuroblast counts will be used to assess overproliferation.

**Expected outcomes:** Proposed predictions, not established results. (1) Mud knockdown will produce a dose-dependent rise in the fraction of neuroblasts with spindle misalignment above 30 degrees, from under 15% in controls to 40-70% in strong knockdown. (2) Apical crescent integrity will stay at or above 90% in all genotypes. (3) Symmetric Miranda partitioning will be tightly coupled to spindle angle: at least 50% symmetric partitioning above 60 degrees versus under 10% at or below 30 degrees, with no significant genotype effect after controlling for angle (likelihood-ratio test, p > 0.05). (4) Hts knockdown will give a milder shift in the angle distribution that falls on the same curve. (5) Insc or Baz perturbation will break this relationship, giving symmetric partitioning even at well-aligned angles. (6) Strong Mud depletion will increase neuroblast numbers per central-brain lobe by at least 20% over controls. Sample sizes of at least 40 neuroblasts per genotype from at least 8 brains will be used. Falsification: if partition outcome depends significantly on genotype at matched angles, or if angle-misaligned neuroblasts still segregate Miranda asymmetrically at more than 30% frequency, the spindle-orientation-dependent model is rejected.

**Novelty:** The supplied literature shows that in mud mutants cortical polarity is normal but spindles misalign, and that loss of Mud can cause symmetric Brat/Prospero segregation and tumor-like overproliferation. However, it does not quantify how much determinant segregation depends on spindle orientation versus polarity-based cortical localization. The penetrance of overproliferation after weakened coupling is also not quantified. This design tests graded weakening with per-cell live imaging, which converts a qualitative mutant phenotype into a quantitative angle-to-segregation relationship. It also includes a direct comparison with Hts, whose role in Pins/Mud phase separation has only been shown biochemically and in spindle orientation assays.

**Feasibility:** Proposed timeline of about 12-14 months. Months 1-3 cover line construction, verification of RNAi lines and knockdown validation by immunostaining. Months 4-8 cover live imaging and angle/segregation quantification. Months 9-11 cover Hts and Insc/Baz comparisons and fixed Mira/Pros/Brat analysis. Months 12-14 cover neuroblast-number analysis and statistics. Pitfalls include RNAi off-target effects (use at least two independent lines), incomplete knockdown that is difficult to grade (validate by Mud immunostaining intensity per cell), phototoxicity in long live-imaging sessions, neuroblast drift or rotation during imaging, and the difficulty of measuring 3D spindle angle. Stock numbers and the availability of reporters have not been verified and must be checked before starting.

## 44. How do epithelial cells sense crowding and choose which cell is extruded?

PubMed search: `epithelial cell extrusion crowding`, genes none, 2 papers

### Sonnet 4.6

**Hypothesis:** In the Drosophila pupal notum epithelium at 25°C, the junctional tension sensor Jub (ajuba LIM protein) acts downstream of apical area reduction to transduce both global (tissue-scale) and local (neighbor-relative) geometric crowding signals into Hippo pathway activation (Wts phosphorylation) and Notch ligand asymmetry (Dl enrichment in larger neighbors), such that RNAi-mediated knockdown of jub using e22c-GAL4 will reduce the fraction of geometrically crowded cells (apical area ≤40% of tissue median AND ≤60% of mean neighbor area) that commit to apoptosis within 4 hours by ≥50% compared to controls, and will disrupt the correlation between apical area rank and apoptotic probability (Spearman ρ reduced from ≥0.6 to ≤0.2, p<0.05), without globally disrupting epithelial architecture as assessed by E-cadherin continuity.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Jub as mechanosensor — test whether jub knockdown in the pupal notum epithelium disrupts pWts accumulation and Yki nuclear exclusion specifically in geometrically crowded cells (global apical area ≤40% tissue median), assessed by quantitative immunofluorescence in fixed nota at 24h APF
- Aim 2: Geometric correlation disruption — determine whether jub knockdown abolishes the statistical correlation between apical area rank and apoptosis probability (live imaging with caspase reporter over 4-hour windows), distinguishing effects on global vs. local geometric signal by measuring Spearman ρ for global area rank and for local neighbor-relative area rank independently
- Aim 3: Pathway independence test — use orthogonal single and double RNAi knockdowns of Hippo (wts) and Notch (N, Dl) pathway components to determine whether global and local geometric signals operate through independent parallel pathways or act sequentially, quantified by whether loss of one pathway shifts the geometric threshold for the other

**Approach:** Using the Drosophila pupal notum as the primary model epithelium (which was used in Cachoux et al. 2023 [37827152]), we will employ the GAL4/UAS system with e22c-GAL4 (broad epithelial driver) to knock down candidate mechanosensors — beginning with jub (Ajuba), which is a known Hippo pathway regulator at junctions — using UAS-jub-RNAi, and subsequently test UAS-crb-RNAi and UAS-Myo-II-RNAi to probe apical actomyosin contributions. Live imaging with a caspase reporter (UAS-apoliner or endogenous Dcp-1 antibody staining) will be combined with automated cell segmentation to measure apical area of each cell relative to tissue-wide median (global signal) and relative to its immediate neighbors (local signal) at the time of apoptosis commitment. Pathway outputs will be assessed by immunostaining for phospho-Wts (pWts, Hippo output), Yki nuclear localization, and Dl membrane distribution in extruding versus non-extruding crowded cells. All experimental genotypes will be compared against two controls: (1) e22c-GAL4/+ driver-only control and (2) UAS-RNAi/+ transgene-only control, with at least n=10 nota per condition and ≥200 apoptotic events scored per replicate across three biological replicates.

**Expected outcomes:** Aim 1: If jub is required for geometry-to-Hippo transduction, jub knockdown will reduce pWts staining intensity in crowded cells (apical area ≤40% tissue median) by ≥40% relative to controls (quantified by immunofluorescence intensity, Mann-Whitney U test, p<0.05, n≥50 cells per condition). Aim 2: The correlation between a cell's apical area rank (smallest to largest) and its probability of apoptosis within a 4-hour window will decrease from Spearman ρ≥0.6 in controls to ρ≤0.2 in jub knockdown animals, indicating disrupted global geometric sensing. Aim 3: Dl asymmetry index (ratio of Dl intensity on neighbor membranes abutting the small cell vs. other interfaces) will decrease from ≥1.5-fold in controls to ≤1.1-fold upon N-RNAi or Dl-RNAi, with selective loss of local (neighbor-relative) apoptosis prediction without affecting global area-based prediction, supporting pathway independence. If jub knockdown does not reduce apoptosis frequency or pathway markers, this would falsify the hypothesis that jub is the primary mechanosensor, redirecting focus to alternative candidates (e.g., Crumbs complex components or Myo-II).

**Novelty:** The literature synthesis explicitly identifies as a gap 'precisely how apical area differences are transduced into Hippo/YAP and Notch pathway activity at the molecular level — identifying the mechanosensors (e.g., specific junctional or cytoskeletal components) that link cell geometry to caspase activation.' Cachoux et al. [37827152] established that both global and local geometric features predict apoptosis commitment via Hippo and Notch, but did not identify the upstream mechanosensory molecule(s). The synthesis also flags as unresolved 'whether the two geometric features act independently, synergistically, or in sequence.' This proposal is the first (to the extent supported by the provided literature) to test a specific candidate mechanosensor (Jub/Ajuba) as a molecular link between apical area and pathway activation, and to design orthogonal RNAi experiments that can dissociate the global (Hippo) and local (Notch/Dl) geometric sensing arms to determine their causal relationship.

**Feasibility:** The Drosophila pupal notum is an established model for live epithelial imaging and has been used in the key paper [37827152]. All proposed tools (GAL4/UAS RNAi, fluorescent junction markers, caspase reporters) are standard in the field. Automated cell segmentation software (e.g., Tissue Analyzer, ilastik) is publicly available. Estimated timeline: month 1–2 — assemble and verify fly lines and balancers; month 3–4 — optimize live imaging protocol and segmentation pipeline in control nota; month 5–8 — execute RNAi knockdown experiments and immunostaining for pWts, Yki, Dl (Aim 1 and 2); month 9–12 — genetic epistasis experiments separating global vs. local signals (Aim 3) and data analysis. Key pitfalls: (1) e22c-GAL4 may show variable expression in pupal notum — if insufficient, nub-GAL4 or A58-GAL4 will be tested as alternatives; (2) jub RNAi efficiency must be validated by qRT-PCR or Western blot before phenotypic scoring; (3) temperature-sensitive effects of GAL4 activity require all crosses and experiments to be performed at a consistent 25°C; (4) the specific BDSC stock numbers listed require verification before ordering, as no stock numbers were confirmed in the provided literature.

### Sonnet 5.5

**Hypothesis:** Proposed: In the developing Drosophila pupal epithelium, a small apical area relative to neighbors (<50% of the mean neighbor area) reduces Yorkie transcriptional activity cell-autonomously, and this reduction is required for commitment to apoptotic extrusion. Specifically, clonal Yorkie gain-of-function in cells with small relative apical area will reduce the fraction that undergo caspase-positive extrusion within 12 h by at least 50% compared with control clones, and clonal Notch knockdown in the small cell itself will lower this fraction by at least 30%.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Area-Yki correlation — measure single-cell absolute and relative apical area, Yki reporter activity and caspase onset by live imaging, and test whether low Yki activity precedes apoptotic commitment in small cells.
- Aim 2: Causal test of Yorkie — use clonal Yki gain and loss of function and acute mechanical perturbation of neighbors to test whether Yki activity is necessary and sufficient to set the probability of extrusion in small-area cells.
- Aim 3: Notch role and junction remodeling — knock down Notch or ligands in the small cell versus its neighbors to distinguish comparator from permissive roles, and image junction components before and during extrusion in committed cells.

**Approach:** Use hs-FLP flip-out clones in a developing Drosophila epithelium, with the pupal notum as the proposed model (suitability requires verification). Live-image a junction marker, a Yorkie transcriptional reporter and a caspase reporter, and segment single cells to measure absolute and relative apical area, reporter intensity and the time to caspase onset and extrusion. Aim 1 correlates area with Yki activity. Aim 2 tests causality by clonal Yki gain and loss of function, and by acute mechanical perturbation (e.g., local laser ablation of neighbors to change apical area). Aim 3 uses Notch receptor and ligand manipulations in the small cell versus its neighbors, and tracks junction components during extrusion. Controls are neutral flip-out clones (e.g., GFP or lacZ only), and cells matched for apical area from non-clonal tissue. Statistics use mixed-effects logistic regression with pupa as a random effect.

**Expected outcomes:** Proposed predictions: (1) Yki reporter intensity will correlate positively with relative apical area (Spearman rho ≥0.4, p<0.01), with the lowest reporter quartile enriched at least 2-fold for later apoptosis. (2) Control small-area cells will extrude at a baseline rate to be measured, and Yki gain-of-function clones will reduce this by ≥50% (p<0.05). (3) Yki knockdown will increase apoptosis in cells with normal area by ≥2-fold. (4) Notch knockdown in the small cell will reduce commitment by ≥30%. If neighbor-side ligand knockdown has a comparable effect, this would support a comparator role for Notch. If only the cell-autonomous knockdown matters, a permissive role is more likely. Failure of Yki manipulation to change extrusion probability (<20% change with n≥150 small cells per genotype) would falsify the hypothesis.

**Novelty:** Cachoux et al. (PMID 37827152) linked apoptosis commitment to small absolute and relative apical area and showed the coupling depends on Hippo/YAP and Notch, but the literature synthesis identifies that the molecular sensor of crowding is not identified, and that how Notch mediates the relative-area coupling is unresolved. This plan tests Yorkie activity as a single-cell readout of relative area with causal clonal perturbations, and separates Notch roles in the small cell from those in its neighbors. The synthesis also notes that the junctional remodeling steps of extrusion (PMID 38205947) have not been connected to early geometric commitment, and Aim 3 addresses this by live junction imaging.

**Feasibility:** Estimated timeline is 12-18 months: 3 months to assemble and verify stocks and reporters, 4 months for Aim 1 imaging and segmentation, 4 months for Aim 2 clonal and mechanical perturbations, and 4 months for Aim 3 and analysis. Pitfalls: Yki manipulation may change growth and cell size and so confound apical area (mitigated by matching area bins and measuring clone size); reporter dynamics may lag area changes; laser ablation may cause wound responses; the apoptotic extrusion studied here may not generalize to live-cell extrusion, which the supplied literature does not address. Whether the Yki reporter has single-cell sensitivity in this tissue requires pilot validation.

## 45. How do cells coordinate organelle positioning with signaling during polarized growth?

PubMed search: `organelle positioning polarized growth signaling`, genes none, 10 papers

### Sonnet 4.6

**Hypothesis:** Golgi outpost positioning in Drosophila class IV da sensory neurons (ddaC) is causally upstream of local EGF/EGFR-dependent dendritic branching signals: specifically, acute disruption of Golgi outpost anterograde transport via neuron-specific RNAi knockdown of BicD (bicaudal D) will reduce Golgi outpost density in terminal dendrite branches by ≥50%, decrease the number of stable tertiary branch additions by ≥40% over a 24-hour live-imaging window in third-instar larvae, and reduce phospho-ERK (dpERK) immunofluorescence intensity at branch points by ≥30% relative to UAS-mCD8::GFP controls, all measured at 25°C in a w1118 background.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Golgi outpost displacement — Quantify the effect of neuron-specific BicD RNAi knockdown on Golgi outpost number, density, and motility in terminal dendrites of class IV da neurons using live ManII::GFP imaging in third-instar larvae
- Aim 2: Branching causality — Determine whether BicD-RNAi-induced Golgi outpost loss reduces stable tertiary dendritic branch additions over 24-hour live imaging, and whether BicD overexpression produces the reciprocal gain-of-function increase in branching
- Aim 3: Signaling epistasis — Test whether Golgi outpost positioning is upstream of local EGFR/dpERK signaling at branch points by assessing dpERK immunofluorescence in BicD-RNAi neurons and by performing rescue with constitutively active EGFR (UAS-EGFR.lambdatop) in the BicD-RNAi background

**Approach:** Using the ppk-GAL4 driver (expressed specifically in class IV da neurons) crossed to UAS-BicD-RNAi lines, we will acutely knock down BicD to disrupt dynein-dependent Golgi outpost anterograde transport in ddaC neurons without affecting neighboring cell types. Golgi outpost dynamics will be monitored by co-expressing UAS-ManII::GFP (a cis-Golgi marker) via live confocal imaging of filleted third-instar larval preparations over 24-hour intervals, recording outpost number, density per branch length, and motility parameters using kymograph analysis. Dendritic branching dynamics will be simultaneously quantified by co-expressing UAS-mCD8::RFP to label the full dendritic arbor, counting stable branch additions and retractions every 2 hours. Phospho-ERK (dpERK) immunofluorescence will be performed on fixed larvae at the wandering third-instar stage to assess local EGFR signaling at dendritic branch points, with intensity normalized to total ERK staining. Controls will include: (1) w1118; ppk-GAL4/UAS-mCD8::GFP (no RNAi), (2) UAS-lacZ-RNAi (scramble control), and (3) UAS-BicD overexpression to test the reciprocal gain-of-function prediction. To test causality directionality, we will also express constitutively active EGFR (UAS-EGFR.lambdatop) in BicD-RNAi backgrounds to determine whether activated downstream signaling rescues branching defects despite Golgi outpost mislocalization.

**Expected outcomes:** In ppk-GAL4 > UAS-BicD-RNAi larvae: (1) ManII::GFP-labeled Golgi outpost density in terminal branches will decrease by ≥50% versus controls (measured as outposts per 100 µm branch length; p < 0.01, Mann-Whitney U, n ≥ 15 neurons per genotype from ≥5 animals); (2) the number of stable tertiary branch additions over 24 hours will decrease by ≥40% (p < 0.01); (3) dpERK immunofluorescence intensity at branch points will decrease by ≥30% (p < 0.05, n ≥ 20 branch points per neuron, ≥10 neurons); (4) UAS-EGFR.lambdatop co-expression in BicD-RNAi neurons will rescue tertiary branch additions to within 20% of controls while failing to restore Golgi outpost density, demonstrating that Golgi outpost positioning is upstream of EGFR signaling in the branching pathway; (5) BicD overexpression will increase outpost density by ≥30% and increase stable branch additions by ≥25% versus controls.

**Novelty:** This experiment directly addresses the identified gap that 'the causal mechanism by which Golgi outpost repositioning triggers or responds to dendrite branching signals remains unknown — it is unclear whether outpost movement is upstream or downstream of local signaling events that stabilize new dendritic branches' (PMID 36512242). Prior work established a correlation between Golgi outpost positioning and dendrite branch growth (PMID 36512242) and demonstrated that BicD coordinates dynein-dependent localization of organelles and mRNA in Drosophila (PMID 16320833), but no published study has (a) causally dissected the Golgi outpost–branching signal relationship using acute motor manipulation, (b) linked BicD-dependent Golgi outpost transport specifically to local EGFR/dpERK signaling at dendritic branch points, or (c) performed the epistasis experiment with constitutively active EGFR to determine directionality of the Golgi outpost–signaling axis in da neurons.

**Feasibility:** All core techniques (ppk-GAL4-driven neuronal expression, ManII::GFP live imaging in larval fillets, dpERK immunofluorescence, dendritic arbor live imaging) are well-established in the field (PMID 36512242, 22425998). The primary technical risk is that BicD knockdown may cause pleiotropic cytoskeletal defects beyond Golgi outpost transport, confounding interpretation; this will be addressed by quantifying general microtubule polarity (UAS-EB1::GFP as a secondary readout) and ensuring RNAi efficiency is confirmed by Western blot on larval CNS lysates. A second risk is GAL4 leakiness or insufficient knockdown; UAS-Dcr-2 co-expression will be included to enhance RNAi efficacy if needed. Live imaging of larval fillets is technically demanding but standard with 2-3 months training. Estimated timeline: months 1-2, fly line assembly and RNAi validation; months 3-5, live imaging experiments and fixed dpERK immunofluorescence; months 6-8, epistasis experiments and statistical analysis. Total estimated timeline: 8-10 months for all three aims.

### Sonnet 5.5

**Hypothesis:** Proposed: In Drosophila stage 8-9 egg chambers, DDLC1-dependent anchoring of gurken mRNA at the oocyte dorsal-anterior corner is required continuously and acts independently of oocyte nucleus anchoring. Germline-specific ddlc1 knockdown induced after the nucleus has reached its dorsal-anterior position (temperature shift of tub-GAL80ts; matα4-GAL4-VP16>ddlc1 RNAi, 25°C to 29°C, mated w1118-background females) will cause gurken mRNA mislocalization (loss of a tight dorsal-anterior crescent) in ≥40% of stage 9 oocytes. In the same oocytes the nucleus will remain in the dorsal-anterior corner in ≥80%, versus ≤10% mislocalized gurken mRNA in GAL80ts-repressed controls. Falsification: if gurken mRNA mislocalization occurs in <15% of late-knockdown oocytes with an anchored nucleus, or if mislocalization occurs only in oocytes with a displaced nucleus, the hypothesis is rejected and the alternative model, in which nuclear position is upstream of gurken mRNA anchoring, is supported.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Temporal separation — test whether late, germline-specific ddlc1 knockdown after nuclear anchoring delocalizes gurken mRNA while the nucleus remains dorsal-anterior.
- Aim 2: Signaling readout — measure how late-onset gurken mRNA mislocalization alters Gurken protein distribution, dorsal follicle cell fate markers, and eggshell dorsal appendage phenotypes.
- Aim 3: Reverse-order test — displace the oocyte nucleus using a DDLC1-independent perturbation and quantify whether gurken mRNA follows or remains anchored.

**Approach:** Use a temperature-sensitive GAL80 system to separate the early role of DDLC1 in nuclear migration from its later role in gurken mRNA anchoring. Flies are raised at 18°C with GAL4 repressed, shifted to 29°C as adults for 24 h, 48 h or 72 h to knock down ddlc1 in the germline (matα4-GAL4-VP16 or a comparable germline driver, to be verified), and ovaries are dissected for fixed-tissue analysis. In stage 7-9 oocytes, gurken mRNA is detected by smFISH or in situ hybridization, nuclear position by Lamin/DAPI staining, and Gurken protein by immunostaining. Oocyte axes are defined with a membrane marker, and the nucleus-to-gurken-mRNA centroid distance is measured blind. Downstream EGFR signaling is read out by a dorsal follicle cell marker (to be verified) and by adult eggshell dorsal appendage phenotypes. Controls are GAL80ts-repressed (18°C) knockdown genotypes, driver-only and RNAi-only flies, and a second non-overlapping ddlc1 RNAi or a rescue construct to exclude off-target effects. A positive control for nuclear mislocalization is a known nuclear positioning perturbation (e.g., a BicD, Lis-1 or Khc loss-of-function condition, to be verified). Aim 3 reverses the order by testing whether a nucleus displaced independently of DDLC1 drags or leaves gurken mRNA behind.

**Expected outcomes:** Proposed predictions: (1) Early knockdown (continuous 29°C from larval stages) will give nuclear mislocalization and gurken mRNA mislocalization in ≥60% of stage 9 oocytes. (2) Late knockdown (24-48 h shift in adults) will give gurken mRNA mislocalization in ≥40% of oocytes with anchored nuclei in ≥80%, assessed by Fisher's exact test (p<0.01, n≥60 oocytes per genotype, ≥3 independent replicates). (3) Reduced dorsal Gurken-dependent follicle cell signaling, with ≥25% of eggs showing fused or reduced dorsal appendages after 72 h knockdown. (4) If nuclear position is upstream, the correlation between nucleus-to-gurken-mRNA centroid distance and gurken mRNA mislocalization will be high (Spearman rho >0.7) and late knockdown will not separate the two; this would falsify the hypothesis.

**Novelty:** The supplied literature reports that DDLC1 binds gurken mRNA and is required for its localization and for oocyte nucleus positioning, but the causal order between the two has not been tested. This design addresses the first identified gap by using temporal separation of the two DDLC1 functions rather than static mutants, and by linking each step to a signaling readout (Gurken-EGFR dorsal patterning), which has not been done in the supplied literature.

**Feasibility:** Estimated timeline is 9-12 months: 2 months for line acquisition and verification of knockdown efficiency, 4 months for the temporal knockdown and imaging series, 2-3 months for rescue and positive-control experiments, and 2 months for quantification. Pitfalls: incomplete temporal separation if DDLC1 or dynein persists after the shift, since protein turnover may delay the effect; ddlc1 RNAi may disrupt oogenesis or egg chamber survival beyond stage 8; GAL80ts leakiness at 18°C; and DDLC1 may act on the nucleus and mRNA through the same dynein complexes, so apparent independence would need confirmation with a second hairpin and rescue. Exact stocks and reagents must be verified before ordering.

## 46. Which genetic changes underlie the rapid seasonal adaptation seen in wild Drosophila populations?

PubMed search: `seasonal adaptation wild populations`, genes none, 5 papers

### Sonnet 4.6

**Hypothesis:** Epistatic interactions between seasonal alleles of Tep3 and Dro6 alter D. melanogaster immune phenotypes by modulating a shared innate immunity signaling node; specifically, flies carrying the autumn-associated allele combination of Tep3 and Dro6 (homozygous for both autumn haplotypes) will show ≥30% higher survival (proportion alive at 7 days post-infection) following Gram-positive bacterial infection (Enterococcus faecalis, OD600=0.1) compared to flies carrying spring-associated allele combinations, and this epistatic effect will be abolished (difference reduced to <10%) when Dro6 expression is knocked down via RNAi in the fat body under standard laboratory conditions (25°C, 12L:12D, w1118 background, mated females, n≥100 per genotype per replicate, 3 independent replicates).

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Allele-combination survival mapping — Generate isogenic CRISPR knock-in lines carrying all four Tep3×Dro6 seasonal allele combinations and quantify 7-day survival after E. faecalis and P. aeruginosa infection to determine whether epistasis is Toll-pathway-specific and non-additive
- Aim 2: Fat-body Dro6 necessity test — Use r4-GAL4-driven UAS-Dro6-RNAi in each Tep3 allelic background to determine whether fat-body Dro6 expression is necessary for the autumn allele-combination survival advantage
- Aim 3: Transcriptomic dissection of epistasis — Perform bulk RNA-seq on fat body and hemocytes from all four allele-combination genotypes at 6h and 24h post-infection to identify the downstream immune signaling nodes (AMP genes, pattern recognition receptors, signaling kinases) that are differentially regulated by the seasonal allele interaction

**Approach:** We will use CRISPR-mediated allele replacement to introduce autumn- and spring-associated SNP haplotypes of Tep3 and Dro6 individually and in combination into an isogenic w1118 background, generating four genotypic classes: autumn-Tep3/autumn-Dro6, autumn-Tep3/spring-Dro6, spring-Tep3/autumn-Dro6, and spring-Tep3/spring-Dro6. Immune challenge assays will use septic injury with E. faecalis (Gram-positive) and Pseudomonas aeruginosa (Gram-negative) to assess pathway specificity (Toll vs. Imd), measuring 7-day survival, pathogen load (CFU counts at 24h), and AMP expression (qRT-PCR of Drosomycin, Diptericin) as readouts. To test the mechanistic necessity of Dro6 in the epistatic effect, fat-body-specific RNAi knockdown of Dro6 will be performed using the r4-GAL4 driver crossed to UAS-Dro6-RNAi lines in each Tep3 allelic background, with UAS-mCherry-RNAi as a scrambled control. Tissue-specific transcriptomics (RNA-seq) of fat body and hemocytes dissected from the four allele-combination genotypes at 6h and 24h post-infection will identify differentially regulated nodes downstream of each allele combination. All assays will be conducted in mated females (5–7 days post-eclosion) at 25°C, 12L:12D, on standard cornmeal-yeast medium, with experimenters blinded to genotype.

**Expected outcomes:** Aim 1: Autumn-Tep3/autumn-Dro6 flies will show ≥30% higher 7-day survival than spring-Tep3/spring-Dro6 flies after E. faecalis infection (log-rank test, p<0.01); single-allele substitution lines will show intermediate survival, indicating non-additive epistasis. Aim 2: Fat-body-specific Dro6 RNAi in autumn-Tep3/autumn-Dro6 flies will reduce the survival advantage to <10% relative to spring-allele controls, confirming Dro6 fat body expression as necessary for epistatic effect (Fisher's exact test on survival proportions, p<0.05). Aim 3: RNA-seq will identify ≥50 differentially expressed immune genes (FDR<0.05, |log2FC|>1.5) in fat body of autumn vs. spring allele-combination flies at 24h post-infection, with pathway enrichment analysis implicating the Toll pathway (Drosomycin expression) as the primary downstream mediator; Gram-negative (Imd pathway) infection will show attenuated or absent allele-combination effects, providing pathway specificity.

**Novelty:** This experiment directly addresses two gaps identified in the literature synthesis: (1) the molecular basis of the Tep3–Dro6 epistatic interaction — described as discovered but mechanistically uncharacterized (gap 4: 'the molecular basis of this epistasis has not been characterized') — by using isogenic allele-replacement lines to isolate allele effects from background variation; (2) the tissue-specific regulation underlying seasonal immune adaptation (gap 3: 'genetic variants driving tissue-specific regulation have not been mapped to causal loci') by combining fat-body-specific RNAi with transcriptomics. Prior work (PMID 29321302) identified the epistatic phenotype in naturally segregating populations but could not distinguish allele-specific from background effects; no prior study has introduced defined seasonal haplotypes into a common isogenic background to test mechanistic necessity of each locus.

**Feasibility:** CRISPR allele replacement in D. melanogaster is routine but depends on the availability of precise seasonal SNP haplotype sequences from PMID 29321302 — these must be obtained from supplementary data or by direct contact with authors before design of guide RNAs. Generation of four knock-in lines is estimated at 6–9 months including validation by Sanger sequencing. RNAi lines for Dro6 may need to be constructed if not available at BDSC/VDRC; this adds 3–4 months. Survival assays and RNA-seq can run in parallel once lines are validated, adding 3–4 months. Total estimated timeline: 12–17 months. Key pitfalls: (a) seasonal haplotypes may involve multiple tightly linked SNPs rather than single-nucleotide changes, complicating precise allele replacement; (b) CRISPR insertion may disrupt regulatory elements if SNPs are in non-coding regions; (c) r4-GAL4 may have activity in tissues beyond fat body, requiring confirmation by UAS-GFP reporter; (d) E. faecalis inoculation dose must be titrated to achieve partial lethality (~50% in control genotype at day 7) to maximize power to detect survival differences.

### Sonnet 5.5

**Hypothesis:** Proposed (not established): In outbred populations reconstructed to differ only in seasonal Tep3 alleles (spring-type vs autumn-type) on a randomized genomic background, the autumn-type Tep3 allele will change 7-day post-infection survival by at least 15 percentage points relative to the spring-type allele in conventionally reared mated females at 25°C, this difference will shrink by at least 50% in axenic flies, and the Tep3 effect size will differ by at least 10 percentage points between spring-type and autumn-type Dro6 backgrounds.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Tep3 x Dro6 factorial effect — test in reconstructed outbred populations whether seasonal Tep3 alleles change post-infection survival and whether Dro6 alleles modify that effect.
- Aim 2: Microbiome dependence — test whether the Tep3 allelic effect is reduced in axenic flies and altered by gnotobiotic re-association with defined bacterial communities.
- Aim 3: Tissue requirement of Tep3 — use fat body- and hemocyte-specific RNAi to test where Tep3 expression is required for survival after infection.

**Approach:** Aim 1 uses a reconstructed-outbred-population design, as in the prior seasonal immunity work [29321302]. Wild-derived lines carrying defined spring-type or autumn-type Tep3 and Dro6 haplotypes (lines to be established and verified by genotyping) are intercrossed for several generations to randomize background, giving a 2x2 Tep3 x Dro6 factorial with at least 3 independent replicate cages per genotype class. Mated females 5-7 days old are reared conventionally or axenically (dechorionated embryos on antibiotic-free sterile food, verified by plating) and infected by septic pricking with a bacterial pathogen to be selected and validated (for example Providencia rettgeri, which is a proposal, not drawn from the supplied literature). The primary endpoints are survival over 7 days and bacterial load at 24 h. A secondary test uses tissue-specific Tep3 RNAi (fat body and hemocyte GAL4 drivers) against GAL4-only, UAS-only, and non-targeting RNAi controls to test whether Tep3 expression is required in those tissues. Gnotobiotic re-association with defined bacterial communities tests whether seasonal microbiome shifts [41611923] modify the allelic effect.

**Expected outcomes:** Proposed predictions: (i) In conventional flies, the Tep3[autumn] vs Tep3[spring] contrast will differ in 7-day survival by at least 15 percentage points (log-rank and Cox mixed-effects model with cage as random effect, P<0.05, at least 150 flies per genotype per condition). (ii) The Tep3 x microbiome interaction term will be significant (P<0.05), with the Tep3 survival difference reduced by at least 50% in axenic flies. (iii) The Tep3 x Dro6 interaction term will be significant (P<0.05) and the Tep3 effect will differ by at least 10 percentage points between Dro6 backgrounds. (iv) Fat-body or hemocyte Tep3 knockdown will reduce survival relative to controls by at least 15 percentage points. Falsification: no Tep3 main effect (<5 percentage points), no interaction terms, or no change under axenic conditions would reject the hypothesis.

**Novelty:** Behrman et al. identified seasonal Tep3 alleles and Tep3 x Dro6 epistasis as contributors to immune phenotypes in wild D. melanogaster. The literature synthesis notes that microbiome composition varies seasonally in D. suzukii, but whether microbiome variation interacts with seasonal host immune alleles has not been tested. This proposal tests that untested interaction by crossing seasonal Tep3/Dro6 genotype with microbiome state. It also adds tissue-resolved functional tests of Tep3, and so addresses part of the question of whether seasonal phenotypic shifts reflect genetic effects, plasticity, or an interaction between them.

**Feasibility:** Estimated timeline is about 12-15 months: months 1-4 to build and genotype reconstructed populations and select a pathogen and dose; months 5-9 for the factorial infection assays under conventional and axenic conditions; months 10-12 for gnotobiotic and RNAi experiments; months 13-15 for analysis. Pitfalls: seasonal allele identity and haplotype phase must be verified before construction; residual linkage to nearby loci in outbred populations may confound the effect; axenic rearing can alter development and fitness, so developmental time and body size must be matched; pathogen dose has to be calibrated to give 30-70% mortality in controls; RNAi efficiency must be confirmed by qPCR; the results are for D. melanogaster only, so generality to other species is untested.

## 47. How did host specialization in D. sechellia evolve at the level of sensory neurons and central circuits?

PubMed search: `Drosophila sechellia host specialization olfactory`, genes none, 7 papers

### Sonnet 4.6

**Hypothesis:** The loss of Gr39a.a and Gr28b.a in D. sechellia is accompanied by central taste circuit reorganization such that gustatory receptor neurons (GRNs) that ancestrally expressed these genes now project to altered glomerular targets in the subesophageal zone (SEZ), and restoring Gr39a.a or Gr28b.a expression in D. sechellia GRNs using the D. melanogaster promoter-GAL4 driver will rescue bitter-compound aversion (measured by two-choice preference index) by ≥40% relative to D. sechellia controls, while central circuit morphology (axon projection volume and glomerular innervation pattern quantified by confocal microscopy) will remain diverged from D. melanogaster by ≥30%, demonstrating that peripheral receptor loss and central circuit rewiring are partially dissociable evolutionary events under standard 25°C conditions in mated females.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: GRN neuroanatomy — Map and compare central axonal projections of Gr39a.a-GAL4- and Gr28b.a-GAL4-positive GRNs in D. melanogaster, D. simulans, and D. sechellia using UAS-mCD8::GFP and confocal microscopy to determine whether GRN projection targets in the SEZ have diverged during host specialization.
- Aim 2: Peripheral rescue behavioral dissection — Quantify the contribution of peripheral Gr39a.a and Gr28b.a receptor loss to reduced bitter aversion in D. sechellia by restoring each receptor individually and in combination using GAL4/UAS-mediated expression and two-choice CAFE assays with canavanine, coumarin, and octanoic acid.
- Aim 3: Central circuit functional imaging — Use GCaMP6s calcium imaging in GRNs and identified downstream SEZ interneurons to determine whether peripheral receptor restoration in D. sechellia is sufficient to restore ancestral second-order neuron response profiles, or whether central circuit divergence limits functional rescue independently of peripheral changes.

**Approach:** We will use a three-pronged approach combining cross-species receptor rescue, intersectional neuroanatomy, and calcium imaging. First, we will generate UAS-Gr39a.a and UAS-Gr28b.a transgenic lines carrying D. melanogaster coding sequences, then drive expression in D. sechellia GRNs using Gr39a.a-GAL4 and Gr28b.a-GAL4 drivers (derived from D. melanogaster promoter regions) introduced into D. sechellia by embryo injection, enabling peripheral rescue in a D. sechellia central circuit background. Second, we will map GRN central projections in D. sechellia and D. melanogaster using membrane-targeted GFP (UAS-mCD8::GFP) driven by the same GAL4 lines, acquiring confocal Z-stacks of SEZ and performing volumetric glomerular reconstruction to detect species differences in axonal projection targets and volumes. Third, we will perform two-choice capillary feeding assays (CAFE) and binary food choice assays on mated females (n ≥ 30 per genotype, at least three independent biological replicates) using canavanine, coumarin, and octanoic acid as test compounds to quantify preference indices; GCaMP6s-based calcium imaging in intact preparations will assess GRN and second-order neuron responses to these compounds before and after peripheral rescue. Controls will include D. melanogaster wild-type, D. sechellia wild-type, UAS-only and GAL4-only sibling controls, and D. simulans for phylogenetic comparison.

**Expected outcomes:** 1. Behavioral rescue: D. sechellia flies expressing UAS-Gr39a.a or UAS-Gr28b.a under the respective GAL4 driver will show preference indices for canavanine and coumarin that are ≥40% more negative (i.e., more aversive) than D. sechellia GAL4-only or UAS-only controls (two-tailed t-test, α = 0.05, power ≥ 0.8 at n = 30). 2. Peripheral physiology rescue: GCaMP6s responses in GRNs of rescued D. sechellia will recover to ≥50% of D. melanogaster response amplitudes for the relevant tastants. 3. Central circuit divergence persistence: Despite peripheral rescue, SEZ axonal projection volumes and glomerular innervation patterns in rescued D. sechellia will remain quantifiably different from D. melanogaster (≥30% difference in projection volume by Imaris volumetric analysis, ANOVA with post-hoc Tukey, α = 0.05), providing evidence for partial dissociability of peripheral and central evolutionary changes. 4. Second-order neuron activity: Calcium imaging of downstream SEZ interneurons will show attenuated responses to bitter compounds in D. sechellia wild-type relative to D. melanogaster, with partial but incomplete rescue in receptor-restored D. sechellia, mirroring the behavioral partial rescue.

**Novelty:** The literature synthesis explicitly identifies two major gaps this experiment addresses: (1) 'The specific gustatory receptor neurons expressing Gr39a.a and Gr28b.a, their central projections, and whether central taste circuits have co-evolved with peripheral receptor changes are unknown' (gap from PMID 36637369); and (2) 'The relative contributions of peripheral sensory neuron changes versus central circuit rewiring to D. sechellia's behavioral specialization have not been formally dissected; cross-species rescue experiments restoring ancestral receptor expression in central versus peripheral neurons would clarify this.' While PMID 32132713 performed analogous cross-species allele transfer for olfactory receptors, no equivalent experiment has been done for gustatory receptors or GRN central projections. This is the first proposed systematic comparison of GRN axonal projection anatomy between D. sechellia and D. melanogaster at glomerular resolution in the SEZ, and the first proposed peripheral-versus-central dissection for the taste modality in this species.

**Feasibility:** Transgenic line generation in D. sechellia is technically feasible but requires an established attP landing site in D. sechellia background; if unavailable, P-element injection can be attempted though efficiency is lower than in D. melanogaster. GAL4/UAS cross-species functionality in D. sechellia has been demonstrated in prior work (PMID 32132713 used analogous approaches for olfactory neurons), providing precedent. The main technical challenge is achieving sufficient GAL4 driver expression from D. melanogaster promoter sequences in a D. sechellia genomic background, which may require enhancer-trap or CRISPR knock-in strategies as fallback. Confocal imaging of D. sechellia SEZ is standard but requires anti-nc82 counterstaining for neuropil registration. Estimated timeline: months 1–6 for transgenic line generation and validation; months 7–12 for behavioral assays; months 13–18 for neuroanatomical mapping; months 19–24 for calcium imaging and data analysis. Potential pitfalls include incomplete GAL4 driver fidelity across species, compensatory upregulation of other GR genes in rescue backgrounds, and difficulty achieving statistically sufficient sample sizes for calcium imaging in D. sechellia due to smaller experimental throughput relative to D. melanogaster.

### Sonnet 5.5

**Hypothesis:** Proposed: In D. sechellia, host specialization depends on two separable neural changes: (i) increased olfactory sensory pooling onto interneurons, which promotes host-odor attraction, and (ii) peripheral gustatory loss of aversion, which only partly depends on Gr39a.a and Gr28b.a. We predict that (a) silencing the interneuron population that receives pooled input from the host-tuned olfactory neurons will reduce D. sechellia attraction to Morinda-fruit odor by ≥30% relative to genetic controls (25°C, mated females, 2-5 days old, p<0.05), without changing octanoic acid feeding acceptance by more than 10%; and (b) a Gr39a/Gr28b double loss-of-function in D. melanogaster will reduce aversion to some bitter compounds by ≥30% but will close less than 50% of the gap to D. sechellia in octanoic acid feeding aversion, which would implicate additional gustatory genes.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Causal role of olfactory pooling — silence the interneurons that receive pooled input from host-tuned olfactory neurons in D. sechellia and measure Morinda-odor attraction and octanoic acid feeding.
- Aim 2: Sufficiency of lost bitter receptors — test Gr39a and Gr28b single and double mutants with rescue in D. melanogaster against D. sechellia for bitter and fatty-acid aversion.
- Aim 3: Peripheral-central integration — combine olfactory-circuit and gustatory manipulations in D. melanogaster to test additive versus interacting effects on host-choice behavior.

**Approach:** Aim 1 uses D. sechellia with a GAL4 driver for the interneuron class that receives pooled input from the host-tuned olfactory neurons. The driver would be made by CRISPR knock-in or taken from existing D. sechellia resources, and its availability must be verified. It is crossed to UAS-Kir2.1 or UAS-TeTxLC for silencing and to UAS-GCaMP for imaging, with GAL4-only, UAS-only and inactive-toxin controls. Attraction to Morinda odor and to the host-tuned receptor's ligand is measured in a T-maze or trap assay. Octanoic acid feeding acceptance is measured in a capillary feeder (CAFE) or dye-based consumption assay to test separability from the olfactory phenotype. Aim 2 uses D. melanogaster Gr39a and Gr28b single and double mutants, generated by CRISPR or from existing stocks, with rescue transgenes. These are compared with wild-type D. melanogaster and D. sechellia in proboscis extension and consumption assays with several bitter compounds and fatty acids (octanoic, hexanoic), and with coumarin and canavanine as compounds for which the responsible genes are unresolved. Aim 3 combines the manipulations in D. melanogaster, by ectopic or enhanced convergence and by gustatory silencing, to test additive versus interacting effects on the choice between a Morinda-like odor plus octanoic acid source and a control source in a two-choice assay.

**Expected outcomes:** Proposed predictions, not established results: (1) Interneuron silencing in D. sechellia lowers the Morinda-odor attraction index by ≥30% versus controls (n≥10 trials of ~30 flies per genotype, one-way ANOVA with Tukey post hoc, p<0.05), while octanoic acid consumption is unchanged (<10% difference). (2) The D. melanogaster Gr39a/Gr28b double mutant shows ≥30% reduced aversion to a subset of bitter compounds. Its octanoic acid and other fatty-acid aversion remains at least 50% of the wild-type D. melanogaster to D. sechellia difference, with rescue restoring wild-type aversion. (3) Combined olfactory and gustatory manipulation in D. melanogaster yields a choice shift greater than the sum of single manipulations (interaction term p<0.05), or a purely additive effect, which would indicate independent peripheral and central contributions. If silencing does not change attraction, or if the double mutant fully reproduces D. sechellia aversion loss, the hypothesis is falsified.

**Novelty:** The literature synthesis identifies several untested gaps: the causal contribution of circuit-level changes such as increased sensory pooling and species-specific central projections to behavior, and the identity of the central neuron types involved, has not been tested; the genes and neurons responsible for lost aversion to fatty acids, coumarin and canavanine remain unresolved; and the central integration of attraction and loss of aversion is unaddressed. This plan tests the causality of pooling with cell-type-specific silencing and directly asks whether olfactory and gustatory evolution are separable or interacting contributors.

**Feasibility:** Estimated 18-24 months. Months 1-6: verify or build D. sechellia and D. melanogaster lines; months 6-14: behavior and imaging; months 14-24: combined manipulations, statistics and replication. Pitfalls: transgenesis in D. sechellia is lower throughput and may need CRISPR knock-in; the identity of the receptor and interneuron class must be confirmed from full text of the supplied circuit paper because only the abstract was summarized; D. sechellia behavior is sensitive to rearing diet and may need Morinda-supplemented or standard medium to be standardized; D. melanogaster is a surrogate and cannot fully reproduce multigenic D. sechellia evolution; interneuron silencing may have broader effects on general olfaction; and the human ortholog relevance is unverified.

## 48. Why is a gene essential in one Drosophila species but dispensable in a close relative?

PubMed search: `gene essentiality species divergence`, genes none, 2 papers

### Sonnet 4.6

**Hypothesis:** The sex-specific failure of D. simulans Nicknack to rescue male (but not female) D. melanogaster Nicknack-null flies is caused by incompatibility between divergent D. simulans Nicknack protein sequence and D. melanogaster male-specific heterochromatin composition on the Y chromosome and/or dosage-compensation-associated chromatin on the X chromosome; specifically, domain-swap chimeric constructs replacing the zinc-finger DNA-binding domain of D. melanogaster Nicknack with that of D. simulans will fail to rescue male viability (≤5% adult male eclosion relative to heterozygous controls) while retaining ≥50% female rescue efficiency, whereas chimeras retaining the D. melanogaster zinc-finger domain will rescue male viability to ≥40% of controls, under standard 25°C conditions in a w1118 genetic background.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Domain mapping of sex-specific rescue — Generate and validate a panel of D. melanogaster/D. simulans Nicknack chimeric transgenes (ZAD-swap, ZNF-swap, full-species) and quantify sex-stratified larval viability rescue in D. melanogaster Nicknack-null animals to identify which protein domain(s) are responsible for male-specific rescue failure.
- Aim 2: Genome-wide heterochromatin binding comparison — Perform sex-stratified H3K9me3 ChIP-seq and HA-ChIP-seq in third-instar larvae expressing each chimeric and full-species Nicknack to determine whether male-specific rescue failure correlates with loss of binding to Y-chromosomal or X-chromosomal pericentromeric heterochromatin domains.
- Aim 3: Dosage compensation interaction test — Assess whether non-rescuing Nicknack variants show altered co-localization or co-immunoprecipitation with D. melanogaster dosage compensation complex components (MSL proteins) in male versus female larval nuclei, to distinguish a heterochromatin composition model from a dosage-compensation-machinery incompatibility model.

**Approach:** Using the GAL4/UAS system combined with CRISPR/Cas9-generated Nicknack-null alleles in D. melanogaster (w1118 background), we will express UAS transgenes encoding (1) full-length D. melanogaster Nicknack, (2) full-length D. simulans Nicknack, and (3) a panel of chimeric Nicknack proteins swapping the ZAD domain, the linker region, and the zinc-finger (ZNF) array independently between the two species under the ubiquitous da-GAL4 or nos-GAL4 driver to assess larval and adult rescue. Rescue efficiency will be quantified as sex-stratified eclosion rates and larval lethality stages scored across ≥200 progeny per genotype in three independent crosses. To identify molecular divergence points, ChIP-seq for HA-tagged chimeric proteins will be performed in wandering third-instar larvae to map heterochromatin binding (marked by H3K9me3) genome-wide, comparing male and female samples separately; Y-chromosomal and X-chromosomal pericentromeric domains will be assessed for differential occupancy. Controls include UAS-GFP (negative), UAS-Dmel-Nicknack (positive), and heterozygous Nicknack/+ flies to establish baseline viability.

**Expected outcomes:** If the zinc-finger DNA-binding domain drives male-specific incompatibility, chimeras retaining the D. melanogaster ZNF array should rescue male eclosion to ≥40% of heterozygous sibling controls (vs. ≤5% for full-length D. simulans Nicknack), while female rescue remains ≥50% for all chimeras. ChIP-seq in male larvae should reveal that non-rescuing chimeras and full-length D. simulans Nicknack fail to bind Y-chromosomal pericentromeric heterochromatin (H3K9me3 domains) at ≥30% of sites occupied by D. melanogaster Nicknack, with no equivalent loss on autosomes. A statistically significant sex × genotype interaction in eclosion rates (two-way ANOVA, p < 0.01, n ≥ 200 per genotype, 3 biological replicates) would support the hypothesis. If instead all domain swaps equally disrupt male rescue, this would suggest the incompatibility arises from distributed sequence divergence or protein–protein interactions rather than DNA-binding specificity alone.

**Novelty:** The literature establishes that D. simulans Nicknack can localize to D. melanogaster heterochromatin yet fails to rescue male viability (PMID 33169670), but explicitly identifies as an open gap 'precisely which molecular differences in D. simulans Nicknack prevent rescue of male (but not female) D. melanogaster Nicknack-null flies' and whether 'sex-specific heterochromatin composition or dosage compensation machinery is the key divergence point.' No prior study has used domain-swap chimeras to dissect ZAD versus ZNF contributions to this sex-specific incompatibility, nor has ChIP-seq been applied in sex-stratified Nicknack-null rescue larvae to map differential heterochromatin targeting. This experiment directly addresses that identified gap with a mechanistic, domain-resolution approach.

**Feasibility:** All proposed genetic tools (CRISPR nulls, PhiC31 integration, GAL4/UAS) are standard in D. melanogaster and feasible in a well-equipped fly lab. Synthetic gene synthesis of chimeric coding sequences is commercially routine. The main technical risk is that chimeric proteins may misfold; this will be mitigated by testing protein expression and nuclear localization by immunofluorescence before proceeding to rescue assays. ChIP-seq from sex-sorted larvae requires careful staging and sexing (by gonad morphology at L3), adding labor; pooling ≥200 larvae per replicate is needed for sufficient ChIP input material. Estimated timeline: 3 months for transgene design and injection, 4 months for line establishment and balancing, 3 months for viability assays, 5 months for ChIP-seq and bioinformatics — approximately 15 months total. A potential pitfall is position-effect variation if multiple landing sites are used; all lines should be integrated at the same attP site (attP2) to control for this. Sex-specific Nicknack-null lethality must be confirmed to occur at the same developmental stage across chimeras before interpreting rescue data.

### Sonnet 5.5

**Hypothesis:** Proposed: Nicknack is dispensable for viability in D. simulans, where Nicknack-null homozygotes will be recovered at ≥70% of the expected Mendelian frequency, because the essential function of D. melanogaster Nicknack depends on a rapidly diverged zinc-finger array that recognizes a male-enriched heterochromatic target. A chimeric protein carrying the D. melanogaster zinc-finger array on the D. simulans backbone will rescue Nicknack-null D. melanogaster males to ≥50% of expected frequency at 25°C, whereas the unmodified D. simulans protein will rescue ≤10% of males.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Test dispensability in a close relative — generate Nicknack-null D. simulans and quantify viability, sex ratio, and fertility against Mendelian expectation.
