- Aim 2: Map the divergent functional region — compare D. melanogaster, D. simulans, and reciprocal ZNF/ZAD chimeric Nicknack transgenes for rescue of Nicknack-null D. melanogaster females and males.
- Aim 3: Identify the male-specific heterochromatic target — profile ortholog binding by CUT&RUN in each sex and test Y-chromosome dependence using altered-Y-content genotypes, with Oddjob as a paralog comparison.

**Approach:** Three approaches test the hypothesis (all proposed, none yet performed). (i) CRISPR/Cas9 Nicknack-null alleles are generated in D. simulans and viability is scored from heterozygous intercrosses, with D. simulans wild-type siblings as controls. (ii) In a Nicknack-null D. melanogaster background, genomic rescue transgenes (native promoter and UTRs, integrated at the same attP landing site) are compared: D. melanogaster Nicknack, D. simulans Nicknack, and two reciprocal chimeras swapping the zinc-finger array versus the ZAD/linker region. The unrescued null and the D. melanogaster rescue serve as negative and positive controls, and eclosion is scored separately by sex. (iii) CUT&RUN (or ChIP-seq) on epitope-tagged D. melanogaster and D. simulans Nicknack in larval tissue maps binding sites in each sex, and a Y-dependence test uses genotypes with altered Y content (e.g., X0 males and XXY females, stocks requiring verification). Oddjob is used as a paralog comparison to test whether any male-specific effect is unique to Nicknack.

**Expected outcomes:** Proposed predictions, not established results. (1) D. simulans Nicknack-null homozygotes are viable at ≥70% of the Mendelian expectation (n ≥ 300 progeny, binomial test against 100% expected, p < 0.05 for deviation). (2) D. simulans Nicknack rescues Nicknack-null D. melanogaster females and rescues ≤10% of males, consistent with prior observations [33169670]. (3) The chimera with the D. melanogaster zinc-finger array rescues males to ≥50% of expected, while the reciprocal chimera does not (Fisher's exact test, p < 0.01). (4) CUT&RUN shows a differential binding profile between the two orthologs at heterochromatic loci, with ≥2-fold enrichment (FDR < 0.05) at male-enriched or Y-linked sites for the D. melanogaster protein. Falsification: Nicknack-null D. simulans is lethal or sub-viable (<30% of expected), or the chimera fails to rescue males, in which case the ZNF array model is rejected.

**Novelty:** Kasinathan et al. (PMID 33169670) showed that Nicknack is necessary for larval development in D. melanogaster and that the D. simulans ortholog rescues Nicknack-null females but not males. The supplied literature identifies gaps this plan addresses. Whether Nicknack is dispensable in species that lack it or have lost it has not been tested. The male-specific rescue failure is unexplained, and the specific heterochromatic targets are not defined in the abstracts. This plan combines a loss-of-function test in a close relative with a domain-swap rescue to turn the heterochromatin-innovation model into a mapped, testable mechanism.

**Feasibility:** Estimated timeline is 14-18 months: 3-4 months for cloning and generating D. simulans mutants, 4-6 months for transgenic lines and rescue crosses, and 4-6 months for CUT&RUN and analysis. Pitfalls: (a) CRISPR editing in D. simulans is less routine than in D. melanogaster and may need optimization; (b) the D. simulans ortholog may be essential or partially required, which would falsify prediction 1 and shift the explanation toward regulatory or background differences; (c) transgene expression level and landing-site effects can confound rescue, so one landing site and qPCR/western expression checks are needed; (d) antibodies against Nicknack may be unavailable, so epitope tagging must be validated for function; (e) maternal contribution may mask early phenotypes in D. simulans, so maternal-zygotic nulls should be scored; (f) Y-content genotypes may have independent fitness effects, so the controls must be matched.

## 49. How does developmental temperature shape adult physiology and behavior, and which effects are adaptive?

PubMed search: `developmental temperature plasticity adult`, genes none, 8 papers

### Sonnet 4.6

**Hypothesis:** Developmental exposure to 29°C (versus 22°C) will reduce adult male fertility (sperm motility and offspring productivity) by ≥30% and reduce female egg production rate by ≥20% in w1118 D. melanogaster, and these effects will be causally mediated by ecdysone-responsive chromatin remodeling: RNAi-mediated knockdown of the ecdysone receptor (EcR) specifically during larval development (using tub-GAL80ts; arm-GAL4 or a heat-inducible larval driver) will attenuate ≥50% of the temperature-induced fertility reduction relative to GAL4-only controls, demonstrating that ecdysone-chromatin signaling is a necessary mechanistic link between developmental temperature and adult reproductive physiology.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Characterize developmental temperature effects on adult reproductive thermal performance curves — Quantify egg production rate, ovariole number, male sperm motility, and pair productivity across a range of adult assay temperatures (18, 22, 25, 29, 32°C) in w1118 flies reared at 22°C versus 29°C, establishing baseline TPC parameters for downstream mechanistic and adaptive tests.
- Aim 2: Test causal necessity of EcR-chromatin signaling in developmental thermal programming of adult fertility — Use tub-GAL80ts; da-GAL4 × UAS-EcR-RNAi to knock down ecdysone receptor specifically during larval development at 29°C and measure whether this rescues adult fertility deficits; validate mechanistic link using CUT&RUN for H3K27ac and H3K4me3 at ecdysone-responsive loci in L3 larvae from each temperature and genotype.
- Aim 3: Test adaptive versus constraint hypothesis for reproductive thermal plasticity — Conduct matched (developmental temperature = adult assay temperature) versus mismatched (developmental ≠ adult assay temperature) fitness assays across 22°C and 29°C in w1118 and in Indian versus Slovak population wild-type lines to determine whether plastic changes in egg production rate and ovariole number confer a fitness advantage in the thermally matching adult environment.

**Approach:** We will use a crossed developmental-temperature × genetic-manipulation design. Wild-type w1118 flies and UAS-EcR-RNAi flies crossed to a temporally controlled larval driver (tub-GAL80ts; arm-GAL4 or tub-GAL80ts; da-GAL4) will be raised at either 22°C or 29°C throughout larval development; GAL80ts will be inactivated at 29°C to allow GAL4-driven EcR knockdown specifically during larval stages, while control crosses (GAL4 alone, UAS alone, and w1118) will be run in parallel. Adults will be shifted to a common 25°C assay temperature to isolate developmental programming from acute adult temperature effects. Primary endpoints will be male sperm motility (CASA analysis or manual microscopy at three adult ages: day 3, 7, 14), male-female pair productivity (offspring counts over 5-day laying windows), and female egg production rate and ovariole number (dissection and staining). Chromatin state at ecdysone-responsive loci will be assessed by CUT&RUN for H3K27ac and H3K4me3 in wandering L3 larvae from each temperature and genotype. A matched-environment fitness assay (adults assayed at the same temperature as larval rearing: 22°C or 29°C) versus mismatched assay will test whether plasticity is adaptive (fitness benefit in matching environment) or constraining.

**Expected outcomes:** At 29°C developmental temperature, w1118 males will show ≥30% reduction in offspring productivity and sperm motility relative to 22°C-raised males (day-7 adults, 25°C assay); w1118 females will show ≥20% reduction in egg production rate. EcR-RNAi knockdown during larval development will attenuate these reductions by ≥50% (i.e., RNAi flies raised at 29°C will show fertility values within 15% of 22°C controls), while GAL4-only and UAS-only controls will show the full wild-type temperature effect. CUT&RUN will show reduced H3K27ac at ecdysone-responsive promoters (e.g., Br-C, E74, E75) in L3 larvae raised at 29°C versus 22°C, and this reduction will be absent or attenuated in EcR-RNAi larvae. Matched-environment assays will reveal that 29°C-reared adults have higher egg production at 29°C assay than 22°C-reared adults (≥10% benefit), supporting partial adaptive plasticity; mismatched controls will confirm that the effect is not solely due to acute adult temperature. Statistical threshold: p < 0.05 with Bonferroni correction for multiple comparisons; n ≥ 20 individuals per genotype × temperature group × replicate, minimum 3 independent biological replicates.

**Novelty:** This experiment directly addresses three identified gaps in the literature synthesis: (1) The molecular mechanisms by which developmental temperature reshapes thermal performance curves for reproduction 'remain uncharacterized at the molecular level' — we test whether ecdysone-chromatin signaling, proposed in butterflies and known to regulate Drosophila gene expression via chromatin remodeling (PMID 33740694) but never causally linked to developmental thermal plasticity of fertility in Drosophila, is necessary for this programming. (2) The 16 candidate genes for male thermal fertility limits 'have not been validated with targeted genetic manipulation' (PMID 35060127) — while we focus on EcR as an upstream regulator, our approach establishes the causal manipulation paradigm the field lacks. (3) 'Whether the observed developmental thermal plasticity in egg production rate and ovariole number is truly adaptive has not been tested with matched developmental and adult environment fitness assays' (PMID 37767738) — our matched/mismatched environment design provides the first direct test of the adaptive plasticity hypothesis for female reproductive traits.

**Feasibility:** All core components (GAL4/UAS, tub-GAL80ts temporal control, EcR-RNAi) are established Drosophila tools. The main technical challenge is achieving consistent 29°C larval rearing without lethality: tub-GAL80ts is inactivated at 29°C, which simultaneously serves as both the developmental temperature treatment and the GAL4 de-repression trigger — this elegant system has been used in the field but requires careful verification that GAL4-driven EcR knockdown does not cause larval lethality; if lethality occurs, a larval-specific driver such as Lsp2-GAL4 (fat body, mid-late larva) could be substituted. CUT&RUN in larval tissue requires dissection of ~50–100 L3 larvae per sample for sufficient chromatin input, which is labor-intensive but established. Population stocks from the Klepsatel laboratory require advance arrangement. Estimated timeline: months 1–2 line acquisition and crossing scheme establishment; months 3–5 developmental temperature rearing and adult phenotyping; months 6–7 CUT&RUN chromatin profiling; months 8–9 matched/mismatched fitness assays and data analysis. Potential pitfalls: (a) EcR knockdown may cause non-specific developmental defects confounding fertility readouts — inclusion of UAS-only and GAL4-only controls at both temperatures is essential; (b) temperature-sensitive GAL80 kinetics at exactly 29°C may be incomplete — pilot titration at 28°C and 30°C recommended; (c) sperm motility scoring requires either CASA setup or standardized manual blind scoring protocol.

### Sonnet 5.5

**Hypothesis:** Proposed (not established): in mated, outbred-background D. melanogaster females held at constant temperature, development at 29 °C will give a beneficial-acclimation advantage only at a matched adult temperature. Specifically, 29 °C-reared females kept at 29 °C will have ≥15% higher 14-day cumulative egg output than 25 °C-reared females kept at 29 °C. Conversely, 29 °C-reared females kept at 25 °C will have ≥15% lower egg output than 25 °C-reared females kept at 25 °C. A second proposed prediction is that partial, prothoracic gland-targeted knockdown of ecdysone signaling during development will reduce the 18 °C vs 29 °C rearing-induced shift in the adult locomotor thermal optimum by ≥50% relative to driver-only and UAS-only controls (alpha = 0.05, genotype x developmental temperature interaction).

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Beneficial acclimation test for reproduction — in a 3 x 3 developmental x adult temperature factorial, test whether matched temperatures raise 14-day female egg output by ≥15% over mismatched temperatures.
- Aim 2: Locomotor thermal performance and morphology — quantify how developmental temperature shifts locomotor Topt and breadth, and whether ovariole number explains the reproductive differences.
- Aim 3: Ecdysone dependence of developmental plasticity — test whether partial prothoracic gland-targeted ecdysone-pathway knockdown reduces the rearing-induced Topt shift by ≥50% relative to controls.

**Approach:** Aims 1-2 use a fully crossed design with 3 developmental temperatures (18, 25 and 29 °C) and 3 adult temperatures (18, 25 and 29 °C). Eggs are collected from parents kept at a common temperature, which limits maternal effects because direct developmental temperature is the main determinant of adult thermal responses, with small maternal effects [42365802]. Larval density is standardized (about 30 eggs per vial) with at least 6 replicate vials per cell and a pooled outbred base population. Adult readouts are daily egg output over 14 days (mated females with standardized males), ovariole number, and locomotor activity in Drosophila activity monitors across a temperature series, from which thermal optimum (Topt) and breadth are estimated by curve fitting. Aim 3 uses ecdysone-pathway knockdown driven by a prothoracic gland GAL4 (line identity requires verification), with driver-only and UAS-only controls at every rearing temperature. Gene-expression or ecdysteroid readouts are taken at the white-prepupal and mid-pupal stages. Because GAL4 activity is itself temperature-dependent, knockdown efficiency is verified by qPCR at each rearing temperature, and an alternative based on 20E feeding is held in reserve.

**Expected outcomes:** Proposed predictions: (1) A significant developmental temperature x adult temperature interaction for 14-day egg output (mixed model with vial as a random effect, p<0.05), with the matched 29/29 °C advantage of ≥15% over 25/29 °C, and the 29 °C-reared flies at least 15% below 25 °C-reared flies at 25 °C. If the interaction is absent, or one developmental temperature is best at every adult temperature, beneficial acclimation is rejected and the effects are better described as costs. (2) Reduced ovariole number in 29 °C-reared females that partly explains the egg-output differences, with locomotor Topt shifting upward after 29 °C rearing. (3) In Aim 3, controls show a rearing-induced Topt shift, while knockdown genotypes show a shift ≥50% smaller. Failure to meet this criterion would argue against a necessary ecdysone role.

**Novelty:** Prior work reports that development at 29 °C reduces adult locomotor activity [35027204], and reproductive fitness is maximized after development at 22-26 °C [37767738]. Developmental heat stress impairs adult reproductive traits (males more than females) [35060127]. The literature synthesis identifies that beneficial acclimation has rarely been tested directly for lifetime fitness, so whether developmental temperature effects are adaptive or merely costly is unresolved. This proposal directly crosses matched and mismatched developmental and adult temperatures for reproductive output and locomotor thermal performance in the same flies. It also tests, for the first time in D. melanogaster as far as the supplied literature shows, the butterfly-derived idea of ecdysone-mediated, possibly chromatin-based mechanisms [33740694], which the synthesis flags as untested in this species.

**Feasibility:** Estimated timeline is 10-12 months: about 2 months for stock setup and pilots, 4 months for the factorial reproduction and locomotor assays (Aims 1-2), and 4-5 months for Aim 3 including qPCR validation. Pitfalls: (a) 29 °C development lengthens or shortens development time, so adult age must be standardized by eclosion and not by egg-lay date. (b) Complete ecdysone disruption is lethal, so only partial knockdown is feasible. Survivor bias and developmental-delay confounds must be assessed, and the alternative is 20E feeding. (c) GAL4 activity is temperature-dependent, so knockdown level must be quantified at each rearing temperature. (d) Constant temperatures are not ecologically realistic, which limits the adaptive inference, and a fluctuating regime could be added later. (e) Exact stock identities and BDSC numbers have not been verified and must be confirmed before ordering.

## 50. How often do classic RNAi or GAL4-based phenotypes reproduce when retested with clean CRISPR alleles in a matched background?

PubMed search: `RNAi off-target CRISPR mutant phenotype reproducibility`, genes none, 4 papers

### Sonnet 4.6

**Hypothesis:** Among Drosophila melanogaster genes assigned a loss-of-function phenotype solely on the basis of RNAi hairpin or GAL4-UAS knockdown data (without independent genetic validation), ≥40% will fail to reproduce that phenotype when retested with matched-background CRISPR-generated null alleles in a panel of ≥20 candidate genes spanning at least three functional categories (ecdysone pathway, heterochromatin regulation, and metabolism), tested in w1118 background under standard 25°C conditions.

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Gene panel curation — systematically identify and rank ≥20 Drosophila genes with published RNAi/GAL4-UAS loss-of-function phenotypes that lack independent null allele validation, spanning ecdysone pathway, heterochromatin regulation, and metabolic/other functional categories, using a defined set of inclusion/exclusion criteria
- Aim 2: CRISPR null allele generation and validation — generate and sequence-confirm matched-background (w1118) CRISPR frameshift or deletion null alleles for all ≥20 panel genes using nos-Cas9 germline injection, and establish homozygous or balanced stocks for phenotyping
- Aim 3: Phenotype concordance assay — score each CRISPR null line and parallel UAS-RNAi line (in matched background, same driver) blindly for the originally reported phenotype, compute the false-positive rate with 95% CI, and identify molecular or structural gene features that predict RNAi-CRISPR concordance versus discordance

**Approach:** A panel of ≥20 genes will be selected from published RNAi/GAL4-UAS screens in which a loss-of-function phenotype (lethality, sterility, developmental delay, or morphological defect) was reported but no independent null allele validation exists. For each gene, a matched-background CRISPR null allele (frameshift or deletion confirmed by Sanger sequencing) will be generated in the w1118 background using gRNA pairs flanking the coding sequence, with Cas9 expressed from the germline via the nos-Cas9 transgene (BDSC #54591). Homozygous null lines will be established and scored blindly for the originally reported phenotype using identical assay conditions (developmental timing assays, viability counts, fertility assays, and confocal imaging of relevant tissues as appropriate to each gene's reported phenotype). Positive controls (known null phenotypes reproduced by CRISPR: e.g., cinnabar, ebony) and negative controls (uninjected w1118) will be included in every experimental block; for genes where RNAi phenotype is scored simultaneously, the same UAS-RNAi line crossed to the same driver in the same w1118 background will be run in parallel. Statistical analysis will use Fisher's exact test for categorical phenotype reproduction with Bonferroni correction (α = 0.05/20); the false-positive rate estimate and 95% CI will be computed across the full panel.

**Expected outcomes:** Based on the three documented cases in the literature synthesis (Cyp6t3, HP6, Wall/pkm) and the broader survey of young essential genes, we predict that ≥40% of candidate genes (i.e., ≥8 of 20) will show complete failure to reproduce the RNAi/GAL4-reported phenotype when tested as matched-background CRISPR nulls, with homozygous null flies being viable and fertile under standard conditions. We further predict that within the subset of false positives, ≥50% will show the original RNAi phenotype reproducible in the parallel RNAi arm, confirming the artifact arises from off-target silencing rather than assay failure. For the subset that does reproduce (predicted ≤60%), we expect a Pearson correlation of r ≥ 0.7 between severity of RNAi knockdown (measured by qRT-PCR, ≥80% transcript reduction) and CRISPR null phenotype score. Statistical threshold for declaring a false positive will be no significant difference (Fisher's exact test p > 0.05 after Bonferroni correction) between CRISPR null and w1118 wild-type controls on the primary phenotype metric reported in the original study.

**Novelty:** The literature synthesis explicitly identifies as a key gap that 'no systematic, genome-wide audit has been conducted to quantify the false-positive rate of RNAi or GAL4-UAS knockdown phenotypes in Drosophila when retested with matched-background CRISPR nulls — the current evidence comes from a handful of individual gene studies, making the global magnitude of the problem unknown.' The proposed experiment is the first systematic, multi-gene panel designed to empirically estimate this false-positive rate with statistical power, rather than reporting individual gene case studies. A secondary gap addressed is the lack of 'practical guidelines for deciding when RNAi data alone is sufficient versus when CRISPR validation is mandatory,' which the phenotype concordance analysis (Aim 3) directly targets.

**Feasibility:** CRISPR null generation in Drosophila is routine and well-established; generation of 20 null lines by injection, selection, and sequencing confirmation is achievable within 6–8 months with standard fly facility throughput (2–3 lines per injection batch, 3–4 batches). The primary technical risk is failure to recover homozygous viable null lines if the gene is truly essential — this is handled by maintaining balanced stocks and scoring lethality itself as a phenotype. A second pitfall is genetic background heterogeneity introduced during line establishment; all CRISPR lines must be backcrossed ≥3 generations into the reference w1118 background before phenotyping. Blind scoring and randomized block experimental design are essential to avoid confirmation bias. Estimated total timeline: 6–8 months for line generation, 3–4 months for phenotyping, 2 months for analysis and write-up, totaling approximately 12–14 months. Budget-limiting factor is sequencing confirmation of all alleles and qRT-PCR for the RNAi parallel arm. The candidate gene panel selection (Aim 1) is the critical bottleneck and must be completed before injection to ensure the panel is unbiased and representative.

### Sonnet 5.5

**Hypothesis:** Proposed: In a pre-registered, random sample of 40 Drosophila melanogaster genes with a published strong RNAi or GAL4-driven phenotype (pupal or earlier lethality, ≥2-day developmental delay, or sterility), no more than 50% will show the same phenotype class when tested with two independent CRISPR null alleles in an isogenized w1118 background at 25°C, and the lower bound of the 95% Wilson confidence interval for concordance will fall below 50%. The hypothesis is refuted if the observed concordance is ≥70% (lower 95% CI bound >50%).

**Aims:**
- Aim 1: Estimate concordance — measure the fraction of 40 randomly sampled published RNAi or GAL4-driven phenotypes reproduced by two independent isogenic CRISPR null alleles, with Wilson 95% CIs overall and by phenotype stratum.
- Aim 2: Partition discordance — for each discordant gene, use a second non-overlapping RNAi line, Cas9-only and driver-alone controls, backcrossing or mapping, and genomic rescue to assign RNAi off-target, background-allele, driver or other causes.
- Aim 3: Test condition dependence and redundancy — for discordant genes with a null phenotype-free result, test stress or altered diet and paralog double mutants to determine whether RNAi phenotypes are artifacts or reflect compensation or conditional function.

**Approach:** Genes will be drawn by random number generation from a pre-specified list of published RNAi or GAL4-driven phenotypes, stratified into lethality, developmental timing and fertility classes (about 13 genes each). Concordance criteria, effect-size thresholds (same phenotype class and at least half the published effect size) and the analysis plan will be registered before any crosses are made. For each gene, two independent CRISPR null alleles (frameshift or deletion, sequence-verified) will be generated in one isogenized w1118 background and tested as homozygotes and over a deficiency or the second allele. The original RNAi phenotype will be re-tested in parallel with the original line, at least one non-overlapping second RNAi line, driver-alone and landing-site controls, and the matched background. Discordant genes will go to a mechanism-partitioning step: RNAi off-target checks, a genomic rescue construct, backcrossing or recombination-mapping of background lethals, and Cas9-only control lines. Phenotyping will be done blind to genotype. Results for concordant and discordant genes will be reported in full.

**Expected outcomes:** Proposed prediction: Observed concordance of 25-50% (about 10-20 of 40 genes), with the Wilson 95% CI lower bound below 50%. Concordance is expected to be higher for essential-gene lethality than for subtle developmental-timing phenotypes. Among discordant genes, we predict that at least one-third will be explained by RNAi off-target effects (second non-overlapping RNAi line fails to reproduce) and at least one-fifth by background lethal or modifier alleles (phenotype segregates away from the target locus). Concordance will be compared between strata with Fisher's exact test (α=0.05), and a CI will be reported for each mechanism category. Finding concordance ≥70% would refute the hypothesis.

**Novelty:** Prior Drosophila reports on Cyp6t3, HP6, Wall and pkm are single-gene or small-set case studies in which CRISPR nulls did not reproduce earlier RNAi or somatic CRISPR phenotypes. No systematic, denominator-based estimate exists of how often RNAi or GAL4-driven phenotypes reproduce with clean CRISPR alleles in a matched genetic background. This design tests a random sample with a pre-specified denominator, reports concordant and discordant cases together to address selection bias, includes driver-alone and background-matched controls to address GAL4-specific confounds, and partitions discordance among RNAi off-target, background-allele and other causes. An Oncopeltus study found that RNAi phenocopied CRISPR homozygous mutants, so concordance is not uniformly poor.

**Feasibility:** Estimated timeline is 18 months: months 1-3 for sampling, pre-registration and isogenization; months 4-10 for CRISPR allele generation and verification (about 80 alleles; a gene whose null is lethal will need balancer-based stocks); months 8-14 for phenotyping and RNAi re-tests; months 13-18 for mechanism partitioning, rescue and analysis. Pitfalls: published RNAi phenotypes may be condition-specific, so the original assay conditions must be reproduced. Null alleles of essential genes need balancers and lethal-phase staging. Genetic compensation or paralog redundancy may cause real RNAi-specific phenotypes to be classed as artifacts, so a subset of discordant genes with paralogs should be tested as double mutants or under stress. Sample size gives a wide CI (about ±15 percentage points), so conclusions are about the broad range of concordance. Gene sampling depends on a complete list of eligible published phenotypes.
